Line Operators in 3d Holomorphic QFT: Meromorphic Tensor Categories and dg-Shifted Yangians
Tudor Dimofte, Wenjun Niu, Victor Py

Abstract
We study line operators and their OPE’s in perturbative 3d holomorphic-topological QFT’s, including holomorphic-topological twists (quarter-BPS sectors) of 3d 𝒩 = 2 \mathcal{N}=2 theories. In particular, we develop the representation theory of the category 𝒞 \mathcal{C} of perturbative line operators and its chiral tensor product, by generalizing techniques introduced by Costello and collaborators. We argue that lines are equivalent to modules for an A ∞ A_{\infty} algebra 𝒜 ! \mathcal{A}^{!} that’s Koszul-dual to bulk local operators. We further establish a non-renormalization theorem for the OPE’s of lines in a large class of theories (dubbed quasi-linear), allowing an exact resummation of quantum corrections. Based on physics arguments, we propose axioms for the full algebraic structure on 𝒜 ! {\mathcal{A}}^{!} , calling it a “dg-shifted Yangian,” which controls the OPE of lines. A key part of the structure is a Maurer-Cartan element r ( z ) ∈ 𝒜 ! ⊗ 𝒜 ! ( ( z − 1 ) ) r(z)\in\mathcal{A}^{!}\otimes\mathcal{A}^{!}(\!(z^{-1})\!) that satisfies an A ∞ A_{\infty} generalization of the Yang-Baxter equation. As examples, we consider 3d 𝒩 = 2 \mathcal{N}=2 gauge theories with arbitrary Chern-Simons levels, linear matter, and superpotential, and explicitly compute 1) perturbative bulk local operators (as A ∞ A_{\infty} -chiral algebras); and 2) the Koszul-duals 𝒜 ! \mathcal{A}^{!} (proving they are dg-shifted Yangians).



1
Introduction

Holomorphic-topological (HT) QFT’s are field theories defined on spacetimes locally of the form
\mathbb{C}^{m}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}
, whose correlation functions and underlying operator algebras have a hybrid local dependance on position: depending holomorphically on
\mathbb{C}^{m}
coordinates, and being locally independent of (
i.e.
depending topologically on)
\mathbb{R}^{n}
coordinates.
Globally, they may be defined on
(2m+n)
-manifolds with holomorphic foliations. Notable examples in 4d, on
\mathbb{C}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}
, include the HT twist (a particular BPS sector) of 4d
{\mathcal{N}}=2
theories
[
Kap06
]
and 4d Chern-Simons theory
[
Cos13a
]
. In 3d, every twist (or minimal BPS sector) of a 3d
{\mathcal{N}}=2
theory defines a HT QFT on
\mathbb{C}\times\mathbb{R}

[
CDF+12
,
ACMV17
,
CDG20
]
. Another class of 3d examples includes HT analogues of Poisson sigma-models
[
KZ25
]
. The 3d HT twist can also be thought of as a lift of the “half twist” of 2d
{\mathcal{N}}=(2,2)
models
[
Wit91
]
.
3d HT QFT’s also play a key role in celestial holography
[
CP22
,
Zen23
,
GP23
]
.

Recent years have seen many developments in the formal structure of HT theories, both physically and mathematically.
For example, it was shown that potential obstructions to perturbative quantization of a classical theory vanish as long as
n\geq 1

[
GRW21
]
. It was also shown that an analogue of the Kontsevich formality theorem holds and renders the perturbative algebra of local operators one-loop exact and free of nontrivial higher operations as long as
n\geq 2

[
GRW21
,
GKW24
]
.

Two important cases of the algebra
{\mathcal{A}}
of local operators are:

•

m=1,n=2
(locally
\mathbb{C}\times\mathbb{R}^{2})
:
{\mathcal{A}}
is an (
E_{2}
,chiral) algebra, which implies that its cohomology is a commutative vertex algebra, with a
\lambda
-bracket of cohomological degree (or ghost number)
-2

[
OY19b
]
. In a twisted 4d
{\mathcal{N}}=2
superconformal theory, it was shown by
[
OY19a
,
Jeo19
]
that
{\mathcal{A}}
“quantizes” to the 4d VOA’s of
[
BLL+13
]
; its graded character computes the 4d Schur index
[
KMMR05
,
GRRY11
]
.

•

m=1,n=1
(locally
\mathbb{C}\times\mathbb{R}
):
{\mathcal{A}}
is an (
E_{1}
,chiral) algebra, which implies that its cohomology is a commutative vertex algebra, with a
\lambda
-bracket of degree
-1

[
OY19b
]
. Some of the first nontrivial examples were worked out in
[
CDG20
,
Zen21
]
.
In a twisted 3d
{\mathcal{N}}=2
theory, the graded character of
{\mathcal{A}}
computes its 3d index
[
Kim09
,
IY11
,
KSV11
,
KW11
]
.
A general geometric perspective on such algebras, based on “raviolo” moduli spaces, was developed in
[
GW23
]
. A feature of the
n=1
case that we will (by necessity) develop in this paper is that, in any interacting theory,
{\mathcal{A}}
is also endowed with higher
A_{\infty}
-like operations.
1
1

1

The higher operations should in principle be governed geometrically by a chain-level model of sheaves on the raviolo, as discussed in
[
AKY24
]
, though this has not yet been made fully concrete.

Our main goal is to move beyond local operators, and initiate a study of
line operators
in 3d HT theories, focusing on their local structure and associated algebraic properties. Since we are just interested in local structure, we’ll fix spacetime to be
\mathbb{C}_{z}\times\mathbb{R}_{t}
. Lines operators extended along
\mathbb{R}_{t}
, placed at a point
z\in\mathbb{C}_{z}
, are expected to form an
A_{\infty}
category
{\mathcal{C}}
, whose morphisms are defined as spaces of local operators at junctions:

(1.1)

Moreover, the OPE of lines at nearby points should define a chiral tensor product in
{\mathcal{C}}
,
i.e.
a coherent family of tensor products
\otimes_{z}:{\mathcal{C}}\boxtimes{\mathcal{C}}\to{\mathcal{C}}
parameterized by
z\in\mathbb{C}^{*}
. Thus, roughly speaking,
{\mathcal{C}}
should be the categorical analogue of a VOA: the extra topological ‘time’ direction
\mathbb{R}_{t}
on which lines are extended increases the category level.

The main results of this paper, outlined in Sec.
1.1
, provide a concrete and computationally effective definition of the category
{\mathcal{C}}
, including its OPE, in any perturbative 3d HT QFT satisfying some mild constraints — including any HT-twisted 3d
{\mathcal{N}}=2
gauge theory, with Chern-Simons couplings, matter, and superpotential. Our approach generalizes the methods introduced by Costello and collaborators to study perturbative defects in QFT
[
Cos13a
,
CWY17
,
CP20
]
, reviewed in
[
PW21
]
. A key feature of this approach is the emergence of an
A_{\infty}
algebra
{\mathcal{A}}^{!}
, Koszul-dual to local operators, such that

{\mathcal{C}}\simeq{\mathcal{A}}^{!}\text{-mod}\,.

(1.2)

As we determine
{\mathcal{A}}^{!}
, we’ll find that in 3d HT QFT’s it carries a new sort of algebraic structure that controls the OPE in
{\mathcal{C}}
; we call this structure a
dg-shifted Yangian
, by analogy with the (generalized) Yangians that appear in 4d HT QFT’s
[
Cos13a
,
CWY17
,
CWY18
,
CW18
,
AN24a
]
.

Mathematically, categories like
{\mathcal{C}}
with a chiral tensor product have been formalized in several different ways. The formalism closest to our approach, and to the physical idea of OPE’s, is Soibelman’s notion of a
meromorphic tensor category

[
Soi97
]
, introduced in the 90’s. This was directly motivated by representation theory of Yangians. A closely related notion is that of a
chiral category
, also known as a
factorization category
, which arose during the development of the geometric Langlands program
[
Gai08
,
Ras21
]
, and later formalized in
[
Gai10
,
Ras14
]
. Chiral categories are not a single category, but rather a sheaf of categories defined over configuration spaces of points on an algebraic curve; the OPE
\otimes_{z}
may (in principle) be reconstructed from this data. Chiral categories are to meromorphic tensor categories what Beilinson-Drinfeld’s chiral algebras
[
BD04
]
are to VOA’s. We won’t use chiral categories in this paper, though they should provide an alternative, conceptually powerful framework.
2
2

2

See
[
HR21
]
for a construction of line operators in topologically twisted 3d
{\mathcal{N}}=4
theories using the perspective of chiral categories.

From a physics perspective, if a 3d HT theory is the twist of a 3d
{\mathcal{N}}=2
QFT, the objects of the category
{\mathcal{C}}
are just the half-BPS line operators in the
{\mathcal{N}}=2
theory. These have been widely studied in many contexts over the past two decades, albeit not (to our knowledge) as a full category with OPE’s.
As codimension-two defects, half-BPS lines of 3d
{\mathcal{N}}=2
gauge theories are closely related to Gukov-Witten defects of 4d Yang-Mills
[
GW06
]
, and their “wild” generalizations
[
Wit07
]
. BPS Wilson lines in 3d
{\mathcal{N}}=2
gauge theory were constructed in
[
GY07
]
; and a basic set of vortex lines, induced from brane intersections, in
[
DGY08
]
. Some of the first computations of partition functions on
S^{3}
and
S^{2}\times S^{1}
with line operators inserted were performed in
[
KWY09
,
KWY12
,
DOP12
]
, later generalized to Seifert manifolds
[
CDFK12
,
CK16
]
. In terms of our category
{\mathcal{C}}
, the
S^{2}\times S^{1}
partition functions compute characters of morphism spaces:

(1.3)

This relation follows from a state-operator correspondence;
cf.

[
GKL12
,
CGS16
]
for an analogous construction in 4d.

Another key observable related to BPS lines in a 3d
{\mathcal{N}}=2
theory is the algebra
{\mathcal{K}}
of loop operators wrapped on the fiber of a twisted
\mathbb{C}\times S^{1}
compactification.
The central role of
{\mathcal{K}}
in gauge theory was brought to light in the Bethe-gauge correspondence
[
NS09
]
, where
{\mathcal{K}}
is computed as the Jacobian ring of an effective twisted superpotential, and identified with Bethe vacua.
In a sigma-model (
e.g.
the IR limit of a gauge theory),
{\mathcal{K}}
becomes the quantum K-theory ring
[
GL01
]
of the target. The relation between quantum K-theory classes and loop operators was developed in
[
JM18
,
JMNT19
,
CK23
]
, and is connected to the action of K-theory on elliptic stable envelopes
[
AO16
,
AO17
]
. We expect that
{\mathcal{K}}
is the cyclic Hochschild homology of our line-operator category
{\mathcal{C}}
,

{\mathcal{K}}\simeq HH_{\bullet}^{S^{1}}({\mathcal{C}})

(1.4)

with a product induced from the OPE in
{\mathcal{C}}
. We hope to explore the connection between
{\mathcal{K}}
and
{\mathcal{C}}
in future work; establishing an isomorphism as in (
1.4
) will likely require a fully nonperturbative description of
{\mathcal{C}}
.

We finally note that an important motivation behind the initial work on 4d HT Chern-Simons and the Yangian
[
Cos13a
,
Cos13b
,
CWY17
,
CWY18
]
was to establish new connections between HT QFT’s and integrability. These connections have been expanded in vast directions in recent years, most involving string theory and/or higher-dimensional QFT’s and defects. A reduction of these ideas to 3d HT theories appeared in
[
VW22
]
, who showed that the state space of HT-twisted pure gauge theory with line operators reproduces the phase space of the finite Gaudin model. It would be very interesting to incorporate the categories and shifted Yang-Baxter equation of this paper in the Gaudin model and related integrable systems; we won’t explore this here.

We’ll now describe in greater detail our methods and results.

1.1
Structure and results

The main perspective on line operators used in this paper is to construct them by coupling bulk 3d HT QFT to 1d topological quantum mechanics.
This is a classic method for defining a large class of line operators. For example, Wilson lines are typically represented by coupling a bulk gauge connection to the state space of a 1d massive particle. Even disorder operators can often be represented this way, by using the coupling to 1d degrees of freedom to source a desired singularity in the bulk fields. In brane constructions of line operators, the 1d quantum mechanics is supplied directly by open-string states on brane intersections.
We will exclusively focus on a perturbative sector of 3d HT QFT’s, where, by definition,
every
line operator
\ell\in{\mathcal{C}}
admits a description as coupling to some quantum mechanics.

In Section
2
, we’ll describe more precisely what we mean by a perturbative 3d HT QFT. We’ll work in the BV-BRST formalism, which is well adapted to capture the field and operator content in an HT theory, naturally organizing fields into holomorphic-topological descendants. We’ll review, following
[
ACMV17
,
CDG20
]
, how twisted 3d
{\mathcal{N}}=2
theories look in this formalism. We’ll also review 1d topological quantum mechanics in a similar vein. Structurally, we will recall that in quantum mechanics the state space
V
is a dg vector space and the operator algebra
\text{End}(V)
is a dg algebra; and that interaction terms in the QM action are in 1-1 correspondence with Maurer-Cartan elements
\mu\in\text{End}(V)
,
i.e.

\mu
of cohomological degree 1 satisfying
Q(\mu)+\mu^{2}=0
.

In Section
3
, we then couple 1d and 3d, following
[
Cos13a
,
CP20
,
PW21
]
, to produce a uniform description of line operators. Given a 3d HT QFT
{\mathcal{T}}
with an algebra of bulk local operators
{\mathcal{A}}
, we define objects
\ell\in{\mathcal{C}}
to be pairs
(V_{\ell},\mu_{\ell})
where
V_{\ell}
is a dg vector space (the state space of some 1d QM) and

\mu_{\ell}\in\text{End}(V_{\ell})\otimes{\mathcal{A}}\,,\qquad\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}m_{k}(\underbrace{\mu_{\ell},...,\mu_{\ell}}_{k})=0

(1.5)

is a Maurer-Cartan element in the product
\text{End}(V_{\ell})\otimes{\mathcal{A}}
, now satisfying the
A_{\infty}
MC equation. We’ll give some examples of how this translates to more familiar descriptions, including vortex lines for matter with and without superpotentials, and Wilson lines in gauge theories.

The trickiest part of this description of line operators is understanding how and why bulk local operators
{\mathcal{A}}
form an
A_{\infty}
algebra — to feed into (
1.5
). The products we’re talking about come from bulk local operators placed along a fixed line in the
\mathbb{R}_{t}
direction, colliding with each other. In
Q
-cohomology, the product should be associative, as well as commutative, due to the existence of the transverse
\mathbb{C}_{z}
direction. However, associativity (and commutativity) only hold up to
Q
-exact terms, which are encoded in the aforementioned
A_{\infty}
operations. Some of these operations were already observed in
[
CDG20
]
, and a general scheme to compute them in perturbative HT theories was developed in
[
GKW24
]
.

We will ultimately compute
{\mathcal{A}}
explicitly as an
A_{\infty}
algebra (in fact, an (
A_{\infty}
,chiral) algebra) in any perturbative HT theory in Section
6
. We defer this until later in the paper because the analysis is a bit technical, and mostly not needed for discussing OPE’s. We do the computation in Sec.
6
by first reducing the 3d theory to a 2d B-model along a holomorphic cigar
\mathbb{C}_{z}
, and analyzing 2d Feynman diagrams. We introduce a mild constraint on bulk interaction terms, called
quasi-linearity
(Sec.
4.2
), which is satisfied by all HT-twisted 3d
{\mathcal{N}}=2
gauge theories with linear matter, superpotential, and CS terms. We’ll argue that

Proposition
6.1

In any perturbative quasi-linear 3d HT theory, both local operators
{\mathcal{A}}
and its Koszul dual
{\mathcal{A}}^{!}
(which we’ll come to later) are determined exactly at tree level, with
A_{\infty}
operations computed by derivatives of the 3d interaction terms.

For example, for 3d
{\mathcal{N}}=2
matter with superpotential
W
,
{\mathcal{A}}
turns out to be a chiral enhancement of the derived critical locus of
W
,
cf.
(
1.14
) below.

An important consequence of the structure of
A_{\infty}
algebras in perturbative HT QFT’s is that any line operator can be represented by
linear
couplings to bulk local operators,
i.e.

\mu_{\ell}=\sum_{i}M^{i}{\mathcal{O}}_{i}
with
M^{i}\in\text{End}(V_{\ell})
and
{\mathcal{O}}_{i}
a linear functional of the bulk fields. (See Sec.
5.2.3
.) We’ll assume linear couplings throughout earlier sections of the paper to simplify our analysis.

In Section
4
, we move on to OPE’s. We consider a configuration of two operators
\ell(z),\ell^{\prime}(0)
, placed at nearby points
z,0\in\mathbb{C}_{z}
, and define their OPE
\ell\otimes_{z}\ell^{\prime}(0)
by asking what line operator at
0\in\mathbb{C}_{z}
would behave the same way in all correlation functions. We compute
\ell\otimes_{z}\ell^{\prime}(0)
explicitly by summing quantum corrections to the insertions of their couplings to 1d QM, and thus establish a key non-renormalization theorem:

Theorem
4.1

In a quasi-linear 3d HT QFT, if
\ell,\ell^{\prime}
are defined by (linear) MC elements
\mu_{\ell},\mu_{\ell^{\prime}}
, then the singular part of the OPE is given by a new MC element

\begin{array}[]{c}\ell(z)\;\leftrightarrow\;(V_{\ell},\mu_{\ell}(z))\,,\qquad\ell^{\prime}(0)\;\leftrightarrow\;(V_{\ell^{\prime}},\mu_{\ell^{\prime}}(0))\\[5.69046pt]
\hskip-28.45274pt\Rightarrow\qquad\ell\otimes_{z}\ell^{\prime}(0)\;\leftrightarrow\;\big(V_{\ell}\otimes V_{\ell^{\prime}},\,\mu_{\ell}(z)+\mu_{\ell^{\prime}}(0)+r_{\ell,\ell^{\prime}}(z)\big)\end{array}

(1.6)

where
r_{\ell,\ell^{\prime}}(z)
is 1) given exactly by a sum of tree-level “ladder” diagrams, which are independent of bulk interactions; 2) independent of bulk fields; and 3) in axial gauge, an element of
\text{End}(V)\otimes\text{End}(V_{\ell^{\prime}})[z^{-1}]
.

In other words, the quantum correction to the OPE of lines is encoded in a correction
r_{\ell,\ell^{\prime}}(z)
to the naive MC element
\mu_{\ell}(z)+\mu_{\ell^{\prime}}(0)
, which takes the same universal form independent of bulk interactions — it can be computed in free field theory.

The remainder of the paper is devoted to working out the
representation theory
underlying the category
{\mathcal{C}}
and its OPE, by applying and generalizing the Koszul-duality methods of
[
Cos13a
,
CP20
,
PW21
]
. We frame the results from the perspective of Tannakian reconstruction in QFT, as reviewed in
[
DN24
]
. (The Tannakian perspective also underlay the construction of the Yangian from Koszul duality in
[
Cos13a
]
.)

In Section
5
, for each perturbative 3d HT QFT, we introduce an
A_{\infty}
algebra
{\mathcal{A}}^{!}
that’s the mathematical Koszul dual of bulk local operators
{\mathcal{A}}
. It’s constructed physically by placing the 3d theory on a compact spatial geometry
\mathbb{CP}^{1}_{z}
, with a mild singularity ‘
{\mathcal{B}}_{\infty}
’ at
z=\infty
that trivializes the state space, so
\text{States}(\mathbb{CP}^{1},{\mathcal{B}}_{\infty})\simeq\mathbb{C}
. Then we define
{\mathcal{A}}^{!}
to be local operators supported at
z=\infty
:

{\mathcal{A}}^{!}:=\begin{array}[]{l}\text{$A_{\infty}$ algebra of local ops supported}\\
\text{on the `defect' ${\mathcal{B}}_{\infty}$ at $z=\infty$}\end{array}\qquad\raisebox{-36.135pt}{\includegraphics[width]{CP1-algebra-intro}}

(1.7)

We argue that there is a faithful representation of the category of lines

\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}:{\mathcal{C}}\overset{\sim}{\rightarrow}{\mathcal{A}}^{!}\text{-mod}\,,\qquad\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}(\ell)=\text{States}(\mathbb{CP}^{1},\ell(0),{\mathcal{B}}_{\infty})=V_{\ell}

(1.8)

that sends each line
\ell
to the vector space
V_{\ell}
used to construct it by coupling to QM. We then seek to reconstruct algebraic structures on
{\mathcal{A}}^{!}
that encode the OPE of lines in
{\mathcal{C}}
, ultimately leading (Section
5.4
) to the definition of a generalized
dg-shifted Yangian
.

We say that
{\mathcal{Y}}
is a dg-shifted Yangian if it’s (roughly)

•

an
A_{\infty}
algebra, triply graded by ghost number (cohomological degree), spin (loop rotation/conformal degree), and fermion number (parity), with

•

translation isomorphisms
\tau_{z}:{\mathcal{Y}}\to{\mathcal{Y}}[z]
satisfying
\tau_{z}\circ\tau_{w}=\tau_{z+w}
,

•

a Maurer-Cartan element
r(z)\in{\mathcal{Y}}\otimes{\mathcal{Y}}(\!(z^{-1})\!)
, of degrees
(1,0,\text{odd})

•

a coproduct
\Delta_{z}:{\mathcal{Y}}\to{\mathcal{Y}}\otimes_{r(z)}{\mathcal{Y}}(\!(z^{-1})\!)
that’s an
A_{\infty}
algebra morphism, where the RHS denotes
{\mathcal{Y}}\otimes{\mathcal{Y}}(\!(z^{-1})\!)
deformed by the MC element
r(z)

We conjecture that

Conjecture
5.2

The Koszul-dual algebra
{\mathcal{A}}^{!}
representing line operators in any perturbative 3d HT QFT is a dg-shifted Yangian.

The coproduct and MC element
r(z)
here simply repackage the OPE (
1.6
) in a universal form. For example, evaluating
r(z)
in any representation
V_{\ell}\otimes V_{\ell^{\prime}}
corresponding to line operators, it becomes
r_{\ell,\ell^{\prime}}(z)
.

Remarkably, the Maurer-Cartan element
r(z)
ends up satisfying many relations reminiscent of a classical r-matrix. In particular, associativity of the OPE requires
r_{23}(z)+(\text{id}\otimes\Delta_{z})(r(z+w))=r_{12}(w)+(\Delta_{w}\otimes\text{id})(r(z))=r_{12}(w)+r_{13}(z+w)+r_{23}(z)
. This in turn implies an
A_{\infty}
generalization of the
Yang-Baxter
equation: letting
\text{MC}(x):=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}m_{k}(x,...,x)
, we’ll show that

\begin{array}[]{l}0=\text{MC}(r_{12}(w)+r_{13}(z+w)+r_{23}(z))-\text{MC}(r_{12}(w))-\text{MC}(r_{13}(z+w))-\text{MC}(r_{23}(z))\\[2.84544pt]
\phantom{0}=[r_{12}(w),r_{13}(z+w)]+[r_{12}(w),r_{23}(z)]+[r_{13}(z+w),r_{23}(z)]+\text{higher $m_{k\geq 3}$ ops}\end{array}

(1.9)

We emphasize, though, that there is no actual braiding in the category
{\mathcal{C}}
!

One way to interpret the relation between
r(z)
and an R-matrix is suggested by Koszul duality itself. In a 3d perturbative topological QFT, local operators
{\mathcal{A}}
have a
(-2)
-shifted Poisson bracket; their Koszul dual
{\mathcal{A}}^{!}
naturally carries an R-matrix that’s dual to the Poisson bracket in
{\mathcal{A}}
, and controls braiding for line operators
[
Lur09
]
,
cf.

[
DN24
, App. C]
. Similarly, in 4d HT QFT, such as holomorphic Chern-Simons the spectral R-matrix of
{\mathcal{A}}^{!}
is dual to the
(-2)
-shifted
\lambda
-bracket in
{\mathcal{A}}
. In 3d HT QFT, we’d expect to find
some
element of degree 1 in
{\mathcal{A}}^{!}\otimes{\mathcal{A}}^{!}
, behaving like a spectral R-matrix, that’s dual to the
(-1)
-shifted
\lambda
-bracket in
{\mathcal{A}}
— and it’s our
r(z)
. Explicitly:

r(z)=\sum_{\alpha,\beta}\Phi^{\alpha}\otimes\Phi^{\beta}\int_{0}^{\infty}d\lambda\,e^{-\lambda z}\,\{\Phi_{\alpha}{}_{\,\lambda\,}\Phi_{\beta}\}

(1.10)

where
\{\Phi_{\alpha}\}
are linear generators for
{\mathcal{A}}
and
\{\Phi_{\alpha}\}
are dual generators for
{\mathcal{A}}^{!}
(App.
A
).

Some further analogies between
r(z)
and R-matrices are discussed in Section
5
. More so, in the companion paper
[
NP25
]
, the second two authors explain how dg-shifted Yangian structures arise naturally from quantization of
1-shifted
Lie bialgebras.

Armed with the explicit computations of
{\mathcal{A}}
and
{\mathcal{A}}^{!}
from Section
6
, we then prove in Section
7
that

Theorem
7.1

The
{\mathcal{A}}^{!}
algebras in HT-twisted 3d
{\mathcal{N}}=2
gauge theories, and matter with superpotential, do have the full structure of dg-shifted Yangians.

This is a purely algebraic statement and derivation. It’s established by combining methods from
A_{\infty}
algebras with contour-integral manipulations more familiar from VOA’s. It would be interesting to prove an analogous result for the recent HT Poisson sigma models of
[
KZ25
]
as well (we don’t consider them here).

1.2
The simplest example

To make the abstract discussion above slightly more concrete, we consider the simplest possible example: the HT twist of a free 3d
{\mathcal{N}}=2
chiral multiplet. It leads to a category
{\mathcal{C}}
that’s the anologue of a free-field VOA in 2d holomorphic QFT. However, just like free-field VOA’s still have non-trivial (albeit simple) OPE’s, line operators in
{\mathcal{C}}
also have nontrivial OPE’s.

The twisted field theory in this case is determined by a bosonic field
X(z)
and a fermionic field
\psi(z)
, whose equations of motion are
d^{\prime}X=d^{\prime}\psi=0
, with
d^{\prime}={\partial}_{t}dt+{\partial}_{\bar{z}}d\bar{z}
. If we expand in modes

X(z)=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}X_{n}z^{-n-1}\,,\qquad\psi(z)=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\psi_{n}z^{-n-1}

(1.11)

then local operators are constructed from the regular
n<0
modes. Conversely, the Koszul dual
{\mathcal{A}}^{!}
is the free graded-commutative algebra generated by singular modes

\qquad{\mathcal{A}}^{!}=\mathbb{C}[X_{n},\psi_{n}]_{n\geq 0}\qquad\text{degrees:}\quad\begin{array}[]{c|c|c|c}&\text{ghost}&\text{spin}&\text{fermion}\\
\hline\cr X_{n}&R&R/2-n-1&\text{even}\\
\psi_{n}&1-R&R/2-n&\text{odd}\end{array}

(1.12)

(Here ‘
R
’ is a free parameter, physically the
U(1)_{R}
charge of the chiral multiplet.)

The simplest line operators are vortex lines
\mathbb{V}_{N}
, for
N\in\mathbb{Z}
, characterized by a singularity
X(z)\sim z^{N}\times\text{(regular)}
,
\psi(z)\sim z^{-N}\times\text{(regular)}
near (say)
z=0
. They are the “global vortex loops” of
[
KWY12
]
.
Each
\mathbb{V}_{N}
corresponds to the
{\mathcal{A}}^{!}
module

\mathbb{V}_{N}\;\simeq\;\begin{cases}{\mathcal{A}}^{!}/(X_{m\geq 0},\psi_{n\geq 0})\simeq\mathbb{C}&N=0\\
{\mathcal{A}}^{!}/(X_{m\geq 0},\psi_{n\geq N})\simeq\mathbb{C}[\psi_{0},...,\psi_{N-1}]&N>0\\
{\mathcal{A}}^{!}/(X_{m\geq|N|},\psi_{n\geq 0})\simeq\mathbb{C}[X_{0},...,X_{|N|-1}]&N<0\end{cases}

(1.13)

The dg-shifted-Yangian structure on
{\mathcal{A}}^{!}
, controlling the OPE, consists of:

•

Shifts:
\tau_{z}(X_{n})=\sum_{m=0}^{n}\big(\begin{smallmatrix}n\\
m\end{smallmatrix}\big)z^{m}X_{n-m}
,
\tau_{z}(\psi_{n})=\sum_{m=0}^{n}\big(\begin{smallmatrix}n\\
m\end{smallmatrix}\big)z^{m}\psi_{n-m}

•

MC element
\displaystyle r(z)=\sum_{n,m\geq 0}(-1)^{n}\bigg(\begin{array}[]{c}n+m\\
m\end{array}\bigg)\frac{\psi_{n}\otimes X_{m}-X_{n}\otimes\psi_{m}}{z^{n+m+1}}

•

Coproduct
\Delta_{z}(X_{n})=\tau_{z}(X_{n})\otimes 1+1\otimes X_{n}
,
\Delta_{z}(\psi_{n})=\tau_{z}(\psi_{n})\otimes 1+1\otimes\psi_{n}

The coproduct and the MC element together determine the OPE of
{\mathcal{A}}^{!}
-modules. Namely, to build an OPE
M\otimes_{z}M^{\prime}
, we first deform the tensor product
M\otimes M^{\prime}
(of vector spaces) by adding a
differential

r(z)
, and then use the coproduct to define the action of
{\mathcal{A}}^{!}
. For example,
\mathbb{V}_{1}\otimes_{z}\mathbb{V}_{-1}
is the module
\mathbb{C}[\psi_{0},X_{0}]
, with a differential
r_{1,-1}(z)=\frac{1}{z}\psi_{0}X_{0}
.

The result of Theorem
4.1
implies that the dg-shifted-Yangian structure of any perturbative HT QFT looks essentially the same, with coproduct and shifted r-matrix given by the same free-field formulas. The main difference in interacting theories is that the
A_{\infty}
algebra
{\mathcal{A}}^{!}
becomes much richer. For example, adding a superpotential
W(X)=\tfrac{1}{d!}X^{d}
to the free chiral induces
A_{\infty}
operations
m_{k}
for
1\leq k\leq d-1
in
{\mathcal{A}}^{!}
, each given by modes of
{\partial}_{X}^{k}W
:

\begin{array}[]{r@{\;}c@{\;}l}Q(\psi_{n})&=&\displaystyle-\frac{1}{(d-1)!}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}0\leq m_{1}+...+m_{d-1}\\
=n+2-d\end{subarray}}X_{m_{1}}X_{m_{2}}\cdots X_{m_{d-3}}X_{m_{d-2}}X_{m_{d-1}}\\
{}[\psi_{n_{1}},\psi_{n_{2}}]&=&\displaystyle-\frac{1}{(d-2)!}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}0\leq m_{1}+...+m_{d-2}\\
=n_{1}+n_{2}+3-d\end{subarray}}X_{m_{1}}X_{m_{2}}\cdots X_{m_{d-3}}X_{m_{d-2}}\\
m_{k}(\psi_{n_{1}},...,\psi_{n_{k}})&=&\displaystyle-\frac{1}{k!(d-k)!}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}0\leq m_{1}+...+m_{d-k}\\
=n_{1}+...+n_{k}+k+1-d\end{subarray}}X_{m_{1}}X_{m_{2}}\cdots X_{m_{d-k}}\quad(k\geq 3)\end{array}

(1.14)

(For fermions
a,b
our convention is that
[a,b]:=ab+ba
.)
(
1.14
) identifies
{\mathcal{A}}^{!}
with the (dual) chiral derived critical locus of
W
. Alternatively, in gauge theory with Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
, we’ll find that
{\mathcal{A}}^{!}
is a deformation of the shifted cotangent loop algebra
(T^{*}[-1]\mathfrak{g})[\lambda]
.

1.3
Boundary vertex algebras

In this last part of the introduction, we comment/speculate briefly on an interesting connection between the structures in this paper and (boundary) vertex algebras and their conformal blocks. We won’t discuss this further in the body of the paper, leaving a detailed study to future work.

Another natural way to study line operators in 3d HT theories involves holomorphic boundary conditions. More precisely, if one introduces a holomorphic boundary condition
\mathbf{b}
, supporting a boundary vertex algebra
{\mathcal{V}}_{\mathbf{b}}
, then there is a functor from line operators of the bulk theory to
{\mathcal{V}}_{\mathbf{b}}
modules,

{\mathcal{F}}_{\mathbf{b}}:{\mathcal{C}}\to{\mathcal{V}}_{\mathbf{b}}\text{-mod}\,,\qquad{\mathcal{F}}_{\mathbf{b}}(\ell)=\text{Ops}(\mathbf{b},\ell)\,,\quad\raisebox{-21.68121pt}{\includegraphics[width]{VA-intro.jpg}}

(1.15)

which sends each line to the vector space of local operators at its junction with the boundary. Moreover, if the boundary condition is rich enough, the functor (
1.15
) should be an equivalence. This idea was first used to relate line operators in Chern-Simons theories to modules of the boundary WZW VOA
[
Wit89
,
MS89
,
EMSS89
]
; it has more recently found many applications in topological twists of 3d
{\mathcal{N}}=4
gauge theories,
e.g.

[
Gai16
,
CG18
,
CCG18
,
CDGG21
,
FGK23
,
BCDN23
]
, as well as in the 3d-3d correspondence, using VOA’s associated to 3-manifolds,
e.g.

[
GGP13
,
CCF+18
,
CCF+22
]
.

The theories we consider in this paper, such as HT twists of 3d
{\mathcal{N}}=2
gauge theories with superpotentials, all admit holomorphic boundary conditions
[
GGP13
,
YS14
,
DGP17
]
; their vertex algebras
{\mathcal{V}}_{\mathbf{b}}
were explicitly constructed in
[
CDG20
]
.
An important and unusual feature of these
{\mathcal{V}}_{\mathbf{b}}
is that they don’t admit stress tensors (
a.k.a.
conformal vectors) unless the bulk 3d theory is fully topological.
It’s then natural to pose the following questions:
how is our formulation of
{\mathcal{C}}
related to boundary vertex algebras
{\mathcal{V}}_{\mathbf{b}}
, and how is the OPE structure manifested from the perspective of
{\mathcal{V}}_{\mathbf{b}}\text{-mod}
?

A useful example of a boundary condition
\mathbf{b}
for HT-twisted
{\mathcal{N}}=2
theory with complexified gauge group
G
, Chern-Simons level
k
, matter representation
V
, and superpotential
W:V\to\mathbb{C}
is “Dirichlet” for the gauge fields and “Neumann” for the matter fields
[
CDG20
]
. Perturbatively, its boundary VOA
{\mathcal{V}}_{\mathbf{b}}
is a semidirect product of a Kac-Moody algebra
\hat{\mathfrak{g}}_{k^{\prime}}
at level
k^{\prime}=k-h^{\vee}(G)-\tfrac{1}{2}I_{2}(V)
and the commutative loop algebra
V^{*}(\!(z)\!)
(where
h^{\vee}
is the dual Coxeter number and
I_{2}(V)
is the quadratic index). There’s also a BRST anomaly equal to
\oint W
, the residue of
W
, lifted to a function on the loop space
LV
.
Altogether, the category of modules should be described geometrically as
\hat{\mathfrak{g}}_{k^{\prime}}
-equivariant matrix factorizations on the loop space
LV
, with potential
\oint W
,

\textstyle{\mathcal{F}}_{\mathbf{b}}:{\mathcal{C}}\overset{\sim}{\to}{\mathcal{V}}_{\mathbf{b}}\text{-mod}\simeq\text{MF}^{\hat{\mathfrak{g}}_{k^{\prime}}}\big(LV,\oint W\big)\qquad\text{(perturbative).}\hskip-57.81621pt

(1.16)

The dg-shifted Yangian of gauge theory from Section
7
provides an indirect construction of OPE’s in this matrix-factorization category.

Schematically, one would expect the OPE in
{\mathcal{V}}_{\mathbf{b}}\text{-mod}
itself to be captured by some
{\mathcal{V}}_{\mathbf{b}}
fusion product.
A more precise relation to the analysis in the current paper lies in equation (
1.8
). Here, the functor
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}
sends a line operator
\ell
to the state space on
\mathbb{P}^{1}
with insertion of
\ell
at
0
and singularity
{\mathcal{B}}_{\infty}
at infinity. In terms of the vertex algebra
{\mathcal{V}}_{\mathbf{b}}
, this state space is simply the
derived
conformal blocks (CB) on
\mathbb{P}^{1}
, with corresponding module
{\mathcal{F}}_{\mathbf{b}}(\ell)
at
0
and singularity
{\mathcal{B}}_{\infty}
at infinity,

\text{States}(\mathbb{CP}^{1},\ell(0),{\mathcal{B}}_{\infty})\simeq\text{CB}(\mathbb{CP}^{1},{\mathcal{F}}_{\mathbf{b}}(\ell),{\mathcal{F}}_{\mathbf{b}}({\mathcal{B}}_{\infty}))

(1.17)

The singularity ensures that the space of conformal blocks associated to the vacuum module is one-dimensional.
The algebra
{\mathcal{A}}^{!}
, moreover, acts naturally on any such space of conformal blocks (as it’s the endomorphisms of
{\mathcal{B}}_{\infty}
), so we get a lifted functor
\text{CB}(\mathbb{CP}^{1},-,{\mathcal{F}}_{\mathbf{b}}({\mathcal{B}}_{\infty})):{\mathcal{V}}_{\mathbf{b}}\text{-mod}\to{\mathcal{A}}^{!}\text{-mod}
. Altogether, this should fit in a commutative diagram

(1.18)

In the paper, we argue that the top arrow is an equivalence. On the bottom, one must be careful to consider CB as a derived functor of
A_{\infty}
categories, since both
{\mathcal{A}}^{!}
and
{\mathcal{V}}_{\mathbf{b}}
have
A_{\infty}
structures. We conjecture that, in this derived setting, the bottom arrow also becomes an equivalence.

In extremely nice situations, the category
{\mathcal{V}}_{\mathbf{b}}\text{-mod}
admits a so-called vertex tensor category structure
[
HLZ10
]
. This vertex tensor structure (which we denote by
\times_{z}
) should be the vertex-algebra-theoretic counterpart of the OPE, as it satisfies

\text{CB}(\mathbb{P}^{1},\ell\times_{z}\ell^{\prime},{\mathcal{B}}_{\infty})=\text{CB}(\mathbb{P}^{1},\ell(z),\ell^{\prime}(0),{\mathcal{B}}_{\infty})\,.

(1.19)

It is therefore natural to conjecture that the functor CB intertwines the vertex tensor structure of
[
HLZ10
]
with the meromorphic tensor structure that we study. In the special case of gauge theory with no matter
(V=0)
, it would be the vertex tensor structure for Kac-Moody at generic level (
\hat{\mathfrak{g}}_{k^{\prime}}\text{-mod}
) and our dg-shifted Yangian from Secs.
6.5
,
7.2
.

If this conjecture is true, then it should provide a powerful computational tool for the study of fusion products for vertex algebras. For example, the definition of vertex tensor product
\times_{z}
requires many additional properties on the modules considered (
e.g.
grading, finiteness,
etc.
), whereas our definition of meromorphic tensor products requires only smoothness. Moreover, we should find (due to (
1.6
)) that the functor CB maps the vertex tensor product
\ell\times_{z}\ell^{\prime}
to the tensor product of individual conformal blocks, deformed by a Maurer-Cartan element

\text{CB}(\ell\times_{z}\ell^{\prime})\simeq\big[\text{CB}(\ell)\otimes\text{CB}(\ell^{\prime}),r_{\ell,\ell^{\prime}}(z)\big]\,.

(1.20)

In particular, since characters are unchanged by the MC deformation, we recover an exact equality of characters

\chi[\text{CB}(\ell\times_{z}\ell^{\prime})]=\chi[\text{CB}(\ell)]\chi[\text{CB}(\ell^{\prime})]\,.

(1.21)

To obtain this good behavior, one must however consider derived fusion products and derived conformal blocks, rather than their classical counterparts.

In the case of 3d QFT’s that are actually topological, such as HT-twisted 3d
{\mathcal{N}}=2
Chern-Simons theory (or pure 3d Chern-Simons as in
[
Wit89
]
, or the topologically twisted 3d
{\mathcal{N}}=4
examples of
[
CG18
]
(etc.)), there is additional structure around. Now the boundary vertex algebra
{\mathcal{V}}_{\mathbf{b}}
does have a stress tensor/conformal element
L
,
i.e.
it is a
vertex operator algebra
. The stress tensor
L
trivializes the
z
-dependence in the vertex tensor product
\ell\times_{z}\ell^{\prime}
, and gives rise to a flat connection on the space of conformal blocks. In the case of
\text{CB}(\mathbb{P}^{1},-,{\mathcal{F}}_{\mathbf{b}}({\mathcal{B}}_{\infty}))
, this is the famous Knizhnik-Zamolodchikov connection
[
TK87
]
. This (schematically) makes
{\mathcal{V}}_{\mathbf{b}}\text{-mod}
an ordinary braided tensor category rather than a category with OPE’s.

We expect that when an HT theory is actually topological, there will be an avatar of the stress tensor
L
in the Koszul-dual algebra
{\mathcal{A}}^{!}
. Namely, we would expect
{\mathcal{A}}^{!}
to admit a derivation
{\mathcal{L}}
of degree
-1
, such that
[Q,{\mathcal{L}}]=T
, where
T
is the infinitesimal translation operator, such that
\tau_{z}=\exp(zT)
. In particular, translations become trivial on the
Q
-cohomology of
{\mathcal{A}}^{!}
. This would allow
z
-dependence in the OPE to be trivialized, and the resulting flat module would (ideally) be identical to the one defined by the KZ connection. We’ll give an example of the derivation
{\mathcal{L}}
for perturbative Chern-Simons in Section
6.6
. It would be interesting to explore this further in the future!

1.4

We would like to thank Meer Ashwinkumar, Kevin Costello, Niklas Garner, Davide Gaiotto, Lukas Müller, Natalie Paquette, Surya Raghavendran, and Pavel Safronov for enlightening discussions and incisive questions on the topics of this work. VP would especially like to thank Lotte Hollands and Sasha Shapiro for discussions in the course of reviewing his Ph.D. thesis. TD’s research is supported by EPSRC Open Fellowship EP/W020939/1.
WN’s research is supported by the Perimeter Institute for Theoretical Physics, which in turn is supported in part by the Government of Canada through the Department of Innovation, Science and Economic Development Canada and by the Province of Ontario through the Ministry of Colleges and Universities.

2
Setup

In this section, we’ll review some of the basic formalism and techniques that we use throughout the paper.

We begin (Section
2.1
) with 3d HT QFT’s in BV-BRST formalism, explaining what we mean by a perturbative theory, following
[
GRW21
,
GKW24
]
. We particularly emphasize the role of symmetries (ghost number/cohomological degree
R
, spin
J
, and fermion number
F
) and of holomorphic-topological descendants, which are helpful in organizing the field and operator content. We also recall (Section
2.2
) how the HT twist of 3d
{\mathcal{N}}=2
gauge theories, with matter, superpotentials, and Chern-Simons interactions, fit into this formalism, following
[
ACMV17
,
CDG20
]
. We discuss where we expect to see additional nonperturbative effects in 3d
{\mathcal{N}}=2
theories that would go beyond the scope of this paper (Section
2.2.1
).

In Section
2.3
, we review the form of the propagator in perturbative 3d HT theories, in different gauges. We’ll mostly use axial gauge for calculations of OPE’s of line operators, but it’s not always appropriate,
e.g.
for insertions of local operators.

In Section
2.4
, we then consider 1d topological QFT’s (
a.k.a.
topological quantum mechanics), which will be the building blocks of (perturbative) line operators. We again introduce symmetries and descendants, and review how deformations of topological QM are classified by solutions to Maurer-Cartan equations. This is a well known subject, but central to the entire paper; other good recent review include
[
PW21
,
GKW24
]

Finally, in Section
2.5
, we review some of the basic physical expectations for the category of line operators and its OPE
\otimes_{z}
in a 3d HT QFT.

We call attention to two simple conventions introduced in this section that we use throughout the paper. Given any local operator
{\mathcal{O}}
in 3d HT QFT (resp. 1d topological QFT) we denote the sum of its descendants in boldface:

\bm{{\mathcal{O}}}:=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}{\mathcal{O}}^{(k)}=\begin{cases}{\mathcal{O}}+{\mathcal{O}}^{(1)}+{\mathcal{O}}^{(2)}&\text{3d HT}\\
{\mathcal{O}}+{\mathcal{O}}^{(1)}&\text{1d top}\end{cases}

(2.1)

Such an
\bm{{\mathcal{O}}}
transforms as a mixed-degree differential form on spacetime, with (potential) local components proportional to
1,d\bar{z},dt,d\bar{z}dt
(in 3d) or just
1,dt
(in 1d).

For elements of an algebra, such as local operators in 1d QFT or the restriction of 3d local operators to a time-like line, sign conventions (including “Koszul sign rules” for
A_{\infty}
algebras) are controlled by fermion number
F
(
a.k.a.
parity). This need not coincide with ghost number/cohomological degree. Commutators and anti-commutators are both written with square brackets; we define

[a,b]:=ab-(-1)^{F(a)F(b)}ba\,.

(2.2)

2.1
3d HT QFT’s

A 3d HT theory may be defined on a 3-manifold with a transverse holomorphic foliation, such that the neighborhood of any point is isomorphic to
\mathbb{C}_{z}\times\mathbb{R}_{t}
. Since we’re interested in local structure, we will just work on global
\mathbb{C}_{z}\times\mathbb{R}_{t}
.

A perturbative HT QFT — one defined by quantizing a free theory, deformed by interactions — has an action that in the BV-BRST formalism takes the general form

S=\int_{\mathbb{C}\times\mathbb{R}}\big[\mathbf{p}_{i}d^{\prime}\mathbf{x}^{i}+{\mathcal{I}}(\mathbf{p},\mathbf{x},{\partial})\big]\,.

(2.3)

Here

d^{\prime}:=\partial_{t}dt+\partial_{\bar{z}}d\bar{z}

(2.4)

is an exterior derivative acting on the complex
\Omega^{\prime}{}^{\bullet}(\mathbb{C}\times\mathbb{R}):=\Omega^{\bullet}(\mathbb{C}\times\mathbb{R})/(dz)\simeq C^{\infty}(\mathbb{C}\times\mathbb{R})[dt,d\bar{z}]
, with
dt,d\bar{z}
treated as fermionic (parity-odd) variables; while

\partial:={\partial}_{z}dz

(2.5)

is the holomorphic derivative, with
dz
treated as a bosonic (parity-even) variable. The kinetic term is formed using the differential operator
d^{\prime}
; while arbitrary powers of
{\partial}
may appear in the interactions
{\mathcal{I}}
.

The fields of the theory are organized into
\Omega^{\prime}
multiforms

\mathbf{x}^{i}\in\Omega^{\prime}{}^{\bullet}(\mathbb{C}\times\mathbb{R})[R_{i}]dz^{J_{i}}\qquad\mathbf{p}_{i}\in\Pi\Omega^{\prime}{}^{\bullet}(\mathbb{C}\times\mathbb{R})[1-R_{i}]dz^{1-J_{i}}\,.

(2.6)

There’s some extra notation here that can be neglected on a first pass. The ‘
\Pi
’ designates a fermion-number (parity) shift in
\mathbf{p}
; while
R
and
J
are shifts in cohomological degree and spin of the fields, which we’ll discuss momentarily. Expanded in components, we have

\mathbf{x}^{i}=\mathbf{x}^{i}{}^{(0)}+\mathbf{x}^{i}{}^{(1)}+\mathbf{x}^{i}{}^{(2)}=(x^{i}{}^{(0)}+x^{i\,(1)}_{\bar{z}}d\bar{z}+x^{i\,(1)}_{t}dt+x_{\bar{z}t}^{i\,(2)}d\bar{z}dt)dz^{j_{i}}\,,

(2.7)

and similarly for
\mathbf{p}_{i}
. The component fields
x^{(0)},x^{(2)}_{\bar{z}t},p^{(1)}_{\bar{z},t}
are bosonic, while
p_{(0)},p_{(2),\bar{z}t},x^{(1)}_{\bar{z},t}
are fermionic. Semi-classically, the BRST operator acts on fields as

Q=\big\{S,-\big\}_{\rm BV}=d^{\prime}+\Big\{\int{\mathcal{I}},-\Big\}_{\rm BV}\,,

(2.8)

where
\{-,-\}_{\rm BV}
is the classical BV bracket, satisfying
\{\mathbf{p}_{i}(z,\bar{z},t),\mathbf{x}^{i}(w,\bar{w},s)\}_{\rm BV}\sim\delta^{(3)}(z-w,\bar{z}-\bar{w},t-s)
. The interaction must satisfy
\big\{\int{\mathcal{I}},\int{\mathcal{I}}\big\}_{\rm BV}=0
to avoid a semi-classical BRST anomaly,
i.e.
for
Q
to be nilpotent.

It is argued in
[
GRW21
,
WW24
]
that there are no further obstructions to perturbative quantization (at all orders) in a large family of 3d HT theories. This family includes all HT-twisted 3d
{\mathcal{N}}=2
theories.

2.1.1
Symmetries

In addition to fermion number
F\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}
, 3d HT theories have

•

Spin(2)_{J}\simeq U(1)_{J}
symmetry from rotations of the
\mathbb{C}_{z}
plane, with charge
J
, nominally valued in
\tfrac{1}{2}\mathbb{Z}
.

•

(potentially) a
U(1)_{R}
ghost-number or cohomological symmetry, with charge
R
, nominally valued in
\mathbb{Z}
, which lifts fermion number in the sense that

F=2J-R\quad(\text{mod 2})\,.

(2.9)

Conventions are such that
R
counts
dt,d\bar{z}
form degree while
J
counts
dz
degree,

\begin{array}[]{c|cccc}&d^{\prime},dt,d\bar{z}&{\partial}&\text{Lagrangian}&Q\\
\hline\cr F&\text{odd}&\text{even}&\text{even}&\text{odd}\\
J&0&1&1&0\\
R&1&0&2&1\end{array}

(2.10)

and the Lagrangian must have
R=2,J=1
in order to be integrated on
\mathbb{C}\times\mathbb{R}
. This implies that the linear operators
\mathbf{x},\mathbf{p}
, and the interaction terms, satisfy

J(\mathbf{x}^{i})+J(\mathbf{p}_{i})=1\,,\quad R(\mathbf{x}^{i})+R(\mathbf{p}_{i})=1\,;\quad J({\mathcal{I}})=1\,,\quad R({\mathcal{I}})=2\,.

(2.11)

We define
J_{i}=J(\mathbf{x}^{i}),\,R_{i}=R(\mathbf{x}^{i})
. The square brackets
[...]
appearing in (
2.6
) are cohomological shifts of the fields encoding their R-charges, while the
dz^{\#}
encode shifts in spin.

Interaction terms (and nonperturbative quantum effects) can break or modify symmetries. In this paper, we’ll exclusively work with theories in which both spin and R-symmetry are preserved — meaning there exists a choice of
\{J_{i}\}
and
\{R_{i}\}
for the
\mathbf{x}^{i}
’s such that
J({\mathcal{I}})=1
and
R({\mathcal{I}})=2
both hold. However, we’ll allow the charges to take rational values

J_{i},R_{i}\in\mathbb{Q}\qquad\text{(but $R_{i}-2J_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}$)}\,.

(2.12)

This amounts to saying that certain covers of
U(1)_{J},U(1)_{R}
are actually acting.

In the HT twist of a 3d
{\mathcal{N}}=2
theory,
U(1)_{R}
is the standard R-symmetry of the underlying SUSY theory; while spin

J=J_{phys}+\frac{1}{2}R

(2.13)

is a diagonal combination of physical spin and R-symmetry (it’s the distinguished combination that leaves the twisting supercharge invariant). It is common for
R
(and thus also
J
) to take rational, or even more general real values. For example, superconformal R-charges, satisfying Z-maximization
[
Jaf10
]
, are typically irrational. The choice of integer/rational/real affects the types of global 3-manifolds one can define an HT theory on, but are not important for the local features that we analyze.

2.1.2
Descendants and universality

The multiforms described above naturally organize fields of an HT theory into “multiplets” containing their descendants. We briefly review this idea, as it underlies the universality of the action (
2.3
).

In any HT QFT with a BRST differential
Q
, the theory does not need to be entirely independent of
t
and
\bar{z}
; rather,
{\partial}_{t}
and
{\partial}_{\bar{z}}
need only act as zero on operators modulo
Q
-exact terms. For local operators, one way to make this precise is to introduce descendants: for each local operator
{\mathcal{O}}
, transforming as a
dt,d\bar{z}
form of some degree in spacetime, one requires the existence of a descendant
{\mathcal{O}}^{(1)}
, such that

d^{\prime}{\mathcal{O}}=Q{\mathcal{O}}^{(1)}+(Q{\mathcal{O}})^{(1)}\,.

(2.14)

In particular, for
Q
-closed operators,
d^{\prime}{\mathcal{O}}
is
Q
-exact. (This generalizes descent in topological QFT’s
[
Wit88a
,
MW97
]
.) One sets
{\mathcal{O}}^{(2)}=({\mathcal{O}}^{(1)})^{(1)}
. There are no further descendants beyond
{\mathcal{O}}^{(2)}
since the
\Omega^{\prime}
complex tops out at degree two.

The structure (
2.14
) is automatic in the HT twist of a 3d
{\mathcal{N}}=2
theory, where
Q
is the twisting supercharge. There,
{\mathcal{O}}^{(1)}
is defined as
\widetilde{Q}{\mathcal{O}}
, where
\widetilde{Q}
is a particular combination of 3d
{\mathcal{N}}=2
supercharges satisfying
[Q,\widetilde{Q}]=d^{\prime}
; see
[
CDG20
, Sec. 3]
. We adopt the convention from (
2.1
): for any local operator
{\mathcal{O}}
, we will use boldface to denote the sum of its descendants, treated as a multiform on spacetime:

\bm{{\mathcal{O}}}:={\mathcal{O}}+{\mathcal{O}}^{(1)}+{\mathcal{O}}^{(2)}\,.

(2.15)

Descent is used to define the
\lambda
-bracket on local operators
[
OY19b
,
GW23
]
, generalizing
E_{n}
brackets in topological theories
[
Get92
,
BBZB+18
]
. Namely, for any local operators
\alpha,\beta\in{\mathcal{A}}
, one sets

\displaystyle\{\alpha_{\,\lambda\,}\beta\}(w,s)

\displaystyle:=(-1)^{F(\alpha)}\oint_{S^{2}_{w,s}}e^{\lambda(z-w)}\alpha^{(1)}(z,t)\beta(w,s)

(2.16)

\displaystyle=(-1)^{F(\alpha)}\oint_{S^{2}_{w,s}}e^{\lambda(z-w)}\bm{\alpha}(z,t)\bm{\beta}(w,s)\,,

where
S^{2}_{w,s}
is a sphere centered at
w,s
. When
\alpha,\beta
are
Q
-closed, the dependence on
S^{2}_{w,s}
is topological, due to (
2.14
) and Stokes’ Theorem.

Now, if a theory has a free limit, so that one can talk about fields and Lagrangians, then one expects fields of the free theory to obey descent equations as well. Suppose that in the free limit there are
Q
-closed fields
\phi_{i}
, which obey the free equation of motion
d^{\prime}\phi_{i}=0
.
Let
\bm{\Phi}_{i}:=\phi_{i}+\phi_{i}^{(1)}+\phi_{i}^{(2)}
. Then the descent equations imply
Q\bm{\Phi}_{i}=d^{\prime}\bm{\Phi}_{i}
,
i.e.
the BRST operator acts as
d^{\prime}
on this multiform. The free BV-BRST action must take the form
\int\mathbf{p}_{i}d^{\prime}\mathbf{x}^{i}
, where, for various
i
, either
\mathbf{\Phi}_{i}=\mathbf{x}^{i}
(with conjugate momentum
\mathbf{p}_{i}
) or
\mathbf{\Phi}_{i}=\mathbf{p}_{i}
(with conjugate momentum
\mathbf{x}^{i}
), depending on fermion number. This exercise explains (heuristically) how to construct an action in the form (
2.3
) for any HT QFT with a free-field limit: just find the “primary” fields satisfying
Q\phi=0
(or equivalently, the fields with EOM
d^{\prime}\phi=0
) and group together their descendants.

2.2
3d
{\mathcal{N}}=2
theories

Our main examples will come from 3d
{\mathcal{N}}=2
QFT’s in the HT twist. We review, following
[
ACMV17
,
CDG20
]
how these look once fields are reorganized in terms of descendants, and the action is recast in a canonical form (
2.3
).

A 3d
{\mathcal{N}}=2
free chiral multiplet reorganizes into a pair
(\mathbf{p},\mathbf{x})=({\mathbf{\Psi}},{\mathbf{X}})
with action
S=\int_{\mathbb{C}\times\mathbb{R}}{\mathbf{\Psi}}d^{\prime}{\mathbf{X}}
. The physical complex “chiral” boson
X
is the leading component of
{\mathbf{X}}
; while a particular fermion
\psi
in the anti-chiral multiplet, usually denoted
\overline{\psi}_{-}
, is the leading component of
{\mathbf{\Psi}}
. R-charge is undetermined, aside from the constraint
R(X)+R(\psi)=1
. Spin is determined by (
2.13
) to be

\begin{array}[]{c|cc}&{\mathbf{X}}&{\mathbf{\Psi}}\\
\hline\cr F&\text{even}&\text{odd}\\
J&\tfrac{1}{2}R(X)&1-\tfrac{1}{2}R(X)\\
R&R(X)&1-R(X)\end{array}

(2.17)

Multiple chirals
({\mathbf{X}}^{i},{\mathbf{\Psi}}_{i})_{i=1}^{n}
may interact via a 3d
{\mathcal{N}}=2
superpotential, a polynomial
W(X^{1},...,X^{n})
with the constraint that
R(W)=2
. It enters the HT action directly:

S=\int_{\mathbb{C}\times\mathbb{R}}{\mathbf{\Psi}}_{i}d^{\prime}{\mathbf{X}}^{i}+W({\mathbf{X}})\,.

(2.18)

A natural generalization, perfectly compatible with all constructions in this paper, would be to allow
W
to depend on
{\mathbf{\Psi}}
’s and holomorphic derivatives
{\partial}
as well. Such terms make sense in the HT twist as long as
\{S,S\}_{\rm BV}=0
, though they are not compatible with the full 3d
{\mathcal{N}}=2
SUSY algebra.

A 3d
{\mathcal{N}}=2
Yang-Mills gauge theory with compact gauge group
G_{c}
is reorganized in the HT twist as an analytically continued BF theory, for the complexified group
G=(G_{c})_{\mathbb{C}}
. The fields
(\mathbf{p},\mathbf{x})=({\mathbf{A}},{\mathbf{B}})
are valued in

\begin{array}[]{l}{\mathbf{A}}\in\Pi\mathfrak{g}\otimes\Omega^{\prime}{}^{\bullet}[1]\\[2.84544pt]
\;\;\;=c+A_{t}dt+A_{\bar{z}}\,d\bar{z}+...\end{array},\quad\raisebox{-5.69046pt}{$\begin{array}[]{l}{\mathbf{B}}\in\mathfrak{g}^{*}\otimes\Omega^{\prime}{}^{\bullet}dz\\[2.84544pt]
\;\;\;=\underbrace{B_{z}dz}_{B}+...\end{array}$}\qquad\text{with}\quad\begin{array}[]{c|cc}&A&B\\
\hline\cr F&\text{odd}&\text{even}\\
J&0&1\\
R&1&0\end{array}\,.

(2.19)

In particular, the 1-form
A=\mathbf{A}^{(1)}=A_{t}dt+A_{\bar{z}}d\bar{z}
is a
G
connection whose
dt
component has been complexified by the 3d
{\mathcal{N}}=2
vectormultiplet scalar. The leading component
c={\mathbf{A}}^{(0)}
is a ghost; while
B={\mathbf{B}}^{(0)}
is a complex scalar cohomologous to a complex linear combination of the curvature
F_{zt}
and the
{\partial}
derivative of the physical vectormultiplet scalar. We will often choose a basis of the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
and a dual basis

\text{bases:}\quad\text{$\{t^{a}\}$ for $\mathfrak{g}$}\,,\quad\text{$\{t_{a}\}$ for $\mathfrak{g}^{*}$}\,,\qquad t^{a}\cdot t_{b}=\delta^{a}{}_{b}\,,\quad[t^{a},t^{b}]=f^{ab}{}_{c}t^{c}\,,

(2.20)

and expand

{\mathbf{A}}={\mathbf{A}}_{a}t^{a}\,,\qquad{\mathbf{B}}={\mathbf{B}}^{a}t_{a}\,.

(2.21)

Note that the components
{\mathbf{A}}_{a}
transform as elements of
\mathfrak{g}^{*}
and
{\mathbf{B}}^{a}
as elements of
\mathfrak{g}
.

The action of HT-twisted Yang-Mills theory takes the form

S=\int_{\mathbb{C}\times\mathbb{R}}{\mathbf{B}}F^{\prime}({\mathbf{A}})=\int_{\mathbb{C}\times\mathbb{R}}{\mathbf{A}}d^{\prime}{\mathbf{B}}-\tfrac{1}{2}{\mathbf{B}}[{\mathbf{A}},{\mathbf{A}}]=\int_{\mathbb{C}\times\mathbb{R}}{\mathbf{A}}_{a}d^{\prime}{\mathbf{B}}^{a}-\tfrac{1}{2}f^{ab}{}_{c}{\mathbf{B}}^{c}{\mathbf{A}}_{a}{\mathbf{A}}_{b}\,.

(2.22)

Its EOM include
F^{\prime}(A)=[D_{\bar{z}},D_{t}]=0
, which says that the holomorphic structure of the gauge bundle along
\mathbb{C}_{z}
commutes with parallel transport in
\mathbb{R}_{t}
.

Gauge theory may be further deformed by a Chern-Simons interaction, which takes the form

S=\int_{\mathbb{C}\times\mathbb{R}}{\mathbf{B}}F^{\prime}({\mathbf{A}})+\tfrac{1}{2}k({\mathbf{A}},{\partial}{\mathbf{A}})=\int_{\mathbb{C}\times\mathbb{R}}{\mathbf{B}}F^{\prime}({\mathbf{A}})+\tfrac{1}{2}k^{ab}{\mathbf{A}}_{a}{\partial}{\mathbf{A}}_{b}\,,

(2.23)

where
k\in\text{Sym}^{2}(\mathfrak{g}^{*})^{\mathfrak{g}}
is an invariant symmetric bilinear form on
\mathfrak{g}
, with components
k^{ab}
. If
G
is a simple reductive group, then
k
is necessarily a multiple of the Killing form, which may be normalized to
\delta^{ab}
, so

k^{ab}=\frac{\widetilde{k}}{4\pi i}\,\delta^{ab}\,,

(2.24)

where
\widetilde{k}\in\mathbb{C}
is the more familiar numerical Chern-Simon level. Perturbatively, there is no constraint on
\widetilde{k}
. Non-perturbatively,
\widetilde{k}
gets quantized to be an integer, or a half-integer if there are further matter fields around
[
Wit89
,
AHI+97
]
.

An important feature of 3d
{\mathcal{N}}=2
Yang-Mills-Chern-Simons theory at sufficiently large level
|\widetilde{k}|>h^{\vee}
(where
h^{\vee}
is the dual Coxeter number) is that it flows to pure Chern-Simons theory in the infrared, at shifted level
\widetilde{k}-h^{\vee}\text{sign}(\widetilde{k})
. In the HT twist, RG flow is trivial, so Yang-Mills-Chern-Simons should be equivalent to pure Chern-Simons. Explicitly, this happens by identifying the missing
z
component of the Chern-Simons gauge field as
A_{z}\sim\frac{1}{k}B
.
Level shifts in the HT twist appear in perturbative computations of operator algebras,
e.g.
boundary vertex algebras
[
CDG20
,
GW19
]
or bulk operator algebras (Section
6.5
).

Finally, consider gauge theory with matter in representation
V
, and corresponding homomorphism
\varphi_{V}:\mathfrak{g}\to\text{End}(V)
, has matter fields

{\mathbf{X}}\in V^{*}\otimes\Omega^{\prime}{}^{\bullet}[R]dz^{R/2}\,,\qquad{\mathbf{\Psi}}\in\Pi V\otimes\Omega^{\prime}{}^{\bullet}[1-R]dz^{1-R/2}\,.

(2.25)

Given a basis and dual basis

\text{bases:}\quad\text{$\{v^{i}\}$ for $V$},\quad\text{$\{v_{i}\}$ for $V^{*}$},\qquad\varphi_{V}(t^{a})v^{i}=(\varphi_{V}^{a})^{i}{}_{j}v^{j}\,,

(2.26)

we expand

{\mathbf{X}}={\mathbf{X}}^{i}v_{i}\,,\qquad{\mathbf{\Psi}}={\mathbf{\Psi}}_{i}v^{i}\,,

(2.27)

with the components
{\mathbf{X}}^{i}
transforming as elements of
V
. This is usually what one means in physics by “a chiral multiplet in representation
V
,” despite that the field itself is an element of
V^{*}
. Different
R
-charges and spins may appear for each irreducible summand of
V
in (
2.25
) as well.
The action then takes the form

S=\int_{\mathbb{C}\times\mathbb{R}}{\mathbf{B}}F^{\prime}({\mathbf{A}})+{\mathbf{\Psi}}d_{\mathbf{A}}^{\prime}{\mathbf{X}}=\int_{\mathbb{C}\times\mathbb{R}}{\mathbf{A}}d^{\prime}{\mathbf{B}}+{\mathbf{\Psi}}d^{\prime}{\mathbf{X}}-\tfrac{1}{2}{\mathbf{B}}[{\mathbf{A}},{\mathbf{A}}]+{\mathbf{\Psi}}\varphi_{V}({\mathbf{A}}){\mathbf{X}}\,.

(2.28)

Here
d^{\prime}_{\mathbf{A}}=d^{\prime}+\varphi_{V}({\mathbf{A}})
is a covariant derivative.
This can be further deformed by Chern-Simons terms
\tfrac{1}{2}\int k({\mathbf{A}},{\partial}{\mathbf{A}})
and/or a superpotential
\int W({\mathbf{X}})
such that
W:V\to\mathbb{C}
is
G
-invariant and has
R=2
.

For ease of reference, we can also spell out the BRST transformation in these theories. In the most general case of gauge theory with matter, CS terms, and superpotential, we have

\begin{array}[]{l}Q{\mathbf{A}}=F^{\prime}({\mathbf{A}})\,,\\
Q{\mathbf{B}}=d_{\mathbf{A}}^{\prime}{\mathbf{B}}+k{\partial}{\mathbf{A}}-\varphi_{V}^{*}({\mathbf{\Phi}}{\mathbf{\Psi}})\,,\end{array}\qquad\begin{array}[]{l}Q{\mathbf{X}}=d^{\prime}_{\mathbf{A}}{\mathbf{X}}\,,\\
Q{\mathbf{\Psi}}_{i}=d^{\prime}_{\mathbf{A}}{\mathbf{\Psi}}_{i}+{\partial}_{{\mathbf{X}}^{i}}W({\mathbf{X}})\,.\end{array}

(2.29)

These semiclassical transformations refine to a rather nontrivial
A_{\infty}
algebra structure on local operators in gauge theory, which we derive later in Section
6
, summarized in (
6.72
).

2.2.1
Nonperturbative considerations

Most twisted
{\mathcal{N}}=2
theories do have some nonperturbative aspects. We recall a bit of what they are, mainly as a warning — as for the most part we will not be considering nonperturbative effects in this paper.

Given just matter with a superpotential, and trivial gauge group, we expect perturbation theory to give an exact analysis of local operators. This is because there are no instanton solutions to the equations of motion: in particular, the EOM for the scalar boson
{\partial}_{t}X={\partial}_{\bar{z}}X=0
don’t admit solutions that are localized in time. We will describe the complete A-infinity algebra of local operators in Section
6
. Similarly, we expect that line operators, as a category (objects and morphisms), are entirely perturbative. However, the OPE of lines might get nonperturbative corrections from solitons. They would be analogous to the instanton corrections in the half-twist of 2d
{\mathcal{N}}=(2,2)
theories
[
DSWW86
,
SW95
]
(or the A-twist of 2d
{\mathcal{N}}=(2,2)

[
Wit88b
]
,
cf.

[
GMW15
]
for local operators in theories with a superpotential), but extended along time
\mathbb{R}_{t}
.

Gauge theories have nonperturbative corrections
both
to local operators (and thus to morphisms among lines) and to the OPE’s of lines. Local operators in 3d HT-twisted gauge theories get enhanced by monopole operators — roughly, by the chiral/BPS monopole operators that appear in the chiral ring
[
AHI+97
]
and by their holomorphic derivatives. Some explicit examples of monopole corrections in the HT twist appeared in
[
Zen21
,
GW21
]
, though they are not fully understood in general.
This must in turn change the nature of the category of line operators
{\mathcal{C}}
, since (on one hand) lines are constructed by coupling QM to local operators; and (conversely) computing endomorphisms of the trivial line must reproduce local operators. In summary:

\quad\begin{array}[]{c|ccc}&\text{free matter}&\text{matter + $W$}&\text{gauge theory}\\
\hline\cr\text{quantization/local operators}&\text{pert}&\text{pert}&\text{NP}\\
\text{line operators (objects + morphisms)}&\text{pert}&\text{pert}&\text{NP}\\
\text{line operators (OPE)}&\text{pert}&\text{NP?}&\text{NP}\end{array}

One basic nonperturbative effect in gauge theories that’s easily incorporated in the twisted formalism above concerns the global nature of the gauge group. Compactness of
G_{c}
(equivalently, reductiveness of
G
) implies that we should remove the constant mode of the ghosts
c
from local operators, and implement invariance under constant
G
gauge transformations by hand, rather than via BRST cohomology.

We also note that in pure
G_{c}=U(1)
gauge theory, monopole operators have a perturbative definition as well,
cf.

[
GW23
, Sec. 5.2]
.
Physically, the chiral monopole operators are
M_{n}\sim e^{in\gamma}
(
n\in\mathbb{Z}
), where
\gamma
is the complexified dual photon. In our BV-BRST formalism, the field
B={\mathbf{B}}^{(0)}
is cohomologous to a (complexified) component of the physical field strength, which in turn is equal to
{\partial}\gamma
. This we may define
\mathbf{M}_{n}
at position
(w,s)\in\mathbb{C}\times\mathbb{R}
by choosing a holomorphic contour
\eta_{w}
in the
\mathbb{C}_{z}
plane that ends at
w
(with the other end off at infinity) and setting

\mathbf{M}_{n}(w,s)=\exp\int_{\eta_{w}\times\{s\}}\!\!n\,{\mathbf{B}}\,,\qquad n\in\mathbb{Z}\,.

(2.30)

In pure
U(1)
theory, the leading component
M_{n}
is
Q
-closed. (It generates a module for the Heisenberg raviolo vertex algebra introduced in
[
GW23
, Sec. 5.2]
.)
In the presence of a Chern-Simons term or matter,
M_{n}
is not
Q
-closed;
e.g.
with Chern-Simons terms it obeys

QM_{n}(w,s)=\bigg(\int_{\eta_{w}\times\{s\}}nk\,{\partial}{\mathbf{A}}\bigg)M_{n}(w,s)=nk\,c(w,s)M_{n}(w,s)\,,

(2.31)

reproducing the well-known fact that the monopole acquires gauge charge.

In course of studying dualities, many interesting examples of 3d
{\mathcal{N}}=2
theories have been have been found with monopole operators in their
superpotentials
. (Some of the first examples of this kind appeared in
[
Kar97
,
Aha97
]
.) This is yet another nonperturbative aspect of gauge theories that would be important to include in the future.

2.3
The propagator

The propagator
G(z,\bar{z},t;w,\bar{w},s)
in a Lagrangian HT QFT is a form on the configuration space of two distinct points in spacetime,
\text{Conf}_{2}(\mathbb{C}\times\mathbb{R})
, that satisfies
3
3

3

Including a factor of
2\pi i
in (
2.32
) is mainly a matter of convention. The precise factor depends on the normalization of the kinetic terms in the action, and it’s convenient to assume we’ve fix the normalization so that a
2\pi i
appears in (
2.32
).

d^{\prime}_{\bar{z},t}G=-d^{\prime}_{\bar{w},s}G=2\pi i\delta^{(3)}(z-w,\bar{z}-\bar{w},t-s)\,.

(2.32)

Note that it must be a 1-form in the odd variables
d\bar{z},dt,d\bar{w},ds
, and a 1-form in the even
dz,dw
. It may be thought of as the 2-point function in free theory

G(z,\bar{z},t;w,\bar{w},s)=\big\langle\mathbf{p}(z,\bar{z},t)\,\mathbf{x}(w,\bar{w},s)\big\rangle\,,

(2.33)

with form degrees in
G
matching the multiforms
\mathbf{p},\mathbf{x}
on the RHS.

The differential operator
d^{\prime}
has a very large kernel, including all holomorphic functions. Correspondingly, there is a great deal of “gauge freedom” in choosing a propagator
G
. In order to be consistent with a computation of 2-point functions, each choice must be aligned with particular choices of boundary conditions on the fields. A common choice is the HT analogue of Lorentz gauge, in which fields (and the propagator) satisfy
{\partial}_{t}x_{t}+2{\partial}_{z}x_{\bar{z}}=0
and
{\partial}_{t}p_{t}+2{\partial}_{z}p_{\bar{z}}=0
, giving (
cf.

[
GW19
, Sec. 3]
,
[
CDG20
, Sec. 5]
)

G^{\rm Lor}=\frac{1}{2}\frac{(\bar{z}-\bar{w})d(t-s)-(t-s)d(\bar{z}-\bar{w})}{\left((t-s)^{2}+|z-w|^{2}\right)^{\frac{3}{2}}}\,dz^{J(p)}\,dw^{J(x)}\,.

(2.34)

This is compatible with balanced decay of the fields in both space and time.

Alternatively, we may consider an axial gauge, in which fields satisfy
x_{\bar{z}}=p_{\bar{z}}=0
and the propagator takes the simpler form

G^{\rm ax}=\frac{\delta^{(1)}(t-s)}{z-w}dz^{J(p)}\,dw^{J(x)}

(2.35)

with
\delta^{(1)}(t-s)=\delta(t-s)(dt-ds)
.
This is usually not appropriate for computing correlation functions of local operators in the presence of (say) an asymptotic vaccum, due to the very slow decay of
\frac{1}{z-w}
at spatial infinity. However, this propagator does capture the singular terms in the OPE of line operators, as two lines get very close to each other. We’ll compare the two gauges further in Section
4.4
.

2.4
Topological quantum mechanics

We will construct line operators by coupling a bulk 3d HT theory to 1d topological quantum mechanics. To set the stage, we collect a few results about topological quantum mechanics and its deformations.

Abstractly, the data of topological quantum mechanics is a vector space
V
of states and an algebra
A
of local operators. If
V
is finite dimensional, then
A=\text{End}(V)
. If
V
is infinite dimensional, then
A
is typically a proper subalgebra of all linear endomorphisms of
V
; at the very least,
V
should be treated as a topological vector space and endomorphisms in
A
are required to be continuous.

In a cohomological setting,
V
is further promoted to a dg vector space,
i.e.
a chain complex with a BRST differential
Q
; and
A
is promoted to a dg algebra, endowed with an induced differential
[Q,-]
. We’ll assume we are working with theories that have a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
fermion number
F
, and a
\mathbb{Z}
(or more generally
\mathbb{Q}
) valued cohomological grading with charge
R
, such that
Q
is odd with
R(Q)=1
.

Just like in 3d HT theories, each local operator
{\mathcal{O}}
in topological QM comes with a descendant
{\mathcal{O}}^{(1)}
, obeying

Q{\mathcal{O}}^{(1)}+(Q{\mathcal{O}})^{(1)}=d_{t}{\mathcal{O}}\,,

(2.36)

where
d_{t}={\partial}_{t}dt
is the 1d exterior derivative. Second and higher descendants of course vanish. We again use boldface to denote sums of descendants,
\bm{{\mathcal{O}}}:={\mathcal{O}}+{\mathcal{O}}^{(1)}
.

2.4.1
Deformations

Deformations of topological QM will be the key to coupling a QM theory to a bulk QFT. Perturbative deformations can be analyzed from two different but ultimately equivalent perspectives. It is useful to be able to translate between them, so we review how this works. This is a classic result in topological field theory; see
e.g.

[
PW21
,
GKW24
,
GO19
]
for further discussion and references.

In a cohomological setting, the only nontrivial perturbative deformations are those that change the differential on the state space and operator algebra,

Q\to Q+M

(2.37)

for some local operator
M\in A
with
F(M)=\text{odd}
and
R(M)=1
. To avoid a BRST anomaly,
i.e.
for the new differential to still be a differential, we must have
(Q+M)^{2}=0
, or

\text{Maurer-Cartan:}\qquad Q(M)+M^{2}=Q(M)+\tfrac{1}{2}[M,M]=0\,.

(2.38)

Deformations are classified by solutions to this Maurer-Cartan equation — up to an equivalence relation that essentially says that
Q
-exact deformations act trivially.

Alternatively, deformations may be constructed by deforming the effective action of the QM theory. “Deforming the action” makes sense even if the original theory isn’t Lagrangian, or the Lagangian is unknown; it is merely a prescription for modifying correlation functions. Suppose we change the action by

\delta S=\int_{\mathbb{R}}M^{\prime}dt\,.

(2.39)

for some local operator
M^{\prime}
with
F(M^{\prime})=\text{even}
and
R(M^{\prime})=0
. Then, by definition, correlation functions of local operators
\langle a_{1}(t_{1})a_{2}(t_{2})...\rangle
are modified by inserting time-ordered, integrated “segments” of the deformation inbetween each consectutive pair of operators:

\displaystyle\langle a_{1}(t_{1})a_{2}(t_{2})...\rangle_{\delta S}

\displaystyle:=\Big\langle T\Big[e^{-\int_{\mathbb{R}\backslash\{t_{1},t_{2},...\}}M^{\prime}dt}a_{1}(t_{1})a_{2}(t_{2})...\Big]\Big\rangle

(2.40)

\displaystyle=\Big\langle T\Big[e^{-\int_{t_{1}+\epsilon}^{\infty}M^{\prime}dt}\Big]a_{1}(t_{1})T\Big[e^{-\int_{t_{2}+\epsilon}^{t_{1}-\epsilon}M^{\prime}dt}\Big]a_{2}(t_{2})T\Big[e^{-\int_{...}^{t_{2}-\epsilon}M^{\prime}dt}\Big]...\Big\rangle\,.

Here ‘
T
’ denotes time ordering, and in the second line we’ve assumed
t_{1}>t_{2}>...
.

This sort of expression should not be unfamiliar: the deformation of the action of quantum mechanics by a Hamiltonian (essentially
M^{\prime}
here) is well known to modify correlation functions in precisely this way! Each
T\Big[e^{-\int_{t+\epsilon}^{t^{\prime}-\epsilon}M^{\prime}dt}\Big]
is “time evolution.”

The key result we now need is that a deformation (
2.39
) is free of BRST anomalies precisely when
M^{\prime}dt
is the first descendant of a local operator
M
(with
F(M)=\,
odd,
R(M)=1
) and
M
satisfies the MC equation,

M^{\prime}dt=M^{(1)}\qquad\text{and}\qquad Q(M)+M^{2}=0\,.

(2.41)

Moreover, the effect of the deformation on the operator algebra in this case is simply to deform the differential
Q_{\delta S}=Q+[M,-]
just like in (
2.37
). Thus, anomaly-free deformations of the action and deformations of the differential are equivalent.

To understand the equivalence a bit better, let’s assume
M^{\prime}dt=M^{(1)}
and consider a the BRST variation of a segment of deformation
T\Big[e^{-\int_{0}^{1}M^{(1)}}\Big]
between (say)
t=0
and
t=1
. When de-exponentiating the deformation, one must be careful to regularize so that multiple operators
M^{(1)}
are never integrated across coincident points. We have:

\displaystyle Q\,T\Big[e^{-\int_{0}^{1}M^{(1)}}\Big]

\displaystyle:=Q\Big(-\int_{1>t>0}M^{(1)}(t)+\frac{1}{2}\int_{1>t_{1}\neq t_{2}>0}T\big[M^{(1)}(t_{1})M^{(1)}(t_{2})\big]+\ldots\Big)

\displaystyle=-\int_{1>t>0}QM^{(1)}(t)+\int_{1>t_{1}>t_{2}>0}Q\big(M^{(1)}(t_{1})M^{(1)}(t_{2})\big)+\ldots

\displaystyle=-\int_{1>t>0}dM+\int_{1>t>0}(QM)^{(1)}+\!\int_{t_{1}>t_{2}}d\big(M(t_{1})M^{(1)}(t_{2})-M^{(1)}(t_{1})M(t_{2})\big)

\displaystyle=-M(1)+M(0)+\int_{1>t>0}\Big((QM)^{(1)}+[M^{(1)},M]\Big)+\ldots

(2.42)

where the descent equation
QM^{(1)}+(QM)^{(1)}=dM
and Stokes’ theorem leads to (1) boundary terms at
t=0,1
and (2) bulk terms proportional to the integral of
(QM)^{(1)}+[M^{(1)},M]
. The latter is just the descendant of the MC equation itself:
\big(Q\,M+M^{2}\big)^{(1)}=(Q\,M)^{(1)}+M^{(1)}M-MM^{(1)}=(Q\,M)^{(1)}+[M^{(1)},M]
; so if (
2.41
) holds then the bulk terms vanish. The cancellations of bulk terms continue to all orders (see
[
PW21
,
GO19
]
), and the entire variation resums to

Q\,T\Big[e^{-\int_{0}^{1}M^{(1)}}\Big]=-M(1)T\Big[e^{-\int_{0}^{1}M^{(1)}}\Big]+T\Big[e^{-\int_{0}^{1}M^{(1)}}\Big]M(0)

(2.43)

In turn, (
2.43
) guarantees that the BRST variation of a deformed correlator (
2.40
) is equivalent to deforming the
Q+[M,-]
variation of the original correlator:

\displaystyle\big\langle Q_{\delta S}(a_{1}(t_{1})a_{2}(t_{2})...)\big\rangle_{\delta S}

\displaystyle:=\Big\langle Q\Big(T\Big[e^{-\int_{t_{1}+\epsilon}^{\infty}M^{(1)}}\Big]a_{1}(t_{1})T\Big[e^{-\int_{t_{2}+\epsilon}^{t_{1}-\epsilon}M^{(1)}}\Big]a_{2}(t_{2})T\Big[e^{-\int_{...}^{t_{2}-\epsilon}M^{(1)}}\Big]...\Big)\Big\rangle

\displaystyle\hskip-93.95122pt=\Big\langle T\Big[e^{-\int_{t_{1}+\epsilon}^{\infty}M^{(1)}}\Big]\Big(Q\,a_{1}(t_{1})+M(t_{1}+\epsilon)a_{1}(t_{1})-(-1)^{F(a_{1})}a_{1}(t_{1})M(t_{1}-\epsilon)\Big)T\Big[e^{-\int_{t_{2}+\epsilon}^{t_{1}-\epsilon}M^{(1)}}\Big]a_{2}(t_{2})...

\displaystyle\hskip-86.72377pt+T\Big[e^{-\int_{t_{1}+\epsilon}^{\infty}M^{(1)}}\Big]a_{1}(t_{1})T\Big[e^{-\int_{t_{2}+\epsilon}^{t_{1}-\epsilon}M^{(1)}}\Big](-1)^{F(a_{1})}\Big(Q\,a_{2}(t_{2})+M(t_{2}+\epsilon)a_{2}(t_{2})-\ldots\Big)\cdots\Big\rangle

\displaystyle\hskip-93.95122pt=\big\langle Q(a_{1}(t_{1})a_{2}(t_{2})...)+[M,a_{1}](t_{1})a_{2}(t_{2})...+(-1)^{F(a_{1})}a_{1}(t_{1})[M,a_{2}](t_{2})...+\cdots\big\rangle_{\delta S}

\displaystyle\hskip-93.95122pt=\big\langle(Q+[M,-])\cdot(a_{1}(t_{1})a_{2}(t_{2})...)\big\rangle_{\delta S}

(2.44)

This ties the deformation of the action by
M^{(1)}
to the algebraic deformation (
2.37
).

2.4.2
Free fields

Many topological quantum mechanics (including all QM with finite-dimensional state spaces) can be engineered by quantizing free fields, deformed by interactions. We’ll use free-field models in many of our examples of line operators.

Analogous to (
2.3
), 1d free fields with interactions have general action

S=\int_{\mathbb{R}_{t}}{\mathbf{a}}^{i}d_{t}\bar{\mathbf{a}}_{i}+I({\mathbf{a}},\bar{\mathbf{a}})

(2.45)

where
{\mathbf{a}},\bar{\mathbf{a}}
are multiforms on
\mathbb{R}_{t}
, and
d_{t}=\partial_{t}dt
is the 1d exterior derivative. Conventions are such that
dt
is odd with
R(dt)=1
; also the 1d Lagrangian must be odd, with
R=1
,
J=0
, to be consistently integrated along
\mathbb{R}_{t}
. This means for each
i
that either

{\mathbf{a}}^{i}\in\Omega^{\bullet}(\mathbb{R}_{t})[R_{i}]dz^{J_{i}}\,,\quad\bar{\mathbf{a}}_{i}\in\Omega^{\bullet}(\mathbb{R}_{t})[-R_{i}]dz^{-J_{i}}

(2.46)

or

\quad{\mathbf{a}}^{i}\in\Pi\Omega^{\bullet}(\mathbb{R}_{t})[R_{i}]dz^{J_{i}}\,,\quad\bar{\mathbf{a}}_{i}\in\Pi\Omega^{\bullet}(\mathbb{R}_{t})[-R_{i}]dz^{-J_{i}}

(2.47)

(
i.e.
the leading components of
{\mathbf{a}}^{i}
and
\bar{\mathbf{a}}_{i}
are both bosonic or both fermionic) and

F(I)=\text{odd}\,,\qquad R(I)=1\,,\qquad J(I)=0\,.

(2.48)

When
I=0
, the BRST operator in (
2.45
) acts as
Q_{0}=d_{t}
on all fields. When
I\neq 0
, the BRST operator acts as

Q{\mathbf{a}}^{i}=d_{t}{\mathbf{a}}^{i}+{\partial}_{\bar{\mathbf{a}}_{i}}I({\mathbf{a}},\bar{\mathbf{a}})\,,\qquad Q\bar{\mathbf{a}}_{i}=d_{t}\bar{\mathbf{a}}_{i}+{\partial}_{{\mathbf{a}}^{i}}I({\mathbf{a}},\bar{\mathbf{a}})\,.

(2.49)

Having
Q^{2}=0
,
i.e.
avoiding a BRST anomaly, is typically a nontrivial constraint on
I
.

The quantization of (
2.45
) at
I=0
can be given a state space
V
consisting of polynomials in the leading components
a^{i}
of the
{\mathbf{a}}^{i}
(either bosons or fermions), and an operator algebra generated by the leading components
a^{i}
and
\bar{a}_{i}
,

V=\mathbb{C}[a^{i}]\,,\qquad A=\mathbb{C}[a^{i},\bar{a}_{i}]/\big([\bar{a}_{i},a^{j}]=\delta_{i}{}^{j}\big)\,.

(2.50)

with canonical commutation relations in the operator algebra

[\bar{a}_{i},a^{j}]=\delta_{i}{}^{j}\,.

(2.51)

(This is the
Q_{0}
-cohomology of the state space and operator algebra.) Recall that
[a^{i},\bar{a}_{j}]:=a^{i}\bar{a}_{j}-(-1)^{F(a^{i})F(\bar{a}_{j})}\bar{a}_{j}a^{i}
denotes either a commutator or anti-commutator, depending on fermion numbers. When acting on
V
,
a^{i}
acts as left multiplication and
\bar{a}_{i}
acts as a derivative
{\partial}_{a^{i}}
.

Adding the interaction term
I({\mathbf{a}},\bar{\mathbf{a}})
deforms
V
to a complex with differential
Q=I(a,\bar{a})
and deforms
A
to an algebra with differential
[Q,-]
. The BRST anomaly vanishes, and
Q^{2}=0
, if any only if the Maurer-Cartan equation holds,

\underbrace{Q_{0}I(a,\bar{a})}_{=0}+I(a,\bar{a})^{2}=I(a,\bar{a})^{2}=0\quad\text{(as an element of $A$)}\,.

(2.52)

An alternative description of theories of type (
2.45
) is as B-type topological twists of 1d
{\mathcal{N}}=2
quantum mechanics with either chiral multiplets (
2.46
) or fermi multiplets (
2.47
), together with a fermionic superpotential
I({\mathbf{a}},\bar{\mathbf{a}})
. The fermionic superpotential that appears here includes both standard E and J terms of 1d
{\mathcal{N}}=2
theories
[
DS86
,
Wit93
,
KL02
]
. For example, if
(\bar{\mathbf{b}}_{j},{\mathbf{b}}^{j})
are fermi multiplets and the remaining
(\bar{\mathbf{a}}_{i},{\mathbf{a}}^{i})
are chirals, standard E and J superpotential terms would be written

I({\mathbf{a}},{\mathbf{b}},\bar{\mathbf{a}},\bar{\mathbf{b}})=\bar{\mathbf{b}}_{j}E^{j}({\mathbf{a}})+{\mathbf{b}}^{j}J_{j}({\mathbf{a}})\,.

(2.53)

The MC equation becomes
I(a,b,\bar{a},\bar{b})^{2}=2E^{j}(a)J_{j}(a)=0
, a familiar constraint.

2.5
The category of line operators

Now, let’s consider the expected structure of line operators in a 3d HT QFT on
\mathbb{C}_{z}\times\mathbb{R}_{t}
in a bit more detail.

Lines inserted at each point
z\in\mathbb{C}
and extended along
\mathbb{R}_{t}
form a linear category
{\mathcal{C}}_{z}
. As explained in the introduction, its objects are the local “labels”
\ell
that define line operators, and its morphism spaces
\text{Hom}_{{\mathcal{C}}_{z}}(\ell,\ell^{\prime})
are the vector spaces of local operators at a junction of lines:

(2.54)

Since we assume we have a cohomological theory, with a BRST operator
Q
, the spaces
\text{Hom}_{{\mathcal{C}}_{z}}(\ell,\ell^{\prime})
become a dg vector space, with differential
Q
. They are endowed with all three gradings/symmetries discussed in Section
2.1.1
: fermion number
F\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}
, cohomological
R
, and rotation
J
in the
\mathbb{C}
plane about the point
z
. Moreover, collision of junctions in the
\mathbb{R}_{t}
direction defines a product on local operators there (
i.e.
a composition of morphisms) that is associative up to
Q
-exact terms (up to homotopy). Altogether, this endows
{\mathcal{C}}_{z}
with the structure of an
A_{\infty}
category. In particular, each endomorphism space
\text{End}_{{\mathcal{C}}_{z}}(\ell):=\text{Hom}_{{\mathcal{C}}_{z}}(\ell,\ell)
is an
A_{\infty}
algebra. If we take
Q
-cohomology, then composition of morphisms becomes associative and
H^{\bullet}(\text{End}_{{\mathcal{C}}_{z}}(\ell),Q)
becomes a standard associative algebra; but simply taking
Q
-cohomology is a forgetful operation that may neglect nontrivial higher operations.

A special case is the identity, or trivial line
\ell={\mathds{1}}
. Its endomorphisms are bulk local operators (inserted at
z
)

\text{End}_{{\mathcal{C}}_{z}}({\mathds{1}})\simeq{\mathcal{A}}=\text{bulk local operators}.

(2.55)

In terms of an HT-twisted 3d
{\mathcal{N}}=2
theory, the objects
\ell\in{\mathcal{C}}_{z}
correspond to half-BPS line operators of the 3d
{\mathcal{N}}=2
theory. Namely, they preserve the supercharge
Q
that defines the HT twist together with a second supercharge
\overline{Q}
that satisfies
[Q,\overline{Q}]={\partial}_{t}
. (In the conventions of
[
DGP17
]
,
Q=\overline{Q}_{-}
and
\overline{Q}=Q_{+}
.) Similarly, after passing to cohomology, local operators at junctions correspond to quarter-BPS local operators in the 3d
{\mathcal{N}}=2
theory that preserve
Q
.

Translation invariance of our QFT implies that for any two points
z,w\in\mathbb{C}
, the categories
{\mathcal{C}}_{z},{\mathcal{C}}_{w}
are isomorphic, via
A_{\infty}
functors

T_{z-w}:{\mathcal{C}}_{w}\overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow}{\mathcal{C}}_{z}\,.

(2.56)

such that in turn
T_{z}\circ T_{z^{\prime}}\simeq T_{z+z^{\prime}}
.
We will often take the isomorphisms (
2.56
) for granted, and just write ‘
{\mathcal{C}}
’ for the category at any given point in
\mathbb{C}
.

In addition, lines in a 3d HT QFT should have an OPE, encoded in a family of functorial tensor products

\otimes_{z}:{\mathcal{C}}_{w+z}\boxtimes{\mathcal{C}}_{w}\to{\mathcal{C}}_{w}\quad(z\in\mathbb{C}^{*})\quad\text{or simply}\quad\otimes_{z}:{\mathcal{C}}\boxtimes{\mathcal{C}}\to{\mathcal{C}}\,.

(2.57)

This collects several pieces of physical data, including:

1.

The tensor product of objects
\ell\otimes_{z}\ell^{\prime}
, defined as the effective line operator at
w\in\mathbb{C}
that is equivalent to an insertion of
\ell
at
w+z
and
\ell^{\prime}
at
w
in all correlation functions (with all other operators inserted further away):

(2.58)

2.

The tensor product of morphisms (local operators), families of linear maps

\begin{array}[]{cccccc}\otimes_{z}:&\text{Hom}(\ell_{1},\ell_{2})&\otimes&\text{Hom}(\ell_{1}^{\prime},\ell_{2}^{\prime})&\to&\text{Hom}(\ell_{1}\otimes_{z}\ell_{1}^{\prime},\ell_{2}\otimes_{z}\ell_{2}^{\prime})\\
&a&&a^{\prime}&\mapsto&a\otimes_{z}a^{\prime}\end{array}

(2.59)

for all
\ell_{1,2},\ell_{1,2}^{\prime}
that preserve the
A_{\infty}
compositions of junctions.
The OPE
a\otimes_{z}a^{\prime}
is defined as the local operator at the junction of
\ell_{1}\otimes_{z}\ell_{1}^{\prime}
and
\ell_{2}\otimes_{z}\ell_{2}^{\prime}
that behaves the same way as inserting
a
and
a^{\prime}
separately in all correlation functions:

(2.60)

This OPE of junctions most closely matches the OPE of local operators in a vertex algebra. In particular, if
\ell_{1}=\ell_{2}=\ell_{1}^{\prime}=\ell_{2}^{\prime}={\mathds{1}}
are all the identity line, then (
2.59
) produces a family of maps
\otimes_{z}:{\mathcal{A}}\otimes{\mathcal{A}}\to{\mathcal{A}}
on bulk local operators, encoding the (nonsingular) OPE in the vertex algebra of bulk local operators.
In general,
a\otimes_{z}a^{\prime}
has local holomorphic dependence on
z
and may be singular at
z=0
.

3.

Natural isomorphisms
(\ell\otimes_{z}\ell^{\prime})\otimes_{z^{\prime}}\ell^{\prime\prime}\simeq\ell\otimes_{z+z^{\prime}}(\ell^{\prime}\otimes_{z^{\prime}}\ell^{\prime\prime})
reflecting OPE associativity.

As prefaced in the introduction, a main goal in the rest of the paper will be to understand the OPE on the category
{\mathcal{C}}
