. We will do this by combining several different physically meaningful representations of
{\mathcal{C}}
, each of which make different features manifest.

3
Line operators from quantum mechanics

We now combine the background of Section
2
to give a systematic construction of line operators in perturbative 3d HT theories, by coupling a bulk 3d QFT to 1d topological quantum mechanics. Abstractly, one arrives at the statement (
cf.

[
Cos13a
,
CP20
]
) that

\text{perturbative line operators $\ell$}\quad\leftrightarrow\quad\text{pairs}\;(V_{\ell},\mu_{\ell})

(3.1)

where
V_{\ell}
is the dg vector space of a 1d quantum mechanics, and
\mu_{\ell}
is a Maurer-Cartan element that encodes the coupling of
V_{\ell}
to the bulk. It satisfies an
A_{\infty}
MC equation in
\text{End}(V_{\ell})\otimes{\mathcal{A}}
, where
{\mathcal{A}}
is the bulk operator algebra.

After reviewing this in Section
3.1
, we devote the rest of the section to explaining how some familiar (and some less familiar) examples of line operators in HT-twisted 3d
{\mathcal{N}}=2
theories arise in this formalism. We consider free matter theory in Section
3.2
rediscover flavor vortex lines (dual to Wilson lines under particle-vortex duality, or Witten’s
SL(2,\mathbb{Z})
action on 3d theories
[
Wit03
,
KWY12
]
). Then in Section
3.3
we consider the effect of adding a superpotential
W
. This restricts the set of non-anomalous vortex lines in a way related to tropical geometry, as we find a new constraint that the residue
\oint W
(computed on an infinitesimal circle linking the line) must vanish. We also encounter the first truly nontrivial solutions to the MC equation for couplings
\mu
, which we illustrate in the XYZ model.

Finally, in Section
3.4
we make some brief observations about perturbative line operators in gauge theory. We recall how Wilson lines are defined in the formalism (
3.1
). Various vortex lines can also be constructed, but keeping them BRST-anomaly-free (making sure
\mu_{\ell}
satisfies the MC equation) depends strongly on the Chern-Simons levels and matter content. We give some examples of hybrid Wilson/vortex lines in abelian theories with matter. We will find a more systematic construction of Wilson/vortex lines in Section
6.5.1
, using representation theory of the Koszul dual
{\mathcal{A}}^{!}
.

3.1
General idea

Let
{\mathcal{T}}
be a 3d holomorphic-topological QFT, with fermion number
F
, ghost number (homological degrees)
R
, and spin
J
. We’d like to construct a line operator along
\mathbb{R}_{t}\times\{0\}\subset\mathbb{R}_{t}\times\mathbb{C}_{z}
,
i.e.
at position
z=0
in the holomorphic plane.

Let
{\mathcal{A}}
denote the algebra of local operators of
{\mathcal{T}}
, restricted to the real line
z=0
.
We recall from Section
2.1.2
that (the cohomology of)
{\mathcal{A}}
is in general a (-1)-shifted Poisson vertex algebra
[
OY19b
,
GW23
,
AKY24
]
, graded by
F,R,J
. The restriction to the line
z=0
ignores the vertex-algebra structure (holomorphic OPE’s) and leaves behind the structure of an
A_{\infty}
algebra, with differential
Q
and gradings
F,R,J
.

It’s useful to compare the structure of the algebra here to that in topological quantum mechanics (Section
2.4
).
Both
are special cases of
A_{\infty}
algebras. In purely 1d QM, local operators form a dg algebra, which we think of as an
A_{\infty}
algebra with trivial higher operations. Its cohomology is an associative algebra. It’s also maximally non-commutative, in the sense that it should have a trivial center.
In a 3d HT theory, local operators at
z=0
form an
A_{\infty}
algebra that can have nontrivial higher operations due to interactions with the “bulk” away from
z=0
. Its cohomology is associative and necessarily commutative, due to the extra bulk directions.

We can create a perturbative line operator
\ell
in
{\mathcal{T}}
that preserves
F,R,J
by

1)

Choosing a 1d topological quantum mechanics, labeled by a state space
V_{\ell}
and a (dg) operator algebra
A_{\ell}\subseteq V_{\ell}
. We additionally assume this QM has an abelian global symmetry with charge
J
.

2)

Choosing an element
\mu_{\ell}\in A_{\ell}\otimes{\mathcal{A}}
with
F(\mu_{\ell})=\text{odd}
,
R(\mu_{\ell})=1
,
J(\mu_{\ell})=0
that we use to deform the action

\delta S=\int_{\mathbb{R}_{t}\times\{z=0\}}\mu_{\ell}^{(1)}=\int_{\mathbb{R}_{t}\times\{z=0\}}\bm{\mu}_{\ell}\,.

(3.2)

Here
\mu_{\ell}^{(1)}
denotes a topological descendant (in the
A_{\ell}
component) and an HT descendant (in the
{\mathcal{A}}
component). The integral just picks off the
dt
component. For example, if
\mu_{\ell}=M\otimes{\mathcal{O}}
with
M\in\text{End}(V_{\ell})
,
{\mathcal{O}}\in{\mathcal{A}}
, then

\mu_{\ell}^{(1)}=M^{(1)}\otimes{\mathcal{O}}+M\otimes{\mathcal{O}}^{(1)}=dt[M^{(1)}_{t}\otimes{\mathcal{O}}-M\otimes{\mathcal{O}}^{(1)}_{t}]+\ldots

(3.3)

We also have
\mu_{\ell}^{(1)}\big|_{dt}=\bm{\mu}_{\ell}\big|_{dt}
with
\bm{\mu}_{\ell}=\mathbf{M}\otimes\bm{{\mathcal{O}}}
, where as usual the multiform descendants are
\mathbf{M}=M+M^{(1)}
,
\bm{{\mathcal{O}}}={\mathcal{O}}+{\mathcal{O}}^{(1)}+{\mathcal{O}}^{(2)}
.

To ensure that the deformation (
3.2
) is (perturbatively) free of BRST anomalies, we must require that
\mu_{\ell}
satisfies a Maurer-Cartan equation. If
{\mathcal{A}}
is a dg algebra (no higher
A_{\infty}
operations) then this is simply the Maurer-Cartan equation (
2.38
), namely
Q(\mu_{\ell})+(\mu_{\ell})^{2}=0
. More generally, when
{\mathcal{A}}
(and thus
A_{\ell}\otimes{\mathcal{A}}
) is a nontrivial
A_{\infty}
algebra, the correct generalization is the
A_{\infty}
Maurer-Cartan equation

\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}m_{k}(\mu_{\ell},...\mu_{\ell})=0\qquad\text{in $A_{\ell}\otimes{\mathcal{A}}$}\,,

(3.4)

where
m_{1}(a)=Q(a)
is the differential,
m_{2}(a,b)=ab
is the multiplication, and
m_{\geq 3}
are the higher products.
See
[
PW21
,
GO19
]
and
[
GMW15
, App. A]
for reviews of how the deformation analysis from Section
2.4.1
is generalized in the presence of higher operations. Roughly, higher operations contribute to higher-order terms in (
2.42
).

That is essentially it. One finds, schematically, that
perturbative line operators in
{\mathcal{T}}
are in 1-1 correspondence with pairs
(V_{\ell},\mu_{\ell})
, where
V_{\ell}
is a dg vector space and
\mu_{\ell}\in\text{End}(V_{\ell})\otimes{\mathcal{A}}
is a solution of the MC equation
, up to a suitable notion of isomorphism on both sides.

In practice, one may want to be more precise about the form of
\mu_{\ell}
—
i.e.
what sorts of couplings are allowed. For example,
{\mathcal{A}}
is typically infinite dimensional, but has spin
J
bounded from below. One may want to allow couplings of the form

\mu_{\ell}=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}M_{n}\otimes{\mathcal{O}}_{n}\,,\qquad M_{n}\in A_{\ell}\,,\;{\mathcal{O}}_{n}\in{\mathcal{A}}

(3.5)

with infinitely many independent
{\mathcal{O}}_{n}
’s, but for each
j\in\mathbb{R}
only finitely many satisfying
J({\mathcal{O}}_{n})\leq j
. The MC equation still makes sense in this case. Formally, one would say that
\mu_{\ell}
belongs to a completed tensor product
A_{\ell}\widehat{\otimes}{\mathcal{A}}
. Physically, such infinite couplings tend to correspond to allowing very “heavy” lines that represent boundary conditions wrapped on an infinitesimal circle. Including them is a choice.

3.1.1
Linear coupings

We want to further simplify the form of the couplings.

In Section
6
we’ll see explicitly how algebras
{\mathcal{A}}
of local operators in perturbative 3d HT theories are generated
linearly
by the elementary fields and their
{\partial}_{z}
derivatives.
Explicitly, for a Lagrangian theory of the form (
2.3
) with fields
\{\mathbf{p}_{i},\mathbf{x}^{i}\}
and some interaction
{\mathcal{I}}(\mathbf{p},\mathbf{x},{\partial})
, we find a model for
{\mathcal{A}}
that is generated (as an algebra) by the subspace of linear operators

{\mathcal{A}}_{1}:=\mathbb{C}\langle{\partial}^{n}p_{i},{\partial}^{n}x^{i}\rangle_{n\geq 0}\subset{\mathcal{A}}\,,

(3.6)

containing the leading components of
\mathbf{p}_{i},\mathbf{x}^{i}
and their derivatives.
{\mathcal{A}}
itself is generated, as a vector space, by polynomials in the linear operators from (
3.6
); it has various relations in its products and higher products determined by the interaction
{\mathcal{I}}
.

In such a situation, we might hope that every perturbative line operator is equivalent to one that’s defined by purely linear couplings,
i.e.
one with some
V_{\ell}
and
\mu_{\ell}
of the form

\mu_{\ell}=\sum_{i,n}\alpha_{i,n}{\partial}^{n}x^{i}+\beta^{i}_{n}{\partial}^{n}p_{i}\,,\qquad\alpha_{i,n},\,\beta^{i}_{n}\in\text{End}(V_{\ell})\,.

(3.7)

This hope turns out to be correct, but it’s rather nontrivial to prove in general. The cleanest argument (conceptually) involves showing that perturbative lines are equivalent to modules for the Koszul-dual algebra
{\mathcal{A}}^{!}
, and then showing that
{\mathcal{A}}^{!}
is linearly generated by the shifted linear dual of
{\mathcal{A}}_{1}
. We’ll develop this chain of reasoning in Section
5.2.3
to prove that linear couplings (
3.7
) are always sufficient.

For some
intuition
of why any coupling is equivalent to a linear one, consider the following example. Suppose we have a line operator constructed from quantum mechanics with space
V
and

\mu=\mu_{0}+M{\mathcal{O}}{\mathcal{O}}^{\prime}\,,\qquad M\in\text{End}(V)\,,\quad{\mathcal{O}},{\mathcal{O}}^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{A}}\,,

(3.8)

where
\mu_{0}
and
M{\mathcal{O}}{\mathcal{O}}^{\prime}
separately satisfy the MC equation, and
{\mathcal{O}},{\mathcal{O}}^{\prime}
are
Q
-closed bulk local operators that commute with each other. This represents a non-linear coupling, which we want to simplify. We define a second line operator by adding an extra 1d free boson
(\mathbf{b},\bar{\mathbf{b}})
and fermion
(\mathbf{a},\bar{\mathbf{a}})
(as in Section
2.4.2
), with a 1d potential
I=\mathbf{a}\mathbf{b}
, so that the QM vector space becomes
\widetilde{V}=V\otimes\mathbb{C}[a,b]
,
Q=Q_{V}+ab
. (Note that
\widetilde{V}
and
V
are quasi-isomorphic.) We also introduce a new coupling

\widetilde{\mu}=\mu_{0}+M\,{\mathcal{O}}\,a-b\,{\mathcal{O}}^{\prime}\,,\qquad Ma,b\in\text{End}(\widetilde{V})\,,\quad{\mathcal{O}},{\mathcal{O}}^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{A}}\,,

(3.9)

which has split up the bulk operators. (This assumes
{\mathcal{O}}^{\prime}
is fermionic; otherwise swap
a
and
b
.)
To see that the modified
(\widetilde{V},\widetilde{\mu})
line is equivalent to the original
(V,\mu)
line, it’s helpful to consider the total deformation to the 1d action:

\delta S_{\rm new}=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\mathbf{a}d\mathbf{\bar{a}}+\mathbf{b}d\mathbf{\bar{b}}+\widetilde{\bm{\mu}}=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\mathbf{a}d\mathbf{\bar{a}}+\mathbf{b}d\mathbf{\bar{b}}+\bm{\mu}_{0}+\mathbf{M}{\mathcal{O}}\hskip-8.5359pt{\mathcal{O}}\mathbf{a}+\mathbf{b}\mathbf{a}-\mathbf{b}{\mathcal{O}}\hskip-8.5359pt{\mathcal{O}}^{\prime}

(3.10)

The quadratic term
\mathbf{b}\mathbf{a}
lets us integrate out both new 1d multiplets, and the equation of motion for
\mathbf{b}
(say) sets
\mathbf{a}={\mathcal{O}}\hskip-8.5359pt{\mathcal{O}}^{\prime}
, recovering

\delta S_{\rm old}=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\bm{\mu}_{0}+\bm{\mu}{\mathcal{O}}\hskip-8.5359pt{\mathcal{O}}{\mathcal{O}}\hskip-8.5359pt{\mathcal{O}}^{\prime}=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\bm{\mu}\,.

(3.11)

By iterating this procedure, multilinear couplings — at least those whose linear factors all commute — can be split up into linear couplings. Generalizing the argument above to the case where
{\mathcal{O}}
and
{\mathcal{O}}^{\prime}
don’t commute can be done by adding extra terms on both sides to satisfy the MC equations; this seems tricky to carry out in full generality.

3.2
Example: free chiral and vortex lines

We’ll now give examples of a few simple families of line operators in HT-twisted 3d
{\mathcal{N}}=2
theories, relating the construction via coupling to quantum mechanics to more familiar physical descriptions.

Consider an HT-twisted free chiral multiplet, on
\mathbb{C}_{z}\times\mathbb{R}_{t}
. As introduced in Section
2.2
, it has an action

S=\int_{\mathbb{C}\times\mathbb{R}}{\mathbf{\Psi}}d^{\prime}{\mathbf{X}}\,,

(3.12)

where
{\mathbf{X}},{\mathbf{\Psi}}
are multiform-valued superfields with leading components
X
(a complex boson) and
\psi
(a complex scalar fermion), with R-charges (
2.17
).

The free chiral also has a
U(1)_{f}
global “flavor” symmetry, under which
{\mathbf{X}},{\mathbf{\Psi}}
have weights
+1,-1
. In the HT twist, the symmetry gets enhanced to the infinite-dimensional group of holomorphic
\mathbb{C}^{*}
-valued functions on
\mathbb{C}_{z}
. Given such a function
g(z)
, the fields transform as

({\mathbf{X}},{\mathbf{\Psi}})\mapsto(g(z){\mathbf{X}},g(z)^{-1}{\mathbf{\Psi}})\,.

(3.13)

One can create nontrivial line operators, sometimes called
flavor vortex lines
, by acting with a singular flavor transformation — one that’s not
\mathbb{C}^{*}
-valued. For
N\in\mathbb{Z}
, let
\mathbb{V}_{N}
denote the vortex line created at (say)
z=0
by the flavor transformation
g(z)=z^{N}
.
4
4

4

We are only interested in behavior near
z=0
. Globally, any meromorphic function
g(z)
that behaves like
z^{N}
near
z=0
and is
\mathbb{C}^{*}
-valued away from
z=0
will do.

Put differently,
\mathbb{V}_{N}
is a disorder operators that requires field to have a singular profile

\mathbb{V}_{N}:\quad\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}{\mathbf{X}}(z)\sim z^{N}\times(\text{regular)}\\[2.84544pt]
{\mathbf{\Psi}}(z)\sim z^{-N}\times(\text{regular})\end{array}\right.\quad\text{near $z=0$}\qquad(N\in\mathbb{Z})\,.

(3.14)

In the underlying 3d
{\mathcal{N}}=2
theory, these are half-BPS line operators, called “global vortex loops” in
[
KWY12
]
.

We can construct such vortex lines by coupling to quantum mechanics in a standard way.
5
5

5

This is similar to the analysis of vortex lines in A-twisted 3d
{\mathcal{N}}=4
theories in
[
DGGH19
, Sec. 4]
.
It’s useful to recall (
e.g.
from
[
CDG20
]
) that the bulk operator algebra
{\mathcal{A}}
is quasi-isomorphic to a commutative vertex algebra with zero differential, generated by
X(z),\psi(z)
and their holomorphic
{\partial}
derivatives. Restricting to
z=0
, we get a (super)commutative polynomial algebra

{\mathcal{A}}=\mathbb{C}[{\partial}^{n}X(0),{\partial}^{n}\psi(0)]_{n=0}^{\infty}

(3.15)

Then:

•

If
N=0
,
\mathbb{V}_{0}={\mathds{1}}
is the trivial/identity line, so there is nothing to do.

•

If
N>0
, introduce
N
1d fermi multiplets
({\mathbf{a}}_{n},\bar{\mathbf{a}}_{n})_{n=0}^{N-1}
, and a Maurer-Cartan element
\mu_{N}=\sum_{n=0}^{N-1}\tfrac{1}{n!}a_{n}{\partial}^{n}X(0)
. Thus, we insert in the bulk path integral

\hskip-14.45377pt\mathbb{V}_{N}(0)=\int\prod_{n=0}^{N-1}D{\mathbf{a}}_{n}D\bar{\mathbf{a}}_{n}\;\exp\!\!\int_{\{0\}\times\mathbb{R}_{t}}\sum_{n=0}^{N-1}\Big[{\mathbf{a}}_{n}d\bar{\mathbf{a}}_{n}+\underbrace{\frac{1}{n!}{\mathbf{a}}_{n}{\partial}^{n}{\mathbf{X}}}_{\bm{\mu}_{N}}\Big]\,,\quad(N>0)\,.

(3.16a)

•

If
N<0
, introduce
|N|
1d bosonic multiplets
({\mathbf{b}}_{n},\bar{\mathbf{b}}_{n})_{n=0}^{|N|-1}
, and a Maurer-Cartan element
\mu_{N}=\sum_{n=0}^{|N|-1}\tfrac{1}{n!}b_{n}{\partial}^{n}\psi(0)
. Thus, we insert in the bulk path integral

\hskip-14.45377pt\mathbb{V}_{N}(0)=\int\prod_{n=0}^{|N|-1}D{\mathbf{b}}_{n}D\bar{\mathbf{b}}_{n}\;\exp\!\!\int_{\{0\}\times\mathbb{R}_{t}}\sum_{n=0}^{|N|-1}\Big[{\mathbf{b}}_{n}d\bar{\mathbf{b}}_{n}+\underbrace{\frac{1}{n!}{\mathbf{b}}_{n}{\partial}^{n}{\mathbf{\Psi}}}_{\bm{\mu}_{N}}\Big]\,,\quad(N<0)\,.

(3.16b)

To see why these couplings actually match the desired flavor vortices, consider the total action with a
\mathbb{V}_{N}
for (say)
N>0
inserted:

S_{3d}+S_{1d}=\int_{\mathbb{C}_{z}\times\mathbb{R}_{t}}{\mathbf{\Psi}}d^{\prime}{\mathbf{X}}+\delta^{(2)}(z,\bar{z})\sum_{n=0}^{N-1}\bigg({\mathbf{a}}_{n}d\bar{{\mathbf{a}}}_{n}+\frac{1}{n!}{\mathbf{a}}_{n}\,{\partial}^{n}{\mathbf{X}}\bigg)\,.

(3.17)

The 1d equations of motion for the
{\mathbf{a}}
’s (or in BV formalism, the
Q
variation of
\bar{\mathbf{a}}
’s) effectively sets
{\partial}^{n}{\mathbf{X}}|_{z=0}=0
,
n=0,...,N-1
, enforcing the constraint that
{\mathbf{X}}(z)\sim z^{N}\times(\text{regular})
. Dually, the bulk EOM for
{\mathbf{\Psi}}
becomes
Q({\mathbf{\Psi}})=\{S+S_{1d},{\mathbf{\Psi}}\}_{BV}=d^{\prime}{\mathbf{\Psi}}-\sum_{n=0}^{N-1}(-1)^{n}{\partial}^{n}\delta^{(2)}(z,\bar{z}){\mathbf{a}}_{n}=0
, which now has an extra source term that sets

{\mathbf{\Psi}}(z)=\sum_{n=0}^{N-1}\frac{{\mathbf{a}}_{n}}{z^{n+1}}+(\text{regular})\,.

(3.18)

Thus the 1d fermions we introduced become identified with the additional polar modes of
{\mathbf{\Psi}}
at the vortex.

Matching (
3.16b
) with vortex lines
\mathbb{V}_{N}
for
N<0
works the same way: the EOM for
\bar{\mathbf{b}}
’s effectively set regular modes of
{\mathbf{\Psi}}
to zero, while the
{\mathbf{b}}
’s become coefficients for new polar modes of
{\mathbf{X}}
.

We note that the charges of the 1d fields above are completely determined by the requirement that
F(\mu_{N})=\text{odd}
,
R(\mu_{N})=1
,
J(\mu_{N})=0
; namely,

\begin{array}[]{c|cccc}&{\mathbf{a}}_{n}&\bar{\mathbf{a}}_{n}&{\mathbf{b}}_{n}&\bar{\mathbf{b}}_{n}\\
\hline\cr F&\text{odd}&\text{odd}&\text{even}&\text{even}\\
R&1-r&r-1&r&-r\\
J&-\tfrac{r}{2}-n&\tfrac{r}{2}+n&\tfrac{r}{2}-1-n&1-\tfrac{r}{2}+n\end{array}\qquad r:=R(X)\hskip-36.135pt

(3.19)

Moreover, the MC equation
Q(\mu_{N})+\mu_{N}^{2}=0
is trivially satisfied for each
N
. The QM operators
a_{n},b_{n}
involved in the couplings are
Q
-closed and they all super-commute, since no
\bar{a}_{n}
or
\bar{b}_{n}
appear; and the bulk algebra
{\mathcal{A}}
has
Q=0
and is super-commutative; so

Q(\mu_{N})=0\,,\qquad\mu_{N}^{2}=0\,.

(3.20)

One can imagine constructing many other line operators in a similar way. As long as the QM operators that appear in a coupling
\mu_{\ell}
are
Q
-closed and (super)commutative, it is guaranteed that the MC equation will be satisfied. Simple generalizations include vortex lines that “skip” modes; for example, we could consider

\mathbb{V}_{[0,2]}:=\int D{\mathbf{a}}_{0}D(...)\exp\!\!\int_{\mathbb{C}\times\{0\}}\Big[{\mathbf{a}}_{0}d\bar{\mathbf{a}}_{0}+{\mathbf{a}}_{2}d\bar{\mathbf{a}}_{2}+{\mathbf{a}}_{0}{\mathbf{X}}+\tfrac{1}{2}{\mathbf{a}}_{2}{\partial}^{2}{\mathbf{X}}\Big]\,,

(3.21)

which effectively sets
X(z)\sim c\,z+z^{3}\times\text{(regular)}
and
\psi(z)\sim\frac{a_{2}}{z^{3}}+\frac{a_{0}}{z}+\text{(regular)}
near
z=0
. It might be noted that mode-skipping lines such as (
3.21
) partially break holomorphic flavor transformations (
3.13
), whereas the standard flavor vortices (
3.16
) preserve the full holomorphic flavor symmetry. However, if one only require that line operators preserve
F,R,J
, then (
3.21
) is a perfectly reasonable example.

A feature that emerges is that it is not possible to give aribtrary poles and zeros to both
{\mathbf{X}}
and
{\mathbf{\Psi}}
. By coupling to 1d operators, any pole created for one of the bulk fields gets “balanced” by a zero for the other. There is a more precise characterization of this phenomenon in terms of a shifted symplectic structure on the space of modes of
X,\psi
that will appear later — in the kinetic term of the
S^{1}
reduction of a 3d HT theory (
5.29
). Line operators will need to be supported on Lagrangian submanifolds with respect to the symplectic structure (because they define boundary conditions for the
S^{1}
reduction).

3.3
Example: matter with a superpotential

Next, consider the HT twist of a theory with
d
chiral matter multiplets
({\mathbf{X}}^{i},{\mathbf{\Psi}}_{i})_{i=1}^{d}
and a polynomial superpotential
W(X)
, whose action is

S_{3d}=\int_{\mathbb{C}\times\mathbb{R}}{\mathbf{\Psi}}_{i}d^{\prime}{\mathbf{X}}^{i}+W({\mathbf{X}})\,.

(3.22)

The presence of the superpotential restricts the set of vortex lines in the theory. A qualitative way to see this is the following. Suppose we attempt to define a disorder line operator, by specifying a singularity near
z=0
for the HT equations of motion. The BRST transformation of
{\mathbf{X}}
’s and associated EOM is unchanged
(Q{\mathbf{X}}=d^{\prime}{\mathbf{X}})
, so we still need a holomorphic profile in the
\mathbb{C}_{z}
plane, with some singularity at
z=0
. However, if we remove a neighborhood of
z=0
, the BRST transformation of the action may acquire a new boundary term due to the superpotential,

Q\,S_{3d}\big|_{\mathbb{C}^{*}\times\mathbb{R}}=\int_{\mathbb{C}^{*}\times\mathbb{R}}d^{\prime}W({\mathbf{X}})=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\oint_{S^{1}_{\epsilon}}W({\mathbf{X}})\,,

(3.23)

where
S^{1}_{\epsilon}
is an infinitesimal circle around
z=0
.
We find that disorder operators have a BRST anomaly unless

\boxed{\oint_{S^{1}_{\epsilon}}W({\mathbf{X}})\;\;\text{vanishes for the specified singularity in ${\mathbf{X}}^{i}(z)$}\,.}

(3.24)

This is an analogue of the “Warner problem”
[
War95
]
for 2d
{\mathcal{N}}=(2,2)
Landau-Ginzburg models in the presence of a boundary, and is the reason that the simplest B-type boundary conditions for 2d LG models are ones on which the superpotential vanishes,
cf.

[
HIV00
]
. More general boundary conditions in 2d are matrix factorizations of the superpotential
[
KL02
]
.

In our case, we expect the full category of operators for a 3d theory with matter
V
and superpotential
W
to be equivalent to matrix factorizations of
\oint W
on the loop space
LV
, as in (
1.16
). This isn’t a perspective that we’ll use in the current paper, but it does provide some additional intuition. The vortex lines with singular profiles

\mathbb{V}_{\vec{N}}:\quad\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}{\mathbf{X}}^{i}(z)\sim z^{N_{i}}\times(\text{regular)}\\[2.84544pt]
{\mathbf{\Psi}}_{i}(z)\sim z^{-N_{i}}\times(\text{regular})\end{array}\right.\quad\text{near $z=0$}

(3.25)

for
\vec{N}\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
such that
\oint W(X)=0
correspond to elementary objects in the matrix-factorization category: they are the structure sheaves of the loci
z^{\vec{N}}\mathbb{C}[\![z]\!]\subset LV
, with trivial matrix factorizations, since the superpotential
\oint W
vanishes on their support.

3.3.1
Tropical polytopes and flavor symmetry

To get an idea of what the constraint
\oint W=0
means, consider the “XYZ model,” three chirals
({\mathbf{X}},{\mathbf{\Psi}}_{X}),\,({\mathbf{Y}},{\mathbf{\Psi}}_{Y}),\,({\mathbf{Z}},{\mathbf{\Psi}}_{Z})
, with
W={\mathbf{X}}{\mathbf{Y}}{\mathbf{Z}}
. There are putative vortex lines
V_{N_{1},N_{2},N_{3}}
for all
(N_{1},N_{2},N_{3})\in\mathbb{Z}^{3}
. However, for
W
to have vanishing residue, we need

N_{1}+N_{2}+N_{3}\geq 0\,.

(3.26)

The sublattice
\mathbb{Z}^{2}\subset\mathbb{Z}^{3}
defined by
N_{1}+N_{2}+N_{3}=0
, which bounds the region (
3.26
), has line operators of a special type. They are all flavor vortices, generated, just as in (
3.13
), by singular flavor transformations for the
U(1)^{2}\subset U(1)^{3}
flavor symmetry that is preserved by the superpotential. The interior points of the region (
3.26
) are not flavor vortices, but are still perfectly legitimate line operators.

We can also consider a general polynomial superpotential, a sum of monomials

W=\sum_{\alpha}c_{\alpha}(X^{1})^{p_{\alpha,1}}(X^{2})^{p_{\alpha_{2}}}\cdots(X^{d})^{p_{\alpha,d}}\,.

(3.27)

The constraint
\oint W=0
requires the singularities in vortex lines
\mathbb{V}_{\vec{N}}
to satisfy

\text{min}_{\alpha}\big\{p_{\alpha,1}N_{1}+\ldots+p_{\alpha,d}N_{d}\big\}\geq 0\,.

(3.28)

This describes a
tropical lattice polytope
; the equation on the LHS is so-called the tropicalization of
W
.

3.3.2
Solving the MC equation for the XYZ model

In order to construct lines by coupling to quantum mechanics in a 3d QFT with superpotential, one need to know the full
A_{\infty}
structure on the algebra
{\mathcal{A}}
of 3d bulk local operators. It is derived in Section
6.4
, where it is described geometrically as a chiralization of the “derived critical locus” of
W
. In general, when
W
is a polynomial of degree
d
, the algebra
{\mathcal{A}}
has nontrivial higher products
m_{k}
for all
k\leq d-1
.

Here we’ll illustrate how things work for a special example: the XYZ model defined in Section
3.3.1
. We’d like to express any of the basic vortex lines
\mathbb{V}_{(N_{1},N_{2},N_{3})}
with
N_{1}+N_{2}+N_{3}\geq 0
(the anomaly-free domain, as in (
3.26
)) via quantum mechanics.

The bulk algebra
{\mathcal{A}}
for the XYZ model is generated by
X,Y,Z,\psi_{X},\psi_{Y},\psi_{Z}
and their
{\partial}_{z}
derivatives at (say)
z=0
. All
m_{k\geq 3}
operations vanish because the superpotential is cubic; but there is a differential (controlled by the first derivatives of
W
) as well as a nontrivial commutator (controlled by the second derivatives of
W
):

\begin{array}[]{c}Q\psi_{X}=YZ\,,\quad Q\psi_{Y}=XZ\,,\quad Q\psi_{Z}=XY\,,\\[2.84544pt]
[\psi_{X},\psi_{Y}]={\partial}Z\,,\quad[\psi_{Y},\psi_{Z}]={\partial}X\,,\quad[\psi_{X},\psi_{Z}]={\partial}Y\,.\end{array}

(3.29)

The bosons
X,Y,Z
are all
Q
-closed and central. The action of
Q
here could have been deduced from the semi-classical action of
Q
on fields (
2.29
), but the commutators are a purely quantum effect. They were already observed in
[
CDG20
, Eqn. 5.18]
, and are a special case of the general
A_{\infty}
structure in Sec.
6.4
.

When all of
N_{1},N_{2},N_{3}\geq 0
(individually),
\mathbb{V}_{N_{1},N_{2},N_{3}}
is constructed the same way same as for free matter. We introduce
N_{1}+N_{2}+N_{3}
1d fermi multiplets
\{{\mathbf{a}}_{n},\bar{\mathbf{a}}_{n}\}_{n=0}^{N_{1}-1}
,
\{{\mathbf{b}}_{m},\bar{\mathbf{b}}_{m}\}_{m=0}^{N_{2}-1}
,
\{{\mathbf{c}}_{p},\bar{\mathbf{c}}_{p}\}_{p=0}^{N_{3}-1}
, coupled to the bulk via an MC element

\mu=\sum_{n=0}^{N_{1}-1}\frac{a_{n}}{n!}{\partial}^{n}X(0)+\sum_{m=0}^{N_{2}-1}\frac{b_{m}}{m!}{\partial}^{m}Y(0)+\sum_{p=0}^{N_{3}-1}\frac{c_{p}}{p!}{\partial}^{p}Z(0)\,.

(3.30)

This trivially satisfies
Q\mu=\mu^{2}=0
, and thus satisfies the MC equation.

When one of the
N
’s is negative, say (WLOG)
N_{1}<0
and
N_{2},N_{3}\geq 0
, we start by introducing
|N_{1}|
1d bosonic multiplets
\{{\mathbf{a}}_{n},\bar{\mathbf{a}}_{n}\}_{n=0}^{|N_{1}|-1}
and
N_{2}+N_{3}
fermi multiplets
\{{\mathbf{b}}_{m},\bar{\mathbf{b}}_{m}\}_{m=0}^{N_{2}-1}
,
\{{\mathbf{c}}_{p},\bar{\mathbf{c}}_{p}\}_{p=0}^{N_{3}-1}
, coupled by an MC element

\mu_{0}=\sum_{n=0}^{|N_{1}|-1}\frac{a_{n}}{n!}{\partial}^{n}\psi_{X}(0)+\sum_{m=0}^{N_{2}-1}\frac{b_{m}}{m!}{\partial}^{m}Y(0)+\sum_{p=0}^{N_{3}-1}\frac{c_{p}}{p!}{\partial}^{p}Z(0)\,.

(3.31)

However, this now satisfies
Q(\mu_{0})=\sum_{n=0}^{|N_{1}|-1}\tfrac{1}{n!}a_{n}{\partial}^{n}(YZ)(0)\neq 0
due to (
3.29
), as well as
(\mu_{0})^{2}=0
. To fix this, we look for a second term
\mu_{1}
that obeys
Q(\mu_{1})=0
and
[\mu_{0},\mu_{1}]+(\mu_{1})^{2}=-Q(\mu_{0})
, so that the sum
\mu=\mu_{0}+\mu_{1}
will obey
Q(\mu)+\mu^{2}=0
. Consider

\mu_{1}=-\sum_{m=0}^{N_{2}-1}\bar{b}_{m}E_{m}-\sum_{p=0}^{N_{3}-1}\bar{c}_{p}F_{p}\,.

(3.32)

If we assume that the
E
’s and
F
’s are functions only of
a,Y,Z
and their derivatives, then we’ve got

-[\mu_{0},\mu_{1}]-(\mu_{1})^{2}=\sum_{m=0}^{N_{2}-1}\frac{E_{m}}{m!}{\partial}^{m}Y+\sum_{p=0}^{N_{3}-1}\frac{F_{p}}{p!}{\partial}^{p}Z=Q(\mu_{0})=\sum_{m=0}^{|N_{1}|-1}a_{n}{\partial}^{n}(YZ)\,.

(3.33)

The inequality
|N_{1}|\leq N_{2}+N_{3}
guarantees that there is always a solution with
E,F
linear in the bulk fields. It’s unique when
|N_{1}|=N_{2}+N_{3}
, and otherwise underconstrained — but all solutions appear to be related by redefinition of the 1d fields, and are thus ultimately equivalent. One solution that sets to zero as many
F
’s as possible is

E_{m}=\sum_{i=0}^{|N_{1}|-1-m}\frac{a_{m+i}}{i!}{\partial}^{i}Z\quad(0\leq m<N_{2})\,,\qquad F_{p}=\sum_{j=N_{2}}^{|N_{1}|-1-p}\frac{a_{p+i}}{i!}{\partial}^{i}Y\quad(0\leq p<N_{2}-|N_{1}|)\,.

(3.34)

For example, the vortex line
\mathbb{V}_{(-1,1,0)}
is uniquely represented by the linear coupling

\mu=a\psi_{X}+bY-\bar{b}(aZ)

(3.35)

involving a bosonic multiplet
({\mathbf{a}},\bar{\mathbf{a}})
and a fermi multiplet
({\mathbf{b}},\bar{\mathbf{b}})
. On the other hand, the vortex line
\mathbb{V}_{(-1,1,1)}
has an entire space of representations with linear couplings to a bosonic multiplet
({\mathbf{a}},\bar{\mathbf{a}})
and two fermi multiplets
({\mathbf{b}},\bar{\mathbf{b}})
,
({\mathbf{c}},\bar{\mathbf{c}})
. Two possibilities are

\mu=a\psi_{X}+bY+cZ-\bar{b}(aZ)\,,\qquad\text{or}\qquad\mu^{\prime}=a\psi_{X}+bY+cZ-\bar{c}(aY)

(3.36)

To go from
\mu
to
\mu^{\prime}
we perform the 1d field redefinition

({\mathbf{a}},\bar{\mathbf{a}},{\mathbf{b}},\bar{\mathbf{b}},{\mathbf{c}},\bar{\mathbf{c}})\mapsto({\mathbf{a}},\bar{\mathbf{a}}+\bar{\mathbf{b}}\bar{\mathbf{c}},{\mathbf{b}}-\bar{\mathbf{c}}{\mathbf{a}},\bar{\mathbf{b}},{\mathbf{c}}+\bar{\mathbf{b}}{\mathbf{a}},\bar{\mathbf{c}})\,,

(3.37)

noting that this preserves the kinetic term
S_{1d}=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\bar{\mathbf{a}}d{\mathbf{a}}+\bar{\mathbf{b}}d{\mathbf{b}}+\bar{\mathbf{c}}d{\mathbf{c}}\,.

Finally, when two
N
’s are negative, say
N_{1}<0,N_{2}<0
and
N_{3}>0
, the commutation relations between bulk fermions play an important role in the MC equation. We introduce
|N_{1}|+|N_{2}|
1d bosonic multiplets
\{{\mathbf{a}}_{n},\bar{\mathbf{a}}_{n}\}_{n=0}^{N_{1}-1}
,
\{{\mathbf{b}}_{m},\bar{\mathbf{b}}_{m}\}_{m=0}^{N_{2}-1}
and
N_{3}
fermi multiplets
\{{\mathbf{c}}_{p},\bar{\mathbf{c}}_{p}\}_{p=0}^{N_{3}-1}
, with the naive coupling

\mu_{0}=\sum_{n=0}^{|N_{1}|-1}\frac{a_{n}}{n!}{\partial}^{n}\psi_{X}(0)+\sum_{m=0}^{|N_{2}|-1}\frac{b_{m}}{m!}{\partial}^{m}\psi_{Y}(0)+\sum_{p=0}^{N_{3}-1}\frac{c_{p}}{p!}{\partial}^{p}Z(0)\,,

(3.38)

which fails to satisfy the MC equation because

Q\mu_{0}=\sum_{n=0}^{|N_{1}|-1}\frac{a_{n}}{n!}{\partial}^{n}(YZ)+\sum_{m=0}^{|N_{2}|-1}\frac{b_{m}}{m!}{\partial}^{m}(XZ)\,,\qquad(\mu_{0})^{2}=\sum_{n=0}^{|N_{1}|-1}\sum_{m=0}^{|N_{2}|-1}\frac{a_{n}b_{m}}{(n+m+1)!}{\partial}^{n+m+1}Z\,.

(3.39)

We satisfy the MC equation by correcting the MC element to
\mu=\mu_{0}+\mu_{1}
, with

\mu_{1}=-\hskip-2.84544pt\sum_{p=0}^{|N_{1}|-1}\bar{c}_{p}\hskip-5.69046pt\sum_{i=0}^{|N_{1}|-1-p}\frac{a_{p+i}}{i!}{\partial}^{i}Y-\sum_{p=0}^{|N_{2}|-1}\bar{c}_{p}\hskip-5.69046pt\sum_{i=0}^{|N_{2}|-1-p}\frac{b_{p+i}}{i!}{\partial}^{i}X-\sum_{n=0}^{|N_{1}|-1}\sum_{m=0}^{|N_{2}|-1}a_{n}b_{m}\bar{c}_{n+m+1}\,.

(3.40)

Technically speaking, the third term, cubic in 1d fields and containing no bulk fields at all, is a deformation of the differential in the 1d quantum mechanics itself; while the first two terms are part of the coupling to the bulk.

For example, the vortex line
\mathbb{V}_{(-1,-1,2)}
is created by introducing two bosonic multiplets
({\mathbf{a}},\bar{\mathbf{a}})
,
({\mathbf{b}},\bar{\mathbf{b}})
and two fermi multiplets
({\mathbf{c}}_{0},\bar{\mathbf{c}}_{0})
,
({\mathbf{c}}_{1},\bar{\mathbf{c}}_{1})
, with

\mu=a\psi_{X}+b\psi_{Y}+c_{0}Z+c_{1}{\partial}Z-\bar{c}_{0}aY-\bar{c}_{0}bX-ab\bar{c}_{1}\,.

(3.41)

The final term deforms the differential on the QM itself;
e.g.
if the 1d state space is the Fock space
V=\mathbb{C}[a,b,c_{1},c_{2}]
the differential becomes
Q=-ab{\partial}_{c_{2}}
.

3.4
Example: gauge theories

As a final example, we’ll consider line operators in HT-twisted 3d
{\mathcal{N}}=2
gauge theories, and their quantum-mechanics realization. There is a great richness of line operators in gauge theory. We expect to find Wilson lines (namely, half-BPS Wilson lines
[
GY07
]
, which are compatible with the HT twist), as well as disorder operators at which the gauge connection or dual-photon fields — or more generally, operators parameterizing the Coulomb branch — have a specified singularity
[
KWY12
,
DOP12
]
. In the presence of matter, we also expect to find vortex-like defects for the matter fields, as long as their profiles are gauge invariant. On the other hand, in the presence of Chern-Simons terms, a pure gauge theory should be equivalent to a topological 3d Chern-Simons theory, so all line operators should become equivalent to standard Wilson lines. We’d like to indicate how this comes about.

3.4.1
Bulk local operators

We’ll only consider coupling 1d QM to perturbative bulk local operators (no monopoles). Nevertheless, the algebra of perturbative bulk operators in gauge theory is remarkably subtle, and we need to know it to solve the MC equation. There is a full derivation in Section (
6.5
). We’ll summarize here the result without matter.

Recall from Section
2.2
that the fields of gauge theory with group
G
are
\{{\mathbf{A}}_{a},{\mathbf{B}}^{a}\}_{a=1}^{\text{dim}\,\mathfrak{g}}
. The perturbative algebra
{\mathcal{A}}
of local operators at
z=0
is a dg algebra, generated by the zero-form components
\{c_{a},B^{a}\}_{a=1}^{\text{dim}\,\mathfrak{g}}
and their
{\partial}
derivatives. Since the potential terms in the action (
2.23
) are cubic, higher
A_{\infty}
operations vanish, but there is a differential and nontrivial commutation relations when
G
is nonabelian:

\begin{array}[]{c}\Big[B^{a},c_{b}\Big]=f^{ac}{}_{b}{\partial}c_{c}\,,\qquad\Big[B^{a},B^{b}\Big]=-f^{ab}{}_{c}{\partial}B^{c}\,,\\[5.69046pt]
Qc_{a}=-\tfrac{1}{2}f^{bc}{}_{a}c_{b}c_{c}\,,\\[5.69046pt]
QB^{a}=(k+h^{\vee}){\partial}c_{a}-f^{ab}{}_{c}c_{b}B^{c}=(k-h^{\vee}){\partial}c_{a}-f^{ab}{}_{c}B^{c}c_{b}\,.\end{array}

(3.42)

Here we’ve written the Chern-Simons form as
k\delta^{ab}
, with
\delta^{ab}
the Killing form in a suitable basis;

h^{\vee}
is the dual Coxeter number, and shifts by it appear due to the nontrivial commutator between
B
and
c
.
The differential in (
3.42
) can be deduced from the action of
Q
on fields (
2.29
), but the commutators are a a purely quantum effect.

3.4.2
Wilson lines and more

To construct a Wilson line we choose a 1d state space
V
with a collection of topological operators
J^{a}\in\text{End}(V)
,
a=1,...,\text{dim}\,\mathfrak{g}
, and introduce a coupling

\mu=J^{a}c_{a}\quad\leftrightarrow\quad\delta S=\int_{\mathbb{R}}J^{a}{\mathbf{A}}_{a}\,.

(3.43a)

As explained already in
[
Cos13a
,
CP20
,
PW21
]
, the MC equation in this case requires

0=Q(\mu)+\mu^{2}=-\tfrac{1}{2}J^{a}f^{bc}{}_{a}c_{b}c_{c}+\tfrac{1}{2}c_{a}c_{b}[J^{a},J^{b}]\quad\Rightarrow\quad[J^{a},J^{b}]=f^{ab}{}_{c}J^{c}\,.

(3.43b)

In other words, the
J^{a}
’s must furnish a representation of
\mathfrak{g}
.

To avoid confusion, it may be useful to keep in mind that this description of Wilson lines is, strictly speaking, perturbative, in the following sense. The local operator
c
(the zero-mode of the ghost) only truly exists in the theory if we take the entire gauge “group” to consist of infinitesimal
\mathfrak{g}
-valued gauge transformations, ignoring the global form of the group
G
. In this context, it makes sense to use the descent procedure to relate couplings to MC elements — and we find that Wilson lines are representations of
\mathfrak{g}
, with no knowledge of
G
.

Nonperturbatively, if
G
is a reductive group (the complexification of a compact physical gauge group), the zero-mode
c
should be dropped from the algebra of local operators, and invariants with respect to global gauge transformations should be taken by hand. Then the deformation to the action is
\delta S=\int_{\mathbb{R}}J^{a}A_{t}dt
still makes sense, but it does not come from an MC element. Moreover, invariance under large gauge transformation requires the
J^{a}
to integrate to a representation of the actual group
G
, rather than just the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
.

We will continue using a perturbative description (involving
c_{a}
’s) and supplement by hand the MC equation to require integrability of operators coupling to them.

The Wilson lines (
3.43
) are anomaly-free for any gauge theory, even with matter.
More generally, we could introduce couplings to
{\mathbf{B}}
’s as well as to higher derivatives of
{\mathbf{A}}
’s and
{\mathbf{B}}
’s. For example, for an
abelian

G=\mathbb{C}^{*}
theory at level
k=0
we can choose
r\in\mathbb{Z}
and
N\in\mathbb{N}
, introduce bosonic 1d multiplets
({\mathbf{b}}_{n},\bar{\mathbf{b}}_{n})_{n=1}^{N}
, and define

\mathbb{W}_{r,N}:\quad\mu=rc+\sum_{n=1}^{N}\tfrac{1}{n!}b_{n}{\partial}^{n}c\,.

(3.44)

At
N=0
, this would just be a Wilson line of charge
r
. For
N>0
, this line operator effectively requires the first
N
derivatives of the connection to vanish at
z=0
while simultaneously allowing
{\mathbf{B}}
to be singular,
{\mathbf{B}}\sim\frac{{\mathbf{b}}_{N}}{z^{N+1}}+\frac{{\mathbf{b}}_{N-1}}{z^{N}}+\cdots+\frac{{\mathbf{b}}_{1}}{z^{2}}+\frac{r}{z}+(\text{regular})
. Now, the chiral monopole operators in abelian gauge theory are
v_{\pm}\sim e^{\pm\gamma}

[
AHI+97
]
, where
\gamma
is the complexified dual photon, satisfying
{\partial}\gamma=B
. Thus, in the presence of the line operator (
3.44
), the monopole operators get an essential singularity

v_{\pm}\sim z^{\pm r}\exp\big[z^{-N}\times(\text{regular})\big]\,.

(3.45)

Dually, in an abelian theory at
k=0
, we could introduce 1d fermi multiplets
({\mathbf{a}}_{n},\bar{\mathbf{a}}_{n})_{n=0}^{N-1}
and a line operator

\mathbb{W}_{0,-N}:\quad\mu=\sum_{n=0}^{N-1}\frac{1}{n!}a_{n}{\partial}^{n}B

(3.46)

This effectively sets to zero
{\mathbf{B}}
and its first
N-1
derivatives (
i.e.
the first
N
derivatives of the complexified dual photon) while allowing the connection to have a singular profile

{\mathbf{A}}\sim\frac{{\mathbf{a}}_{N-1}}{z^{N}}+\cdots+\frac{{\mathbf{a}}_{0}}{z}+\text{(regular)}\,.

(3.47)

One could generalize this to non-abelian theories as well, as long as (
3.46
) is modified to be gauge-invariant. See for example
[
VW22
, Sec. 2.3.2]
.

3.4.3
Chern-Simons terms

Adding a Chern-Simons level in abelian theory will remove all the line operators (
3.44
), (
3.46
) when
N\neq 0
. Namely, the
\mathbb{W}_{0,-N}
lines no longer satisfy the MC equation because
Q({\partial}^{n}B)=k{\partial}^{n+1}c\neq 0
. Dually, in the
\mathbb{W}_{r,N}
lines all the terms
b_{n}{\partial}^{n}c
and associated deformations
\int_{\mathbb{R}}{\mathbf{b}}_{n}{\partial}^{n}{\mathbf{A}}
become Q-exact; thus any
\mathbb{W}_{r,N}
line becomes equivalent to the standard Wilson line
\mathbb{W}_{r,0}
. This is the behavior we were looking for, since the theory at
k\neq 0
is equivalent to pure abelian Chern-Simons.

In abelian Chern-Simons theory we also expect an equivalence of Wilson lines
\mathbb{W}_{r,0}\simeq\mathbb{W}_{r+k,0}
with charges shifted by the level. The equivalence comes from acting with a singular gauge transformation
g(z)=z
in the neighborhood of
z=0
to transform
\mathbb{W}_{r,0}
to
\mathbb{W}_{r+k,0}
(similar to (
3.13
), except now the symmetry is gauged). Alternatively, the equivalence could be seen coming from a monopole operator that defines an invertible junction between
\mathbb{W}_{r,0}
and
\mathbb{W}_{r+k,0}
, producing an isomorphism in the category of lines. This feature is difficult to see from the current perturbative perspective, but can be added by hand.

For non-abelian
G
, we similarly expect that when the Chern-Simons level satisfies
|k|>h^{\vee}
, the HT-twisted theory is equivalent to pure topological Chern-Simons (at level
k-h^{\vee}\text{sign}(k)
), so only Wilson lines should remain. A perturbative proof of this statement would involve showing that the algebra (
3.42
) is quasi-isomorphic to a finite-dimensional algebra
\mathbb{C}[c_{a}]_{a=1}^{\text{dim}\,\mathfrak{g}}
generated just by the zero-modes of the ghosts. This is easily conceivable: even in the non-abelian case, no combinations of
B
’s will be
Q
-closed due to the Chern-Simons term, and all derivatives
\{{\partial}^{n}c_{a}\}_{n\geq 1}
seem to become
Q
-exact for the same reason. (In Section
6.6
, we’ll give an explicit form of this argument for the Koszul-dual algebra
{\mathcal{A}}^{!}
, proving that derivatives
{\partial}_{z}
become
Q
-exact.)

3.4.4
Adding matter

With additional matter, line operators in a gauge theory become extremely rich. All the vortex lines from Sections
3.2
–
3.3
are in play, as long as they can be made gauge-invariant — by coupling the 1d QM fields to the bulk connection in the appropriate representation. They can further be superposed with Wilson lines, or gauge singularities of the form (
3.44
), (
3.46
).

The complete perturbative bulk algebra for gauge theory with matter is in Sec.
6.5.3
. As an illustrative example, let’s consider
G_{c}=U(1)
gauge theory, with a chiral of charge
q
, so the fields
({\mathbf{X}},{\mathbf{\Psi}})
have charges
(q,-q)
. The bulk algebra at
z=0
is generated by bosons
B,X
and fermions
c,\psi
(and their derivatives), with

\begin{array}[]{c}[B,X]=-q{\partial}X\,,\quad[B,\psi]=q{\partial}\psi\,,\quad[X,\psi]=-q{\partial}c\\[2.84544pt]
[B,B]=[B,c]=[c,c]=[c,X]=[c,\psi]=0\\[2.84544pt]
Q\,c=0\,,\quad Q\,B=\big(k+\tfrac{1}{2}q\big){\partial}c-q\psi X\,,\qquad Q\,X=q\,cX\,,\quad Q\,\psi=-q\,c\psi\,.\end{array}

(3.48)

In this theory, one can try to create a matter vortex
\mathbb{V}_{N}
of charge
N>0
(say) just as in (
3.16a
), by introducing
N
1d fermi multiplets
({\mathbf{a}}_{n},\bar{\mathbf{a}}_{n})_{n=0}^{N-1}
with a coupling

\mu_{0}=\sum_{n=0}^{N-1}\frac{1}{n!}a_{n}{\partial}^{n}X\,.

(3.49)

This doesn’t satisfy the MC equation, since
Q(\mu_{0})=\sum_{n=0}^{N-1}\frac{q}{n!}a_{n}{\partial}^{n}(cX)
; but with a correction

\mu_{1}=-q\sum_{n=0}^{N-1}\bar{a}_{n}\sum_{i=0}^{N-1-n}\frac{{\partial}^{i}c}{i!}a_{n+i}

(3.50)

the total MC element
\mu=\mu_{0}+\mu_{1}
does satisfy the MC equation.
The correction simply couples the QM multiplets to the bulk gauge field, deforming the 1d action by
\int_{\mathbb{R}}\bm{\mu}_{1}=-q\int_{\mathbb{R}}\bar{\mathbf{a}}_{0}{\mathbf{A}}{\mathbf{a}}_{0}-q\int_{\mathbb{R}}\bar{\mathbf{a}}_{1}{\mathbf{A}}{\mathbf{a}}_{1}-q\int_{\mathbb{R}}\bar{\mathbf{a}}_{0}{\partial}{\mathbf{A}}{\mathbf{a}}_{1}-...,
which ensures that the original coupling (
3.49
) is invariant under axial gauge transformations near
z=0
. We have effectively created the vortex

\mathbb{V}_{N}:\quad{\mathbf{X}}\sim z^{N}\times(\text{regular})\,,\qquad{\mathbf{\Psi}}=\frac{{\mathbf{a}}_{N-1}}{z^{N}}+\ldots+\frac{{\mathbf{a}}_{0}}{z}+(\text{regular})\sim z^{-N}\times(\text{regular})\,.

(3.51)

We can further superpose a matter vortex with a Wilson line of charge
r\in\mathbb{Z}
by adding an ‘
rc
’ term to the coupling,
e.g.

\mathbb{V}_{N}^{(r)}:\quad\mu=rc+\sum_{n=0}^{N-1}\frac{1}{n!}a_{n}{\partial}^{n}X-q\sum_{n=0}^{N-1}\bar{a}_{n}\sum_{i=0}^{N-1-n}\frac{{\partial}^{i}c}{i!}a_{n+i}\quad(N>0)\,.

(3.52)

Nonperturbatively, one expects to be able to “screen” matter vortices: acting with a singular gauge transformation
g(z)=z^{p}
in a neighborhood of
z=0
should induce an equivalence between
\mathbb{V}_{N}^{(r)}
and
\mathbb{V}_{N+qp}^{(r+kp)}
, where
k
is the bulk Chern-Simons level. The equivalence seems difficult to see from the current perspective.

4
OPE’s and non-renormalization

In this section, we take the general construction of line operators via quantum mechanics from Section
3
, and use it to analyze their perturbative OPE’s.

The basic problem we’d like to address is the following.
We consider a general perturbative 3d HT QFT
{\mathcal{T}}
, with action
S=\int\mathbf{p}_{i}d^{\prime}\mathbf{x}^{i}+{\mathcal{I}}(\mathbf{p},\mathbf{x},{\partial})
, and a pair of line operators
\ell,\ell^{\prime}
, constructed from 1d state spaces
V,V^{\prime}
and couplings
\mu_{\ell},\mu_{\ell^{\prime}}
. Let’s insert
\ell
at a point
z\in\mathbb{C}
and
\ell^{\prime}
at
w\in\mathbb{C}
(both extended along
\mathbb{R}_{t}
), by taking the respective couplings

\mu_{\ell}=\mu_{\ell}(z)\in\text{End}(V)\otimes{\mathcal{A}}\big|_{z}\,,\qquad\mu_{\ell^{\prime}}=\mu_{\ell^{\prime}}(w)\in\text{End}(V^{\prime})\otimes{\mathcal{A}}\big|_{w}

(4.1)

to depend on separate copies of the bulk algebra
{\mathcal{A}}
, restricted to lines
\{z\}\times\mathbb{R}_{t}
and
\{w\}\times\mathbb{R}_{t}
in
\mathbb{C}\times\mathbb{R}_{t}
.
In terms of path integrals, we’re tensoring with
V\otimes V^{\prime}
and inserting

\ell(z)\otimes\ell^{\prime}(w)=\exp\int_{\mathbb{R}_{t}}\bm{\mu}_{\ell}(z)\;\exp\int_{\mathbb{R}_{t}}\bm{\mu}_{\ell^{\prime}}(w)\qquad\raisebox{-28.90755pt}{\includegraphics[width]{LineOPEzw}}

(4.2)

When
z
is close to
w
, we expect this insertion to be equivalent — in all correlation functions — to a single insertion at
w
:

\displaystyle(\ell\otimes_{z-w}\ell^{\prime})(w)

\displaystyle\simeq\exp\int_{\mathbb{R}_{t}}\Big[\bm{\mu}_{\ell}(z)+\bm{\mu}_{\ell^{\prime}}(w)+\mathbf{r}_{\ell,\ell^{\prime}}(w,z-w)\Big]

(4.3)

\displaystyle\simeq\exp\int_{\mathbb{R}_{t}}\bigg[\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{n!}(z-w)^{n}{\partial}^{n}\bm{\mu}_{\ell}(w)+\bm{\mu}_{\ell^{\prime}}(w)+\mathbf{r}_{\ell,\ell^{\prime}}(w,z-w)\bigg]

Here
r_{\ell,\ell^{\prime}}\in\text{End}(V)\otimes\text{End}(V^{\prime})\otimes{\mathcal{A}}\big|_{w}\otimes C^{\infty}(D^{*}_{z-w})
captures potential quantum corrections due to interactions between the two lines. In general, its coefficients may be any smooth functions in an infinitesimal punctured neighborhood of
z=w
, denoted
D^{*}_{z-w}
, with an arbitrary singularity as
z\to w
.

The main result of this section is that the quantum corrections
r_{\ell,\ell^{\prime}}
are highly structured. For the strongest form of the result, we’ll introduce the notion of a
quasi-linear
theory (Section
4.2
): roughly, one whose fields can be split into multiple subsets, such that each interaction terms is at-most-linear in the bosons
or
the fermions from one of the subsets. All twisted 3d
{\mathcal{N}}=2
gauge theories, with linear matter, CS terms, and arbitrary superpotentials, are quasi-linear. We then prove the following in Section
4.3
:

Theorem 4.1
.

Suppose we have either a quasi-linear 3d HT QFT, or one with at most cubic interactions; and line operators
\ell,\ell^{\prime}
that preserve gradings
F,R,J
and
are defined by coupling
linearly
to elements of the perturbative bulk algebra
{\mathcal{A}}
. Then
(\ell\otimes_{z-w}\ell^{\prime})(w)\simeq\exp\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}_{t}}\bigg[\bm{\mu}_{\ell}(z)+\bm{\mu}_{\ell^{\prime}}(w)+\mathbf{r}_{\ell,\ell^{\prime}}(z,z-w)\bigg]\,,

where perturbative quantum corrections are give by the sum of single contractions

\int_{\mathbb{R}}\mathbf{r}_{\ell,\ell^{\prime}}(z,z-w)=\sum\;\raisebox{-21.68121pt}{\includegraphics[width]{OPE-diag-mu.jpg}}

(4.4)

In particular, bulk interactions terms
{\mathcal{I}}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{p},{\partial})
play no direct role in the quantum correction (
4.4
) — there are no Feynman diagrams with bulk vertex insertions.
Bulk interactions are of course important for solving the MC equations that define the line operators themselves. Indeed, we will see later in Sections
5
–
7
that there exist highly nontrivial constraints on the form of
r(z-w)
that involve bulk interactions. These constraints ensure, for example, that the OPE of lines is associative.

In Section
4.4
we explicitly compute the correction to the OPE. It follows directly from Theorem
4.1
that, in axial gauge, the correction is meromorphic and takes the exact form

\displaystyle r_{\ell,\ell^{\prime}}(z-w)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i}\sum_{n,m=0}^{\infty}\bigg(\frac{{\partial}\mu_{\ell}}{{\partial}({\partial}^{n}x^{i})}\frac{{\partial}\mu_{\ell^{\prime}}}{{\partial}({\partial}^{m}p_{i})}-\frac{{\partial}\mu_{\ell^{\prime}}}{{\partial}({\partial}^{m}x^{i})}\frac{{\partial}\mu_{\ell}}{{\partial}({\partial}^{n}p_{i})}\bigg)\frac{(-1)^{n}(n+m)!}{(z-w)^{n+m+1}}

(4.5)

\displaystyle\in\text{End}(V)\otimes\text{End}(V^{\prime})\otimes\mathbb{C}[(z-w)^{-1}]\,.

(We’ve used a linear basis
\{{\partial}^{n}x^{i},{\partial}^{n}p_{i}\}
for bulk local operators as in (
3.6
).) We comment on the general structure of
r_{\ell,\ell^{\prime}}
in other gauges in Sections
4.4.2
,
4.4.3
, arguing that axial gauge captures leading singularities, and that other gauges may deviate from axial gauge by
Q
-exact terms.

4.1
Warm-up: free chiral

To get a feeling for how OPE’s work, let’s consider a simple example. Take the bulk theory
{\mathcal{T}}
to be a free chiral multiplet, with the two simplest flavor vortex lines of charge
\pm
from Section
3.2
,

\mathbb{V}_{1}:\quad V_{1}=\mathbb{C}[a]\,,\quad\mu_{1}=aX\qquad\text{and}\qquad\mathbb{V}_{-1}:\quad V_{-1}=\mathbb{C}[b]\,,\quad\mu_{-1}=b\psi\,,

(4.6)

engineered by coupling (respectively) to a 1d fermi multiplet
({\mathbf{a}},\bar{\mathbf{a}})
and bosonic multiplet
({\mathbf{b}},\bar{\mathbf{b}})
. Recall that, effectively,
\mathbb{V}_{1}
gives
X
a first-order zero and
\psi
a first-order pole and
\mathbb{V}_{-1}
does the opposite.

We’d like to find the OPE
(\mathbb{V}_{1}\otimes_{z-w}\mathbb{V}_{-1})(w)
, so we consider the insertion

\mathbb{V}_{1}(z)\otimes\mathbb{V}_{-1}(w)=\int D{\mathbf{a}}D\bar{\mathbf{a}}D{\mathbf{b}}D\bar{\mathbf{b}}\;e^{\int_{\mathbb{R}}{\mathbf{a}}d\bar{\mathbf{a}}+{\mathbf{a}}{\mathbf{X}}(z)}\;e^{\int_{\mathbb{R}}{\mathbf{b}}d\bar{\mathbf{b}}+{\mathbf{b}}{\mathbf{\Psi}}(w)}\,.

(4.7)

inside the bulk path integral, or a bulk correlation function. We care about the interaction
e^{\int_{\mathbb{R}}{\mathbf{a}}{\mathbf{X}}(z)}\;e^{\int_{\mathbb{R}}{\mathbf{b}}{\mathbf{\Psi}}(w)}
and its behavior in bulk correlation functions, possibly in the presence of
arbitrary collections of other operators
. We’ll still denote the correlation function simply as
\big\langle e^{\int_{\mathbb{R}}{\mathbf{a}}{\mathbf{X}}(z)}\;e^{\int_{\mathbb{R}}{\mathbf{b}}{\mathbf{\Psi}}(w)}\big\rangle
. De-exponentiating this, we get

\Big\langle\sum_{n,m=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{n!m!}\int_{t_{1},...,t_{n}}\int_{s_{1},...,s_{m}}{\mathbf{a}}(t_{1}){\mathbf{X}}(t_{1},z)\cdots{\mathbf{a}}(t_{n}){\mathbf{X}}(t_{n},z)\;{\mathbf{b}}(s_{1}){\mathbf{\Psi}}(s_{1},w)\cdots{\mathbf{b}}(s_{n}){\mathbf{\Psi}}(s_{m},w)\Big\rangle

(4.8)

We do not need to worry about time-ordering or regularizing singularities when multiple operators coincide in this simple example, because as long as
z\neq w
all the operators here have non-singular collisions.

We now contract each pair of bulk fields, replacing

{\mathbf{X}}(t_{i},z){\mathbf{\Psi}}(s_{j},w)\;\leadsto\;:\!{\mathbf{X}}(t_{i},z){\mathbf{\Psi}}(s_{j},w)\!:+\;G(z,\bar{z},t_{i};w,\bar{w},s_{j})\,,

(4.9)

where
G
is the free-field propagator from Section
2.3
. Recall that, in this superfield formalism,
G
is a 1-form on the configuration space of two points in
\mathbb{C}\times\mathbb{R}
. It is symmetric in the two sets of variables,
G(z,\bar{z},t_{i};w,\bar{w},s_{j})=G(w,\bar{w},s_{j};z,\bar{z},t_{i})
. The normal-ordered combination
:\!{\mathbf{X}}(t_{i},z){\mathbf{\Psi}}(s_{j},w)\!:
can simply be defined as
{\mathbf{X}}(t_{i},z){\mathbf{\Psi}}(s_{j},w)-G(z,\bar{z},t_{i};w,\bar{w},s_{j})
; it has the usual property that it’s non-singular as insertion points of
{\mathbf{X}}
and
{\mathbf{\Psi}}
collide, and comes with a prescription that it should not have any internal contractions when inserted in correlation functions with other operators. Summing over all possible contractions in (
4.8
), we find

(
4.8
)

\displaystyle=\Big\langle\sum_{n,m=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{n!m!}\int_{t_{1},...,t_{n}}\int_{s_{1},...,s_{m}}\sum_{k=0}^{\text{min}(n,m)}:\prod_{i=1}^{n-k}{\mathbf{a}}(t_{i}){\mathbf{X}}(t_{i},z)\prod_{j=1}^{m-k}{\mathbf{b}}(s_{j}){\mathbf{\Psi}}(s_{j},w):

\displaystyle\hskip 28.90755pt\times k!\bigg(\!\!\begin{array}[]{c}n\\
k\end{array}\!\!\bigg)\bigg(\!\!\begin{array}[]{c}m\\
k\end{array}\!\!\bigg)\prod_{i=0}^{k-1}{\mathbf{a}}(t_{n-i}){\mathbf{b}}(s_{m-i})G(z,\bar{z},t_{n-i};w,\bar{w},s_{m-i})\Big\rangle\,.

Here ‘
k
’ counts the number of pairs of fields we replace with a propagator. For fixed
k
, there are
\small\bigg(\!\!\begin{array}[]{c}n\\
k\end{array}\!\!\bigg)
choices of
{\mathbf{X}}
’s and
\small\bigg(\!\!\begin{array}[]{c}m\\
k\end{array}\!\!\bigg)
choices of
{\mathbf{\Psi}}
’s, as well as
k!
ways to match
{\mathbf{X}}
’s to
{\mathbf{\Psi}}
’s, providing the prefactor. By symmetry, we then relabel/reorder the time variables so that the contractions only involve the final
k

t
’s and
s
’s.

To keep things simple for the moment, let’s use the axial-gauge propagator from (
2.35
),
G^{\rm ax}(z,\bar{z},t_{n-i};w,\bar{w},s_{m-i})=\frac{\delta^{(1)}(t_{n-i}-s_{m-i})}{z-w}dz^{\frac{r}{2}}dw^{1-\frac{r}{2}}
. We can strip off the powers of
dz
and
dw
, since they simply cancel against
dz^{-\frac{r}{2}}
from each
{\mathbf{a}}
and
dw^{\frac{r}{2}-1}
from each
{\mathbf{b}}
; the cancellation is guaranteed by the fact that the couplings
{\mathbf{a}}{\mathbf{X}}
and
{\mathbf{b}}{\mathbf{\Psi}}
have spin zero. Then each contraction allows us to do an integration, using the delta-function to set
t_{n-i}=s_{m_{i}}
, and we’re left with

(
4.1
)

\displaystyle=\Big\langle\sum_{n,m=0}^{\infty}\sum_{k=0}^{\text{min}(n,m)}:\prod_{i=1}^{n-k}\int_{t_{i}}{\mathbf{a}}(t_{i}){\mathbf{X}}(t_{i},z)\prod_{j=1}^{m-k}\int_{s_{j}}{\mathbf{b}}(s_{j}){\mathbf{\Psi}}(s_{j},w):

\displaystyle\hskip 50.58878pt\frac{1}{k!(n-k)!(m-k)!}\prod_{i=0}^{k-1}\;\int_{t_{n-i}}{\mathbf{a}}(t_{n-i}){\mathbf{b}}(t_{n-i})\frac{1}{z-w}\Big\rangle\hskip 50.58878pt

(4.14)

\displaystyle=\Big\langle\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\sum_{n,m=k}^{\infty}:\frac{1}{(n-k)!}\bigg[\int_{\mathbb{R}}{\mathbf{a}}{\mathbf{X}}(z)\bigg]^{n-k}\frac{1}{(m-k)!}\bigg[\int_{\mathbb{R}}{\mathbf{b}}{\mathbf{\Psi}}(w)\bigg]^{m-k}:\frac{1}{k!}\bigg[\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{1}{z-w}{\mathbf{a}}{\mathbf{b}}\bigg]^{k}\Big\rangle

\displaystyle=\Big\langle\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\sum_{n,m=0}^{\infty}:\frac{1}{n!}\bigg[\int_{\mathbb{R}}{\mathbf{a}}{\mathbf{X}}(z)\bigg]^{n}\frac{1}{m!}\bigg[\int_{\mathbb{R}}{\mathbf{b}}{\mathbf{\Psi}}(w)\bigg]^{m}:\frac{1}{k!}\bigg[\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{1}{z-w}{\mathbf{a}}{\mathbf{b}}\bigg]^{k}\Big\rangle

\displaystyle=\Big\langle\exp\int_{\mathbb{R}}\Big[{\mathbf{a}}{\mathbf{X}}(z)+{\mathbf{b}}{\mathbf{\Psi}}(w)+\frac{1}{z-w}{\mathbf{a}}{\mathbf{b}}\Big]\Big\rangle\,.

(4.15)

We further expand the coupling
{\mathbf{a}}{\mathbf{X}}(z)
around
w
to get the final form

\hskip-28.90755pt(\mathbb{V}_{1}\otimes_{z-w}\mathbb{V}_{-1})(w)=\int D{\mathbf{a}}D\bar{\mathbf{a}}D{\mathbf{b}}D\bar{\mathbf{b}}\,\exp\int_{\mathbb{R}}\Big[{\mathbf{a}}d\bar{\mathbf{a}}+{\mathbf{b}}d\bar{\mathbf{b}}+\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{n!}(z-w)^{n}{\mathbf{a}}{\partial}^{n}{\mathbf{X}}(w)+{\mathbf{b}}{\mathbf{\Psi}}(w)+\frac{1}{z-w}{\mathbf{a}}{\mathbf{b}}\Big]

(4.16)

Comparing to (
4.3
) and Theorem
4.1
, we identify the new interaction term

r_{1,-1}=\frac{1}{z-w}ab

(4.17)

This is induced from the Feynman diagram

(4.18)

which is the only
connected
Feynman diagram involving the interaction vertices
{\mathbf{a}}{\mathbf{X}}
and
{\mathbf{b}}{\mathbf{\Psi}}
.
The chain of manipulations above (
4.15
) simply rederive an elementary result from field theory, that the sum of all Feynman diagrams involving arbitrary numbers of
{\mathbf{a}}{\mathbf{X}},\,{\mathbf{b}}{\mathbf{\Psi}}
vertices is the exponential of the sum of fully connected Feynman diagrams.

4.1.1
Fusion to the identity line

We comment briefly on why the quantum correction in (
4.15
) is not just reasonable but in fact
necessary
for analyzing the behavior of line operators
\mathbb{V}_{1}
and
\mathbb{V}_{-1}
as they get close to each other.

One might intuitively expect that as
z\to w
the vortex lines
\mathbb{V}_{1}(z)
and
\mathbb{V}_{-1}(w)
annihilate each other, leaving behind the identity
\mathbb{V}_{0}={\mathds{1}}
. Such limits turn out to be very subtle. If we include local operators on the lines, and evaluate their correlation functions, the
z\to w
limit is typically singular. Nevertheless, we could assume that there are
no
local operators inserted anywhere else, and attempt to take the limit.

Consider (
4.16
). As long as
z\neq w
(and there are no local operators inserted), we can freely redefine the quantum-mechanics fields as

({\mathbf{a}},\bar{\mathbf{a}},{\mathbf{b}},\bar{\mathbf{b}})\;\mapsto\;((z-w){\mathbf{a}},(z-w)^{-1}\bar{\mathbf{a}},{\mathbf{b}},\bar{\mathbf{b}})\,,

(4.19)

while preserving the QM kinetic terms. This defines an isomorphic line operator, which has a regular limit as
z\to w
,

\displaystyle\int D{\mathbf{a}}D\bar{\mathbf{a}}D{\mathbf{b}}D\bar{\mathbf{b}}\,\exp\int_{\mathbb{R}}\Big[{\mathbf{a}}d\bar{\mathbf{a}}+{\mathbf{b}}d\bar{\mathbf{b}}+\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{n!}(z-w)^{n+1}{\mathbf{a}}{\partial}^{n}{\mathbf{X}}(w)+{\mathbf{b}}{\mathbf{\Psi}}(w)+{\mathbf{a}}{\mathbf{b}}\Big]

\displaystyle\hskip 57.81621pt\underset{z\to w}{\longrightarrow}\int D{\mathbf{a}}D\bar{\mathbf{a}}D{\mathbf{b}}D\bar{\mathbf{b}}\,\exp\int_{\mathbb{R}}\Big[{\mathbf{a}}d\bar{\mathbf{a}}+{\mathbf{b}}d\bar{\mathbf{b}}+{\mathbf{b}}{\mathbf{\Psi}}(w)+{\mathbf{a}}{\mathbf{b}}\Big]\,.

(4.20)

The coupling
{\mathbf{a}}{\mathbf{b}}
makes all the QM fields massive, and we can now integrate them out, using the EOM for
{\mathbf{b}}
to set
{\mathbf{\Psi}}(w)=-{\mathbf{a}}
. (This is not a constraint on
{\mathbf{\Psi}}
; it just says how
{\mathbf{\Psi}}
is related to the QM field that’s been removed.) After integrating out the QM fields, we are left with a trivial line operator, as expected.

Alternatively, we could have rescaled
({\mathbf{b}},\bar{\mathbf{b}})\mapsto((z-w){\mathbf{b}},(z-w)^{-1}\bar{\mathbf{b}})
in (
4.16
), then taken the
z\to w
limit, to recover a trivial line operator. Either way, it’s the quantum correction
{\mathbf{a}}{\mathbf{b}}
that ultimately allows us to fully integrate out 1d fields.

In contrast, had the quantum correction proportional to
{\mathbf{a}}{\mathbf{b}}
been
absent
, there would be no way to integrate out the QM fields. A naive collision limit of
\mathbb{V}_{1}
and
\mathbb{V}_{-1}
(without the correction) would have led to a nontrivial superposition of the two vortices, rather than the identity line. Different rescaling of QM fields during the limit could remove couplings to the bulk, but there would never be a way to fully integrate out the QM degrees of freedom themselves.

As might already be evident here, collision limits of line operators in HT theories are very subtle. Unlike in the case of 3d topological QFT’s, such collisions are not “functorial”: they are typically not unique (
e.g.
many rescalings of QM fields are possible), and they do not preserve spaces of local operators and their correlation functions. We refer to such collision limits as
fusions
, denoted
\odot
. For example, we argued above that there exists a fusion

\mathbb{V}_{1}\odot\mathbb{V}_{-1}\simeq{\mathds{1}}\,.

(4.21)

More examples of fusion, including in the XYZ model and SQED, appear in the PhD thesis
[
Py-25
]
; we hope to revisit fusion more systematically in the future.

4.2
Quasi-linear field theories

We’d like to generalize the OPE analysis of
4.1
to any perturbative HT theory (as in Section
2.1
), with fields
(\mathbf{p}_{i},\mathbf{x}^{i})
and action
\int_{\mathbb{C}\times\mathbb{R}}\big[\mathbf{p}_{i}d^{\prime}\mathbf{x}^{i}+{\mathcal{I}}(\mathbf{p},\mathbf{x},{\partial})\big]
, where the interaction is some polynomial in the fields and their holomorphic derivatives.
To obtain a strong non-renormalization theorem, we find that we must impose a mild constraint on the interactions, which we call
quasi-linearity
. We define the constraint here and argue that all HT-twisted 3d
{\mathcal{N}}=2
gauge/matter theories satisfy it. We’ll explain how to use the constraint in Section
4.3
.

We say that a 3d HT theory is
quasi-linear
if 1) we can write the interaction as a sum of terms

{\mathcal{I}}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{p},{\partial})=\sum_{\alpha\in\Lambda}{\mathcal{I}}^{(\alpha)}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{p},{\partial})\,;

(4.22)

and 2) we can find a corresponding partition of the set
S
that indexes the pairs of superfields (in some chosen basis)

(\mathbf{x}^{i},\mathbf{p}_{i})_{i\in S}\,,\qquad S=\bigsqcup_{\alpha\in\Lambda}S_{\alpha}\,,

(4.23)

such that, for each
\alpha\in\Lambda
and for each
i\in S_{\alpha}
, either

•

{\mathcal{I}}^{(\alpha)}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{p},{\partial})
is at most linear in
\mathbf{x}^{i}
, and all other
{\mathcal{I}}^{(\beta\neq\alpha)}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{p},{\partial})
are independent of
\mathbf{x}^{i}
; or

•

{\mathcal{I}}^{(\alpha)}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{p},{\partial})
is at most linear in
\mathbf{p}_{i}
, and all other
{\mathcal{I}}^{(\beta\neq\alpha)}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{p},{\partial})
are independent of
\mathbf{p}_{i}
.

(By “at most linear” we mean a polynomial of degree one.)

Consider, for example, HT-twisted gauge theory with arbitrary CS term, linear matter representation, and superpotential. The action from Section
2.2
is

\int_{\mathbb{C}\times\mathbb{R}}\mathbf{A}d^{\prime}\mathbf{B}+{\mathbf{\Psi}}d^{\prime}\mathbf{X}-\tfrac{1}{2}\mathbf{B}[\mathbf{A},\mathbf{A}]+\tfrac{1}{2}k(\mathbf{A},{\partial}\mathbf{A})+{\mathbf{\Psi}}\varphi_{V}(\mathbf{A})\mathbf{X}+W(\mathbf{X})\,.

(4.24)

We take

\begin{array}[]{l@{\qquad}l@{\quad}l}{\mathcal{I}}={\mathcal{I}}^{(1)}+{\mathcal{I}}^{(2)}\,,&{\mathcal{I}}^{(1)}=-\tfrac{1}{2}\mathbf{B}[\mathbf{A},\mathbf{A}]+\tfrac{1}{2}k(\mathbf{A},{\partial}\mathbf{A})\,,&{\mathcal{I}}^{(2)}={\mathbf{\Psi}}\varphi_{V}(\mathbf{A})\mathbf{X}+W(\mathbf{X})\,;\\[5.69046pt]
S=S_{1}\sqcup S_{2}\,,&(\mathbf{B},\mathbf{A})\in S_{1}\,,&(\mathbf{X},{\mathbf{\Psi}})\in S_{2}\,.\end{array}

(4.25)

This is quasi-linear because

•

for
\alpha=1
:
{\mathcal{I}}^{(1)}
is at most linear in
\mathbf{B}
and
{\mathcal{I}}^{(2)}
is independent of
\mathbf{B}
;

•

for
\alpha=2
:
{\mathcal{I}}^{(2)}
is at most linear in
{\mathbf{\Psi}}
and
{\mathcal{I}}^{(1)}
is independent of
{\mathbf{\Psi}}
.

4.3
Perturbative non-renormalization

Let’s now return to the computation of OPE’s, in a perturbative HT theory with action
\int_{\mathbb{C}\times\mathbb{R}}\big[\mathbf{p}_{i}d^{\prime}\mathbf{x}^{i}+{\mathcal{I}}(\mathbf{p},\mathbf{x},{\partial})\big]
. We consider two general perturbative line operators
\ell,\ell^{\prime}
defined by solving the Maurer-Cartan equation to couple quantum mechanics with state spaces
V,V^{\prime}
to bulk local operators (as in Section
3
). We assume that
the couplings are represented linearly
in terms of bulk local operators. Thus they take the form

\mu_{\ell}=\sum_{i,n\geq 0}\tfrac{1}{n!}\big(\alpha_{i,n}{\partial}^{n}x^{i}+\beta^{i}_{n}{\partial}^{n}p_{i}\big)\,,\qquad\mu_{\ell^{\prime}}=\sum_{j,m\geq 0}\tfrac{1}{m!}\big(\alpha_{j,m}^{\prime}{\partial}^{m}x^{j}+\beta^{\prime}{}^{j}_{m}{\partial}^{m}p_{j}\big)\,,

(4.26)

for collections of QM operators
\alpha_{i,n},\beta^{i}_{n}\in\text{End}(V)
and
\alpha_{j,m}^{\prime},\beta^{\prime}{}^{j}_{m}\in\text{End}(V^{\prime})
.
We’d like to establish Theorem
4.1

4.3.1
General Feynman diagrams

We analyze Feynman diagrams that can give perturbative quantum corrections to the insertion

\displaystyle\ell(z)\otimes\ell^{\prime}(w)

\displaystyle=\exp\int_{t\in\mathbb{R}}\bm{\mu}_{\ell}(z,t)\;\exp\int_{s\in\mathbb{R}}\bm{\mu}_{\ell^{\prime}}(w,s)

(4.27)

\displaystyle=\sum_{a,b=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{a!b!}\int_{t_{1},...,t_{a}}\text{T}\Big[\bm{\mu}_{\ell}(z,t_{1})...\bm{\mu}_{\ell}(z,t_{a})\Big]\int_{s_{1},...,s_{b}}\text{T}\Big[\bm{\mu}_{\ell^{\prime}}(w,s_{1})...\bm{\mu}_{\ell^{\prime}}(w,s_{b})\Big]\,,

where T denotes time-ordering. It is sufficient to consider connected Feynman diagrams; all others are products of connected diagrams, and the final re-exponentiated answer should simply contain the sum of connected diagrams. A connected Feynman diagram could have

•

some number
v_{\ell}
of vertices along
\ell
or
\ell^{\prime}
, where propagators will contract with a single copy of
{\partial}^{n}\mathbf{x}(z),{\partial}^{n}\mathbf{p}(z),{\partial}^{m}\mathbf{x}(w),
or
{\partial}^{m}\mathbf{p}(w)

•

some number
v
of bulk vertices (insertions of the interaction
{\mathcal{I}}(\mathbf{p},\mathbf{x},{\partial})
)

•

some numbers
e
of bulk-bulk propagators (connecting
v
vertices);
e_{\ell}
of bulk-line propagators (connecting
v
vertices to
v_{\ell}
vertices); and
e_{\ell\ell}
of line-line propagators (connecting
v_{\ell}
vertices on different lines)

Moreover, there is a fundamental constraint from preserving cohomological degree,
a.k.a.
ghost number symmetry. For a connected diagram to contribute to the effective, re-exponentiated action, it must be a one-form and thus have degree
R=1
(just like any interaction in QM). Line vertices also have
R=1
, while bulk vertices have
R=2
(for their
dt
,
d\bar{z}
indices). Propagators contracting pairs of bulk fields, whether in the bulk or on lines, effectively have
R=-1
. Thus, we require

2v+v_{\ell}-(e+e_{\ell}+e_{\ell\ell})=1\,.

(4.28)

The simplest connected diagram satisfying (
4.28
) has
v_{\ell}=2,e_{\ell\ell}=1
, and contracts a
{\partial}^{n}\mathbf{x}(z)
on
\ell
with a
{\partial}^{m}\mathbf{p}(w)
on
\ell^{\prime}
(or vice versa), with a single line-line propagator:

(4.29)

This gives a “tree-level” quantum correction, which we already found in the free-chiral example. We’d like to show that there are no other admissible diagrams.

Note that, since the couplings (
4.26
) are linear in bulk fields, the vertices on the lines are necessarily univalent. Thus the diagram (
4.29
) is the unique connected diagram with
e_{\ell\ell}>0
. To search for other connected diagrams, let us assume that
e_{\ell\ell}=0
. Then each univalent vertex on a line is connected to a unique bulk-line propagator, so
v_{\ell}=e_{\ell}
, and the degree constraint (
4.28
) simplifies to

\boxed{2v=e+1}\,.

(4.30)

Here’s an example of a diagram that satisfies (
4.30
), with
v=5
and
e=9
:

(4.31)

To rule out (
4.31
) (and all other possibilities), we need to further constrain potential interactions.

4.3.2
Constraint from quasi-linear theories

Suppose we have a quasi-linear theory, as defined in Section
4.2
.
Let’s split the bulk vertices and propagators as

v=\sum_{\alpha\in\Lambda}v_{\alpha}\,,\qquad e=\sum_{\alpha\in\Lambda}e_{\alpha}\,,

(4.32)

where
v_{\alpha}
is the number of insertions of
{\mathcal{I}}^{(\alpha)}
(or a term therein) and
e_{\alpha}
is the number of propagators connecting any
(\mathbf{x}^{i},\mathbf{p}_{i})
pair within the set
S_{\alpha}
. The quasi-linearity condition then forces

e_{\alpha}\leq v_{\alpha}\quad\forall\,\alpha\in\Lambda\,.

(4.33)

To see this, let’s assume for simplicity that the quasi-linearity condition below (
4.23
) is satisfied entirely with
\mathbf{x}^{i}
fields. (If instead it’s satisfied with
\mathbf{p}_{i}
’s, just relabel
\mathbf{x}^{i}\leftrightarrow\mathbf{p}_{i}
; the bose/fermi distinction between these two fields is not important here.) Thus
{\mathcal{I}}^{(\alpha)}
is at most linear in
\mathbf{x}^{i}
and all other
{\mathcal{I}}^{(\beta\neq\alpha)}
are independent of
\mathbf{x}^{i}
, for all
i
in
S_{\alpha}
.
Then each ‘
\mathbf{x}
’ end of a propagator counted by
e_{\alpha}
must connect to a bulk vertex of type
{\mathcal{I}}^{(\alpha)}
, and no two propagators can connect to the same
{\mathcal{I}}^{(\alpha)}
vertex this way because
{\mathcal{I}}^{(\alpha)}
contains
\mathbf{x}
at most linearly. This implies (
4.33
).

To illustrate this in gauge theory (
4.25
), let
v_{1}
be the number of
\mathbf{B}\mathbf{A}\mathbf{A}
and
k\mathbf{A}{\partial}\mathbf{A}
vertices; let
v_{2}
be the number of
{\mathbf{\Psi}}\mathbf{A}\mathbf{X}
and
W(\mathbf{X})
vertices; let
e_{1}
be the number of
\mathbf{B}_{a}\!-\!\mathbf{A}^{a}
propagators; and let
e_{2}
be the number of
\mathbf{X}^{i}\!-\!{\mathbf{\Psi}}_{i}
propagators. Since
{\mathbf{B}}
only appears linearly in
\mathbf{B}\mathbf{A}\mathbf{A}
vertices (and not at all in the others), we have
e_{1}\leq v_{1}
. Since
{\mathbf{\Psi}}
only appears linearly in
{\mathbf{\Psi}}\mathbf{A}\mathbf{X}
vertices (and not at all in the others), we also have
e_{2}\leq v_{2}
.

Summing (
4.33
) over
\alpha
we obtain
e\leq v
.
Combined with (
4.30
), we get
2v=1+e\leq 1+v
, whence

\boxed{v\leq 1}\,.

(4.34)

The remaining possibilities can quickly be ruled out. For
v=0
, the only available diagram is our desired (
4.29
). For
v=1
, (
4.30
) requires
e=1
, and the only diagram is

(4.35)

This does not contribute because the bulk interaction vertices
{\mathcal{I}}
are (by definition) already normal-ordered, so there are no contractions of fields within the same vertex.

4.3.3
Constraint for cubic field theories

An alternative to considering quasi-linear theories is to simply require all interactions terms to be at most cubic in the fields
\mathbf{x},\mathbf{p}
. In this case, bulk vertices will be at most 3-valent, leading to the constraint

2e\leq 3v\,.

(4.36)

This in turn implies
2v\leq\frac{3}{2}v+1
, or
\boxed{v\leq 2}
.

We’ve already ruled out
v=1
in (
4.35
). At
v=2
, the degree constraint (
4.30
) requires
e=3
. Diagrams with a propagator connecting a vertex to itself do not contribute, for the same reason as in (
4.35
). The only remaining diagram with
v=2
is

(4.37)

Here, however, we observe that the propagator
\langle\mathbf{p}_{i}(z,\bar{z},t)\mathbf{x}^{j}(w,\bar{w},s)\rangle=\delta_{i}{}^{j}G_{i}(z,\bar{z},t;w,\bar{w},s)
for free fields is a one-form on configuration space
\text{Conf}^{\,2}(\mathbb{C}\times\mathbb{R})=(\mathbb{C}\times\mathbb{R})^{2}\backslash\Delta
(it’s a one-form in
dt,d\bar{z},ds,d\bar{w}
indices). Moreover, given any gauge condition that treats all
\mathbf{x}
’s and
\mathbf{p}
’s, for all
i
,
symmetrically
— such as Lorentz gauge, or axial gauge — the propagator will be independent of
i
and will be symmetric,
G(z,\bar{z},t;w,\bar{w},s)=G(w,\bar{w},s;z,\bar{z},t)
. In any such gauge, the diagram (
4.37
) necessarily vanishes, because three identical one-forms get wedged with each other. Therefore, the only remaining option is
v=0
, establishing Theorem
4.1
.

4.3.4
Non-linear couplings

As a caveat, we note that our assumption above that line operators are represented by linear couplings to bulk operators is
essential
for the non-renormalization theorem. Without linear couplings, the vertices on the lines can have any valency. Then there are infinitely many admissible connected diagrams aside from (
4.29
), such as

(4.38)

It’s instructive to analyze this more general situation. The degree constraint generalizing (
4.30
) becomes

2v+v_{\ell}=e+e_{\ell}+e_{\ell\ell}+1

(4.39)

and the inequality due to quasi-linearity becomes

e+e_{\ell}+e_{\ell\ell}\leq v+v_{\ell}

(4.40)

which together imply
2v+v_{\ell}\leq v+v_{\ell}+1
, whence
\boxed{v\leq 1}
. Thus, there is still at most one bulk vertex. However, there can be arbitrarily many vertices on the lines, as in the diagrams of (
4.38
).

4.4
The exact tree-level correction

Having established that the only diagrams contributing to the OPE (in quasi-linear theories or cubic theories) are the direct line-line contractions (
4.29
), with no bulk vertices inserted, we can go ahead and compute the exact perturbative quantum correction to the RHS of (
4.27
). Since bulk interactions play no role, the answer looks essentially the same as in the free theory of Section
4.1
.

4.4.1
Axial gauge

Let’s first work in axial gauge, with propagator
G^{\rm ax}(z,\bar{z},t_{n-i};w,\bar{w},s_{m-i})=\frac{\delta^{(1)}(t_{n-i}-s_{m-i})}{z-w}dz^{\frac{r}{2}}dw^{1-\frac{r}{2}}
.

We note that, beyond tree level, axial gauge has been known to give incorrect answers for calculations in holomorphic field theories.
6
6

6

We thank Kevin Costello for pointing this out, in the context of NHMV amplitudes computed using twistor space. The phenomenon unfortunately does not seem to be well documented in the literature.
In the present setting, the combinatorial non-renormalization theorem from Section
4.3
guarantees that there are no higher-loop corrections — in fact, no corrections with bulk insertions at all — so axial gauge should be a valid choice.

More so, we expect axial gauge to capture the leading singularities in the OPE. If we place
\ell
at
z_{1}
and
\ell^{\prime}
at
0
, then bringing
z_{1}
close to
0
can be effected by a coordinate rescaling
z\to\epsilon z
in the
\mathbb{C}_{z,\bar{z}}
plane. For very small
\epsilon
, the ‘
d\bar{z}
’ components of all fields (
x_{\bar{x}},p_{\bar{z}}
, etc.) will become vanishingly small relative to the ‘
dt
’ components; thus all fields will effectively be cast into axial gauge. (We’ll return to this argument in Section
4.4.2
.)

A contraction between a single
\bm{\mu}_{\ell}(z,t)=\sum_{i,n\geq 0}\frac{1}{n!}\big[\bm{\alpha}_{i,n}(t){\partial}^{n}\mathbf{x}^{i}(z,t)+\bm{\beta}^{i}_{n}(t){\partial}^{n}\mathbf{p}_{i}(z,t)\big]
and
\bm{\mu}_{\ell^{\prime}}(w,s)=\sum_{j,m\geq 0}\frac{1}{m!}\big[\bm{\alpha}_{j,m}^{\prime}(s){\partial}^{m}\mathbf{x}^{j}(w,s)+\bm{\beta}^{\prime}{}^{j}_{m}(s){\partial}^{m}\mathbf{p}_{j}(w,s)\big]
in (
4.27
)
evaluates to

\displaystyle\hskip-21.68121pt\bm{\mu}_{\ell}\overbracket{(z,t)\bm{\mu}_{\ell^{\prime}}(w},s)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i,j}\sum_{m,n\geq 0}\frac{1}{n!m!}\Big[\bm{\alpha}_{i,n}(t)\bm{\beta}^{\prime}{}^{j}_{m}(s){\partial}^{n}\overbracket{\mathbf{x}^{i}(z,t){\partial}^{m}\mathbf{p}_{j}}(w,s)-\bm{\alpha}_{j,m}^{\prime}(s)\bm{\beta}^{i}_{n}(t){\partial}^{m}\overbracket{\mathbf{x}^{j}(w,s){\partial}^{n}\mathbf{p}_{i}}(z,t)\Big]

\displaystyle=\sum_{i,j}\sum_{m,n\geq 0}\frac{1}{n!m!}\Big[\bm{\alpha}_{i,n}(t)\bm{\beta}^{\prime}{}^{j}_{m}(s)\delta_{ij}{\partial}_{z}^{n}{\partial}_{w}^{m}\frac{\delta^{(1)}(t-s)}{z-w}-\bm{\alpha}_{j,m}^{\prime}(s)\bm{\beta}^{i}_{n}(t)\delta_{ij}{\partial}^{m}_{w}{\partial}^{n}_{z}\frac{\delta^{(1)}(s-t)}{w-z}\Big]

\displaystyle=\sum_{m,n\geq 0}\frac{(-1)^{n}(n+m)!}{n!m!}\frac{\sum_{i}\big[\bm{\alpha}_{i,n}(t)\bm{\beta}^{\prime}{}^{i}_{m}(t)-\bm{\alpha}_{i,m}^{\prime}(t)\bm{\beta}^{i}_{n}(t)\big]}{(z-w)^{n+m+1}}\delta^{(1)}(t-s)

(4.41)

Note that the minus sign in the second term in the first line comes from commuting the fermionic operators
\bm{\alpha}
and
\mathbf{p}
past each other. We’ve also stripped off
dz
’s and
dw
’s, as they just cancel against corresponding terms from
\bm{\alpha}
and
\bm{\beta}
, keeping track of spin.
We denote the QM operator from the last line as

\displaystyle\mathbf{r}(t,z-w)

\displaystyle:=\sum_{m,n\geq 0}(-1)^{n}\bigg(\!\!\begin{array}[]{c}n+m\\
n\end{array}\!\!\bigg)\frac{\sum_{i}\big[\bm{\alpha}_{i,n}(t)\bm{\beta}^{\prime}{}^{i}_{m}(t)-\bm{\alpha}_{i,m}^{\prime}(t)\bm{\beta}^{i}_{n}(t)\big]}{(z-w)^{n+m+1}}

\displaystyle\in\text{End}(V)\otimes\text{End}(V^{\prime})\otimes\mathbb{C}[[(z-w)^{-1}]]\,.

We can now sum over all contractions in (
4.27
) exactly the same way we did for a free chiral. Indeed, the derivation in (
4.1
)–(
4.16
) carries over nearly identically, with the replacements
{\mathbf{a}}{\mathbf{X}}\leadsto\bm{\mu}_{\ell}
,
{\mathbf{b}}{\mathbf{\Psi}}\leadsto\bm{\mu}_{\ell^{\prime}}
,
\frac{{\mathbf{a}}{\mathbf{b}}}{z-w}\leadsto\mathbf{r}
, and the inclusion of extra time-ordering along each line. We find that, in correlation functions (potentially with insertions of other operators),

\displaystyle(\ell\otimes_{z-w}\ell^{\prime})(w)

\displaystyle=\sum_{a,b=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{a!b!}\int_{t_{1},...,t_{a}}\text{T}\Big[\bm{\mu}_{\ell}(z,t_{1})...\bm{\mu}_{\ell}(z,t_{a})\Big]\int_{s_{1},...,s_{b}}\text{T}\Big[\bm{\mu}_{\ell^{\prime}}(w,s_{1})...\bm{\mu}_{\ell^{\prime}}(w,s_{b})\Big]

\displaystyle=\sum_{a,b=0}^{\infty}\sum_{k=0}^{\text{min}(a,b)}\frac{k!}{a!b!}\bigg(\!\!\begin{array}[]{c}a\\
k\end{array}\!\!\bigg)\bigg(\!\!\begin{array}[]{c}b\\
k\end{array}\!\!\bigg)\text{T}\Big[\int_{t_{1},...,t_{a-k}}:\prod_{i=1}^{a-k}\bm{\mu}_{\ell}(z,t_{i})\int_{s_{1},...,s_{b-k}}\prod_{i=1}^{b-k}\bm{\mu}_{\ell^{\prime}}(w,s_{j}):

\displaystyle\hskip 144.54pt\times\int_{t_{a-k+1},...,t_{a}}\prod_{i=1}^{k}\mathbf{r}(t_{a-i},z-w)\;\Big]

\displaystyle=\exp\int_{t\in\mathbb{R}}\bm{\mu}_{\ell}(z,t)+\bm{\mu}_{\ell^{\prime}}(w,t)+\mathbf{r}(t,z-w)

(4.48)

\displaystyle=\exp\int_{t\in\mathbb{R}}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{(z-w)^{n}}{n!}{\partial}^{n}\bm{\mu}_{\ell}(w,t)+\bm{\mu}_{\ell^{\prime}}(w,t)+\mathbf{r}(t,z-w)\,.

4.4.2
Other gauges: universal behavior

Working in axial gauge is not always convenient, or appropriate. For example, in a global spacetime, the choice of gauge we use must be compatible with global boundary conditions. Here we comment a bit further on the structure of quantum corrections to the OPE of line operators in
any
gauge.

Since our HT theory must ultimately be independent of gauge at a local level, we might expect that the total sum of all corrections to the OPE induced by computing it in a different gauge is cohomologically trivial —
i.e.

Q
-exact after de-exponentiating all time-ordered exponentials and keeping careful track of boundary terms.
This seems difficult to check/prove in full generality, though we’ll indicate later how it comes about in Lorentz gauge.

We can also use symmetry to partially constrain the behavior of any propagator. Let’s denote our general propagator as
G(z,\bar{z},t;w,\bar{w},s)
. It is a (distributional) 1-form on configuration space
\text{Conf}^{\,2}(\mathbb{C}\times\mathbb{R})
, though we only care about its
dt
and
ds
components, as all others will vanish upon insertion in the diagram (
4.29
) and integration of the two vertices along the support of the lines. If we assume that we use a gauge that’s invariant under translations, then these components of the propagator will take the form

G(z,\bar{z},t;w,\bar{w},s)_{s,t}=g(z-w,\bar{z}-\bar{w},t-s)d(t-s)

(4.49)

for some function
g
. The OPE will get contributions from contractions of derivatives
{\partial}^{n}\mathbf{x}(z,t)
and
{\partial}^{m}\mathbf{p}(w,s)
, as in (
4.29
), which evaluates to

(-1)^{m}{\partial}_{z}^{m+n}g(z-w,\bar{z}-\bar{w},t-s)d(t-s)\,.

(4.50)

It is convenient to use translation symmetry to set
w=\bar{w}=s=0
, and thus analyze the distribution
(-1)^{m}{\partial}_{z}^{m+n}g(z,\bar{z},t)dt
. Its behavior is constrained as follows:

•

Due to the scaling argument discussed at the beginning of Section
4.4.1
, every gauge must approach axial gauge in the limit
z\to 0
. Thus, the leading singularity is given by the holomorphic-gauge propagator

(-1)^{m}{\partial}_{z}^{m+n}g(z,\bar{z},t)dt\sim\frac{(-1)^{n}(n+m)!}{z^{n+m+1}}\delta(t)dt+\text{less singular in $z$}

(4.51)

•

If
t\neq 0
then the
z\to 0
limit must be regular (because the local operators
{\partial}^{n}\mathbf{x}
and
{\partial}^{m}\mathbf{p}
can have a singular 2-point function only when their supports coincide). Thus, the subleading singular terms are distributions supported at
t=0
, and should be proportional to
\delta(t)dt
and various derivatives
{\partial}_{t}^{p}\delta(t)dt
.

•

The entire expansion (
4.51
) must have spin
J=n+m
. This constrains it to be an expansion in
|z|
(which has
J=0
), so that subleading terms are of the form
C\frac{1}{z^{n+m}}|z|^{a}\times{\partial}_{t}^{p}\delta(t)dt
for various
a
and
p
and constants
C
.

Going back through the computation of Section
4.4.1
, we conclude that in
any
gauge the OPE of line operators takes the general form

\displaystyle(\ell\otimes_{z-w}\ell^{\prime})(w)\sim

(4.52)

\displaystyle\hskip 28.90755pt\exp\int_{\mathbb{R}}\big[\bm{\mu}_{\ell}(w)+\bm{\mu}_{\ell^{\prime}}(w)+\mathbf{r}(z-w)+\text{(subleading, non-meromorphic, singular terms)}\big]\,,

modulo terms regular as
z\to w
, which may be nonlocal along
\mathbb{R}
.

4.4.3
Lorentz gauge

We can be more precise if we specialize to Lorentz gauge (
2.34
). The time components of its propagator give

(-1)^{m}{\partial}_{z}^{m+n}g(z,\bar{z},t)=\frac{(-1)^{n}}{2}{\partial}_{z}^{n+m}\frac{\bar{z}\,dt}{(t^{2}+|z|^{2})^{\frac{3}{2}}}\,.

(4.53)

We claim that there exists an expansion near
z\to 0

\frac{1}{2}{\partial}^{n}\frac{\bar{z}\,dt}{(t^{2}+|z|^{2})^{\frac{3}{2}}}={\partial}^{n}\frac{1}{z}\delta(t)dt+C_{n,1}\frac{\bar{z}}{z^{n}}\delta^{\prime\prime}(t)dt+\ldots+C_{n,n}\frac{\bar{z}^{n}}{z}{\partial}_{t}^{2n}\delta(t)dt+o(|z|^{n})\,,

(4.54)

for some numerical coefficients
C_{n,k}
. To see this, we integrate against a test function
f(t)
,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}}f(t)\,{\partial}_{z}^{n}\frac{\bar{z}\,dt}{(t^{2}+|z|^{2})^{\frac{3}{2}}}
