\displaystyle=\frac{(-1)^{n}(2n+1)!!}{2^{n+1}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}f(t)\,\frac{\bar{z}^{n+1}\,dt}{(t^{2}+|z|^{2})^{\frac{3}{2}+n}}

\displaystyle=\frac{(-1)^{n}(2n+1)!!}{2^{n+1}z^{n+1}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{f(|z|t)\,dt}{(t^{2}+1)^{\frac{3}{2}+n}}

(4.55)

From here, we use Taylor’s theorem with remainder to approximate
f(|z|t)=f(0)+f^{\prime}(0)|z|t+...+\frac{1}{(2n)!}f^{(2n)}(0)|z|^{2n}t^{2n}+\frac{1}{(2n+1)!}f^{(2n+1)}(0)|z|^{2n+1}t^{2n+1}+h(|z|t)|z|^{2n+1}t^{2n+1}
, for some function
h
that tends to zero as
|z|\to 0
. Moreover, it’s reasonable to assume that
h
grows slowly enough as
t\to\infty
so that the integral
\int\frac{h(|z|t)t^{2n+1}}{(t^{2}+1)^{3/2+n}}
converges — since the original integral (
4.55
) is assumed to converge. Then, noting that only even powers of
t
give a nonzero integral, we get

\eqref{Lor-integral}=(-1)^{n}n!\frac{1}{z^{n+1}}f(0)+\frac{(-1)^{n}(n-1)!}{4}\frac{\bar{z}}{z^{n}}f^{\prime\prime}(0)+\ldots+\frac{(-1)^{n}}{2^{n}(2n)!!}\frac{\bar{z}^{n}}{z}f^{(2n)}(0)+o(|z|^{n})\,,

(4.56)

which justifies (
4.54
).

Deriving further regular terms in the series is tricky, because the Taylor expansion under the integrand cannot be continued beyond order
2n+1
(since the integral won’t converge). Instead, the complete
|z|\to 0
asymptotics of (
4.55
) can be derived be Fourier-transforming
f(|z|t)
, then doing the
dt
integral to get modified Bessel functions, and taking the
|z|\to 0
asymptotics of the Bessel functions. The asymptotics of Bessel functions at small argument involve non-analytic, logarithmic terms, which ultimately result in corrections to (
4.54
) at order
|z|^{n+1}
and higher that are not local — they are not proportional to delta functions and their derivatives at
t=0
. This produces the regular corrections to (
4.52
).

We note that, in Lorentz gauge, the nonlocal corrections to (
4.52
) are not just regular but
vanishing
as
z\to w
.

There is one final observation to make. In Lorentz gauge, all subleading singular corrections (
4.54
) are proportional to derivatives of delta functions. Therefore, the correction to the OPE of lines with couplings
\bm{\mu}_{\ell}(z,t)=\sum_{i,n\geq 0}\frac{1}{n!}\big[\bm{\alpha}_{i,n}(t){\partial}^{n}\mathbf{x}^{i}(z,t)+\bm{\beta}^{i}_{n}(t){\partial}^{n}\mathbf{p}_{i}(z,t)\big]
and
\bm{\mu}_{\ell^{\prime}}(w,s)=\sum_{j,m\geq 0}\frac{1}{m!}\big[\bm{\alpha}_{j,m}^{\prime}(s){\partial}^{m}\mathbf{x}^{j}(w,s)+\bm{\beta}^{\prime}{}^{j}_{m}(s){\partial}^{m}\mathbf{p}_{j}(w,s)\big]
involves singular terms of the form

\exp\int_{\mathbb{R}}\bigg[\bm{\mu}_{\ell}+\bm{\mu}_{\ell^{\prime}}+\mathbf{r}+\sum_{n,m}\sum_{k=1}^{n+m}C_{n+m,k}\frac{\bar{z}^{k}}{z^{n+m+1-k}}\big[({\partial}_{t}^{2k}\bm{\alpha}_{i,n})\bm{\beta}^{\prime}{}^{i}_{m}-({\partial}_{t}^{2k}\bm{\alpha}^{\prime}_{i,n})\bm{\beta}^{i}_{m}\big]\bigg]

(4.57)

(modulo signs). In topological quantum mechanics, we may rewrite
{\partial}_{t}=[Q,\widetilde{Q}]
in terms of the BRST and descent operators. If we moreover impose the simplifying assumption that all the QM operators
\bm{\alpha},\bm{\beta},\bm{\alpha}^{\prime},\bm{\beta}^{\prime}
are
Q
-closed, then the corrections immediately become
Q
-exact:

({\partial}_{t}^{2k}\bm{\alpha}_{i,n})\bm{\beta}^{\prime}{}^{i}_{m}-({\partial}_{t}^{2k}\bm{\alpha}^{\prime}_{i,n})\bm{\beta}^{i}_{m}=Q\big[({\partial}_{t}^{2k-1}\widetilde{Q}\bm{\alpha}_{i,n})\bm{\beta}^{\prime}{}^{i}_{m}-({\partial}_{t}^{2k-1}\widetilde{Q}\bm{\alpha}^{\prime}_{i,n})\bm{\beta}^{i}_{m}\big]\,.

(4.58)

This is in line with the expectation that the deviation from axial gauge should be
Q
-exact.

5
Koszul duality and dg-shifted Yangians

In this section, we introduce Koszul-dual algebras
{\mathcal{A}}^{!}
as a way to represent perturbative line operators, adapting
[
Cos13a
]
and the general procedure outlined in
[
CP20
,
PW21
]
to the context of 3d HT QFT’s.
7
7

7

For mathematical background on Koszul duality, we refer the reader to
[
LV12
]
.
This perspective has several virtues. First, it lets us describe the entire category of perturbative line operators
{\mathcal{C}}
(including its morphisms) as a category of
A_{\infty}
-modules for the Koszul-dual,

{\mathcal{C}}\simeq{\mathcal{A}}^{!}\text{-mod}\,.

(5.1)

Second, it lets us reinterpret (using Tannakian principles) the data of OPE’s of lines, and their associativity relations, in terms of universal structure in
{\mathcal{A}}^{!}
: the structure of a “dg-shifted Yangian.”

We’ll begin in Section
5.1
with two concrete physical constructions of the Koszul-dual algebra
{\mathcal{A}}^{!}
in a perturbative 3d HT QFT
{\mathcal{T}}
. The first construction (Section
5.1.1
) involves compactifying space to a
\mathbb{CP}^{1}_{z}
, with a particular singularity
{\mathcal{B}}_{\infty}
at
z=\infty
that makes the state space one-dimensional; then we define
{\mathcal{A}}^{!}
to be the algebra of local operators supported on the timelike line at
z=\infty
. Equivalently (Section
5.2
) we may first reduce
{\mathcal{T}}
on a spatial circle, to obtain a 2d B-model
{\mathcal{T}}[S^{1}]
with infinitely many fields — the modes of the original 3d fields. In
{\mathcal{T}}[S^{1}]
, the identity line
{\mathds{1}}\in{\mathcal{C}}
and the singularity
{\mathcal{B}}_{\infty}
both define natural boundary conditions, the former where only regular modes survive, and the latter where only polar modes survive. We again define
{\mathcal{A}}^{!}=\text{End}({\mathcal{B}}_{\infty})
to be local operators on
{\mathcal{B}}_{\infty}
.

Explicitly, if we expand 3d fields in modes on
\mathbb{C}^{*}_{z}
as
\mathbf{x}^{i}=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathbf{x}^{i}_{n}z^{-n-1}\,,\;\mathbf{p}_{i}=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathbf{p}_{i,n}z^{-n-1}
, we find that
{\mathcal{A}}
and
{\mathcal{A}}^{!}
are generated by regular and polar modes:

{\mathcal{A}}=\mathbb{C}[x^{i}_{-n-1},p_{i,-n-1}]_{n\geq 0}\,,\qquad{\mathcal{A}}^{!}=\mathbb{C}[p_{i,n},x^{i}_{n}]_{n\geq 0}\,.

(5.2)

Bulk interactions
{\mathcal{I}}
induce further
A_{\infty}
operations on both
{\mathcal{A}}
and
{\mathcal{A}}^{!}
, studied in Section
6
.

The key (physical) results of Section
5.1
are that
\mathbb{CP}^{1}
compactification must define
A_{\infty}
functors on configurations of lines

{\mathcal{F}}:\begin{array}[]{ccc}{\mathcal{C}}_{z_{1}}\boxtimes{\mathcal{C}}_{z_{2}}\boxtimes\cdots\boxtimes{\mathcal{C}}_{z_{n}}&\to&{\mathcal{A}}^{!}\text{-mod}\\
\ell_{1}(z_{1})\otimes\ell_{2}(z_{2})\otimes\cdots\ell_{n}(z_{n})&\mapsto&\text{States}(\mathbb{CP}^{1},\ell_{1}(z_{1})\otimes\cdots\ell_{n}(z_{n}),{\mathcal{B}}_{\infty})\end{array}

(5.3)

and that the restriction of the functor to a
single
line

{\mathcal{F}}:{\mathcal{C}}\overset{\sim}{\to}{\mathcal{A}}^{!}\text{-mod}

(5.4)

is an equivalence of categories (Section
5.2.3
).
Underlying the equivalence is the existence of a “universal” Maurer-Cartan element
\mu\in{\mathcal{A}}^{!}\otimes{\mathcal{A}}
, given explicitly by

\mu=\sum_{n\geq 0,i}\big[-p_{i,n}\otimes x^{i}_{-n-1}+x^{i}_{n}\otimes p_{i,-n-1}\big]\,.

(5.5)

The MC element
\mu
enters physically in the transversality and completeness relations of
{\mathds{1}}
and
{\mathcal{B}}_{\infty}
, viewed as boundaries of the 2d theory
{\mathcal{T}}[S^{1}]
.
It sets up a 1-1 correspondence, up to isomorphisms

\begin{array}[]{c@{\;}c@{\;}c}\left\{\begin{array}[]{c}\text{perturbative lines $\ell\in{\mathcal{C}}$, \emph{i.e.}}\\
\text{spaces $V_{\ell}$ with $\mu_{\ell}\in\text{End}(V_{\ell})\otimes{\mathcal{A}}$}\end{array}\right\}&\leftrightarrow&\left\{\begin{array}[]{c}\text{${\mathcal{A}}^{!}$-modules, \emph{i.e.}\ spaces $V_{\ell}$ }\\
\text{with an action $\rho_{\ell}:{\mathcal{A}}^{!}\to\text{End}(V_{\ell})$}\end{array}\right\}\\
V_{\ell},\,\mu_{\ell}=(\rho_{\ell}\otimes 1)(\mu)&\reflectbox{$\mapsto$}&V_{\ell},\,\rho_{\ell}\end{array}

(5.6)

such that
\mu_{\ell}=(\rho_{\ell}\otimes 1)(\mu)
. In other words, a line operator with linear couplings
\mu_{\ell}=\sum_{i,n}\frac{1}{n!}(\alpha_{i,n}{\partial}^{n}x^{i}+\beta^{i}_{n}{\partial}^{n}p_{i})
corresponds to the representation
\rho_{\ell}(p_{i,n},x^{i}_{n})=(-\alpha_{i,n},\beta^{i}_{n})
.

The correspondence (
5.6
), together with the fact that
{\mathcal{A}}^{!}
is linearly generated, further guarantees that all perturbative line operators can be represented by linear couplings to
{\mathcal{A}}
. We spell this out in Section
5.2.3
. We’ve assumed this important property in earlier sections of the paper.

To make (
5.6
) seem a bit less abstract, we give some examples of line operators as
{\mathcal{A}}^{!}
-modules, for the free chiral and the XYZ model, in Section
5.3
.
A few gauge-theory examples are discussed later in the paper (Sec.
6.5
).

In Sections
5.4
and
5.5
, we then return to the matter of OPE’s. We consider

1.

translations
T_{z}:{\mathcal{C}}_{w}\overset{\sim}{\to}{\mathcal{C}}_{z+w}
of perturbative lines;

2.

the OPE with quantum corrections
\mathbf{r}_{\ell,\ell^{\prime}}
;

3.

associativity of the OPE for arbitrary collections of lines

and reconstruct this data as additional structure (and properties thereof) in
{\mathcal{A}}^{!}
. We arrive this way at the definition of a dg-shifted Yangian (Def.
5.1
), and
Conjecture
5.2
: the Koszul-dual algebra
{\mathcal{A}}^{!}
in any perturbative 3d HT QFT is a dg-shifted Yangian.

Here’s a short summary of the results. First, translations simply induce dual translations
\tau_{z}:{\mathcal{A}}^{!}\to{\mathcal{A}}^{!}
. Next, a key observation is that in the OPE of lines from Section
4
,

(\ell\otimes_{z}\ell^{\prime})(0)\sim\exp\int_{\mathbb{R}}\big[\bm{\mu}_{\ell}(z)+\bm{\mu}_{\ell^{\prime}}(0)+\mathbf{r}_{\ell,\ell^{\prime}}(z)\big]\,,

(5.7)

has a universal form, when using axial gauge. Namely, the quantum correction
r_{\ell,\ell^{\prime}}(z)\in\text{End}(V\otimes V^{\prime})[z^{-1}]
can be expressed as
r_{\ell,\ell^{\prime}}(z)=(\rho_{\ell}\otimes\rho_{\ell^{\prime}})(r(z))
via (
5.6
), where

r(z)=\sum_{m,n\geq 0}\bigg(\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}n+m\\[-2.84544pt]
n\end{array}\bigg)\frac{p_{i,n}\otimes x{}^{i}_{m}-x^{i}_{n}\otimes p_{i,m}}{(-1)^{n}z^{n+m+1}}\in{\mathcal{A}}^{!}\otimes{\mathcal{A}}^{!}[\![z^{-1}]\!]\,.

(5.8)

More so, assuming that
\ell,\ell^{\prime}
were BRST-anomaly-free line operators, the quantum-corrected coupling on the RHS of the OPE (
5.7
) must also define a BRST-anomaly-free line operator. This means that for every
\ell,\ell^{\prime}
,
\mu_{\ell}(z)+\mu_{\ell}(0)+r_{\ell,\ell^{\prime}}(z)
is an MC element in
\text{End}(V_{\ell}\otimes V_{\ell^{\prime}})\otimes{\mathcal{A}}
, an extremely nontrivial constraint. (We give some examples in Section
5.3.3
.) For Koszul-dual algebras, it translates to the facts that 1)
r(z)
is an MC element in
{\mathcal{A}}^{!}\otimes{\mathcal{A}}^{!}
and 2) the coproduct, defined on linear elements by

\Delta_{z}(x^{i}_{n}):=\tau_{z}(x^{i}_{n})\otimes 1+1\otimes x^{i}_{n}\,,\qquad\Delta_{z}(p_{i,n})=\tau_{z}(p_{i,n})\otimes 1+1\otimes p_{i,n}\,,

(5.9)

is a map of
A_{\infty}
algebras
\Delta_{z}:{\mathcal{A}}^{!}\to{\mathcal{A}}^{!}\otimes_{r(z)}{\mathcal{A}}^{!}
, where the RHS is deformed by
r(z)
.

Finally, associativity of the OPE (Sec.
5.4.4
) is essentially the statement that

\ell_{1}(z_{1})\otimes\ell_{2}(z_{2})\otimes\cdots\otimes\ell_{n}(z_{n})\sim\exp\int_{\mathbb{R}}\bigg[\sum_{i=1}^{n}\bm{\mu}_{\ell_{i}}(z_{i})+\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq n}\mathbf{r}_{\ell_{i},\ell_{j}}(z_{i}-z_{j})\bigg]

(5.10)

in any correlation function, no matter in which order (and around which
z_{i}
’s) we choose to expand the LHS. In terms of
{\mathcal{A}}^{!}
, associativity is fully captured by the property

r_{23}(z)+(\text{id}\otimes\Delta_{z})(r(z+w))=r_{12}(w)+(\Delta_{w}\otimes\text{id})(r(z))=r_{12}(w)+r_{13}(z+w)+r_{23}(z)

(5.11)

in
{\mathcal{A}}^{!}{}^{\otimes 3}[\![z^{-1},w^{-1},(z-w)^{-1}]\!]
, which is reminiscent of quasi-triangularity. Associativity also implies that
r(z)
satisfies an
A_{\infty}
analogue of the Yang-Baxter equation (YBE).

We collect all these structures/properties of
{\mathcal{A}}^{!}
in a definition of dg-shifted Yangians. Physically, the analogy between our
{\mathcal{A}}^{!}
and (generalized) Yangians comes from comparing lines in 3d HT theories to lines in 4d HT theories, as developed in
[
Cos13a
,
CWY17
,
CWY18
]
(see also
[
Kap06
,
CW18
,
AN24a
,
AN24b
]
). Both are controlled by algebras with a chiral coproduct
\Delta_{z}
. In 4d, quantum corrections similar to (
5.8
) give rise to a spectral R-matrix of cohomological degree zero, which quantizes a symmetric monoidal structure to a braided monoidal structure — and thus satisfies the quantum YBE. In 3d, quantum corrections give rise to our dg-shifted “spectral r-matrix”
r(z)
of degree
1
, controlling singular terms in the OPE. It thus “quantizes” a symmetric monoidal structure to a meromorphic monoidal structure — and satisfies our
A_{\infty}
YBE.
8
8

8

One might also compare with 5d HT theories on
\mathbb{C}\times\mathbb{C}\times\mathbb{R}
, whose line operators are represented as modules for (generalized) affine Yangians. In this case, quantum corrections similar to (
5.8
) enter the coproduct
\Delta_{z}
itself,
cf.

[
GRZ23
, Eqn. 5.4]
,
[
Ash24
, Eqn. 3.5]
.

The analogy with standard Yangians is tightest for HT-twisted gauge theory with group
G
, matter
V
, and vanishing superpotential. Then
{\mathcal{A}}^{!}
is a quantization of a dg-shifted Lie bialgebra structure on the positive loop algebra of the cotangent Lie algebra
T^{*}[-1](\mathfrak{g}\ltimes\Pi V)
. The bialgebra structure arises precisely from a shifted version of Yang’s r-matrix. This perspective is developed in
[
NP25
]
.

Another analogy with Yangians and quantum groups is explored in Appendix
A
, where we show that the shifted r-matrix
r(z)
in
{\mathcal{A}}^{!}
inverts the shifted
\lambda
-bracket in
{\mathcal{A}}
.

Caveat:

This section and the remainder of the paper invoke many tensor products of
A_{\infty}
algebras. In general, products of
A_{\infty}
algebras are only defined up to quasi-isomorphism,
cf.

[
Lod11
]
.
Nevertheless, in perturbative HT QFT, tensor products of
{\mathcal{A}}
and
{\mathcal{A}}^{!}
are canonical: both
{\mathcal{A}}
and
{\mathcal{A}}^{!}
are constructed as deformations of free commutative algebras by bulk interactions (Section
6
); so
{\mathcal{A}}^{\otimes n}\otimes{\mathcal{A}}^{!}{}^{\otimes m}
can be defined similarly, as a deformation of a free commutative algebra by a sum of
(n+m)
independent bulk interaction terms.

5.1
Koszul duality in 3d HT theories

We outline here how a canonical Koszul dual algebra
{\mathcal{A}}^{!}
arises in perturbative 3d HT QFT’s, and why its modules represent perturbative line operators. For further details, we refer the reader to
[
Cos13a
,
CP20
,
PW21
,
Zen23
]
, mathematical background in
[
LV12
]
, as well as the review of Tannakian principles in physics in
[
DN24
]
. Our analysis in particular generalizes Sections 6-7 of
[
PW21
]
, which consider 3d HT free matter and pure gauge theories.

We’ll begin here by defining
{\mathcal{A}}^{!}
as an algebra of operators acting at
z=\infty
. Then, in Section
5.2
, we’ll use an equivalent perspective via 2d reduction to relate this to couplings
\mu_{\ell}
that we’ve used to define perturbative lines so far.

5.1.1
Boundary condition on
\mathbb{CP}^{1}

A starting point for Koszul duality is to find a boundary condition near spatial infinity — an asymptotic vacuum — that trivializes the state space, making it one-dimensional. Such a boundary condition always exists for perturbative 3d HT QFT’s, and is in fact canonical. To make it more concrete, it’s useful to think of “compactifying” the spatial
z,\bar{z}
plane to a
\mathbb{CP}^{1}
, and imposing a boundary condition at
z=\infty
.

Suppose that a perturbative theory has fields
(\mathbf{x}^{i},\mathbf{p}_{i})
. Recall that
\mathbf{x}^{i}
and
\mathbf{p}_{i}
transform as sections of the
\bar{\partial}
-complex on
\mathbb{CP}^{1}
, valued in the
J(x^{i})
and
J(p_{i})
powers of the canonical bundle, respectively. (Here
J(x^{i}),J(p_{i})
are the spins of the fields.)
Letting
z^{\prime}=1/z
be a local coordinate near infinity, we’ll impose

{\mathcal{B}}_{0}:\begin{cases}\mathbf{x}^{i}(z)\to\text{finite}\\
\mathbf{p}_{i}(z)\to\text{finite}\end{cases}\text{as $z\to 0$}\qquad{\mathcal{B}}_{\infty}:\begin{cases}(z^{\prime})^{2J(x^{i})-1}\mathbf{x}^{i}(z^{\prime})\to\text{finite}\\
(z^{\prime})^{2J(p_{i})-1}\mathbf{p}_{i}(z^{\prime})\to\text{finite}\end{cases}\text{as $z^{\prime}\to 0$}

(5.12)

The ‘
{\mathcal{B}}_{0}
’ conditions just impose regularity of the fields at
z=0
. The ‘
{\mathcal{B}}_{\infty}
’ conditions allow a singularity at
z=\infty
when
J
is positive, and require some order of vanishing when
J
is negative. Fields with this behavior are equivalent to regular sections of the
\bar{\partial}
complex valued in
{\mathcal{O}}(-1)
. (The equivalence just comes from redefining fields
\widetilde{\mathbf{x}}^{i}(z^{\prime}):=(z^{\prime})^{2J(x^{i})-1}\mathbf{x}^{i}(z^{\prime})
in a patch near infinity, and similarly for
\mathbf{p}_{i}
.)

Now, in a free field theory, the equations of motion along
\mathbb{CP}^{1}
require
\mathbf{x}^{i},\mathbf{p}_{i}
to be elements of
\bar{\partial}
-cohomology. But the bundle
{\mathcal{O}}(-1)
has vanishing cohomology: once
\mathbf{x}^{i},\mathbf{p}_{i}
are locally holomorphic, the respective boundary conditions (
5.12
) force
\mathbf{x}^{i},\mathbf{p}_{i}\equiv 0
on
\mathbb{CP}^{1}
. The state space is a quantization of the unique solution
\mathbf{x}^{i},\mathbf{p}_{i}\equiv 0
(geometrically, a point), and is thus one-dimensional, as desired.

In an interacting theory, the EOM are deformed. However, if the interactions are treated perturbatively, the interacting state space can be constructed by starting with the free-field state space and deforming it —
e.g.
by adding appropriate differentials corresponding to the interaction terms. When the free-field state space is already one-dimensional, there is no room for interactions to alter it, so we expect it to remain one-dimensional in general:

\text{States}(\mathbb{CP}^{1},{\mathcal{B}}_{0},{\mathcal{B}}_{\infty})\;=\;\;\raisebox{-39.74872pt}{\includegraphics[width]{CP1-id}}

(5.13)

(It’s important here that that we’re working perturbatively, with infinitesimally small interactions; otherwise new global solutions on
\mathbb{CP}^{1}
could be introduced and the argument would break down.)

The reason that trivializing the state space is important is that it allows us to “measure” the QM state spaces that create line operators. Suppose that
\ell
is a line operator constructed by coupling bulk local operators to QM with state space
V
, using a coupling
\mu
. If we insert
\ell
at
z=0
(or in fact any other finite point along
\mathbb{CP}^{1}
), the
{\mathcal{B}}_{\infty}
boundary condition kills all the bulk fields,
decoupling
them from the QM, and recovering

\text{States}(\mathbb{CP}^{1},\ell,{\mathcal{B}}_{\infty})\;=\;\;\raisebox{-39.74872pt}{\includegraphics[width]{CP1-ell}}\,.

(5.14)

Moreover, if we define

{\mathcal{A}}^{!}:=\begin{array}[]{l}\text{$A_{\infty}$ algebra of local ops supported}\\
\text{on the `defect' ${\mathcal{B}}_{\infty}$ at $z=\infty$}\end{array}\qquad\raisebox{-36.135pt}{\includegraphics[width]{CP1-algebra}}

(5.15)

then

•

{\mathcal{A}}^{!}
naturally acts on every state space
\text{States}(\mathbb{CP}^{1},\ell,{\mathcal{B}}_{\infty})
, for all
\ell
in the category of line operators
{\mathcal{C}}

•

Given two lines
\ell,\ell^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{C}}
and a local operator
{\mathcal{O}}\in\text{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}(\ell,\ell^{\prime})
at their junction, we get a linear map of state spaces
{\mathcal{O}}_{*}:V\to V
. The action of
{\mathcal{A}}^{!}
commutes with
{\mathcal{O}}_{*}
, since they are supported at different points on
\mathbb{CP}^{1}
:

(5.16)

Indeed, the action of
{\mathcal{A}}^{!}
will also commute with the differential on local operators at junctions of lines, as well as with higher
A_{\infty}
operations of local operators at junctions, because these are all defined by physical correlation functions supported away from
z=\infty
, whereas
{\mathcal{A}}^{!}
is supported at
z=\infty
. All this commutativity amounts to the mathematical statement that there is an
A_{\infty}
functor

{\mathcal{F}}:\begin{array}[]{ccc}{\mathcal{C}}&\to&{\mathcal{A}}^{!}\text{-mod}\\
\ell&\mapsto&\text{States}(\mathbb{CP}^{1},\ell,{\mathcal{B}}_{\infty})\,.\end{array}

(5.17)

5.1.2
Tannakian perspective

To put the current construction in a representation-theoretic context, it may be helpful to interpret the role/appearance of
{\mathcal{A}}^{!}
from a Tannakian perspective.
We start with a superficial, lightning review of Tannakian ideas; see
[
DN24
]
, especially the introduction and many further references therein, for a broader discussion of Tannakian constructions in QFT.
In Tannakian formalism, one represents a category
C
by producing a functor
F
from
C
to vector spaces — a so-called fiber functor — and defining
A:=\text{End}(F)
to be the algebra of natural transformations of the functor. It is then automatic that each vector space gets an action by
A
, so there’s a representation

F:C\to A\text{-mod}\,.

(5.18)

Moreover, if
F
preserves additional structures on
C
(
e.g.
a tensor product),
A
gets endowed with corresponding structures (
e.g.
coproducts).

Now, the procedure of measuring a state space on
\mathbb{CP}^{1}
, with
{\mathcal{B}}_{\infty}
at infinity, defines an analogue of a so-called fiber functor on our category of line operators
{\mathcal{C}}
. The measurement actually makes sense for multiple line operators as well: Given lines
\ell_{1},...\ell_{n}\in{\mathcal{C}}
placed at
z_{1},...,z_{n}\in\mathbb{C}=\mathbb{CP}^{1}\backslash\{\infty\}
we get a state space

\text{States}\big(\mathbb{CP}^{1},\ell_{1}(z_{1})\otimes\ell_{2}(z_{2})\otimes\cdots\otimes\ell_{n}(z_{n}),{\mathcal{B}}_{\infty}\big)\in\text{Vect}\,,

(5.19)

where ‘Vect’ is the category of dg vector spaces.
The state spaces define functors of categories, for every collection of points on
\mathbb{C}
,

{\mathcal{F}}_{(z_{1},...,z_{n})}:{\mathcal{C}}_{z_{1}}\boxtimes{\mathcal{C}}_{z_{2}}\boxtimes\cdots\boxtimes{\mathcal{C}}_{z_{n}}\to\text{Vect}\,.

(5.20)

The functors for different collections of points on
\mathbb{C}
cannot be completely independent. We know that translation isomorphisms can be used to shift the points around. Moreover, the OPE of lines contains (in principle) precisely the information that one needs to relate
{\mathcal{C}}_{z_{1}}\otimes{\mathcal{C}}_{z_{2}}
to
{\mathcal{C}}_{z_{2}}
(etc.), and thus to relate
{\mathcal{F}}_{z_{1},z_{2}}
to
{\mathcal{F}}_{z_{2}}
(etc.). Altogether, what one has is an entire system of functors
{\mathcal{F}}_{\bullet}
for all possible collections of points, and relations among them induced by translations and the OPE.

The endomorphism algebra of the entire system of functors should be precisely
{\mathcal{A}}^{!}
,

\text{expect:}\qquad\boxed{\text{End}({\mathcal{F}}_{\bullet})={\mathcal{A}}^{!}}

(5.21)

The physical reason to expect this is that
\text{End}({\mathcal{F}}_{\bullet})
represents operators on
\mathbb{CP}^{1}
that 1) are localized in time (so they act on
\mathbb{CP}^{1}
state spaces); and 2) commute with all local operators at junctions of any collection of lines at any finite points
(z_{1},...,z_{n})
on
\mathbb{CP}^{1}
. Property (2) is what makes some
a\in\text{End}({\mathcal{F}}_{\bullet})
a natural transformation. In order for (2) to be satisfied, the operators should be localized near infinity, and we recover the definition of
{\mathcal{A}}^{!}
from (
5.15
).

We warn the reader that
{\mathcal{F}}_{\bullet}
does
not
preserve tensor products of lines:
{\mathcal{F}}_{z_{1},z_{2}}\big(\ell_{1}(z_{1})\otimes\ell_{2}(z_{2})\big)\;/\hskip-9.04166pt\simeq{\mathcal{F}}_{z_{1}}(\ell_{1}(z_{1}))\otimes{\mathcal{F}}_{z_{2}}(\ell_{1}(z_{2}))
(etc.). Thus it is not a monoidal fiber functor. Its failure to preserve products, and the structure induced in order to correct this, will be the subject of Section
5.4
.

5.2
Koszul duality via 2d reduction

An important question about the functor (
5.17
) for a
single
line operator remains:

•

Is the functor
{\mathcal{C}}\to{\mathcal{A}}^{!}\text{-mod}
an
equivalence
of categories?

We’ll argue that it is, for
perturbative
3d HT QFT’s.
Moreover, we’ll show how to explicitly “invert” the functor, going from the structure of an
{\mathcal{A}}^{!}
-module on
V_{\ell}
to the MC element
\mu_{\ell}
used to couple
V_{\ell}
to bulk local operators, in order to define a perturbative line
\ell
as in Section
3
. In other words, we’ll give a concrete physical realization of the correspondence (
5.6
).

To proceed, it’s very helpful to work with a 2d rewriting of a 3d HT theory — a reduction to 2d that keeps all modes, and is thus equivalent to the original theory. In the 2d reduction, the singularity
{\mathcal{B}}_{\infty}
on
\mathbb{CP}^{1}
will become an ordinary boundary condition; we will be able to place it at finite distance, and in particular to “invert” it (by gluing to a second, dual boundary condition) in order to invert the functor
{\mathcal{C}}\to{\mathcal{A}}^{!}\text{-mod}
.

5.2.1
Reduction to 2d

Consider a Lagrangian theory with fields
(\mathbf{x}^{i},\mathbf{p}_{i})
and interaction
{\mathcal{I}}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{p},{\partial})
on
\mathbb{C}_{z}^{*}\times\mathbb{R}_{t}
. We’ll remove the origin for the moment, because we may want to place nontrivial line operators there. We let

z=re^{i\theta}\,,\qquad\bar{z}=re^{-i\theta}

(5.22)

and consider the circle fibration (
cf.

[
PW21
, Sec. 6.5]
,
[
Zen23
, Sec. 2]
)

\begin{array}[]{ccc}S^{1}&\to&\mathbb{C}_{z}^{*}\times\mathbb{R}_{t}\\
&&\downarrow\\
&&\;\;\mathbb{R}_{r}\times\mathbb{R}_{t}\,.\end{array}

(5.23)

Schematically, the 3d action is

S=\int_{\mathbb{C}^{*}_{z}\times\mathbb{R}_{t}}\mathbf{L}\,=\int_{\mathbb{C}^{*}_{z}\times\mathbb{R}_{t}}\mathbf{p}_{i}d^{\prime}\mathbf{x}^{i}+{\mathcal{I}}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{p},{\partial})\,,

(5.24)

with multiforms
\mathbf{x}^{i}\in\Omega^{\prime}{}^{\bullet}(\mathbb{C}\times\mathbb{R})[R_{i}]dz^{J_{i}}
,
\mathbf{p}_{i}\in\Omega^{\prime}{}^{\bullet}(\mathbb{C}\times\mathbb{R})[1-R_{i}]dz^{1-J_{i}}
, where
\Omega^{\prime}{}^{\bullet}(\mathbb{C}\times\mathbb{R})=\Omega^{\bullet}(\mathbb{C}\times\mathbb{R})/(dz)
is the de Rham complex of
\mathbb{C}\times\mathbb{R}
, modulo
dz
.
If we change coordinates from
(z,\bar{z},t)
to
(z,r,t)
then each multiform field transforms as,
e.g.
,

\begin{array}[]{c}\mathbf{x}=x+x_{t}dt+x_{\bar{z}}d\bar{z}+x_{\bar{z}t}d\bar{z}dt=x+x_{t}dt+x_{r}dr+x_{rt}drdt\quad(\text{mod}\,dz)\\[5.69046pt]
\displaystyle\Rightarrow\qquad x_{\bar{z}}=\frac{z}{2r}x_{r}\,,\qquad x_{\bar{z}t}=\frac{z}{2r}x_{rt}\,.\end{array}

(5.25)

The exterior derivative simply becomes
d^{\prime}={\partial}_{r}dr+{\partial}_{t}dt
. The Lagrangian itself (the part that contributes to the action) has
R=2
and
J=1
, and thus must transform as
\mathbf{L}=L_{\bar{z}t}d\bar{z}dtdz=L_{rt}drdtdz
with
L_{\bar{z}t}=\frac{z}{2r}L_{rt}
. The action then becomes

S=\int_{\mathbb{C}_{z}^{*}\times\mathbb{R}_{t}}L_{\bar{z}t}d\bar{z}dtdz=\int_{\mathbb{C}_{z}^{*}\times\mathbb{R}_{t}}drdtdz\,L_{rt}(z,r,t)=\int_{\{r>0\}\times\mathbb{R}_{t}}drdt\oint_{S^{1}}dz\,L_{rt}(z,r,t)\,.

(5.26)

Let’s now expand the multiform fields into modes along
S^{1}
:

\begin{array}[]{l@{\,}l@{\,}ll}\mathbf{x}^{i}(z)&\displaystyle=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathbf{x}^{i}_{n}(r,t)\,z^{-n-1}&\displaystyle=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathbf{x}^{i}_{n}(r,t)r^{-n-1}\,e^{-i(n+1)\theta}&\quad\in\Omega^{\bullet}(\mathbb{R}^{2})(\!(z)\!)\,[R_{i}]dz^{J_{i}}\,,\\[5.69046pt]
\mathbf{p}_{i}(z)&\displaystyle=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathbf{p}_{i,n}(r,t)\,z^{-n-1}&\displaystyle=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathbf{p}_{i,n}(r,t)r^{-n-1}\,e^{-i(n+1)\theta}&\quad\in\Omega^{\bullet}(\mathbb{R}^{2})(\!(z)\!)\,[1-R_{i}]dz^{1-J_{i}}\,.\end{array}

(5.27)

We use integer-valued moding in order to be compatible with inserting the trivial line
{\mathds{1}}
at
z=0
, which requires all fields to be regular.
9
9

9

Other choices of moding may be possible, and would detect other line operators that have no morphisms to those in the category
{\mathcal{C}}
described here. We don’t consider them in this paper.
Assuming that the interaction
{\mathcal{I}}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{p},{\partial})
is a polynomial in the fields and
{\partial}
, let

{\mathcal{I}}=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}{\mathcal{I}}_{n}(r,t)z^{-n-1}

(5.28)

be its corresponding mode expansion. Each
{\mathcal{I}}_{n}
is a (typically infinite) sum of monomials in the
\mathbf{x}^{i}_{k}
and
\mathbf{p}_{i,k}
. Then we find (ignoring factors of
2\pi i
)

S=\int_{\{r>0\}\times\mathbb{R}_{t}}\bigg[\sum_{n+m=-1}\mathbf{p}_{i,n}d\mathbf{x}^{i}_{m}+{\mathcal{I}}_{0}(\mathbf{x}^{i}_{k},\mathbf{p}_{i,k})\Bigg]\;=:S_{2d}

(5.29)

Here
d={\partial}_{r}dr+{\partial}_{t}dt
is the 2d exterior derivative.

Altogether, the 3d theory on
\mathbb{C}^{*}_{z}\times\mathbb{R}_{t}
becomes equivalent to a 2d theory with infinitely many pairs of fields
(\mathbf{x}^{i}_{n},\mathbf{p}_{i,n})
, and interaction
{\mathcal{I}}_{0}=\oint_{S^{1}}{\mathcal{I}}
. The kinetic term
\sum_{n}\mathbf{p}_{i,-n-1}d\mathbf{x}^{i}_{n}
identifies the 2d theory as a generalized B-model, whose “target space” is the infinite-dimensional space with bosonic coordinates
x^{i}_{n}
.

5.2.2
Boundaries and algebras for the 2d theory

Now let’s come back to line operators. The category of line operators
{\mathcal{C}}
in the 3d theory, placed at
z=0
and viewed as an
A_{\infty}
category (forgetting translations and the OPE), is equivalent to the category of
boundary conditions
of the effective 2d theory at
r=0
. This equivalence can be viewed as a state-operator correspondence for line operators:

(5.30)

In particular, the identity line
{\mathds{1}}\in{\mathcal{C}}
corresponds to the boundary condition
{\mathcal{B}}_{0}
(the same one as in (
5.12
)) that makes all fields regular at
z=0
, namely

{\mathds{1}}\;\leftrightarrow\;{\mathcal{B}}_{0}:\quad\mathbf{x}^{i}_{n}|_{r=0}=\mathbf{p}_{i,n}|_{r=0}=0\quad\text{for $n\geq 0$}\,;

(5.31)

and the bulk algebra of local operators restricted to
z=0
becomes identified with local operators on the boundary
{\mathcal{B}}_{0}
of the 2d reduction:

\begin{array}[]{c@{}c@{}c@{}c@{}c}{\mathcal{A}}&\simeq&\text{End}_{\mathcal{C}}({\mathds{1}})&\simeq&\text{End}_{\text{Bdy}}({\mathcal{B}}_{0})\\
\text{(bulk local ops, at $z=0$)}&&\text{(local ops on the line ${\mathds{1}}$)}&&\text{(local ops on the boundary ${\mathcal{B}}_{0}$)}\end{array}

(5.32)

In a free theory, the algebra
{\mathcal{A}}
is a free, graded-commutative polynomial algebra

{\mathcal{A}}\simeq\mathbb{C}[x^{i}_{-n-1},p_{i,-n-1}]_{n\geq 0}\,,

(5.33)

generated by the zero-form components of the modes that aren’t set to zero by the boundary condition (
5.31
). (Only the zero-form parts survive in the cohomology of
Q=d
.) Of course we knew this: from a 3d perspective,
x^{i}_{-n-1}=\frac{1}{n!}{\partial}^{n}x(0)
and
p^{i}_{-n-1}=\frac{1}{n!}{\partial}^{n}p(0)
the operators we’ve been using all along to couple to QM, as in Section
3.2
. If we introduce interactions
{\mathcal{I}}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{p},{\partial})
, then,
perturbatively
, the algebra
{\mathcal{A}}
is still generated by the modes in (
5.33
), but has corrections to its BRST operator, commutation relations, and higher
A_{\infty}
operations. We’ll compute the corrections in Section
6
.

Now, the boundary condition at
z=\infty
on
\mathbb{CP}^{1}
described in (
5.12
)
also
corresponds to a boundary condition of the 2d theory. After circle reduction, the radial
r
direction is topological, so we can bring
r=\infty
in to a finite point, say ‘
r_{1}
’. (This is a key simplifying feature of the reduction.) Written in terms of modes (
5.27
), the spin dependence drops out, and the boundary condition “at infinity” just becomes

{\mathcal{B}}_{\infty}:\quad\mathbf{x}^{i}_{n}|_{r=r_{1}}=\mathbf{p}_{i,n}|_{r=r_{1}}=0\quad\text{for $n<0$}\,,

(5.34)

and

\begin{array}[]{ccc}{\mathcal{A}}^{!}&\simeq&\text{End}_{\text{Bdy}}({\mathcal{B}}_{\infty})\\
\text{(local ops near $z=\infty$)}&&\text{(local ops on the boundary ${\mathcal{B}}_{\infty}$)}\end{array}

(5.35)

We immediately deduce that in a free theory
{\mathcal{A}}^{!}
will be a free, graded-commutative polynomial algebra

{\mathcal{A}}^{!}=\mathbb{C}[x^{i}_{n},p_{i,n}]_{n\geq 0}\,.

(5.36)

Once again, if we introduce
perturbative
interactions
{\mathcal{I}}
, then
{\mathcal{A}}^{!}
is still generated by
x^{i}_{n},p_{i,n}
as in (
5.36
), but with deformed BRST differential, commutators, and
A_{\infty}
operations.

10
10

10

There is a small caveat here. Boundary conditions for the reduced 2d theory
{\mathcal{T}}[S^{1}]
on a half-space
H
have a potential BRST anomaly when the interaction term
{\mathcal{I}}_{0}
fails to vanish at the boundary, since
Q\int_{H}{\mathcal{I}}_{0}=\int_{H}d{\mathcal{I}}_{0}=\int_{{\partial}H}{\mathcal{I}}_{0}
. On the
{\mathcal{B}}_{0}
boundary condition, we always have
{\mathcal{I}}_{0}|_{{\mathcal{B}}_{0}}=0
, consistent with the fact that the identity line
{\mathds{1}}
in 3d is anomaly-free. On the
{\mathcal{B}}_{\infty}
boundary condition,
{\mathcal{I}}_{0}|_{{\mathcal{B}}_{\infty}}=0

unless
the original 3d interaction
{\mathcal{I}}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{p},{\partial})
has linear terms in
\mathbf{x}
,
\mathbf{p}
(independent of
{\partial}
). In this latter case, the anomaly can be incorporated into representation theory: a version of Koszul duality still holds, but
{\mathcal{A}}^{!}
must be treated as a curved
A_{\infty}
algebra, with
m_{0}={\mathcal{I}}_{0}|_{{\mathcal{B}}_{\infty}}
. We won’t consider anomalous examples.

Since the
r
direction is topological in 2d, it is very easy to see that the state space on a strip bounded by
{\mathcal{B}}_{0}
and
{\mathcal{B}}_{\infty}
— which is the same as the state space on
\mathbb{CP}^{1}
in 3d — must be one-dimensional:

(5.37)

Namely, using topological invariance, we can squeeze the strip to infinitesimal width while keeping the state space unchanged (up to isomorphism). In the infinitesimal strip, the boundary conditions force
\mathbf{x}^{i}_{n}=\mathbf{p}_{i,n}=0
for all
n
, independent of bulk interactions. There is a single massive vacuum, and its quantization gives state space
\mathbb{C}
.

5.2.3
Completeness and MC elements

Next, consider placing any other line operator
\ell\in{\mathcal{C}}
at
z=0
. We make no assumption about whether
\ell
is constructed by coupling to QM or not. Upon circle compactification,
\ell
becomes equivalent to a boundary condition
{\mathcal{B}}_{\ell}
for the 2d theory at
r=0
; and measuring the
({\mathcal{B}}_{\ell},{\mathcal{B}}_{\infty})
strip state space defines our functor (
5.17
)

{\mathcal{F}}:\ell\mapsto\raisebox{-7.68236pt}{$\begin{array}[]{l}\text{States}({\mathcal{B}}_{\ell},{\mathcal{B}}_{\infty})\\
\simeq\text{States}(\mathbb{CP}^{1},\ell,{\mathcal{B}}_{\infty})\end{array}$}\in{\mathcal{A}}^{!}\text{-mod}

(5.38)

We’d like to argue that the representation
{\mathcal{F}}:{\mathcal{C}}\to{\mathcal{A}}^{!}\text{-mod}
is faithful. To do so, we’ll place the
({\mathcal{B}}_{\ell},{\mathcal{B}}_{\infty})
strip on the interval
[0,r_{1}]
next to a second copy of the 2d theory on a half-space
[r_{1},\infty)
, with boundary condition
{\mathcal{B}}_{0}
at
r=r_{1}
, as on the top-left:

(5.39)

We label the fields in the half-space
\tilde{\mathbf{x}}^{i},\tilde{\mathbf{p}}_{i}
, to distinguish them from the strip.
Then we add a “universal” coupling
\exp\int_{\mathbb{R}_{t}}\bm{\mu}
at
r=r_{1}
(shown on the top-right), where

\bm{\mu}=\sum_{i}\sum_{n\geq 0}\Big[(-1)^{F(x^{i})}\mathbf{x}^{i}_{n}\tilde{\mathbf{p}}_{i,-n-1}+(-1)^{F(p_{i})}\mathbf{p}_{i,n}\tilde{\mathbf{x}}^{i}_{-n-1}\Big]\,.

(5.40)

The full bulk+interface theory near
r=r_{1}
has a combined (classical) action

\displaystyle S_{\text{int}}=\int_{\mathbb{R}_{r}\times\mathbb{R}_{t}}\sum_{i,n\geq 0}\Big[\big(\mathbf{x}_{-n-1}d\mathbf{p}_{n}+\mathbf{p}_{-n-1}d\mathbf{x}_{n}+{\mathcal{I}}_{0}\big)\chi_{\leq r_{1}}(r)

(5.41)

\displaystyle-\big(\mathbf{x}_{n}\delta^{(1)}(r-r_{1})\tilde{\mathbf{p}}_{-n-1}+\mathbf{p}_{n}\delta^{(1)}(r-r_{1})\tilde{\mathbf{x}}_{-n-1}\big)+\big(\tilde{\mathbf{x}}_{n}d\tilde{\mathbf{p}}_{-n-1}+\tilde{\mathbf{p}}_{n}d\tilde{\mathbf{x}}_{-n-1}+\tilde{\mathcal{I}}_{0}\big)\chi_{\geq r_{1}}(r)\Big]\,,

where
\chi_{\leq r_{1}}(r)=1
if
r\leq r_{1}
and
0
otherwise, and similar for
\chi_{\geq r_{1}}
.
The EOM near
r=r_{1}
have interface terms that set
\mathbf{x}_{n}=\tilde{\mathbf{x}}_{n}
and
\mathbf{p}_{n}=\tilde{\mathbf{p}}_{n}
at
r=r_{1}
for all
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, effectively gluing the theory back together. For example, for
n\geq 0
,
\{S_{\rm int},\tilde{\mathbf{x}}_{n}(r)\}_{\rm BV}=\mathbf{x}_{n}\delta^{(1)}(r-r_{1})+d\tilde{\mathbf{x}}_{n}-\tilde{\mathbf{x}}_{n}\delta^{(1)}(r-r_{1})
sets
\mathbf{x}_{n}=\tilde{\mathbf{x}}_{n}
at
r=r_{1}
. Therefore, the universal coupling
\exp\int\bm{\mu}
deforms the decoupled interface on the LHS of (
5.39
) to a transparent, or identity interface.
11
11

11

This property was referred to as “completeness” of the
({\mathcal{B}}_{\infty},{\mathcal{B}}_{0})
pair, in the Tannakian context of
[
DN24
]
. It’s important to note that completeness only holds
perturbatively
here. For example, in a gauge theory, additional sums over bundles and insertions of boundary monopole operators are required to glue a theory back together from two halves — the universal coupling given by (
5.40
) is not sufficient.

We can interpret the deformed theory in two ways:

1)

If we first use the deformation
\exp\int\bm{\mu}
to get a transparent interface, we recover the 2d theory on a full half-space
[0,\infty)
with the original boundary condition
{\mathcal{B}}_{\ell}
at
r=0
(bottom-right of (
5.39
)). Thus we’ve inverted the functor
{\mathcal{F}}:{\mathcal{C}}\to{\mathcal{A}}^{!}\text{-mod}
, showing that it must be faithful.

2)

If we first squeeze the
[0,r_{1}]
strip to infinitesimal width, we get the vector space
V_{\ell}:=\text{States}({\mathcal{B}}_{\ell},{\mathcal{B}}_{\infty})
there, with its
{\mathcal{A}}^{!}
action (bottom-left of (
5.39
)). Suppose that the generators of
{\mathcal{A}}^{!}
act by some endomorphisms

\begin{array}[]{lcl}x^{i}_{n}&\mapsto&(-1)^{F(x^{i})}\beta^{i}_{n}\\
p_{i,n}&\mapsto&(-1)^{F(p_{i})}\alpha_{i,n}\end{array},\qquad\beta^{i}_{n},\alpha_{i,n}\in\text{End}(V_{\ell})\qquad(n\geq 0)

(5.42)

Then turning on the deformation
\exp\int\bm{\mu}
produces a theory on the half-space
[r_{1},\infty)
, where at
r=r_{1}
the boundary condition
{\mathcal{B}}_{0}
has been tensored with
V_{\ell}
, and coupled with the MC element

\mu\big|_{\text{evaluated on \eqref{rep-univ}}}=\sum_{i,n\geq 0}\Big[\alpha_{i,n}\tilde{x}^{i}_{-n-1}+\beta^{i}_{n}\tilde{p}_{i,-n-1}\Big]=:\mu_{\ell}

(5.43)

Comparing (1) and (2), we find that — if we work perturbatively —
every

\ell\in{\mathcal{C}}
can be represented by coupling to QM with linear couplings! The QM vector space required is
V_{\ell}=\text{States}({\mathcal{B}}_{\ell},{\mathcal{B}}_{\infty})
, and the couplings are computed by (
5.42
)–(
5.43
).

We have argued that
{\mathcal{F}}:{\mathcal{C}}\to{\mathcal{A}}^{!}\text{-mod}
is faithful. To see that it’s in fact an equivalence, we define
{\mathcal{F}}^{\vee}:{\mathcal{A}}^{!}\text{-mod}\to{\mathcal{C}}
for any
{\mathcal{A}}^{!}
-module
\rho_{M}:{\mathcal{A}}^{!}\to\text{End}(M)
by
{\mathcal{F}}^{\vee}(M,\rho_{M}):=(M,\mu_{M})
, with

\mu_{M}=(\rho_{M}\otimes 1)(\mu)=\sum_{i,n}\Big[(-1)^{F(x^{i})}\rho_{M}(x^{i}_{n})p_{i,-n-1}+(-1)^{F(p_{i})}\rho_{M}(p_{i,n})x^{i}_{-n-1}\Big]\,.

(5.44)

The construction in (
5.39
) shows
{\mathcal{F}}^{\vee}\circ{\mathcal{F}}\simeq\text{id}_{{\mathcal{C}}}
. Dually, we have that
{\mathcal{F}}\circ{\mathcal{F}}^{\vee}(M,\rho_{M})={\mathcal{F}}(M,\mu_{M})
is represented by states on a
({\mathcal{B}}_{0},{\mathcal{B}}_{\infty})
strip, tensored with
M
and deformed by
\mu_{M}
. On one hand, transversality (
5.37
) says that
{\mathcal{F}}(M,\mu_{M})\simeq M\otimes\mathbb{C}=M
with trivial deformation (since the right boundary sets
x^{i}_{-n-1}=p_{i,-n-1}=0
and thus
\mu_{M}=0
). On the other hand, at the left boundary, the EOM in the bulk-boundary action
S=\int_{\{0\}\times\mathbb{R}_{t}}\bm{\mu}_{M}+\int_{\mathbb{R}_{r\geq 0}\times\mathbb{R}_{t}}\sum_{n\geq 0}\big(\mathbf{x}^{i}_{-n-1}d\mathbf{p}_{i,n}+\mathbf{p}_{i,-n-1}d\mathbf{x}^{i}_{n}+{\mathcal{I}}_{0}\big)
set
x^{i}_{n}\big|_{0}=\rho_{M}(x^{i}_{M})
and
p_{i,n}\big|_{0}=\rho_{M}(p_{i,n})
for
n\geq 0
(also these fields are constant through the bulk and equal to their values on the right boundary), which determines the action of
{\mathcal{A}}^{!}
on the strip state space to be given by
\rho_{M}
. Thus
{\mathcal{F}}\circ{\mathcal{F}}^{\vee}\simeq\text{id}_{{\mathcal{A}}^{!}\text{-mod}}
.

There’s one final picture that’s useful to consider. Consider two strips of the
{\mathcal{T}}[S^{1}]
theory, one bounded by
{\mathcal{B}}_{0}
and its dual (180
∘
rotation) on both sides, and one bounded by
{\mathcal{B}}_{\infty}
and its dual on both sides. The
state spaces
on the respective strips can be identified with the underlying vector spaces of
{\mathcal{A}}
and
{\mathcal{A}}^{!}
, respectively, by state-operator correspondence. More so, local operators on the boundaries give
{\mathcal{A}}
the structure of an
{\mathcal{A}}\otimes{\mathcal{A}}^{\rm op}
module, and give
{\mathcal{A}}^{!}
the structure of an
{\mathcal{A}}^{!}{}^{\rm op}\otimes{\mathcal{A}}^{!}
module, as on the LHS:

(5.45)

Let’s glue the two strips to each other, using a deformation
\exp\int_{\mathbb{R}}(-\bm{\mu}_{21})
with the orientation-reversal of the universal MC element (
5.40
). Then transversality (
5.37
) implies that we must recover the trivial vector space, as on the RHS.

Algebraically, we learn that the vector space
{\mathcal{A}}\otimes{\mathcal{A}}^{!}
, treated as a left
{\mathcal{A}}\otimes{\mathcal{A}}^{!}
module, is deformed by the MC element
-\mu_{21}\in{\mathcal{A}}^{\rm op}\otimes{\mathcal{A}}^{!}{}^{\rm op}
(acting on the right) to a trivial module:

({\mathcal{A}}\otimes{\mathcal{A}}^{!},(-\mu_{21})\cdot)\simeq\mathbb{C}\qquad\text{as an ${\mathcal{A}}\otimes{\mathcal{A}}^{!}$ module}\,.

(5.46)

This is in fact easy to see from the explicit form of
\mu
in (
5.40
)

5.2.4
Summary

The setup here has provided all the ingredients required for a mathematical manifestation of Koszul duality. Let’s summarize. We’ve found two
A_{\infty}
algebras
{\mathcal{A}},{\mathcal{A}}^{!}
, both linearly generated by respective subspaces
{\mathcal{A}}_{1}=\mathbb{C}\langle x^{i}_{-n-1},p_{i,-n-1}\rangle_{n\geq 0}
,
{\mathcal{A}}_{1}^{!}\langle p_{i,n},x^{i}_{n}\rangle_{n\geq 0}
, satisfying

{\mathcal{A}}_{1}^{!}=(\Pi{\mathcal{A}}_{1}[1])^{*}\,,

(5.47)

where
\Pi
denotes parity shift,
[1]
denotes cohomological shift, and
()^{*}
is linear dual. Namely,
p_{i,n}
is the dual of
x^{i}_{-n-1}
and
x^{i}_{n}
is the dual of
p_{i,-n-1}
.

The universal coupling (
5.40
) provides our universal MC element
\mu\in{\mathcal{A}}^{!}\otimes{\mathcal{A}}
as in (
5.5
).
In particular, by using both gluing/completeness and transversality, we find that
({\mathcal{A}}\otimes{\mathcal{A}}^{!},(-\mu_{21})\cdot)\simeq\mathbb{C}
as an
{\mathcal{A}}\otimes{\mathcal{A}}^{!}
module. This ensures mathematically that the algebras
{\mathcal{A}},{\mathcal{A}}^{!}
are indeed Koszul duals, and that there’s an equivalence of categories
{\mathcal{C}}\simeq{\mathcal{A}}^{!}\text{-mod}
. The the objects of
{\mathcal{C}}
can be thought of as pairs
(V_{\ell},\mu_{\ell})
(deformations of
V_{\ell}\otimes{\mathcal{A}}
), and they correspond to modules
\rho_{\ell}:{\mathcal{A}}^{!}\to\text{End}(V_{\ell})
via
\mu_{\ell}=(\rho_{\ell}\otimes 1)(\mu)
as in (
5.6
). The gluing in (
5.42
)–(
5.43
) is the physical realization of this correspondence.

In order to be precise about the equivalence
{\mathcal{C}}\simeq{\mathcal{A}}^{!}\text{-mod}
, one should further impose (and carefully match) finiteness conditions on both sides. There are multiple consistent choices. Physically, we are often interested in line operators that can be constructed with a finite number of linear couplings, which corresponds to
smooth
modules for
{\mathcal{A}}^{!}
— meaning all
x^{i}_{n},p_{i,n}
act as zero for sufficiently large
n
. We also typically want to couple to quantum mechanics that’s not necessarily finite dimensional, but can be built from a finite number of free fields, which corresponds to
finite-rank
, a.k.a.
coherent
modules for
{\mathcal{A}}^{!}
. All the examples in this paper are both smooth and coherent.

Convention:
It is convenient to remove the
(-1)^{F}
’s from (
5.40
), (
5.42
), etc. by twisting (redefining) the algebra
{\mathcal{A}}^{!}
by the automorphism
(-1)^{F}
, which flips the sign of any odd element. We will implement this in the remainder of the paper. Note that twisting also changes the overall sign of the MC element
r(z)
introduced in Section (
5.4
) below.

5.3
Interlude: some examples

We include a few concrete examples to illustrate how some of the basic line operators considered earlier in the paper translate to modules for a Koszul-dual algebra. Some further examples for gauge theory are included later in Section
6.5.1
. Readers mainly interested in the theoretical developments may skip this.

With a view to our next goal of combining Koszul duality and OPE’s, we also include in Section
5.3.3
an example of the nontrivial constraints imposed on quantum corrections, essentially saying that
\mu_{\ell}(z)+\mu_{\ell^{\prime}}(w)+r_{\ell,\ell^{\prime}}(z-w)
is itself an MC element for any pair of lines. We illustrate this for two interesting vortex lines in the XYZ model.

5.3.1
Line operators as modules: free chiral

For a free chiral, bulk local operators
{\mathcal{A}}=\mathbb{C}[X_{-n-1},\psi_{-n-1}]_{n\geq 0}
are a graded-commutative algebra freely generated by the regular modes
X_{-n-1}=\tfrac{1}{n!}{\partial}^{n}X,\;\psi_{-n-1}=\tfrac{1}{n!}{\partial}^{n}\psi
at (say)
z=0
. The Koszul-dual
{\mathcal{A}}^{!}=\mathbb{C}[X_{n},\psi_{n}]_{n\geq 0}
is a graded-commutative algebra freely generated by the ‘polar’ modes at
z=0
. After a
(-1)^{F}
twist, the universal MC element is
\mu=\sum_{n\geq 0}\psi_{n}X_{-n-1}+X_{n}\psi_{-n-1}
, and trivially satisfies the MC equation because
Q=0
and the algebras are commutative.

Consider the simple flavor vortex lines
\mathbb{V}_{N}
from Section (
3.2
). They correspond to the following representations of
{\mathcal{A}}^{!}
. The identity line is (as always) the trivial module

\mathbb{V}_{0}={\mathds{1}}:\qquad V=\mathbb{C}\,,\qquad\rho(X_{n})=\rho(\psi_{n})=0\quad\forall n\geq 0\,,

(5.48)

while

\mathbb{V}_{N>0}:\quad V=\mathbb{C}[a_{0},...,a_{N-1}]\,,\quad\begin{array}[]{l}\rho(\psi_{k})=a_{k}\;\;\text{(if $k<N$)},\\
\rho(X_{n})=\rho(\psi_{n})=0\;\;\text{(otherwise)}\end{array}

(5.49)

\mathbb{V}_{N<0}:\quad V=\mathbb{C}[b_{0},...,b_{|N|-1}]\,,\quad\begin{array}[]{l}\rho(X_{k})=b_{k}\;\;\text{(if $k<|N|$)},\\
\rho(X_{n})=\rho(\psi_{n})=0\;\;\text{(otherwise)}\end{array}

(5.50)

are represented on finitely-generated fermionic and bosonic Fock spaces, respectively.

5.3.2
Line operators as modules: XYZ model

The XYZ model is more interesting. The bulk algebra
{\mathcal{A}}
from (
3.29
) is a nontrivial dg algebra. It is generated by regular modes
(X_{n},Y_{n},Z_{n},\psi_{X,n},\psi_{Y,n},\psi_{Z,n})_{n<0}
, with

\begin{array}[]{c}\displaystyle Q\,\psi_{X,n}=\hskip-10.84006pt\sum_{m+p=n-1}\hskip-10.84006ptY_{m}Z_{p}\,,\qquad Q\,\psi_{Y,n}=\hskip-10.84006pt\sum_{m+p=n-1}\hskip-10.84006ptX_{m}Z_{p}\,,\qquad Q\,\psi_{Z,n}=\hskip-10.84006pt\sum_{m+p=n-1}\hskip-10.84006ptX_{m}Y_{p}\,,\\[2.84544pt]
[\psi_{X,n},\psi_{Y,m}]=Z_{n+m}\,,\qquad[\psi_{Y,n},\psi_{Z,m}]=X_{n+m}\,,\qquad[\psi_{X,n},\psi_{Z,m}]=Y_{n+m}\,.\end{array}

(5.51)

We’ll see in Section
6.4
that the Koszul-dual algebra in a theory with superpotential looks nearly identical, with a few signs flipped. Here, after a
(-1)^{F}
automorphism twist,
{\mathcal{A}}^{!}=\mathbb{C}[X_{n},Y_{n},Z_{n},\psi_{X,n},\psi_{Y,n},\psi_{Z,n}]_{n\geq 0}
is generated by ‘polar’ modes, with

\hskip-14.45377pt\begin{array}[]{c}\displaystyle Q\,\psi_{X,n}=-\hskip-10.84006pt\sum_{m+p=n-1}\hskip-10.84006ptY_{m}Z_{p}\,,\qquad Q\,\psi_{Y,n}=-\hskip-10.84006pt\sum_{m+p=n-1}\hskip-10.84006ptX_{m}Z_{p}\,,\qquad Q\,\psi_{Z,n}=-\hskip-10.84006pt\sum_{m+p=n-1}\hskip-10.84006ptX_{m}Y_{p}\,,\\[2.84544pt]
[\psi_{X,n},\psi_{Y,m}]=-Z_{n+m}\,,\qquad[\psi_{Y,n},\psi_{Z,m}]=-X_{n+m}\,,\qquad[\psi_{X,n},\psi_{Z,m}]=-Y_{n+m}\,.\end{array}

(5.52)

All higher
A_{\infty}
operations vanish.

As always, the identity line
{\mathds{1}}
corresponds to the trivial module
\mathbb{C}
, where
\rho
maps all generators to zero.

More interestingly, consider the two elementary vortex lines
\ell\simeq\mathbb{V}_{(-1,1,0)}
and
\ell^{\prime}\simeq\mathbb{V}_{(2,-1,-1)}
, which were constructed by coupling to QM in (
3.35
) and (
3.41
), respectively. We’ve got:

\begin{array}[]{ll}\ell:&V=\mathbb{C}[a,b]\,,\quad\mu_{\ell}=a\psi_{X}+bY-\bar{b}aZ\\[2.84544pt]
\ell^{\prime}:&V^{\prime}=\mathbb{C}[c,d,e_{0},e_{1}]\,,\quad\underbrace{c\psi_{Y}+d\psi_{Z}+e_{0}X+e_{1}{\partial}X-\bar{e}_{0}(cZ+dY)}_{\mu_{\ell^{\prime}}}\underbrace{-\,\bar{e}_{1}cd}_{\text{deform $V^{\prime}$}}\,,\end{array}

(5.53)

with bosonic QM operators
a,\bar{a}={\partial}_{a},c,\bar{c}={\partial}_{c},d,\bar{d}={\partial}_{d}
and fermionic QM operators
b,\bar{b}={\partial}_{b},e_{0},\bar{e}_{0}={\partial}_{e_{0}},e_{1},\bar{e}_{1}={\partial}_{e_{1}}
.

Our convention is that the “couplings”
\mu_{\ell}
,
\mu_{\ell^{\prime}}
are the parts of the MC elements in (
5.53
) that are strictly linear in the bulk fields. For
\ell
, that’s the entire MC element. For
\ell^{\prime}
, it excludes the final term
-\bar{e}_{1}cd
, which is independent of bulk fields. We instead think of
-\bar{e}_{1}cd
as deforming the QM space
V^{\prime}
(and thus the algebra
\text{End}(V^{\prime})
), by adding a differential
Q_{V^{\prime}}=-cd{\partial}_{e_{1}}
.

Writing the coupling for
\ell
in modes (generators of
{\mathcal{A}}
) as

\mu_{\ell}=a\psi_{X,-1}+bY_{-1}-\bar{b}aZ_{-1}\,,

(5.54)

we can translate the line operator to a representation
\rho_{\ell}:{\mathcal{A}}^{!}\to\mathbb{C}[a,b,\bar{a},\bar{b}]\subseteq\text{End}(V)
that sends

X_{0}\mapsto a\,,\qquad\psi_{Y,0}\mapsto b\,,\qquad\psi_{Z,0}\mapsto-\bar{b}a\,,

(5.55)

with all other generators vanishing. Let’s check that this is a map of dg algebras. From (
5.52
) we have
Q(X_{0})=Q(\psi_{Y,0})=Q(\psi_{Z,0})=0
, consistent with the fact that
Q
vanishes on
\text{End}(V)
. However, there’s a nontrivial relation
[\psi_{Y,0},\psi_{Z,0}]=-X_{0}
, which maps correctly under
\rho_{\ell}
to
[b,-\bar{b}a]=a
.

Similarly writing the coupling of
\ell^{\prime}
in modes as

\mu_{\ell^{\prime}}=c\psi_{Y,-1}+d\psi_{Z,-1}+e_{0}X_{-1}+e_{1}X_{-2}-\bar{e}_{0}(cZ_{-1}+dY_{-1})\,,

(5.56)

we find that this encodes a representation
\rho_{\ell^{\prime}}:{\mathcal{A}}^{!}\to\text{End}(V^{\prime})
with

Y_{0}\mapsto c\,,\quad Z_{0}\mapsto d\,,\quad\psi_{X,0}\mapsto e_{0}\,,\quad\psi_{X,1}\mapsto e_{1}\,,\quad\psi_{Z,0}\mapsto-\bar{e}_{0}c\,,\quad\psi_{Y,0}\mapsto-\bar{e}_{0}d

(5.57)

and other generators vanishing. To see this is a map of dg algebras, we compare

\begin{array}[]{c}Q(\rho_{\ell^{\prime}}(\psi_{X,1}))=Q(e_{1})=[-\bar{e}_{1}cd,e_{1}]=-cd\quad\text{vs.}\quad\rho_{\ell^{\prime}}(Q(\psi_{X,1}))=\rho_{\ell^{\prime}}(-Y_{0}Z_{0})=-cd\\[2.84544pt]
[\rho_{\ell^{\prime}}(\psi_{X,0}),\rho_{\ell^{\prime}}(\psi_{Z,0})]=[e_{0},-\bar{e}_{0}c]=-c\qquad\text{vs.}\qquad\rho_{\ell^{\prime}}[\psi_{X,0},\psi_{Z,0}]=\rho_{\ell^{\prime}}(-Y_{0})=-c\\[2.84544pt]
[\rho_{\ell^{\prime}}(\psi_{X,0}),\rho_{\ell^{\prime}}(\psi_{Y,0})]=[e_{0},-\bar{e}_{0}d]=-d\qquad\text{vs.}\qquad\rho_{\ell^{\prime}}[\psi_{X,0},\psi_{Y,0}]=\rho_{\ell^{\prime}}(-Z_{0})=-d\end{array}

(5.58)

Note that the nontrivial differential
Q=[-\bar{e}_{1}cd,-]
in
\text{End}{V^{\prime}}
is essential in the first line.

5.3.3
OPE’s and the MC equation

Finally, we’d like to illustrate in a concrete example how the MC equation interacts with OPE’s. This is simply motivation for the subsequent sections, in which we systematize all this structure using dg-shifted Yangians.

The key point we want to illustrate is that in any OPE of line operators
\ell,\ell^{\prime}
, represented by spaces and couplings
(V,\mu_{\ell})
and
(V^{\prime},\mu_{\ell^{\prime}})
,

(\ell\otimes_{z-w}\ell^{\prime})(z)\sim\exp\int_{\mathbb{R}}\big[\bm{\mu}_{\ell}(z)+\bm{\mu}_{\ell^{\prime}}(w)+\mathbf{r}_{\ell,\ell^{\prime}}(z-w)\big]\,,

(5.59)

the fact that the RHS is also effectively a line operator requires the combination
\mu_{\ell}(z)+\mu_{\ell^{\prime}}(w)+r_{\ell,\ell^{\prime}}(z-w)
to be an MC element, in
\text{End}(V\otimes V^{\prime})\otimes{\mathcal{A}}[(z-w)^{-1}]
. Moreover, the MC equation has multiple, noninteracting contributions. Let
\text{MC}(x):=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}m_{k}(x^{\otimes k})
. We already know that
\text{MC}(\mu_{\ell}(z))=\text{MC}(\mu_{\ell^{\prime}}(w))=0
(for the individual lines
\ell,\ell^{\prime}
).
Since the quantum correction
r_{\ell,\ell^{\prime}}
is independent of bulk fields, it must also satisfy an MC equation by itself,

\text{MC}(r_{\ell,\ell^{\prime}})=Q(r_{\ell,\ell^{\prime}})+(r_{\ell,\ell^{\prime}})^{2}=0\qquad\text{in}\;\text{End}(V\otimes V^{\prime})[(z-w)^{-1}]\,.

(5.60)

The remaining terms in the MC equation for
\mu_{\ell}(z)+\mu_{\ell^{\prime}}(w)+r_{\ell,\ell^{\prime}}(z-w)
look like

[\mu_{\ell}(z),\mu_{\ell^{\prime}}(w)]+[\mu_{\ell}(z)+\mu_{\ell^{\prime}}(w),r_{\ell,\ell^{\prime}}(z-w)]+\text{(higher $A_{\infty}$ ops)}=0\,.

(5.61)

We’d like to illustrate how these hold using the vortex lines
\ell=\mathbb{V}_{(-1,1,0)}
and
\ell^{\prime}=\mathbb{V}_{(2,-1,-1)}
in the XYZ model above, defined by couplings (
5.53
). Using the general form of quantum corrections
r_{\ell,\ell^{\prime}}
in (
4.48
) we get

(\ell\otimes_{z-w}\ell^{\prime})(w)\sim\exp\int_{\mathbb{R}}\big[\mu_{\ell}(z)+\mu_{\ell^{\prime}}(w)\underbrace{-\frac{ae_{0}}{z-w}-\frac{ae_{1}}{(z-w)^{2}}+\frac{bc}{z-w}-\frac{\bar{b}ad}{z-w}}_{r(z-w)}\bigg]\,.

(5.62)

The quantum correction come (as usual) from contractions
\psi_{X}(z)X(w)\sim\frac{1}{z-w}
, etc.

To compute the MC equation for the RHS of (
5.62
), where bulk operators are placed at different points
z,w
, we need a slight generalization of the bulk operator algebra
{\mathcal{A}}
. It can be derived from the mode expansions (
5.51
). The algebra including commutators at generic points looks like (see Sec.
6.4
for details)

\begin{array}[]{c}Q\psi_{X}(z)=Y(z)Z(z)\,,\quad Q\psi_{Y}(z)=X(z)Z(z)\,,\quad Q\psi_{Z}(z)=X(z)Y(z)\,,\\[5.69046pt]
\displaystyle[\psi_{X}(z),\psi_{Y}(w)]=\frac{Z(z)-Z(w)}{z-w}\,,\qquad\quad[\psi_{Y}(z),\psi_{Z}(w)]=\frac{X(z)-X(w)}{z-w}\,,\\[5.69046pt]
\displaystyle[\psi_{X}(z),\psi_{Z}(w)]=\frac{Y(z)-Y(w)}{z-w}\,.\end{array}

(5.63)

Now let’s check that (
5.60
)–(
5.61
) hold, as they must due to self-consistency of perturbative QFT! The MC equation for
r
is

Q(r)=\frac{acd}{(z-w)^{2}}\,,\qquad r^{2}=-\frac{[b,\bar{b}]cad}{(z-w)^{2}}=\frac{cad}{(z-w)^{2}}\quad\Rightarrow\quad Q(r)+r^{2}=0\,.

(5.64)

(Note that the first term comes from the differential
Q_{V^{\prime}}
in
\text{End}(V^{\prime})
.) We also have

\begin{array}[]{c}\displaystyle[\mu_{\ell}(z),\mu_{\ell^{\prime}}(w)]\overset{\eqref{XYZ-alg-zw}}{=}ac\frac{Z(z)-Z(w)}{z-w}+ad\frac{Y(z)-Y(w)}{z-w}\,,\\[11.38092pt]
\displaystyle[\mu_{\ell}(z),r]=-\frac{adY(z)}{z-w}-\frac{acZ(z)}{z-w}\,,\qquad[\mu_{\ell^{\prime}}(w),r]=\frac{a(cZ(w)+dY(w))}{z-w}\,.\end{array}

(5.65)

ensuring that
[\mu_{\ell}(z),\mu_{\ell^{\prime}}(w)]+[\mu_{\ell}(z)+\mu_{\ell^{\prime}}(w),r]=0
. There are no higher terms in (
5.61
), since the bulk algebra
{\mathcal{A}}
in the XYZ model has no higher
A_{\infty}
operations.

The cancellations that lead to (
5.60
)–(
5.61
) here were quite nontrivial. We want to systematize and reinterpret them via Koszul duality.

5.4
Translations, OPE, and associativity

We’d next like to explain what additional structures the dual algebra
{\mathcal{A}}^{!}
in an HT-twisted theory should contain, by virtue of the fact that the category of lines
{\mathcal{C}}
that
{\mathcal{A}}^{!}
represents has translation symmetries and an associative OPE. We’ll derive these one at a time, starting with translations (which induce translation automorphisms
\tau_{z}
in
{\mathcal{A}}^{!}
), then the OPE (which induces a chiral coproduct
\Delta_{z}
, deformed by a shifted r-matrix
r(z)
); and finally explaining how chiral-commutativity of the OPE implies co-commutativity of
\Delta_{z},r(z)
and how associativity of the OPE implies identities for
\Delta_{z},r(z)
that resemble a quasi-triangular structure.

Along the way, we’ll begin expressing generators for
{\mathcal{A}}
and
{\mathcal{A}}^{!}
in terms of generating functions (
5.69
), analogous to “fields” of vertex algebras. This introduces a convenient, compact formalism that we’ll adopt for the remainder of the paper.

5.4.1
Translation isomorphisms

Recall that the category
{\mathcal{C}}
is actually a collection of categories
{\mathcal{C}}_{w}
, labelled by the points
w\in\mathbb{C}
where line operators are inserted. Since translations in the complex plane are a symmetry, there must be corresponding isomorphisms
T_{z}:{\mathcal{C}}_{w}\overset{\sim}{\to}{\mathcal{C}}_{w+z}
for each
z\in\mathbb{C}
, with
T_{z}\circ T_{z^{\prime}}\simeq T_{z+z^{\prime}}
.

Dually, suppose we’re representing each
{\mathcal{C}}_{w}
as
{\mathcal{A}}^{!}\text{-mod}
in a perturbative HT QFT, using the functor

{\mathcal{F}}_{w}:{\mathcal{C}}_{w}\to{\mathcal{A}}^{!}\text{-mod}\,,\qquad{\mathcal{F}}_{w}(\ell)=\text{States}(\mathbb{CP}^{1},\ell(w),{\mathcal{B}}_{\infty})

(5.66)

that inserts a line at
w
.
In computing the state space on the RHS, it cannot matter where on
\mathbb{CP}^{1}
the line
\ell
is actually inserted, since shifting coordinates
w\mapsto w+z
on
\mathbb{CP}^{1}
is a symmetry that preserves the point at infinity. Formally, this means that there should exist natural isomorphisms
T_{z}:{\mathcal{F}}_{w}\overset{\sim}{\to}{\mathcal{F}}_{w+z}
, which relate every pair of state spaces
T_{z,\ell}:{\mathcal{F}}_{w}(\ell)\overset{\sim}{\to}{\mathcal{F}}_{w+z}(T_{z}(\ell))
. In turn, these induce automorphisms
\tau_{z}:{\mathcal{A}}^{!}\to{\mathcal{A}}^{!}
(for each
z\in\mathbb{C}
) of the algebra
{\mathcal{A}}^{!}
such that, for all
a\in{\mathcal{A}}^{!}
and
\ell\in{\mathcal{C}}_{w}
, acting with
\tau_{z}(a)
on
{\mathcal{F}}_{w}(\ell)
becomes equivalent to acting with
a
on
{\mathcal{F}}_{w+z}(T_{z}\ell)
.

T_{z}\circ\tau_{z}(a)\big|\raisebox{-2.84544pt}{${\mathcal{F}}_{w}(\ell)$}=a\big|\raisebox{-2.84544pt}{${\mathcal{F}}_{w+z}(T_{z}\ell)$}\,.

(5.67)

If we think of
a
itself as a natural transformation
a:{\mathcal{F}}_{w}\to{\mathcal{F}}_{w}
, then we simply have:

\tau_{z}(a):=T_{-z}\circ a\circ T_{z}\,.

(5.68)

In a perturbative HT QFT, we can find an explicit form for
\tau_{z}
by looking investigating MC elements. Suppose we have fields
(\mathbf{x}^{i},\mathbf{p}_{i})
, with modes
(x^{i}_{n},p_{i,n})_{n<0}
generating bulk local operators
{\mathcal{A}}
and modes
(x^{i}_{n},p_{i,n})_{n\geq 0}
generating the Koszul-dual
{\mathcal{A}}^{!}
. Let’s group these in formal generating functions

\begin{array}[]{ll}{\mathcal{A}}:&x^{i}(u)_{+}:=\sum_{n<0}x^{i}_{n}u^{-n-1}\,,\qquad p_{i}(u)_{+}:=\sum_{n<0}p_{i,n}u^{-n-1}\\[5.69046pt]
{\mathcal{A}}^{!}:&x^{i}(u)_{-}:=\sum_{n\geq 0}x^{i}_{n}u^{-n-1}\,,\qquad p_{i}(u)_{-}:=\sum_{n\geq 0}p_{i,n}u^{-n-1}\end{array}

(5.69)

Then the MC element associated to a representation
\rho_{\ell}:{\mathcal{A}}^{!}\to\text{End}(V)
, representing a line operator inserted at
0\in\mathbb{C}
, has the universal form
\mu_{\ell}(0)=\rho_{\ell}(\mu(0))
, with

\displaystyle\mu(0)

\displaystyle=\sum_{n\geq 0}\Big[p_{i,n}\tfrac{1}{n!}{\partial}^{n}x^{i}(0)+x^{i}_{n}\tfrac{1}{n!}{\partial}^{n}p_{i}(0)\Big]=\sum_{n\geq 0}\Big[p_{i,n}x^{i}_{-n-1}+x^{i}_{n}p_{i,-n-1}\Big]

\displaystyle=\oint_{u}\Big[p_{i}(u)_{-}x^{i}(u)_{+}+x^{i}(u)_{-}p_{i}(u)_{+}\Big]

(5.70)

The contour integral should be interpreted algebraically, as picking out the coefficient of
u^{-1}
. We observe that the integrand only has singularities at
u=0
(due to
p(u)_{-},x(u)_{-}
) and at
u=\infty
(due to
x(u)_{+},p(u)_{+}
) — the formal series expansion around any other finite point
u=u_{0}
is regular — so the contour can be taken to run along any counterclockwise circle encircling the origin in the
u
-plane.

The same line
\ell
inserted at
w\in\mathbb{C}
is represented by
\mu_{\ell}(w)=\rho_{\ell}(\mu(w))
, with

\mu(w)=\sum_{i,n\geq 0}\Big[p_{i,n}\tfrac{1}{n!}{\partial}^{n}x^{i}(w)+x^{i}_{n}\tfrac{1}{n!}{\partial}^{n}p_{i}(w)\Big]=\oint_{u}\Big[p_{i}(u)_{-}x^{i}(u+w)_{+}+x^{i}(u)_{-}p_{i}(u+w)_{+}\Big]\,,

(5.71)

noting that the integrand still has singularities only at
u=0
and
u=\infty
(since
x_{+},p_{+}
are regular functions), and the contour encircles
u=0
.
Now for any
w,z\in\mathbb{C}
, we have

\displaystyle\mu(w+z)

\displaystyle=\oint_{u}\Big[p_{i}(u)_{-}x^{i}(u+w+z)_{+}+x^{i}(u)_{-}p_{i}(u+w+z)_{+}\Big]

\displaystyle\qquad=\oint_{u>z}\Big[p_{i}(u-z)_{-}x^{i}(u+w)_{+}+x^{i}(u-z)_{-}p_{i}(u+w)_{+}\Big]\,.

(5.72)

After shifting integration variables
u\mapsto u-z
, the new integrand has singularities at
u=z
and
u=\infty
, and the new contour must encircle (take the algebraic residue of) the singularity at
u=z
. This can be accomplished by placing the contour in a region where
|u|>|z|
, which we indicate by writing
\oint_{u>z}
. This means algebraically that the terms
\frac{1}{(u-z)^{n}}
in
p_{-}
and
x_{-}
can be expanded as series in
z/u
— but
not
in
u/z
.

For any line
\ell
, the translation
T_{z}:\ell(w)\mapsto\ell(w+z)
relates its insertions at
w
and
w+z
; and thus the automorphism
\tau_{z}
in
{\mathcal{A}}^{!}
must satisfy
\rho_{\ell}(\tau_{z}\,\mu(w))=\rho_{\ell}(\mu(w+z))
. Since
\ell
is arbitrary, this implies

\tau_{z}\,\mu(w)=\mu(w+z)\,.

(5.73)

Then by comparing the RHS of (
5.71
) and (
5.72
) we read off

\tau_{z}:\;x^{i}(u)_{-},p_{i}(u)_{-}\mapsto x^{i}(u-z)_{-},p_{i}(u-z)_{-}\qquad\text{(expanded in $z/u$)\,.}

(5.74)

Expanding
\sum_{n\geq 0}x_{n}(u-z)^{-n-1}=\sum_{n,p\geq 0}x_{n}u^{-n-1}\big(\begin{smallmatrix}n+m\\
m\end{smallmatrix}\big)(z/u)^{m}
and taking modes, we get

\tau_{z}:\;\;x^{i}_{n}\mapsto\sum_{m=0}^{n}\begin{pmatrix}n\\
m\end{pmatrix}z^{m}x^{i}_{n-m}\,,\quad p_{i,n}\mapsto\sum_{m=0}^{n}\begin{pmatrix}n\\
m\end{pmatrix}z^{m}p_{i,n-m}\,.

(5.75)

Note that if we think of
z
as a formal parameter (rather than a fixed number) the translation
\tau_{z}
in (
5.75
) sends any element of
{\mathcal{A}}^{!}
to a finite polynomial in
z
,
i.e.

\tau_{z}:{\mathcal{A}}^{!}\to{\mathcal{A}}^{!}[z]\,.

(5.76)

Infinitesimally,
\tau_{z}
is generated by a derivation
T:{\mathcal{A}}^{!}\to{\mathcal{A}}^{!}
representing
-{\partial}_{z}
:

\tau_{z}=\exp(zT)\,,\quad\bigg\{\!\!\!\begin{array}[]{r@{\,}c@{\,}l}T(x^{i}_{n})&=&n\,x^{i}_{n-1}\\
T(p_{i,n})&=&n\,p_{i,n-1}\end{array}\,,\qquad R(T)=0\,,\;F(T)=\text{even},\;J(T)=1\,.

(5.77)

5.4.2
OPE, coproduct, and shifted r-matrix

Next, consider any two lines
\ell,\ell^{\prime}
placed
12
12

12

We could have placed them at
z,w
; but up to a translation, we may assume that
w=0
.
at points
z,0\in\mathbb{C}
. The OPE provides a family of functors
{\mathcal{C}}_{z}\boxtimes{\mathcal{C}}_{0}\to{\mathcal{C}}_{0}
,

\ell(z)\otimes\ell^{\prime}(0)\mapsto\underbrace{(\ell\otimes_{z}\ell^{\prime})}_{\text{OPE}}(0)\qquad(z\neq 0)\,.

(5.78)

Consider, then, the state space on
\mathbb{CP}^{1}
in the presence of the two lines, which we expect to be preserved by taking the OPE,

\begin{array}[]{ccc}{\mathcal{F}}_{0}(\ell\otimes_{z}\ell^{\prime})&=&\text{States}\big(\mathbb{CP}^{1},(\ell\otimes_{z}\ell^{\prime})(0),{\mathcal{B}}_{\infty})\\[-8.5359pt]
\rotatebox{-90.0}{$\simeq$}&&\rotatebox{-90.0}{$\simeq$}\\[5.69046pt]
{\mathcal{F}}_{(z,0)}(\ell(z)\otimes\ell^{\prime}(0))&=&\text{States}(\mathbb{CP}^{1},\ell(z)\otimes\ell^{\prime}(0),{\mathcal{B}}_{\infty})\,.\end{array}

(5.79)

Suppose that the functor
{\mathcal{F}}
were monoidal with respect to the OPE. This means that there are natural isomorphisms of vector spaces

\qquad J_{z;\ell,\ell^{\prime}}:\;{\mathcal{F}}_{0}(\ell\otimes_{z}\ell^{\prime})\overset{\sim}{\to}{\mathcal{F}}_{z}(\ell)\otimes{\mathcal{F}}_{0}(\ell^{\prime})\qquad(?)

(5.80)

Physically, being monoidal means that putting a theory on
\mathbb{CP}^{1}
with
{\mathcal{B}}_{\infty}
at infinity trivializes all the bulk dynamics, to the extent that there are no longer any interactions between
\ell(z)
and
\ell^{\prime}(0)
— so that evaluating the state space just reduces to the product of the individual quantum mechanics associated to
\ell
and to
\ell^{\prime}
.
Then, by a standard result in representation theory (
cf.

[
EGNO15
]
), there would be an induced family of coproducts on
{\mathcal{A}}^{!}
,

\Delta_{z}:{\mathcal{A}}^{!}\to{\mathcal{A}}^{!}\otimes{\mathcal{A}}^{!}\qquad(z\neq 0)\,,

(5.81)

such that
a
acts on
{\mathcal{F}}_{0}(\ell\otimes_{z}\ell^{\prime})
the same way that
\Delta_{z}(a)
acts on
{\mathcal{F}}_{z}(\ell)\otimes{\mathcal{F}}_{0}(\ell^{\prime})
,
i.e.

J_{z;\ell,\ell^{\prime}}\circ\,a\big|\raisebox{-2.84544pt}{${\mathcal{F}}_{0}(\ell\otimes_{z}\ell^{\prime})$}=\Delta_{z}(a)\big|\raisebox{-2.84544pt}{$J_{z;\ell,\ell^{\prime}}\big({\mathcal{F}}_{z}(\ell)\otimes{\mathcal{F}}_{0}(\ell^{\prime})\big)$}\,.

(5.82)

Formally, one would define
\Delta_{z}(a):=J_{z;\ell,\ell^{\prime}}\circ a\circ J_{z;\ell,\ell^{\prime}}^{-1}
.

In 4d holomorphic-topological theories, such as the holomorphic Chern-Simons theory studied in
[
Cos13a
,
CWY17
]
, this is exactly what happens: a vacuum at infinity trivializes the bulk dynamics sufficiently so that evaluating state spaces becomes monoidal with respect to OPE. Then the Koszul-dual algebra gains a family of standard coproducts
\Delta_{z}
. (In
[
Cos13a
,
CWY17
]
, the Koszul dual is Drinfeld’s Yangian.) The 4d fiber functor
does
fail to preserve quantum corrections to the braiding of lines as they move past each other in the topological plane; this failure is compensated by introducing a meromorphic R-matrix in
{\mathcal{A}}^{!}
.

In 3d HT theories, the perturbative analysis of the OPE from Section
4
shows that it’s the basic product that isn’t preserved. Let’s work in axial gauge, so the OPE is given exactly by Thm.
4.1
and (
4.4.1
)–(
4.48
).
Suppose we have line operators
\ell,\ell^{\prime}
in a perturbative HT QFT that are constructed by coupling to dg vector spaces
V,V^{\prime}
(respectively), with linear MC elements
\mu_{\ell},\mu_{\ell^{\prime}}
— and corresponding representation
\rho_{\ell},\rho_{\ell^{\prime}}
of
{\mathcal{A}}^{!}
. Then the OPE is

(\ell\otimes_{z}\ell^{\prime})(0)\simeq\exp\int_{\mathbb{R}}\big[\bm{\mu}_{\ell}(z)+\bm{\mu}_{\ell^{\prime}}(0)+\mathbf{r}_{\ell,\ell^{\prime}}(z)\big]\,,

(5.83)

where
\mu_{\ell},\mu_{\ell^{\prime}}
, and
r_{\ell,\ell^{\prime}}(z)

all
take a universal form. Namely,
\mu_{\ell}(z)=\rho_{\ell}(\mu(z))
, and
\mu_{\ell^{\prime}}(0)=\rho_{\ell^{\prime}}(\mu(0))
are given by (
5.70
) and (
5.71
), while
r_{\ell,\ell^{\prime}}(z)=(\rho_{\ell}\otimes\rho_{\ell^{\prime}})(r(z))
with

\displaystyle r(z)

\displaystyle=\sum_{m,n\geq 0}\bigg(\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}n+m\\
n\end{array}\bigg)\frac{x^{i}_{n}p^{\prime}_{i,m}-p_{i,n}x^{\prime}{}^{i}_{m}}{(-1)^{n}z^{n+m+1}}\quad\in{\mathcal{A}}^{!}\otimes{\mathcal{A}}^{!}

(There’s an extra minus sign in
r
compared to Sec.
4
due to twisting
{\mathcal{A}}^{!}
by
(-1)^{F}
.)

The r-matrix has a contour-integral formulation, similar to the universal MC element
\mu
in (
5.71
). However, since the singularity structure of the ‘fields’
x(s)_{-},p(s)_{-}
in
{\mathcal{A}}^{!}
is different from the ‘fields’
x(s)_{+},p(s)_{+}
in
{\mathcal{A}}
, its interpretation is a bit different. We have:

r(z)=\oint_{s<z}\Big[x^{i}(s)_{-}p^{\prime}_{i}(s+z)_{-}-p_{i}(s)_{-}x^{\prime}{}^{i}(s+z)_{-}\Big]=-\oint_{s<z}\Big[x^{i}(s-z)_{-}p^{\prime}_{i}(s)_{-}-p_{i}(s-z)_{-}x^{\prime}{}^{i}(s)_{-}\Big]

(5.86)

On the LHS, the integrand has singularities at
s=0
and
s=-z
, and we’re only taking the residue at
s=0
. Thus the contour lies in a region
|s|<|z|
. In particular, terms
\frac{1}{(s+z)^{n}}
in
p^{\prime},x^{\prime}
are expanded as series in
s/z
(not
z/s
). On the RHS, we can “shift” integration variables
s\mapsto s-z
, but this actually involves flipping the contour so that it picks up the residue from
p^{\prime},x^{\prime}
rather than
x,p
; the contour is flipped at the cost of a minus sign, as indicated. We also have

r(z)=\oint_{s,s^{\prime}<z}\frac{x^{i}(s)_{-}p_{i}^{\prime}(s^{\prime})_{-}-p_{i}(s)_{-}x^{\prime}{}^{i}(s^{\prime})_{-}}{z+s-s^{\prime}}\,.

(5.87)

Here we expand
(z+s-s^{\prime})^{-1}=\sum_{k\geq 0}(s^{\prime}-s)^{k}z^{-k-1}
and take the coefficient of
s^{-1}
and
s^{\prime}{}^{-1}
. The relation between (
5.87
) and (
5.86
) comes from
(z+s-s^{\prime})^{-1}=\delta(s^{\prime}-s-z)
.

Now consider the state space
{\mathcal{F}}_{0}(\ell\otimes_{z}\ell^{\prime})=\text{States}(\mathbb{CP}^{1},\ell\otimes_{z}\ell^{\prime}(0),{\mathcal{B}}_{\infty})
, The boundary conditions on bulk fields
x_{+},p_{+}
set
\mu_{\ell}=\mu_{\ell^{\prime}}=0
in (
5.83
); but
r_{\ell,\ell^{\prime}}(z)
, which is independent of bulk fields, survives. We find

\displaystyle{\mathcal{F}}_{0}(\ell\otimes_{z}\ell^{\prime})

\displaystyle=V\otimes V^{\prime}\quad\text{deformed by the MC element $r_{\ell,\ell^{\prime}}(z)\in\text{End}(V)\otimes\text{End}(V^{\prime})$}

\displaystyle=:V\otimes_{r_{\ell,\ell^{\prime}}(z)}V^{\prime}

(5.88)

in contrast to
{\mathcal{F}}_{z}(\ell)\otimes{\mathcal{F}}_{0}(\ell^{\prime})=V\otimes V^{\prime}
(undeformed). Thus the functor
{\mathcal{F}}
is not quite monoidal, and its failure to be monoidal can be compensated by
r(z)
.

Each coupling
\mu_{\ell}(z)+\mu_{\ell^{\prime}}(0)
may be viewed as an MC element in
\text{End}(V)\otimes_{r_{\ell,\ell^{\prime}}(z)}\text{End}(V^{\prime})\otimes{\mathcal{A}}
. It determines a representation of
{\mathcal{A}}^{!}
in
\text{End}(V)\otimes_{r_{\ell,\ell^{\prime}}(z)}\text{End}(V^{\prime})
in the usual way, by sending generators
(x_{n},p_{n})
to the coefficients of
(p_{-n-1},x_{-n-1})
in the coupling
\mu_{\ell}(z)+\mu_{\ell^{\prime}}(0)
.
Writing out

\mu_{\ell}(z)+\mu_{\ell^{\prime}}(0)=(\rho_{\ell}\otimes\rho_{\ell^{\prime}})\oint_{u>z}\Big[\big(p_{i}(u-z)_{-}+p^{\prime}_{i}(u)_{-}\big)x^{i}(u)_{+}+\big(x^{i}(u-z)_{-}+x^{\prime}{}^{i}(u)_{-}\big)p_{i}(u)_{+}\Big]

(5.89)

for all
\ell,\ell^{\prime}
, we see that this representation of
{\mathcal{A}}^{!}
is induced by a family of coproducts
\Delta_{z}
, given explicitly on generators by

\begin{array}[]{ll}\Delta_{z}x^{i}(u)_{-}=x^{i}(u-z)_{-}\otimes 1+1\otimes x^{i}(u)_{-}&\quad=\tau_{z}\,x^{i}(u)_{-}\otimes 1+1\otimes x^{i}(u)_{-}\\[2.84544pt]
\Delta_{z}p_{i}(u)_{-}=p_{i}(u-z)_{-}\otimes 1+1\otimes p_{i}(u)_{-}&\quad=\tau_{z}\,p_{i}(u)_{-}\otimes 1+1\otimes p_{i}(u)_{-}\end{array}

(5.90)

(Borrowing Hopf-algebra language, one might say that the linear generators
x^{i},p_{i}
are
primitive elements
for this chiral coproduct.)
However, since
\text{End}(V)\otimes_{r_{\ell,\ell^{\prime}}(z)}\text{End}(V^{\prime})=(\rho_{\ell}\otimes\rho_{\ell^{\prime}})({\mathcal{A}}^{!}\otimes_{r(z)}{\mathcal{A}}^{!})
, the coproduct is now a map of
A_{\infty}
algebras

\Delta_{z}:{\mathcal{A}}^{!}\to{\mathcal{A}}^{!}\otimes_{r(z)}{\mathcal{A}}^{!}\qquad(z\neq 0)\,,

(5.91)

where the RHS denotes
{\mathcal{A}}^{!}\otimes{\mathcal{A}}^{!}
deformed by the MC element
r(z)
.

If we view
z
as a formal parameter, then

r(z)\in{\mathcal{A}}^{!}\otimes{\mathcal{A}}^{!}[\![z^{-1}]\!]\quad\text{and}\quad\Delta_{z}:{\mathcal{A}}^{!}\to{\mathcal{A}}^{!}\otimes_{r(z)}{\mathcal{A}}^{!}[\![z^{-1},z]

(5.92)

where on the RHS we adjoin formal power series in
z^{-1}
(as required for the the deformation by
r(z)
to make sense) and finite polynomials in
z
(as required for the shifts
\tau_{z}
in (
5.90
) to make sense). With
z
a formal parameter of degrees
J(z)=-1
,
R(z)=0
,
F(z)=\text{even}
, the MC element
r(z)
becomes homogeneous, with

R(r(z))=1\,,\qquad J(r(z))=0\,,\qquad F(r(z))=\text{odd}\,.

(5.93)

We’ll refer to
r(z)
as a
shifted r-matrix
, by analogy with (classical)
r
-matrices in the theory of Hopf algebras, in particular in Yangians and their generalizations. Both
\Delta_{z}
and
r(z)
satisfy a surprising number of properties that have standard analogues for Yangians, despite the fact that
r(z)
is not manifestly related to braiding, and has homological degree 1 (rather than 0). We describe these properties next.

5.4.3
Co-commutativity

Consider the fact that we can take an OPE of
\ell(z)
and
\ell^{\prime}(0)
in two different ways. We have been “bringing”
\ell(z)
to
0\in\mathbb{C}
, but we could equally well have “brought”
\ell^{\prime}(0)
to the point
z\in\mathbb{C}
. We expect these to be equivalent, up to a translation:

T_{z}\cdot(\ell\otimes_{z}\ell^{\prime})(0)\simeq(\ell^{\prime}\otimes_{-z}\ell)(z)

(5.94)

Comparing MC elements as in (
5.83
) for the two OPE’s, we must have

\mu_{\ell}(z)+\mu_{\ell^{\prime}}(0)+r_{\ell,\ell^{\prime}}(z)\;\overset{?}{=}\;\mu_{\ell^{\prime}}(0)+\mu_{\ell}(z)+r_{\ell^{\prime},\ell}(-z)

(5.95)

This is true for all
\ell,\ell^{\prime}
if and only if

r_{21}(-z)=r(z)\,.

(5.96)

(We’ve also implicitly used translation invariance
(\tau_{w}\otimes\tau_{w})r(z)=r(z)
for any
w
.)
Property (
5.96
) does hold in any perturbative theory, as seen easily from (
5.87
).

The identity (
5.94
) for the OPE now translates to a symmetry of
\Delta_{z}
:

(\tau_{-z}\otimes\tau_{-z})\circ\Delta_{z}=\Delta_{z}\circ\tau_{-z}=\Delta_{-z}^{\rm op}\,.

(5.97)

This is easy to check from the explicit formulas (
5.90
). The equality makes sense because (
5.96
) allows us to identify
{\mathcal{A}}^{!}\otimes_{r(z)}{\mathcal{A}}^{!}
(where the LHS lands) with
P({\mathcal{A}}^{!}\otimes_{r(-z)}{\mathcal{A}}^{!})
(where the RHS lands), where
P
permutes the factors. This property is sometimes called
weak co-commutativity
of
\Delta_{z}
.

5.4.4
Associativity and
A_{\infty}
Yang-Baxter

Finally, we describe the consequences of associativity, starting with associativity of three lines and generalizing to arbitrary configurations of
n
lines. We’d like to explain that associativity of any number of lines is controlled by a simple set of identities (
5.100
)–(
5.102
) for the coproduct in
{\mathcal{A}}^{!}
, and that it in turn implies an
A_{\infty}
analogue of the Yang-Baxter equation for
r(z)
.

For three lines
\ell(z+w)\otimes\ell^{\prime}(z)\otimes\ell^{\prime\prime}(0)
, associativity of the OPE means that

\ell\otimes_{z+w}(\ell^{\prime}\otimes_{z}\ell^{\prime\prime})(0)\simeq(\ell\otimes_{w}\ell^{\prime})\otimes_{z}\ell^{\prime\prime}(0)\,.

(5.98)

Let’s examine this in terms of MC elements, starting with the LHS.

There are two ways to construct the LHS. On one hand, we observe that the OPE
(\ell^{\prime}\otimes_{z}\ell^{\prime\prime})(0)
has an MC element
\mu_{\ell^{\prime}}(z)+\mu_{\ell^{\prime\prime}}(0)+r_{\ell^{\prime},\ell^{\prime\prime}}(z)
. Further taking its OPE with
\ell(z+w)
must add the MC element
\mu_{\ell}(z+w)
of
\ell^{\prime\prime}
, together with the interaction
r_{\ell,\ell^{\prime}}(w)
between
\ell
,
\ell^{\prime}
(the result of contracting bulk fields in
\mu_{\ell}
with bulk fields in
\mu_{\ell^{\prime}}
) and the interaction
r_{\ell,\ell^{\prime}}(z+w)
between
\ell
,
\ell^{\prime\prime}
(from contractions between
\mu_{\ell}
and
\mu_{\ell^{\prime\prime}}
). Thus:

\ell\otimes_{z+w}(\ell^{\prime}\otimes_{z}\ell^{\prime\prime})(0):\quad\exp\int_{\mathbb{R}}\Big[\bm{\mu}_{\ell}(z+w)+\bm{\mu}_{\ell^{\prime}}(z)+\bm{\mu}_{\ell^{\prime\prime}}(0)+\mathbf{r}_{\ell,\ell^{\prime}}(w)+\mathbf{r}_{\ell,\ell^{\prime\prime}}(z+w)+\mathbf{r}_{\ell^{\prime},\ell^{\prime\prime}}(z)\Big]

(5.99)

On the other hand, we must get this same interaction term, in a universal way, by repeatedly applying the coproduct in
{\mathcal{A}}^{!}
and working “from the outside in.” We first apply
\Delta_{z+w}
to “split off”
\ell
from
\ell^{\prime}\otimes_{z}\ell^{\prime\prime}
, and add a correction
r(z+w)
to the MC element in
V\otimes(V^{\prime}\otimes V^{\prime\prime})
. Then we apply
1\otimes\Delta_{z}
to “split off”
\ell^{\prime}
from
\ell^{\prime\prime}
, and add a correction
r_{\ell^{\prime},\ell^{\prime\prime}}(z)
to the MC element in
V^{\prime}\otimes V^{\prime\prime}
. For the two calculations to agree for all triples
\ell,\ell^{\prime},\ell^{\prime\prime}
, we must have

r_{12}(w)+r_{13}(z+w)+r_{23}(z)=(\text{id}\otimes\Delta_{z})r(z+w)+r_{23}(z)\,.

(5.100)

Note that
r_{23}(z)
cancels out, to give a familiar formula from Hopf algebras
(\text{id}\otimes\Delta_{z})r(z+w)=r_{12}(w)+r_{13}(z+w)
(albeit now playing a different role).
It’s easy to check this is true, using formula (
5.90
) for the coproduct and (
5.86
) for the r-matrix:

\displaystyle(\text{id}\otimes\Delta_{z})r(z+w)

\displaystyle=(\text{id}\otimes\Delta_{z})\oint_{s<z+w}\Big[x^{i}(s)_{-}p^{\prime}_{i}(s+z+w)_{-}-p_{i}(s)_{-}x^{\prime}{}^{i}(s+z+w)_{-}\Big]

\displaystyle=\oint_{s<z+w}\Big[x^{i}(s)_{-}\Big(p^{\prime}_{i}(s+w)_{-}\otimes 1+1\otimes p^{\prime\prime}_{i}(s+z+w)\Big)

\displaystyle\hskip 57.81621pt-p_{i}(s)_{-}\Big(x^{\prime}{}^{i}(s+w)_{-}\otimes 1+1\otimes x^{\prime}{}^{i}(s+z+w)_{-}\Big)\Big]

\displaystyle=r_{12}(w)+r_{13}(z+w)\,.

(5.101)

Similarly, there are two ways to construct the RHS of (
5.98
). Successively taking OPE’s and computing corrections to the MC elements again produces the symmetric expression (
5.99
). (Thus, associativity holds for a triple of lines.)
On the other hand, in terms of coproducts, we’re led to the second identity

r_{12}(w)+r_{13}(z+w)+r_{23}(z)=(\Delta_{w}\otimes\text{id})r(z)+r_{12}(w)\,.

(5.102)

This is easily verified from formulas (
5.90
) and (
5.86
).

Now let’s generalize to an arbitrary collection of
n
lines. What we’d like to show is that for any
\ell_{1}(z_{1})\otimes\cdots\otimes\ell_{n}(z_{n})
, with OPE’s taken in any order and expanded around any point, the total MC element takes the manifestly symmetric form

\ell_{1}(z_{1})\otimes\cdots\otimes\ell_{n}(z_{n}):\quad\exp\int_{\mathbb{R}}\rho_{\ell_{1}}\otimes...\otimes\rho_{\ell_{n}}\Big[\sum_{1\leq i\leq n}\bm{\mu}_{i}(z_{i})+\mathbf{r}^{(n)}\Big]\,,

(5.103)

with total (universal) quantum correction

r^{(n)}:=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq i<j\leq n\end{subarray}}r_{ij}(z_{i}-z_{j})\;\;\in\;{\mathcal{A}}^{!}{}^{\otimes n}[\![(z_{i}-z_{j})^{-1}]\!]_{i<j}\,.

(5.104)

Here
r_{ij}
denotes a copy of the r-matrix (
5.86
) using the
i^{th}
and
j^{th}
factors of
{\mathcal{A}}^{!}{}^{\otimes n}
.
We claim that, at a purely algebraic level, the two identities (
5.100
)–(
5.102
) alone are sufficient to guarantee this.

Suppose the statement is true for any
n-1
lines, indexed as
\ell_{1}(z_{1})\otimes\cdots\otimes\ell_{k-1}(z_{k-1})\otimes\ell_{k+1}(z_{k+1})\otimes\cdots\otimes\ell_{n}(z_{n})
, with symmetric MC element

r^{(n-1)}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq i<j\leq n\\
i,j\neq k\end{subarray}}r_{ij}(z_{i}-z_{j})\;\;\in\;{\mathcal{A}}^{!}{}^{\otimes n-1}[\![(z_{i}-z_{j})^{-1}]\!]_{i<j;i,j\neq k}\,.

(5.105)

To produce the MC element for
n
lines, we act with the coproduct
\Delta_{z_{k}-z_{k+1}}
on the
k
-th factor and add
r_{k,k+1}(z_{k}-z_{k+1})
(for new quantum corrections). We need to show

r_{k,k+1}(z_{k}-z_{k+1})+(\text{id}^{\otimes k-1}\otimes\Delta_{z_{k}-z_{k+1}}\otimes\text{id}^{\otimes n-k-1})\,r^{(n-1)}=r^{(n)}

(5.106)

This follows immediately from (
5.100
) and (
5.102
). (The cases
k=1
and
k=n
