must be considered separately, but still follow directly from (
5.100
)–(
5.102
).)

A very concise, schematic, way to summarize associativity is

\displaystyle r^{(n)}

\displaystyle=(r+\Delta)^{n-1}(0)

(5.107)

\displaystyle=r+\Delta(r+\Delta(r+\ldots+\Delta(r)...))\,.

That is, if we act
n-1
times with the coproduct and add
r
, acting on any tensor factors in any order, we will always obtain the same symmetric combination
r^{(n)}
. Its parameters
\{z_{i}\}
will depend on the parameters
r(w)+\Delta_{w}
at each step.

An interesting consequence of associativity is a shifted,
A_{\infty}
analogue of the Yang-Baxter equation. Since the twisted coproduct
\Delta_{z}:{\mathcal{A}}^{!}\to{\mathcal{A}}^{!}\otimes_{r(z)}{\mathcal{A}}^{!}[\![z^{-1},z]
is a morphism of
A_{\infty}
algebras, each operator

r_{k,k+1}(z)+\text{id}^{\otimes k-1}\otimes(\Delta_{z})\otimes\text{id}^{\otimes n-k-1}:{\mathcal{A}}^{!}{}^{\otimes n-1}\to{\mathcal{A}}^{!}{}^{\otimes n}[\![z^{-1},z]

(5.108)

must preserve MC elements. Therefore, each
r^{(n)}
in (
5.104
) must satisfy the MC equation in
{\mathcal{A}}^{!}{}^{\otimes n}[\![(z_{i}-z_{j})^{-1}]\!]
.
13
13

13

An alternative way to argue this is that
r^{(n)}
is the universal quantum correction to an OPE of
n
lines, and is independent of bulk fields, and so must satisfy an MC equation on its own.
Let’s write the MC equation as

\text{MC}(x):=\sum_{d=1}^{\infty}m_{d}(x^{\otimes d})=Q(x)+\tfrac{1}{2}[x,x]+\sum_{d=3}^{\infty}m_{d}(x,...,x)

(5.109)

Since
r^{(2)}=r
and
r^{(3)}
satisfy
\text{MC}(r^{(2)})=\text{MC}(r^{(3)})=0
, we have

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=\text{MC}(r^{(3)}(z+w,z,0))-\text{MC}(r_{12}(w))-\text{MC}(r_{13}(z+w))-\text{MC}(r_{23}(z))

\displaystyle=\text{MC}(r_{12}(w)+r_{13}(z+w)+r_{23}(z))-\text{MC}(r_{12}(w))-\text{MC}(r_{13}(z+w))-\text{MC}(r_{23}(z))

\displaystyle=[r_{12}(w),r_{13}(z+w)]+[r_{12}(w),r_{23}(z)]+[r_{13}(z+w),r_{23}(z)]

(5.110)

\displaystyle\hskip 7.22743pt+\sum_{d\geq 3}m_{d}\big[(r_{12}(w)+r_{13}(z+w)+r_{23}(z))^{\otimes d}-r_{12}(w)^{\otimes d}-r_{13}(z+w)^{\otimes d}-r_{23}(z)^{\otimes d}\big]

in
{\mathcal{A}}^{!}{}^{\otimes 3}[\![z^{-1},w^{-1},(z+w)^{-1}]\!]
. When higher
m_{d\geq 3}
operations vanish, we are left with

[r_{12}(w),r_{13}(z+w)]+[r_{12}(w),r_{23}(z)]+[r_{13}(z+w),r_{23}(z)]=0

(5.111)

which is the classical
Yang-Baxter
equation, for our shifted r-matrix. In general, we find an
A_{\infty}
generalization of Yang-Baxter equation given by (
5.110
).

The remaining equations
\text{MC}(r^{(n)})=0
for
n>4
all follow from
\text{MC}(r^{(2)})=\text{MC}(r^{(3)})=0
when higher
m_{\geq 3}
operations vanish. When higher
A_{\infty}
operations are present, they seem to provide addition nontrivial constraints.

5.5
dg-shifted Yangians

The structures in Section
5.4
motivate the following abstract definition. Let
R,F,J
as usual denote cohomological, parity (fermion number), and spin gradings; and let
z
be a formal parameter with
R(z)=0
,
F(z)=\text{even}
,
J(z)=-1
.

Definition 5.1
.

A (generalized)
dg-shifted Yangian
is an
A_{\infty}
algebra
{\mathcal{Y}}
, with gradings
R,F,J
, and further endowed with

\text{\emph{translation `automorphisms':}}\quad\tau_{z}:{\mathcal{Y}}\to{\mathcal{Y}}[z]\,,\qquad\tau_{z}=\exp(z\,T)

(5.112a)

generated by an
A_{\infty}
derivation
T:{\mathcal{Y}}\to{\mathcal{Y}}
of degrees
(0,\text{odd},1)
(so
\tau_{z}
are graded
A_{\infty}
morphisms satisfying
\tau_{z}\tau_{w}=\tau_{z+w}
);

\begin{array}[]{ll}\text{\emph{a Maurer-Cartan element:}}&r(z)\in{\mathcal{Y}}\otimes{\mathcal{Y}}[\![z^{-1},z]\quad\text{of degrees}\;(1,\text{odd},0)\\[5.69046pt]
\text{\emph{and a twisted coproduct:}}&\Delta_{z}:{\mathcal{Y}}\to{\mathcal{Y}}\otimes_{r(z)}{\mathcal{Y}}[\![z^{-1},z]\end{array}

(5.112b)

that’s a graded
A_{\infty}
algebra morphism; and a unit ‘
1
’ and

\text{\emph{counit:}}\quad\varepsilon:{\mathcal{A}}^{!}\to\mathbb{C}

(5.112c)

that’s also an
A_{\infty}
morphism, where
\mathbb{C}
denotes the trivial algebra.
These satisfy

\text{\emph{counit axioms:}}\quad\varepsilon(1)=1\,,\quad(\varepsilon\otimes\text{id})\circ\Delta_{z}=\text{id}\,,\quad(\text{id}\otimes\varepsilon)\circ\Delta_{z}\simeq\tau_{z}

(5.112d)

\begin{array}[]{l}\text{\emph{translation invariance}}\\
\text{\emph{ \& weak co-commutativity:}}\end{array}\quad\begin{array}[]{c}\varepsilon(T(a))=0\,,\quad\varepsilon(\tau_{z}(a))=\varepsilon(a)\quad\forall\,a\in{\mathcal{Y}}\\[2.84544pt]
(\tau_{w}\otimes\tau_{w})r(z)=r(z)\,,\quad r(-z)=r_{21}(z)\\[2.84544pt]
(\tau_{z}\otimes\tau_{z})\circ\Delta_{-z}=\Delta_{-z}\circ\tau_{z}=\Delta_{z}^{\rm op}\end{array}

(5.112e)

and

\begin{array}[]{l}\text{\emph{co-associativity:}}\quad r_{23}(z)+(\text{id}\otimes\Delta_{z})(r(z+w))=r_{12}(w)+(\Delta_{w}\otimes\text{id})(r(z))\\[2.84544pt]
\hskip 144.54pt=r_{12}(w)+r_{13}(z+w)+r_{23}(z)\,.\end{array}

(5.112f)

We also recall from the end of Section
5.4.4
that
\Delta_{z}
being a (co)associative algebra morphism implies that
r(z)
will satisfy the
A_{\infty}
Yang-Baxter equation (
5.110
).

We argued in Section
5.4
, by consistency of OPE’s in 3d HT QFT’s, that

Conjecture 5.2
.

(Physics theorem) In a perturbative 3d HT QFT, the
A_{\infty}
algebra
{\mathcal{A}}^{!}
that’s Koszul-dual to local operators has the structure of a dg-shifted Yangian.

Let’s quickly summarize. In perturbative HT QFT with local operators
{\mathcal{A}}
, the Koszul dual
{\mathcal{A}}^{!}
is such that the universal coupling

\mu=\oint_{u}\Big[p_{i}(u)_{-}x^{i}(u)_{+}+x^{i}(u)_{-}p_{i}(u)_{+}\Big]

(5.113)

is an MC element in
{\mathcal{A}}^{!}\otimes{\mathcal{A}}
. We represent line operators as
{\mathcal{A}}^{!}
-modules. In particular, a line
\ell(z)=T_{z}\ell(0)
has MC element
\rho_{\ell}(\mu(z))=\rho_{\ell}(\tau_{z}\mu)
, with
\tau_{z}
acting on
{\mathcal{A}}^{!}
via (
5.74
).
We did not mention the counit previously; it is simply defined by the action of
{\mathcal{A}}^{!}
on the identity line
{\mathds{1}}
, whose state space (
5.37
) is trivial, so
\varepsilon:=\rho_{{\mathds{1}}}:{\mathcal{A}}^{!}\to\mathbb{C}
. Identities (
5.112d
)–(
5.112e
) for the counit are induced by
T_{z}({\mathds{1}})\simeq{\mathds{1}}
and
{\mathds{1}}\otimes_{z}\ell\simeq\ell
,
\ell\otimes_{z}{\mathds{1}}\simeq T_{z}\ell
in the category of lines.
The coproduct is given by

\Delta(a)=\tau_{z}(a)\otimes 1+1\otimes a\quad\forall\;\text{linear generators $a\in{\mathcal{A}}^{!}$}

(5.114)

and the universal r-matrix by

r(z)=\oint_{s<z}\Big[x^{i}(s)_{-}p_{i}^{\prime}(s+z)_{-}-p_{i}(s)_{-}x^{\prime}{}^{i}(s+z)_{-}\Big]

(5.115)

which manifestly satisfy (
5.112e
), (
5.112f
).

The universal coupling
\mu(z)+\mu^{\prime}(0)+r(z)
associated to an OPE of two lines must itself satisfy an MC equation. The part independent of bulk fields is an MC equation for
r(z)
alone, which says that
r(z)
is an MC element that can deform the algebra
{\mathcal{A}}^{!}\otimes{\mathcal{A}}^{!}
. The MC equation for the entire sum
\mu(z)+\mu^{\prime}(0)+r(z)
then becomes equivalent to the statement that
\Delta_{z}:{\mathcal{A}}^{!}\to{\mathcal{A}}^{!}\otimes_{r(z)}{\mathcal{A}}^{!}[\![z^{-1},z]
is a morphism of
A_{\infty}
algebras.

These MC equations are highly nontrivial statements in a given QFT. They depend on the precise
A_{\infty}
structures of
{\mathcal{A}}
and
{\mathcal{A}}^{!}
. We prove that they do indeed hold in examples of HT-twisted (perturbative) 3d
{\mathcal{N}}=2
theories in Section
7
.

5.6
The special case of TQFT

We include a short remark about topological theories, and additional structure that
{\mathcal{A}}^{!}
acquires in this case.

Some QFT’s that are defined as holomorphic-topological theories may in fact be fully topological. Examples include topological twists of 3d
{\mathcal{N}}=4
theories, expressed as deformations of HT twists (as described in
[
Gar22
,
HR21
]
); or HT-twisted 3d
{\mathcal{N}}=2
pure Chern-Simons theory at non-critical level. Perturbatively, the HT twist of gauge theory with nonzero level
k
is equivalent to perturbative Chern-Simons at level
k-h^{\vee}
. In these cases, translation functors
T_{z}:{\mathcal{C}}_{w}\to{\mathcal{C}}_{w+z}
on the category of lines should be “trivial,” in the sense that they are all isomorphic to the identity functor.

From the prespective of boundary vertex algebras, the triviality of translations is encoded in the existence of a stress-energy tensor (
a.k.a.
conformal element)
L(z)
. In particular, the action of
\exp(zL_{-1})
identifies
T_{z}
with the identity functor
T_{0}
. For non-critical level Chern-Simons theory, this stress-energy tensor is given by the famous Segal-Sugawara construction.

One can ask for an analogous trivialization of
T_{z}
when representing
{\mathcal{C}}\simeq{\mathcal{A}}^{!}\text{-mod}
. The minimal structure we expect to be present is the following: we would like the derivation
T
that generates translations
\tau_{z}=\exp(z\,T)
in
{\mathcal{A}}^{!}
to be exact. In other words, for a TQFT, we expect that there exists a second derivation

{\mathcal{L}}:{\mathcal{A}}^{!}\to{\mathcal{A}}^{!}\qquad\text{of degrees $R({\mathcal{L}})=-1$, $F({\mathcal{L}})=\text{odd}$, $J({\mathcal{L}})=1$}

(5.116a)

such that

T=[Q,{\mathcal{L}}]=Q{\mathcal{L}}+{\mathcal{L}}Q\,.

(5.116b)

The existence of this
{\mathcal{L}}
implies that
T=0
on the cohomology of
Q
, since if an element
a\in{\mathcal{A}}^{!}
is
Q
-closed, then
T(a)
is
Q
-exact:
T(a)=[Q,{\mathcal{L}}]a=Q({\mathcal{L}}\,a)
.
A sophisticated reformulation of this statement is that the derivation
T
, which defines a class in the Hochschild cohomology of
{\mathcal{A}}^{!}
, is homotopic to the zero derivation. In principle, this means that the functor
T_{z}
generated by
T
should be equivalent to the identity functor (after all, the Hochschild cohomology of
{\mathcal{A}}^{!}
is the space of endomorphisms of the identity functor on
{\mathcal{A}}^{!}\text{-mod}
). We leave it to future work to develop this point more precisely.

6
A_{\infty}
structure on local operators and their dual

Our last major goal in this paper is to verify explicitly that the Koszul-dual algebras
{\mathcal{A}}^{!}
in perturbative HT QFT’s really do have the structure of dg-shifted Yangians, as introduced in Section
5.4
. In order to do this, we must be able to compute and utilize the full
A_{\infty}
structure on
{\mathcal{A}}^{!}
. In this section, we’ll explain how to do so.

In fact, computing
{\mathcal{A}}^{!}
as an
A_{\infty}
algebra in a perturbative HT QFT turns out to be just as easy (or hard) as computing bulk local operators
{\mathcal{A}}
as an
A_{\infty}
algebra. We’ll tackle both at the same time.

The approach we’ll take is to compactify a 3d HT QFT to two dimensions along
\mathbb{C}^{*}
, as discussed in Section
5.2.1
. Then the 3d HT action

S=\int_{\mathbb{C}^{*}_{z}\times\mathbb{R}_{t}}\mathbf{p}_{i}d^{\prime}\mathbf{x}^{i}+{\mathcal{I}}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{p},{\partial})\,,

(6.1)

reduces to a 2d action for a topological B-model involving infinitely many fields

S_{\rm 2d}=\int_{\mathbb{R}_{r}\times\mathbb{R}_{t}}\bigg[\sum_{n+m=-1}\mathbf{p}_{i,n}d\mathbf{x}^{i}_{m}+{\mathcal{I}}_{0}(\{\mathbf{x}^{i}_{k}\},\{\mathbf{p}_{i,k}\})\Bigg]\,,

(6.2)

where
\mathbf{x}^{i}=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathbf{x}^{i}_{n}z^{-n-1}
,
\mathbf{p}_{i}=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathbf{p}_{i,n}z^{-n-1}
, and
{\mathcal{I}}_{0}=\oint_{z}{\mathcal{I}}
is the coefficient of
z^{-1}
in the expansion of the original interaction
{\mathcal{I}}
. The algebras
{\mathcal{A}}
and
{\mathcal{A}}^{!}
are now on the same footing: they are endomorphism algebras of two boundary conditions for the 2d topological theory

{\mathcal{A}}=\text{End}({\mathcal{B}}_{0})\,,\qquad{\mathcal{A}}^{!}=\text{End}({\mathcal{B}}_{\infty})\qquad\quad\raisebox{-14.45377pt}{\includegraphics[width]{CP1-2d-alg}}

(6.3)

where
14
14

14

Technically speaking,
{\mathcal{B}}_{0}
and
{\mathcal{B}}_{\infty}
are objects of different categories, since in the compactification of (
5.37
)
{\mathcal{B}}_{0}
appears as a left boundary and
{\mathcal{B}}_{\infty}
appears as a right boundary. They are related by
180
-degree rotation, or a dualization operation. In 2d conventions, we should be careful to do computations with all boundary conditions placed on the same side, in the same orientation. Then we actually have
{\mathcal{A}}^{!}=\text{End}({\mathcal{B}}_{\infty}^{\vee})^{\rm op}
, where
{\mathcal{B}}_{\infty}^{\vee}
is
{\mathcal{B}}_{\infty}
viewed as a left boundary.

\begin{array}[]{ll}{\mathcal{B}}_{0}:&\mathbf{x}^{i}_{n}\big|_{\rm bdy}=\mathbf{p}_{i,n}\big|_{\rm bdy}=0\quad\text{for $n\geq 0$}\\[2.84544pt]
{\mathcal{B}}_{\infty}:&\mathbf{x}^{i}_{n}\big|_{\rm bdy}=\mathbf{p}_{i,n}\big|_{\rm bdy}=0\quad\text{for $n<0$}\,.\end{array}

(6.4)

Our problem then boils down to computing the boundary algebras
{\mathcal{A}},{\mathcal{A}}^{!}
in the 2d B-model (
6.2
). It’s well known that the
A_{\infty}
operations on boundary algebras in a B-model are controlled by derivatives of the interaction
{\mathcal{I}}_{0}
; mathematically,
{\mathcal{I}}_{0}
is called a “cyclic function” that generates a deformation of an
A_{\infty}
algebra,
cf.

[
KS09
, Rmk. 10.1.5]
. An explicit QFT-based computational framework was described in the lecture notes
[
DNP21
]
. We’ll generalize the approach of
[
DNP21
]
to the present setting, as follows.

We begin by proving a non-renormalization result (generalizing the non-renormalization theorem of
[
GW19
]
for Chern-Simons theory):

Proposition 6.1
.

The perturbative algebras
{\mathcal{A}}
and
{\mathcal{A}}^{!}
are deformations of the free graded-commutative algebras
\mathbb{C}[x^{i}_{n},p^{i}_{n}]_{n<0}
and
\mathbb{C}[x^{i}_{n},p^{i}_{n}]_{n\geq 0}
(respectively), with
A_{\infty}
operations computed exactly by tree-level and one-loop Feynman diagrams involving the interaction
{\mathcal{I}}_{0}
. For quasi-linear theories, the one-loop diagrams vanish and
A_{\infty}
operations are exact at tree level, given by the diagrams in (
6.15
) below.

We establish the general result in Section
6.1
by degree counting, and specialize to quasi-linear theories in Section
6.2
. Recall that quasi-linear theories, introduced in Section
4.2
, include all HT twists of 3d
{\mathcal{N}}=2
gauge/matter/Chern-Simons theories with superpotentials. Our argument for vanishing of the one-loop correction to
{\mathcal{A}}^{!}
is slightly non-rigorous, but becomes rigorous after proving Koszul duality of
{\mathcal{A}}
and
{\mathcal{A}}^{!}
for twisted 3d
{\mathcal{N}}=2
theories in Section
7
.

In the remainder of the section, we give explicit formulas for the
A_{\infty}
operations in
{\mathcal{A}}
and
{\mathcal{A}}^{!}
, both in terms of modes
x^{i}_{n},p_{i,n}
and generating functions or “fields”
x^{i}(z),p_{i}(z)
. We compute the operations in a general perturbative QFT in Section
6.3
. In Section
6.4
we specialize to matter with a superpotential, showing how
{\mathcal{A}}
becomes a chiralization of the derived critical locus of the superpotential. In Section
6.5
, we then consider gauge theory with Chern-Simons terms and matter, but vanishing superpotential. Such gauge theories can compactly be rewritten as gauge theories for “Takiff algebras”
\mathfrak{h}=\mathfrak{g}\ltimes V
, and
{\mathcal{A}}
and
{\mathcal{A}}^{!}
take the form of loop algebras, deformed by Chern-Simons terms:

{\mathcal{A}}\approx\lambda^{-1}(T^{*}[-1]\mathfrak{h})[\lambda^{-1}]\,,\qquad{\mathcal{A}}^{!}\approx(T^{*}[-1]\mathfrak{h})[\lambda]\,.

(6.5)

We’ll also mention (Sec.
6.5.1
) how Wilson lines, and some simple generalizations in the presence of matter, appear as modules for
{\mathcal{A}}^{!}
. We reincorporate superpotentials, for completely general HT-twisted
{\mathcal{N}}=2
Lagrangian theories, in Section
6.5.3
.

Our results generalize the Koszul duals in
[
PW21
, Secs. 6-7]
for 3d HT free matter and pure gauge theory at critical CS level, obtained with slightly different techniques.

Finally, in Section
6.6
, we consider the special case of perturbative pure gauge theory (no matter), with a non-degenerate Chern-Simons level. This is a topological theory, whose line operators should to be equivalent to modules for a boundary Kac-Moody algebra,

{\mathcal{C}},\otimes_{z}\;\simeq\;({\mathcal{A}}^{!},\Delta_{z})\text{-mod}\;\simeq\;(\hat{\mathfrak{g}}_{k}\text{-mod},\otimes_{\hat{\mathfrak{g}}_{k}})\,.

(6.6)

In particular, the OPE
\otimes_{z}
, which maps to the fusion product for
\hat{\mathfrak{g}}_{k}
, should be locally constant.
We’ll write down the explicit derivation
{\mathcal{L}}:{\mathcal{A}}^{!}\to{\mathcal{A}}^{!}
that makes the product in
{\mathcal{A}}^{!}
-mod locally constant.

We remark that, once
{\mathcal{A}}
and
{\mathcal{A}}^{!}
are known as
A_{\infty}
algebras, the MC element

\mu_{13}(z)+\mu_{23}(0)+r_{12}(z)\in{\mathcal{A}}^{!}\otimes{\mathcal{A}}^{!}\otimes{\mathcal{A}}\,[\![z^{-1},z]\!]

(6.7)

associated to an OPE of two lines (as in (
5.59
), (
5.83
)) controls most of their remaining algebraic structure. (The subscripts above refer to the tensor factors that the
\mu
’s and
r
are elements of.) In Section
7
we’ll prove that (
6.7
) is indeed an MC element in twisted
{\mathcal{N}}=2
theories, and conclude that
{\mathcal{A}}^{!}
has the structure of a dg-shifted Yangian, by dualizing (
6.7
) to an
A_{\infty}
algebra map
\Delta_{z}:{\mathcal{A}}^{!}\to{\mathcal{A}}^{!}\otimes_{r(z)}{\mathcal{A}}^{!}[\![z^{-1},z]
. Alternatively, we can dualize the two
{\mathcal{A}}^{!}
factors to obtain an
A_{\infty}
algebra map

{\mathcal{A}}\otimes{\mathcal{A}}\to{\mathcal{A}}(\!(z)\!)\,.

(6.8)

This is the
chiral
product on bulk local operators, turning
{\mathcal{A}}
into an (
A_{\infty}
,chiral) algebra.

6.1
Non-renormalization for boundary algebras

In a free theory
({\mathcal{I}}=0)
, the algebras of local operators on both
{\mathcal{B}}_{0}
and
{\mathcal{B}}_{\infty}
are graded-commutative algebras, freely generated by the zero-form components of the fields that survive at the boundary,

{\mathcal{A}}=\mathbb{C}[x^{i}_{n},p_{i,n}]_{n<0}\,,\qquad{\mathcal{A}}^{!}=\mathbb{C}[x^{i}_{n},p_{i,n}]_{n\geq 0}\,.

(6.9)

When bulk interactions are turned on, the differential, product, and higher operations in these algebras are deformed perturbatively. Specifically, each
m_{d}
operation (where
m_{1}=Q
is the differential) is corrected by connected Feynman diagrams with

•

d
boundary insertions of
\mathbf{x}^{i}_{n}
or
\mathbf{p}_{i,n}
(with
n<0
for
{\mathcal{B}}_{0}
or
n\geq 0
for
{\mathcal{B}}_{\infty}
)

•

v
bulk insertions of the interaction
{\mathcal{I}}_{0}=\oint{\mathcal{I}}(\mathbf{x}^{i},\mathbf{p}_{i},{\partial})

•

e_{\partial}=d
propagators connecting boundary to bulk vertices; and
e
propagators connecting bulk to bulk vertices.

Schematically, for example:

m_{4}(x^{i_{1}}_{n_{1}},p_{i_{2},n_{2}},x^{i_{3}}_{n_{3}},x^{i_{4}}_{n_{4}})=\raisebox{-36.135pt}{\includegraphics[width]{bdy-gen}}

(6.10)

In particular, there cannot be any boundary-boundary propagators. This is because the 2d propagator corresponding to (
6.2
) contracts
\mathbf{p}_{i,n}
with
\mathbf{x}^{i}_{-n-1}
, so one end or the other necessarily vanishes on both
{\mathcal{B}}_{0}
and
{\mathcal{B}}_{\infty}
.

Furthermore, in these diagrams, the bulk vertices are integrated over a 2d half-space. Of the boundary vertices, which are “time ordered” along the boundary, the first and last are fixed, but all intermediate vertices are integrated along the boundary while preserving the ordering. This picks out 1-form descendants of the boundary
\mathbf{x}^{i}_{n},\mathbf{p}_{i,n}
insertions, and integrates their product along a
(d-2)
-simplex. The boundary integration is required in order for the
m_{d}
operations defined this way to satisfy
A_{\infty}
relations,
cf.

[
DNP21
, Sec. 4.5]
.

Now, the
m_{d}
operation should have cohomological degree
R(m_{d})=2-d
(lowering the degree of the boundary insertions — whatever they are — by
d-2
). By the same calculus as in Section
4.3.1
, each bulk vertex contributes
+2
to the degree of a diagram and each propagator contributes
-1
, so this means that

2v-e_{\partial}-e=2-d\,,

(6.11)

which (using
e_{\partial}=d
) implies

2v-e=2

(6.12)

Now let’s assume that our 3d HT theory is quasi-linear, as defined in Section
4.2
. Then the reduced 2d theory will be quasi-linear as well (by simply placing all
n\in\mathbb{Z}
modes of a given field within the same partition
S_{\alpha}
). We obtain from (
4.33
), after summing over partitions, a second relation

e\leq v\,.

(6.13)

Combining this with (
6.12
), we get

2=2v-e\geq 2v-v=v\quad\Rightarrow\quad v\leq 2

(6.14)

This leaves just two possibilities. One is
v=1
and (due to (
6.12
))
e=0
, which leads to the tree-level diagrams

(6.15)

The other is
v=2
and (due to (
6.12
))
e=2
. There are no self-contractions of bulk vertices, because they are (by definition) normal-ordered; so the
e=2
edges must connect the
v=2
bulk vertices to each other. This leads to 1-loop diagrams of the form

(6.16)

with some
d_{1}
boundary vertices connected to one bulk vertex, and
d_{2}
connected to the other,
d=d_{1}+d_{2}
.

6.2
Vanishing of the one-loop correction

We’d next like to argue that all one-loop diagrams of the form (
6.16
) actually vanish, in the presence of either
{\mathcal{B}}_{0}
or
{\mathcal{B}}_{\infty}
boundary conditions.

The choice of boundary conditions is important. The boundary condition
{\mathcal{B}}_{0}
is defined by circle reduction of the bulk 3d theory with no line operators inserted. Indeed, when we are computing the algebra
{\mathcal{A}}=\text{End}({\mathcal{B}}_{0})
, we’re just computing the algebra of local operators in the smooth 3d bulk, arranged along a particular (arbitrary) time-like line. Thus, the contribution of 1-loop diagrams (
6.16
) could also have been computed directly in 3d, using the 3d Lorentz-gauge propagator (
2.34
). The 3d computation would insert two copies of the interaction
{\mathcal{I}}
, contracted by two propagators:

\raisebox{-28.90755pt}{\includegraphics[width]{loop-3d}}\begin{array}[]{ll}\sim&\langle\mathbf{p}_{i}(z,\bar{z},t)\mathbf{x}^{i}(w,\bar{w},s)\rangle\langle\mathbf{p}_{j}(w,\bar{w},s)\mathbf{x}_{i}(z,\bar{z},t)\rangle\\
&=\;G^{\rm Lor}(z,\bar{z},t;w,\bar{w},s)G^{\rm Lor}(w,\bar{w},s;z,\bar{z},t)\end{array}

(6.17)

Here we assumed that a particular
\mathbf{p},\mathbf{x}
in the top vertex were contracted with their conjugates in the bottom vertex; but it could just as well have been two
\mathbf{p}
’s in the top vertex with two
\mathbf{x}
’s in the bottom.
The key point is that the 3d propagator is symmetric
G^{\rm Lor}(z,\bar{z},t;w,\bar{w},s)=G^{\rm Lor}(w,\bar{w},s;z,\bar{z},t)
, and is a 1-form (in the odd variables
d\bar{z},dt,d\bar{w},ds
), so (
6.17
) vanishes.

Therefore, in perturbation theory, bulk local operators
{\mathcal{A}}=\text{End}({\mathcal{B}}_{0})
are determined entirely at tree level, with quantum corrections from diagrams of the form (
6.15
).

We can’t immediately use the same argument for
{\mathcal{A}}^{!}=\text{End}({\mathcal{B}}_{\infty})
, because the 3d lift of
{\mathcal{B}}_{\infty}
may be a nontrivial defect near infinity on
\mathbb{CP}^{1}
, as described in Section (
5.1.1
). If the defect is nontrivial, the bulk propagator would need to be slightly modified, and the modification could ruin the symmetry that led to a vanishing of (
6.17
). We’ll use two alternative analyses to establish that there’s no one-loop correction in
{\mathcal{A}}^{!}
:

•

Here, we’ll do a calculation after reduction to 2d, which is slightly non-rigorous due to needing to sum infinite modes running in the loop. We’ll explain that the sums look essentially identical for
{\mathcal{A}}
and
{\mathcal{A}}^{!}
; and since the correction to
{\mathcal{A}}
vanishes by (
6.17
), so must the correction to
{\mathcal{A}}^{!}
.

•

In Section
7
we’ll prove (by checking an MC equation) that our proposed tree-level computation of
{\mathcal{A}}^{!}
is actually the correct Koszul-dual of
{\mathcal{A}}
.

The 2d analysis proceeds as follows. Consider diagram (
6.16
), and suppose (WLOG) we contract some
\mathbf{p}_{i},\mathbf{x}^{j}
in the top vertex with their
\mathbf{x}^{i},\mathbf{p}_{j}
conjugated in the bottom vertex. Expanding fields into modes, this looks like

\raisebox{-28.90755pt}{\includegraphics[width]{loop-2d}}=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\langle\mathbf{p}_{i,n}(r,t)\mathbf{x}^{i}_{-n-1}(r^{\prime},t^{\prime})\rangle\langle\mathbf{p}_{j,n+m}(r^{\prime},t^{\prime})\mathbf{x}^{j}_{-n-m-1}(r,t)\rangle

(6.18)

Namely, at each vertex
{\mathcal{I}}_{0}=\oint{\mathcal{I}}
the mode-numbers of
\mathbf{x},\mathbf{p}
and the total ‘external’ legs must sum to
-2
; and along each propagator the mode-numbers must sum to
-1
. We keep fixed the external mode number, here denoted
m
(as these external edges will be further contracted with local operators whose
A_{\infty}
product we’re computing). We are left with a single of modes
n\in\mathbb{Z}
running through the loop.

In the absence of boundary conditions, the 2d propagator in Lorentz gauge is

\langle\mathbf{p}_{i,n}(r,t)\mathbf{x}^{j}_{m}(r^{\prime},t^{\prime})\rangle=\tfrac{1}{2\pi}\delta_{i}{}^{j}\delta_{n+m,-1}P(r,t;r^{\prime},r^{\prime})\,,

(6.19)

where

P(r,t;r^{\prime},r^{\prime}):=\text{`$d(\theta-\theta^{\prime})$'}=\frac{(t-t^{\prime})d(r-r^{\prime})-(r-r^{\prime})d(t-t^{\prime})}{(t-t^{\prime})^{2}+(r-r^{\prime})^{2}}\,.

(6.20)

Notably,
P
is a one-form (in the odd variables
dr,dt,dr^{\prime},dt^{\prime}
) and is symmetric
P(r,t;r^{\prime},t^{\prime})=P(r^{\prime},t^{\prime};r,t)
.

In the presence of the
{\mathcal{B}}_{0}
boundary, the propagator must be modified (essentially choosing a different gauge) so that it’s compatible with the vanishing of all
n\geq 0
modes of
\mathbf{x}
and
\mathbf{p}
. An easy way to do this is the following. Let
\sigma
(respectively
\sigma^{\prime}
) a reflection across the boundary in the
r,t
coordinates (resp. the
r^{\prime},t^{\prime}
coordinates). It acts by pull-back on the one-form propagator. So if the boundary lies at
r=0
, we have
\sigma P(r,t;r^{\prime},t^{\prime}):=P(-r,t;r^{\prime},t^{\prime})
and
\sigma^{\prime}P(r,t;r^{\prime},t^{\prime}):=P(r,t;-r^{\prime},t^{\prime})
. Then we implement the correct boundary conditions on the propagator in the presence of
{\mathcal{B}}_{0}
by anti-symmetrizing with respect to reflections, for the fields that should vanish at the boundary:

\langle\mathbf{p}_{i,n}(r,t)\mathbf{x}^{j}_{m}(r^{\prime},t^{\prime})\rangle=\tfrac{1}{2\pi}\delta_{i}{}^{j}\delta_{n+m,-1}\times\begin{cases}\tfrac{1}{2}(\text{id}-\sigma)P(r,t;r^{\prime},r^{\prime})&n\geq 0\\
\tfrac{1}{2}(\text{id}-\sigma^{\prime})P(r,t;r^{\prime},r^{\prime})&m\geq 0\,.\end{cases}

(6.21)

For
{\mathcal{B}}_{\infty}
, the opposite
n<0
modes must vanish, so instead we have

\langle\mathbf{p}_{i,n}(r,t)\mathbf{x}^{j}_{m}(r^{\prime},t^{\prime})\rangle=\tfrac{1}{2\pi}\delta_{i}{}^{j}\delta_{n+m,-1}\times\begin{cases}\tfrac{1}{2}(\text{id}-\sigma)P(r,t;r^{\prime},r^{\prime})&n<0\\
\tfrac{1}{2}(\text{id}-\sigma^{\prime})P(r,t;r^{\prime},r^{\prime})&m<0\,.\end{cases}

(6.22)

The result for
both

{\mathcal{B}}_{0}
and
{\mathcal{B}}_{\infty}
and for all modes can be combined in the single expression, if we notice that
\sigma P=-\sigma^{\prime}P
(this follows easily from (
6.20
))). Then

\langle\mathbf{p}_{i,n}(r,t)\mathbf{x}^{j}_{m}(r^{\prime},t^{\prime})\rangle=\langle\mathbf{x}^{j}_{n}(r,t)\mathbf{p}_{i,m}(r^{\prime},t^{\prime})\rangle=\tfrac{1}{4\pi}\delta_{i}{}^{j}\delta_{n+m,-1}(\text{id}-\epsilon_{n}\epsilon_{\mathcal{B}}\sigma)P(r,t;r^{\prime}t^{\prime})\,.

(6.23)

where
\epsilon_{n}=1
if
n\geq 0
and
-1
if
n<0
; and
\epsilon_{B}=1
for
{\mathcal{B}}_{0}
and
-1
for
{\mathcal{B}}_{\infty}
.

Using (
6.23
) we find that the loop contribution (
6.18
) becomes

\displaystyle\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\langle\mathbf{p}_{i,n}(r,t)\mathbf{x}^{i}_{-n-1}(r^{\prime},t^{\prime})\rangle\langle\mathbf{x}^{j}_{-n-m-1}(r,t)\mathbf{p}_{j,n+m}(r^{\prime},t^{\prime})\rangle

\displaystyle\qquad=\frac{1}{16\pi^{2}}\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}(P-\epsilon_{n}\epsilon_{\mathcal{B}}\sigma P)(P-\epsilon_{-n-m-1}\epsilon_{\mathcal{B}}\sigma P)

\displaystyle\qquad=\frac{1}{16\pi^{2}}\epsilon_{\mathcal{B}}\,P\sigma P\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}(\epsilon_{n}-\epsilon_{-n-m-1})

\displaystyle\qquad=\frac{1}{8\pi^{2}}\epsilon_{\mathcal{B}}\,P\sigma P\times\Big(\sum_{n\geq\max(0,-m)}1-\sum_{n<\min(0,m)}1\Big)\,.

(6.24)

Here
P
is shorthand for
P(r,t;r^{\prime},t^{\prime})
; and we have used
P^{2}=(\sigma P)^{2}=0
to simplify.

The virtue of (
6.24
) is that is demonstrates that the 1-loop correction to
{\mathcal{B}}_{0}
and
{\mathcal{B}}_{\infty}
boundary algebras must be identical, up to the sign contained in
\epsilon_{{\mathcal{B}}}
. However, we already know (by the 3d analysis in (
6.17
)) that the correction for
{\mathcal{B}}_{0}
must vanish. Thus the correction for
{\mathcal{B}}_{\infty}
vanishes as well. In terms of the formula (
6.24
), the vanishing should come about through a regularization of the sums in the right-most factored, so that the two divergent pieces with opposite signs exactly cancel each other.

6.3
Tree-level
A_{\infty}
algebras

We can now describe the general form of the perturbative boundary algebras
{\mathcal{A}}
and
{\mathcal{A}}^{!}
. Let’s start with
{\mathcal{A}}
. It is generated by
\{x_{n}^{i},p_{i,n}\}_{n<0}
as in a free theory (
6.9
), with (say)
x
’s bosonic and
p
’s fermionic and charges

\begin{array}[]{c|ccc}&R\;\text{(coh.)}&J\;\text{(spin)}&F\;\text{(parity)}\\
\hline\cr x^{i}_{n}&R_{i}&J_{i}-n-1&\text{even}\\
p_{i,n}&1-R_{i}&-J_{i}-n&\text{odd}\end{array}

(6.25)

where
J_{i}=\tfrac{1}{2}R_{i}
if this comes from the HT twist of a 3d
{\mathcal{N}}=2
QFT.

For a free theory, the algebra is just the graded-commutative algebra
\mathbb{C}[x_{n}^{i},p_{i,n}]
. Interactions correct this by adding
A_{\infty}
operations, and from Sections
6.1
and
6.2
we now know that the only diagrams to consider are the tree-level diagrams (
6.15
) with a single bulk vertex
{\mathcal{I}}_{0}
and any number
d
of boundary vertices. Diagrams with
d
boundary vertices add an
m_{d}
operation to the
A_{\infty}
algebra. An explicit computation of these diagrams, in a 2d B-model in twisted BV formalism, can be found in the lecture notes
[
DNP21
]
.

Let’s use
\{\mathbf{Z}^{a}\}
to denote all 2d superfields (both
\mathbf{x}^{i}_{n}
and
\mathbf{p}_{j,m}
for all
i,j,n,m
) and
\hat{\mathbf{Z}}_{a}
to denote their conjugates under the symplectic pairing in the kinetic term;
e.g.

\mathbf{Z}^{a}=\mathbf{x}^{i}_{n}\quad\Rightarrow\quad\hat{\mathbf{Z}}_{a}=\mathbf{p}_{i,-n-1}\,;\qquad\text{or}\quad\mathbf{Z}^{a}=\mathbf{p}_{j,m}\quad\Rightarrow\quad\hat{\mathbf{Z}}_{a}=\mathbf{x}^{j}_{-m-1}\,.

(6.26)

Then 2d propagators pair
\langle\mathbf{Z}^{a}\hat{\mathbf{Z}}_{b}\rangle\sim\delta^{a}{}_{b}P
(with additional projections as in (
6.23
) in the presence of boundary conditions). In the Feynman diagram (
6.15
), an insertion of
d
boundary vertices
\mathbf{Z}^{a_{1}},...,\mathbf{Z}^{a_{d}}
contracts with conjugates
\hat{\mathbf{Z}}_{a_{1}},...,\hat{\mathbf{Z}}_{a_{d}}
in the bulk interaction vertex
{\mathcal{I}}_{0}
to produce a correction to
m_{d}(Z^{a_{1}},...,Z^{a_{d}})
given by

\displaystyle m_{d}(Z^{a_{1}},...,Z^{a_{d}})=\raisebox{-36.135pt}{\includegraphics[width]{bdy-treeZ}}

\displaystyle\hskip 14.45377pt=\frac{1}{d!}\sum_{\text{contractions}}\int_{\Delta^{d-2}\times(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}_{+})}\mathbf{Z}^{a_{1}}(t_{1})\cdots\mathbf{Z}^{a_{d}}(t_{d})\;:{\mathcal{I}}_{0}(r,t):

\displaystyle\hskip 14.45377pt=\frac{(-1)^{s}}{d!}\int_{\Delta^{d-2}\times(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}_{+})}P(0,t_{1};r,t)\cdots P(0,t_{d};r,t)\frac{{\partial}}{{\partial}\hat{\mathbf{Z}}_{a_{1}}}\cdots\frac{{\partial}}{{\partial}\hat{\mathbf{Z}}_{a_{d}}}:{\mathcal{I}}_{0}(r,t):

(6.27)

where the sign
s=F(\hat{Z}_{a_{d-1}})+F(\hat{Z}_{a_{d-3}})+F(\hat{Z}_{a_{d-5}})+\ldots
is a sum of fermion numbers of every second operator, and is due to commuting operators in
{\mathcal{I}}_{0}
past pairs of
\mathbf{Z}^{a}\mathbf{Z}_{a}
’s in order to get the propagators/contractions to appear in the order shown. The double-dots
::
on
{\mathcal{I}}_{0}
denote normal-ordering; in particular, there are no self-contractions within
{\mathcal{I}}_{0}
. The integral (over a half-space for the bulk insertion, and over a simplex
\Delta^{d-2}
for all order-preserving configurations of intermediate boundary vertices) is convergent.

Up to a factor that can be absorbed in the normalization of
{\mathcal{I}}_{0}
, one now finds corrections to the
m_{d}
operations give by

{\mathcal{A}}:\qquad\begin{array}[]{r@{\;}c@{\;}l}Q(Z^{a}):=m_{1}(Z^{a})&=&\displaystyle\frac{{\partial}}{{\partial}\hat{Z}_{a}}{\mathcal{I}}_{0}\bigg|_{{\mathcal{B}}_{0}}\\[8.5359pt]
\delta m_{2}(Z^{a},Z^{b})&=&\displaystyle(-1)^{F(\hat{Z}_{a})}\frac{1}{2}\frac{{\partial}}{{\partial}\hat{Z}_{a}}\frac{{\partial}}{{\partial}\hat{Z}_{b}}{\mathcal{I}}_{0}\bigg|_{{\mathcal{B}}_{0}}\\[8.5359pt]
m_{3}(Z^{a},Z^{b},Z^{c})&=&\displaystyle(-1)^{F(\hat{Z}_{b})}\frac{1}{6}\frac{{\partial}}{{\partial}\hat{Z}_{a}}\frac{{\partial}}{{\partial}\hat{Z}_{b}}\frac{{\partial}}{{\partial}\hat{Z}_{c}}{\mathcal{I}}_{0}\bigg|_{{\mathcal{B}}_{0}}\\
&\vdots&\\
m_{d}(Z^{a_{1}},...,Z^{a_{d}})&=&\displaystyle(-1)^{F(\hat{Z}_{a_{d-1}})+F(\hat{Z}_{a_{d-3}})+...}\frac{1}{d!}\frac{{\partial}}{{\partial}\hat{Z}_{a_{1}}}\cdots\frac{{\partial}}{{\partial}\hat{Z}_{a_{d}}}{\mathcal{I}}_{0}\bigg|_{{\mathcal{B}}_{0}}\,.\end{array}

(6.28)

Note that the interaction vertex
{\mathcal{I}}_{0}
contains both positive and negative modes. The boundary variables
Z^{a_{i}}
only contain negative modes (as appropriate on
{\mathcal{B}}_{0}
), so the conjugate variables
\hat{Z}_{a_{i}}
only contain positive modes; and the notation ‘
...\big|_{{\mathcal{B}}_{0}}
’ means that we should set all positive modes to zero (evaluating on the boundary condition
{\mathcal{B}}_{0}
) after taking derivatives.

All operations except
m_{2}
(the product) were zero in the free-field algebra
\mathbb{C}[x^{i}_{n},p_{i,n}]_{n<0}
. Therefore, (
6.28
) simply defines what the
m_{d\neq 2}
operations are. The product is graded-commutative in
\mathbb{C}[x^{i}_{n},p_{i,n}]_{n<0}
, and (
6.28
) deforms it by introducing a nonzero graded commutator. Using
F(Z^{a})+F(\hat{Z}_{0})=1
, we find

[Z^{a},Z^{b}]:=m_{2}(Z^{a},Z^{b})-(-1)^{F(Z^{a})F(Z^{b})}m_{2}(Z^{b},Z^{a})=(-1)^{F(Z^{a})+1}\frac{{\partial}}{{\partial}\hat{Z}_{a}}\frac{{\partial}}{{\partial}\hat{Z}_{b}}{\mathcal{I}}_{0}\,.

(6.29)

It is not at all obvious that operations (
6.28
) actually define the structure of an
A_{\infty}
algebra. Indeed, for arbitrary interaction
{\mathcal{I}}_{0}=\oint{\mathcal{I}}
, they
won’t
! The condition that (
6.28
) consistently define an
A_{\infty}
algebra is equivalent to the Maurer-Cartan equation (or the quantum BV master equation) for the 3d bulk interaction
{\mathcal{I}}
; it generalizes the semi-classical condition
\{\int{\mathcal{I}},\int{\mathcal{I}}\}_{\text{BV}}=0
from Section
2.1
and is required for a full perturbative quantization. The condition is guaranteed to hold for HT twists of 3d
{\mathcal{N}}=2
gauge/matter theories
[
GRW21
,
WW24
]
.

Let’s also note that the 3d interaction has charges
R({\mathcal{I}})=2
,
J({\mathcal{I}})=1
,
F({\mathcal{I}})=0
, which implies that
{\mathcal{I}}_{0}=\oint{\mathcal{I}}
has
R({\mathcal{I}}_{0})=2
,
J({\mathcal{I}}_{0})=0
,
F({\mathcal{I}}_{0})=0
. Moreover, 2d conjugate pairs satisfy
R(Z^{a})+R(\hat{Z}_{a})=1
,
J(Z^{a})+J(\hat{Z}_{a})=0
,
F(Z^{a})+F(\hat{Z}_{a})=1
(mod 2). This correctly implies that the
A_{\infty}
operations obtained from Feynman diagrams have
R(m_{d})=2-d
,
J(m_{d})=0
,
F(m_{d})=d
(mod 2).

6.3.1
Modification for
{\mathcal{A}}^{!}

In a free theory, the algebra of local operators on
{\mathcal{B}}_{\infty}
starts out as the graded commutative algebra
\mathbb{C}[x^{i}_{n},p_{i,n}]_{n\geq 0}
, involving positive rather than negative modes, with the same charges as in (
6.25
). In an interacting theory, it’s again corrected by tree-level diagrams.
If we rotate
{\mathcal{B}}_{\infty}
by
180^{\circ}
, and reorient it, to make it an upward-oriented left boundary condition at
r=0
just like
{\mathcal{B}}_{0}
, then its algebra of local operators would be given by
exactly the same
calculation as in (
6.27
), leading to the formulas in (
6.28
). The only difference would be that the boundary operators
Z^{a}
contain positive modes, the conjugates
\hat{Z}_{a}
contain negative modes, and we evaluate
(...)\big|_{{\mathcal{B}}_{\infty}}
after taking derivatives, setting negative modes to zero.

We do, however, need to compensate for relative positions/orientations, since we want to view
{\mathcal{B}}_{\infty}
as the right boundary condition shown in (
6.3
) in order for Koszul duality to work out. This amounts to 1) taking the “opposite” of the naive boundary algebra due to the flipped orientation; and 2) choosing an oriented volume measure for the integral in (
6.27
) (and thus a corresponding order for the propagators) that’s appropriate for the right boundary.

Altogether, this will just introduce some extra overall signs in the
m_{d}
operations, which depend in an intricate way on the fermion numbers of the fields involved. Rather than giving a universal formula for the signs here, we will state what they are for examples we care about: HT twists of 3d
{\mathcal{N}}=2
gauge theories with CS terms, matter, and superpotentials. Then we’ll justify in Section
7
that the signs are correct, by verifying the MC equation for the universal MC element in
{\mathcal{A}}^{!}\otimes{\mathcal{A}}
.

There is one other potential difference between
{\mathcal{A}}^{!}
and
{\mathcal{A}}
. Both boundary algebras could acquire an anomaly, turning them mathematically into curved
A_{\infty}
algebras, from a diagram with zero boundary vertices:

{\mathcal{A}}:\quad m_{0}={\mathcal{I}}_{0}\big|_{{\mathcal{B}}_{0}}\,,\qquad\text{or}\qquad{\mathcal{A}}^{!}:\quad m_{0}=\pm{\mathcal{I}}_{0}\big|_{{\mathcal{B}}_{\infty}}\,.

(6.30)

Setting non-negative modes to zero, the residue
{\mathcal{I}}_{0}\big|_{{\mathcal{B}}_{0}}=0
will always vanish, so
{\mathcal{A}}
has no curvature term. However, if
{\mathcal{I}}
has a term linear in the 3d fields,
e.g.

{\mathcal{I}}=\mathbf{x}^{i}+...
for some
i
, then
{\mathcal{I}}_{0}
will not vanish on
{\mathcal{B}}_{\infty}
,
e.g.

{\mathcal{I}}_{0}\big|_{{\mathcal{B}}_{\infty}}=\oint{\mathcal{I}}\big|_{{\mathcal{B}}_{\infty}}=\mathbf{x}^{i}_{0}
, making
{\mathcal{A}}^{!}
a curved
A_{\infty}
algebra. This is the same anomaly discussed in Footnote
10
. We’ll assume in the following that
{\mathcal{I}}
has no linear terms, though the abstract representation theory of
{\mathcal{A}}^{!}
would work equally well with a curvature term.

6.4
Matter with superpotential: chiral critical locus

We’ll now specialize the general computation of perturbative
{\mathcal{A}},{\mathcal{A}}^{!}
algebras from Section
6.3
to 3d
{\mathcal{N}}=2
theories. We begin with 3d Landau-Ginzburg models, with chiral multiplets
({\mathbf{X}}^{i},{\mathbf{\Psi}}_{i})_{i=1}^{N}
and a polynomial superpotential of degree
d
, depending only on the
{\mathbf{X}}
’s,

W(\mathbf{X})=\tfrac{1}{2}a^{(2)}_{ij}\mathbf{X}^{i}\mathbf{X}^{j}+\tfrac{1}{6}a^{(3)}_{ijk}\mathbf{X}^{i}\mathbf{X}^{j}\mathbf{X}^{k}+\ldots+\tfrac{1}{d!}a^{(d)}_{i_{1}...i_{d}}\mathbf{X}^{i_{1}}...\mathbf{X}^{i_{d}}\,,

(6.31)

with some fully symmetric tensors of coefficients
a^{(k)}
. Reduction to 2d gives
{\mathcal{I}}_{0}=\oint W
,

{\mathcal{I}}_{0}=\tfrac{1}{2}a^{(2)}_{ij}\hskip-7.22743pt\sum_{n+m=-1}\hskip-7.22743pt\mathbf{X}^{i}_{n}\mathbf{X}^{j}_{m}+\tfrac{1}{6}a^{(3)}_{ijk}\hskip-14.45377pt\sum_{n+m+p=-2}\hskip-7.22743pt\mathbf{X}^{i}_{n}\mathbf{X}^{j}_{m}\mathbf{X}_{p}^{k}+\ldots+\tfrac{1}{d!}a^{(d)}_{i_{1}...i_{d}}\hskip-21.68121pt\sum_{n_{1}+...+n_{d}=1-d}\hskip-21.68121pt\mathbf{X}^{i_{1}}_{n_{1}}...\mathbf{X}^{i_{d}}_{n_{d}}\,.

(6.32)

The algebra of bulk local operators
{\mathcal{A}}
now becomes a chiralized version of the derived critical locus of
W
. Applying (
6.28
)–(
6.29
), we find that its underlying vector space is the same as the graded-commutative algebra

{\mathcal{A}}=\mathbb{C}[X^{i}_{n},\psi_{i,n}]_{n<0}\,,

(6.33)

but there are now nonzero operations involving the
\psi
’s, which take the general form

\displaystyle m_{k}(\psi_{i_{1},n_{1}},...,\psi_{i_{k},n_{k}})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{k!}\frac{{\partial}}{{\partial}X^{i_{1}}_{-n_{1}-1}}...\frac{{\partial}}{{\partial}X^{i_{k}}_{-n_{k}-1}}{\mathcal{I}}_{0}(X)\bigg|_{{\mathcal{B}}_{0}}

(6.34)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{k!}\oint z^{n_{1}+...+n_{k}}{\partial}_{X^{i_{1}}}...{\partial}_{X^{i_{k}}}W(X(z))\Big|_{{\mathcal{B}}_{0}}\

for all
1\leq k<d
(with
m_{2}
correcting the graded-commutative product). Explicitly:

\displaystyle Q(\psi_{i,n})

\displaystyle=a^{(2)}_{ij}X^{j}_{n}+\frac{1}{2}a^{(3)}_{ijk}\hskip-7.22743pt\sum_{m+p=n-1}\hskip-7.22743ptX^{j}_{m}X^{k}_{p}+\ldots+\frac{1}{(d-1)!}a^{(d)}_{ii_{2}...i_{d}}\hskip-21.68121pt\sum_{n_{2}+...+n_{d}=n+2-d}\hskip-21.68121ptX^{i_{2}}_{n_{2}}...X^{i_{d}}_{n_{d}}

\displaystyle\tfrac{1}{2}[\psi_{i,n},\psi_{j,m}]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}a^{(3)}_{ijk}X^{k}_{n+m}+\ldots+\frac{1}{2!(d-2)!}a^{(d)}_{iji_{3}...i_{d}}\hskip-28.90755pt\sum_{n_{3}+...+n_{d}=n+m+3-d}\hskip-28.90755ptX^{i_{3}}_{n_{3}}...X^{i_{d}}_{n_{d}}

\displaystyle m_{3}(\psi_{i,n},\psi_{j,m},\psi_{k,p})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{3!}a^{(4)}_{ijkl}X^{l}_{n+m+p}+\ldots+\frac{1}{3!(d-3)!}a^{(d)}_{ijki_{4}...i_{d}}\hskip-28.90755pt\sum_{n_{4}+...+n_{d}=n+m+p+4-d}\hskip-28.90755ptX^{i_{4}}_{n_{4}}...X^{i_{d}}_{n_{d}}

\displaystyle\hskip 28.90755pt\vdots

\displaystyle m_{d-1}(\psi_{i_{1},n_{1}},...,\psi_{i_{d},n_{d}})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{(d-1)!}a^{(d)}_{i_{1}...i_{d}}X^{i_{d}}_{n_{1}+...+n_{d-1}}

(6.35)

Note that all the sums on the RHS are
finite
, since the indices are restricted to be strictly negative. They could be interpreted as sums over lattice simplices. Higher operations
m_{k}
for
k\geq d
all vanish. Any operations involving
X
’s (or both
X
’s and
\psi
’s) vanish as well; the
X
’s remain standard commuting variables.

The relation between
{\mathcal{A}}
and the derived critical locus of
W
is through a quotient operation. Let

{\mathcal{A}}\to{\mathcal{A}}^{(-1)}=\mathbb{C}[X^{i}_{-1},\psi_{i,-1}]

(6.36)

be the map that sends
X^{i}_{n},\psi_{i,n}\mapsto 0
for
n\leq-2
. Inspecting the relations (
6.35
), one finds that this is a morphism of
A_{\infty}
algebras, where the RHS is simply the graded-commutative dg algebra with differential

Q(\psi_{i,-1})=\frac{{\partial}}{{\partial}X^{i}_{-1}}W(X_{-1})\,,

(6.37)

and vanishing higher operations. This makes
{\mathcal{A}}^{(-1)}\simeq\text{dCrit}(W)
isomorphic to the usual derived critical locus.

The Koszul-dual algebra
{\mathcal{A}}^{!}
is nearly identical in structure. It’s generated by the non-negative modes

{\mathcal{A}}^{!}=\mathbb{C}[X^{i}_{n},\psi_{i,n}]_{n\geq 0}\,,

(6.38)

with additional relations among the odd variables
\psi_{i,n}
. After a twist by
(-1)^{F}
as in Section
5.2.4
, we find (and will verify in Section
7
) that all signs in the relations can be neatly repackaged by simply flipping the sign of the superpotential
W\mapsto-W
. Thus the relations are

\displaystyle m_{k}(\psi_{i_{1},n_{1}},...,\psi_{i_{k},n_{k}})

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{k!}\frac{{\partial}}{{\partial}X^{i_{1}}_{-n_{1}-1}}...\frac{{\partial}}{{\partial}X^{i_{k}}_{-n_{k}-1}}{\mathcal{I}}_{0}(X)\bigg|_{{\mathcal{B}}_{\infty}}

(6.39)

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{k!}\oint z^{n_{1}+...+n_{k}}{\partial}_{X^{i_{1}}}...{\partial}_{X^{i_{k}}}W(X(z))\Big|_{{\mathcal{B}}_{\infty}}\

for all
1\leq k<d
; or explicitly

\displaystyle-Q(\psi_{i,n})

\displaystyle=a^{(2)}_{ij}X^{j}_{n}+\frac{1}{2}a^{(3)}_{ijk}\hskip-7.22743pt\sum_{m+p=n-1}\hskip-7.22743ptX^{j}_{m}X^{k}_{p}+\ldots+\frac{1}{(d-1)!}a^{(d)}_{ii_{2}...i_{d}}\hskip-21.68121pt\sum_{n_{2}+...+n_{d}=n+2-d}\hskip-21.68121ptX^{i_{2}}_{n_{2}}...X^{i_{d}}_{n_{d}}

\displaystyle-\tfrac{1}{2}[\psi_{i,n},\psi_{j,m}]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}a^{(3)}_{ijk}X^{k}_{n+m}+\ldots+\frac{1}{2!(d-2)!}a^{(d)}_{iji_{3}...i_{d}}\hskip-28.90755pt\sum_{n_{3}+...+n_{d}=n+m+3-d}\hskip-28.90755ptX^{i_{3}}_{n_{3}}...X^{i_{d}}_{n_{d}}

\displaystyle-m_{3}(\psi_{i,n},\psi_{j,m},\psi_{k,p})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{3!}a^{(4)}_{ijkl}X^{l}_{n+m+p}+\ldots+\frac{1}{3!(d-3)!}a^{(d)}_{ijki_{4}...i_{d}}\hskip-28.90755pt\sum_{n_{4}+...+n_{d}=n+m+p+4-d}\hskip-28.90755ptX^{i_{4}}_{n_{4}}...X^{i_{d}}_{n_{d}}

\displaystyle\hskip 28.90755pt\vdots

\displaystyle-m_{d-1}(\psi_{i_{1},n_{1}},...,\psi_{i_{d},n_{d}})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{(d-1)!}a^{(d)}_{i_{1}...i_{d}}X^{i_{d}}_{n_{1}+...+n_{d-1}}

(6.40)

All the sums on the RHS are still finite, as all the modes are now restricted to be non-negative.

6.4.1
Reformulation via fields

It’s convenient, especially for checking the relations of a dg-shifted Yangian in the next section, to repackage the above
A_{\infty}
relations in terms of generating functions

\begin{array}[]{ll}{\mathcal{A}}:&X^{i}(z)_{+}:=\sum_{n<0}X^{i}_{n}z^{-n-1}\,,\qquad\psi_{i}(z)_{+}:=\sum_{n<0}\psi_{i,n}z^{-n-1}\\[5.69046pt]
{\mathcal{A}}^{!}:&X^{i}(z)_{-}:=\sum_{n\geq 0}X^{i}_{n}z^{-n-1}\,,\qquad\psi_{i}(z)_{-}:=\sum_{n\geq 0}\psi_{i,n}z^{-n-1}\,,\end{array}

(6.41)

as introduced in Section
5.4
. Physically,
X^{i}(z)_{+}
and
\psi_{i}(z)_{+}
represent bulk local operators inserted at a point
z\in\mathbb{C}_{z}
, and a time coordinate that’s been suppressed. The
A_{\infty}
relations govern products and higher products in the locally constant time direction.

Let us also denote

X^{i}(z):=X^{i}(z)_{+}+X^{i}(z)_{-}\,,\qquad\psi_{i}(z):=\psi_{i}(z)_{+}+\psi_{i}(z)_{-}\,.

(6.42)

Then the 2d interaction is

{\mathcal{I}}_{0}=\oint_{y}W(X(y)):=\text{Res}_{y=0}W(X(y))=-\text{Res}_{y=\infty}W(X(y))\,.

(6.43)

Note that the only singularities in
W(y)
are at
y=0
and
y=\infty
, so the contour can be thought of as placed on any counterclockwise circle around the origin. Moreover, the symplectic pairing of conjugate variables
X^{i}_{n}
and
\psi_{i,-n-1}
can be written

\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\delta X^{i}_{n}\wedge\delta\psi_{i,-n-1}=\oint_{y}\delta X(y)\wedge\delta\psi(y)=\oint_{y}\Big[\delta X(y)_{+}\wedge\delta\psi(y)_{-}+\delta X(y)_{-}\wedge\delta\psi(y)_{+}\Big]\,.

(6.44)

Now, for
{\mathcal{A}}
, we have

Q(\psi_{i}(z)_{+})=\frac{\delta}{\delta X^{i}(z)_{-}}{\mathcal{I}}_{0}\bigg|_{{\mathcal{B}}_{0}}=\oint_{y}\frac{{\partial}}{{\partial}X^{i}(y)_{-}}W(X(y))\delta(y-z)\bigg|_{{\mathcal{B}}_{0}}=\oint_{y>z}\frac{{\partial}_{X^{i}_{+}}W(X(y)_{+})}{y-z}\,.

(6.45)

In the second step, we’ve rewritten a functional derivative as a ordinary derivative times a delta function; and in the final step we note that after restricting to
{\mathcal{B}}_{0}
, so the function
{\partial}_{X^{i}_{+}}W(X(y)_{+})
only has a singularity at
y=\infty
, the delta function can be represented by

\delta(y-z)=\frac{1}{y-z}=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{z^{n}}{y^{n+1}}

(6.46)

in the expansion regime
|y|>|z|
. Algebraically, the final integral just takes the residue at
y=z
; it can also be thought of as a contour integral on a circle with
|y|>|z|
. The result of the integral is of course
Q(\psi_{i}(z)_{+})={\partial}_{X^{i}_{+}}W(X(z)_{+})
, in agreement with (
6.35
).

The remaining operations can now be recast the same way, and one can verify algebraically that the answer is equivalent to (
6.35
):

\displaystyle Q(\psi_{i}(z)_{+})

\displaystyle=\oint_{y>z}\frac{{\partial}_{X^{i}_{+}}W(X(y)_{+})}{y-z}={\partial}_{X^{i}_{+}}W(X(z)_{+})\,,

\displaystyle[\psi_{i}(z)_{+},\psi_{j}(w)_{+}]

\displaystyle=\oint_{y>z,w}\frac{{\partial}_{X^{i}_{+}}{\partial}_{X^{j}_{+}}W(X(y)_{+})}{(y-z)(y-w)}=\frac{{\partial}_{X^{i}_{+}}{\partial}_{X^{j}_{+}}W(X(z)_{+})-{\partial}_{X^{i}_{+}}{\partial}_{X^{j}_{+}}W(X(w)_{+})}{z-w}\,,

\displaystyle\hskip-21.68121ptm_{n}(\psi_{i_{1}}(z_{1})_{+},...,\psi_{i_{n}}(z_{n})_{+})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n!}\oint_{y>z_{1},...,z_{n}}\frac{{\partial}_{X^{i_{1}}_{+}}...{\partial}_{X^{i_{n}}_{+}}W(X(y)_{+})}{(y-z_{1})...(y-z_{n})}=\frac{1}{n!}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\frac{{\partial}_{X^{i_{1}}_{+}}...{\partial}_{X^{i_{n}}_{+}}W(X(z_{j})_{+})}{\prod_{k\neq j}(z_{j}-z_{k})}\,.

(6.47)

A special case of this is the equal-space commutator

[\psi_{i}(z)_{+},\psi_{j}(z)_{+}]={\partial}_{z}{\partial}_{X^{i}_{+}}{\partial}_{X^{j}_{+}}W(X(z)_{+})\,.

(6.48)

This relation was found in
[
CDG20
, Eqn. 5.18]
via 3d bulk Feynman diagrams.

For the Koszul-dual
{\mathcal{A}}^{!}
, the basic functional manipulations look like

-Q(\psi_{i}(z)_{-})=\frac{\delta}{\delta X^{i}(z)_{+}}{\mathcal{I}}_{0}\bigg|_{{\mathcal{B}}_{\infty}}=\oint_{y}\frac{{\partial}}{{\partial}X^{i}(y)_{+}}W(X(y))\delta(y-z)\bigg|_{{\mathcal{B}}_{\infty}}=\oint_{y<z}\frac{{\partial}_{X^{i}_{-}}W(X(y)_{-})}{z-y}\,.

(6.49)

In particular, once we restrict to
{\mathcal{B}}_{\infty}
so that
W(X(y)_{-})
is only singular at the origin, the delta function can be represented by

\delta(y-z)=\frac{1}{z-y}=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{y^{n}}{z^{n+1}}\,,

(6.50)

expanded in the regime
|y|<|z|
. The final integral picks up the negative of the residue at
y=z
. Taking into account the sign change
W\mapsto-W
, the operations now look like

\displaystyle Q(\psi_{i}(z)_{-})

\displaystyle=-\oint_{y<z}\frac{{\partial}_{X^{i}_{-}}W(X(y)_{-})}{z-y}=-{\partial}_{X^{i}_{-}}W(X(z)_{-})\,,

\displaystyle[\psi_{i}(z)_{-},\psi_{j}(w)_{-}]

\displaystyle=-\hskip-7.22743pt\oint_{y<z,w}\hskip-7.22743pt\frac{{\partial}_{X^{i}_{-}}{\partial}_{X^{j}_{-}}W(X(y)_{-})}{(z-y)(w-y)}=-\frac{{\partial}_{X^{i}_{-}}{\partial}_{X^{j}_{-}}W(X(z)_{-})-{\partial}_{X^{i}_{-}}{\partial}_{X^{j}_{-}}W(X(w)_{-})}{w-z}\,,

\displaystyle\hskip-21.68121ptm_{n}(\psi_{i_{1}}(z_{1})_{-},...,\psi_{i_{n}}(z_{n})_{-})

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{n!}\oint_{y<z_{1},...,z_{n}}\frac{{\partial}_{X^{i_{1}}_{-}}...{\partial}_{X^{i_{n}}_{-}}W(X(y)_{-})}{(z_{1}-y)...(z_{n}-y)}=-\frac{1}{n!}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\frac{{\partial}_{X^{i_{1}}_{-}}...{\partial}_{X^{i_{n}}_{-}}W(X(z_{j})_{-})}{\prod_{k\neq j}(z_{k}-z_{j})}\,.

(6.51)

6.5
Gauge theory and loop algebras

We’ll now consider HT-twisted perturbative gauge theory, with Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
and matter representation
V
. We allow a Chern-Simons level, but assume for the moment that the matter superpotential vanishes, so the action takes the form (
cf.
Section
2.2
)

S=\int_{\mathbb{C}\times\mathbb{R}}{\mathbf{A}}d^{\prime}{\mathbf{B}}+{\mathbf{\Psi}}d^{\prime}{\mathbf{X}}+{\mathcal{I}}\,,\quad{\mathcal{I}}=\tfrac{1}{2}f^{ab}{}_{c}{\mathbf{A}}_{b}{\mathbf{A}}_{a}{\mathbf{B}}^{c}+\tfrac{1}{2}k^{ab}{\mathbf{A}}_{a}{\partial}{\mathbf{A}}_{b}+(\varphi^{a}_{V})^{i}{}_{j}{\mathbf{\Psi}}_{i}{\mathbf{A}}_{a}{\mathbf{X}}^{j}\,.

(6.52)

It will be easy to add a matter superpotential at the end.

To simplify the structure a bit, it’s useful to observe that this perturbative gauge theory can be rewritten as gauge theory for a semi-direct product algebra

\mathfrak{h}:=\mathfrak{g}\ltimes V\,,\qquad\text{basis}\;\;\{t^{I}\}=\{t^{a}\}\cup\{v^{i}\}\,,

(6.53)

with basis
\{t^{I}\}
combining the bases of
\mathfrak{g}
and
V
. The fields of the new gauge theory are

{\mathbf{A}}_{I=a}={\mathbf{A}}_{a}\,,\quad{\mathbf{A}}_{I=i}={\mathbf{\Psi}}_{i}\,,\qquad{\mathbf{B}}^{I=a}={\mathbf{B}}^{a}\,,\qquad{\mathbf{B}}^{I=i}={\mathbf{X}}^{i}\,,

(6.54)

and the structure constants
f^{IJ}{}_{K}=-f^{JI}{}_{K}
and level
k^{IJ}=k^{JI}
of
\mathfrak{h}
are such that

f^{ab}{}_{c}=f^{ab}{}_{c}\,,\quad f^{ai}{}_{j}=(\varphi_{V}^{a})^{i}{}_{j}\,,\quad f^{ij}{}_{a}=f^{ij}{}_{k}=0\,;\qquad k^{ab}=k^{ab}\,,\quad k^{ai}=k^{ij}=0\,.

(6.55)

In this formulation, the action is simply

S=\int_{\mathbb{C}\times\mathbb{R}}{\mathbf{A}}_{I}d^{\prime}\mathbf{{\mathbf{B}}}^{I}+{\mathcal{I}}\,,\qquad{\mathcal{I}}=\tfrac{1}{2}f^{IJ}{}_{K}{\mathbf{A}}_{J}{\mathbf{A}}_{I}{\mathbf{B}}^{K}+\tfrac{1}{2}k^{IJ}{\mathbf{A}}_{I}{\partial}{\mathbf{A}}_{J}\,,

(6.56)

with reduced 2d interaction term

{\mathcal{I}}_{0}=\tfrac{1}{2}f^{IJ}{}_{K}\hskip-14.45377pt\sum_{m+n+p=-2}\hskip-14.45377pt{\mathbf{A}}_{J,m}{\mathbf{A}}_{I,n}{\mathbf{B}}^{K}_{p}+\tfrac{1}{2}k^{IJ}\hskip-7.22743pt\sum_{n+m=-2}\hskip-7.22743pt(n+1)\,{\mathbf{A}}_{I,n}{\mathbf{A}}_{J,m}\,.

(6.57)

Since the interaction is cubic, both
{\mathcal{A}}
and
{\mathcal{A}}^{!}
will be dg algebras, with
m_{3}
and higher
A_{\infty}
operations vanishing. The algebras are generated by fermions
c_{I,n}
(the leading components of the superfields
{\mathbf{A}}_{I,n}
, so denoted because they are BRST ghosts) and bosons
B^{I}_{n}
(the leading components of
{\mathbf{B}}^{I}_{n}
),

{\mathcal{A}}=\mathbb{C}[B^{I}_{n},c_{I,n}]_{n<0}\,,\qquad{\mathcal{A}}^{!}=\mathbb{C}[B^{I}_{n},c_{I,n}]_{n\geq 0}\,.

(6.58)

From (
6.28
)–(
6.29
) we find that the relations in
{\mathcal{A}}
are

\begin{array}[]{c}[B^{I}_{n},B^{J}_{m}]=-f^{IJ}{}_{K}B^{K}_{n+m}\,,\qquad[B^{I}_{n},c_{J,m}]=f^{IK}{}_{J}c_{K,m+n}\,,\\[5.69046pt]
\displaystyle Q(c_{I,n})=-\tfrac{1}{2}f^{IJ}{}_{K}\hskip-7.22743pt\sum_{m+p=n-1}\hskip-7.22743ptc_{I,m}c_{J,p}\,,\qquad Q(B^{I}_{n})=-n\,k^{IJ}c_{J,n-1}-f^{IJ}{}_{K}\hskip-7.22743pt\sum_{m+p=n-1}\hskip-7.22743pt:c_{J,m}B^{K}_{p}:\,.\end{array}

(6.59a)

The normal ordering in the final equation, coming from normal ordering of the interaction vertex
{\mathcal{I}}_{0}
, is important, since
c
and
B
don’t commute. Indeed, for each
m+p=n-1
we have
:c_{J,m}B_{p}^{K}\!:\,=c_{J,m}B_{p}^{K}+\tfrac{1}{2}f^{KL}{}_{J}c_{L,n-1}=B_{p}^{K}c_{J,m}-\tfrac{1}{2}f^{KL}{}_{J}c_{L,n-1}
. There are
|n|=-n
terms in the sum, whence

\displaystyle Q(B^{I}_{n})

\displaystyle\textstyle=-n(k^{IJ}+h^{IJ})c_{J,n-1}-f^{IJ}{}_{K}\hskip 0.0pt\sum_{m+p=n-1}\hskip 0.0ptc_{J,m}B^{K}_{p}

\displaystyle\textstyle=-n(k^{IJ}-h^{IJ})c_{J,n-1}-f^{IJ}{}_{K}\hskip 0.0pt\sum_{m+p=n-1}\hskip 0.0ptB^{K}_{p}c_{J,m}\,,

(6.59b)

where

h^{IJ}:=\tfrac{1}{2}f^{IK}{}_{L}f^{JL}{}_{K}\,.

(6.60)

is a generalization of the dual Coxter “number” for
\mathfrak{h}
, expressed as an invariant bilinear form. When
\mathfrak{g}
is a simple Lie algebra, we could more simply write

h^{ab}=\big(h^{\vee}+\tfrac{1}{2}I_{2}(V)\big)\delta^{ab}\,,\qquad h^{ai}=h^{ij}=0\,,

(6.61)

where
h^{\vee}
is the usual dual Coxeter number of
\mathfrak{g}
,
I_{2}(V)
is the quadratic index of the matter representation, and
\delta^{ab}
is the Killing form on
\mathfrak{g}
.

The Koszul-dual
{\mathcal{A}}^{!}
should take the same form, up to replacing negative modes with positive modes, and swapping some signs. We claim that the correct choice of signs — after a twist by
(-1)^{F}
as in Sec
5.2.4
— gives

\begin{array}[]{c}[B^{I}_{n},B^{J}_{m}]=f^{IJ}{}_{K}B^{K}_{n+m}\,,\qquad[B^{I}_{n},c_{J,m}]=-f^{IK}{}_{J}c_{K,m+n}\,,\\[5.69046pt]
\displaystyle Q(c_{I,n})=\tfrac{1}{2}f^{IJ}{}_{K}\hskip-7.22743pt\sum_{m+p=n-1}\hskip-7.22743ptc_{I,m}c_{J,p}\,,\qquad Q(B^{I}_{n})=n\,k^{IJ}c_{J,n-1}+f^{IJ}{}_{K}\hskip-7.22743pt\sum_{m+p=n-1}\hskip-7.22743pt:c_{J,m}B^{K}_{p}:\,.\end{array}

(6.62a)

Namely, just as for matter with a superpotential, the sign of every operation is simply reversed. To undo the normal ordering, we now have
:c_{J,m}B_{p}^{K}\!:\,=c_{J,m}B_{p}^{K}-\tfrac{1}{2}f^{KL}{}_{J}c_{L,n-1}=B_{p}^{K}c_{J,m}+\tfrac{1}{2}f^{KL}{}_{J}c_{L,n-1}
for each of the
|n|=n
terms with
m+p=n-1
, so

\displaystyle Q(B^{I}_{n})

\displaystyle\textstyle=n(k^{IJ}+h^{IJ})c_{J,n-1}+f^{IJ}{}_{K}\hskip 0.0pt\sum_{m+p=n-1}\hskip 0.0ptc_{J,m}B^{K}_{p}

\displaystyle\textstyle=n(k^{IJ}-h^{IJ})c_{J,n-1}+f^{IJ}{}_{K}\hskip 0.0pt\sum_{m+p=n-1}\hskip 0.0ptB^{K}_{p}c_{J,m}\,.

(6.62b)

6.5.1
Loop algebra, Lie algebra, and Wilson lines

The Koszul-dual algebra (
6.62
) in gauge theory (with vanishing superpotential) has a nice geometric interpretation. Observe that there are successive quotients of dg algebras

{\mathcal{A}}^{!}\to{\mathcal{A}}^{!}/(c_{I,n\geq 0})\to{\mathcal{A}}^{!}/(c_{I,n\geq 0},B^{I}_{n>0})

(6.63)

The right-most algebra, containing only the zero-modes
\{B^{I}_{0}\}
with zero differential and relations
[B^{I}_{0},B^{J}_{0}]=f^{IJ}{}_{K}B^{K}_{0}
, is isomorphic to the enveloping algebra of the finite Lie algebra
\mathfrak{h}
. The middle algebra, containing all
\{B^{I}_{n\geq 0}\}
with zero differential and relations
[B^{I}_{n},B^{J}_{m}]=f^{IJ}{}_{K}B^{K}_{n+m}
, is isomorphic to the enveloping algebra of the positive loop algebra
\mathfrak{h}[\lambda]
, where ‘
\lambda
’ is a formal loop parameter and we’ve identified
B^{I}_{n}\leftrightarrow B^{I}\lambda^{n}
.
{\mathcal{A}}^{!}
itself is a deformation of (the enveloping algebra of) the loop algebra of the shifted
cotangent
Lie algebra
(T^{*}[1]\mathfrak{h})[\lambda]
. Thus, in summary

\begin{array}[]{ccccc}{\mathcal{A}}^{!}&\to&{\mathcal{A}}^{!}/(c_{I,n\geq 0})&\to&{\mathcal{A}}^{!}/(c_{I,n\geq 0},B^{I}_{n>0})\\
\rotatebox{90.0}{$\approx$}&&\rotatebox{90.0}{$\simeq$}&&\rotatebox{90.0}{$\simeq$}\\
U\big((T^{*}[1]\mathfrak{h})[\lambda]\big)&\to&U\big(\mathfrak{h}[\lambda]\big)&\to&U(\mathfrak{h})\end{array}

(6.64)

Mathematically, the standard convention for the subalgebra of
{\mathcal{A}}^{!}
generated by
\{c_{I,n}\}_{n\geq 0}
, which is a copy of Chevalley-Eilenberg cochains for the negative loop algebra
\lambda^{-1}\mathfrak{h}[\lambda^{-1}]
, would swap the signs of all
c_{I}
’s. In particular,
Q(c_{I,n})=\sum_{m+p=n-1}(-c_{I,m}\tfrac{1}{2}f^{IJ}{}_{K})c_{J,p}
as well as
Q(B^{I}_{n})=...+\sum_{m+p=n-1}:c_{J,m}f^{JI}{}_{K}B^{K}_{p}:
, so that
Q
acts as a gauge transformation with coefficient
c
. The standard math convention is related to our current one by undoing our
(-1)^{F}
twist, which precisely negates all the
c_{I}
’s.

Physically, an important and immediate consequence of the map
{\mathcal{A}}^{!}\to U(\mathfrak{h})
is the existence of Wilson lines. The map can be composed with a further quotient

{\mathcal{A}}^{!}\to U(\mathfrak{h})\to U(\mathfrak{g})

(6.65)

Wilson lines are the finite-dimensional representations of
{\mathcal{A}}^{!}
that factor through this quotient, thus determined by finite-dimensional representations of
\mathfrak{g}
. Given such a representation
\rho:\mathfrak{g}\to\text{End}(W)
on a vector space
W
, extending to

\rho:{\mathcal{A}}^{!}\to\text{End}(W)\,,\qquad\rho(c_{I,n\geq 0})=\rho(B^{I}_{n>0})=\rho(B^{i}_{0})=0\,,\quad\rho(B^{a}_{0})=\rho(t^{a})

(6.66)

the Wilson line itself is constructed by the MC element

\rho(\mu)=\rho\Big(\sum_{n\geq 0}B^{I}_{n}c_{I,-n-1}+c^{I}_{n}B_{I,-n-1}\Big)=\rho(t^{a})c_{a,-1}\,,

(6.67)

in agreement with (
3.43
).

When there is matter, we also see that any representation of
\mathfrak{h}
will define a line operator as well, through the map
{\mathcal{A}}^{!}\to U(\mathfrak{h})
. Such lines combine Wilson lines with matter vortices, induced by couplings of the form
\rho(t^{i})c_{i,-1}=\rho(v^{i})\psi_{i,-1}
, which set

X^{i}(z)\sim\frac{\rho(v^{i})}{z}+\text{regular}

(6.68)

near the support of a line.

6.5.2
Reformulation via fields

We can recast the relations in
{\mathcal{A}}
and
{\mathcal{A}}^{!}
in terms of generating functions

\begin{array}[]{ll}{\mathcal{A}}:&B^{I}(z)_{+}:=\sum_{n<0}B^{I}_{n}z^{-n-1}\,,\qquad c_{I}(z)_{+}:=\sum_{n<0}c_{I,n}z^{-n-1}\,,\\[5.69046pt]
{\mathcal{A}}^{!}:&B^{I}(z)_{-}:=\sum_{n\geq 0}B^{I}_{n}z^{-n-1}\,,\qquad c_{I}(z)_{-}:=\sum_{n\geq 0}c_{I,n}z^{-n-1}\,,\end{array}

(6.69)

just like we did in Section
6.4.1
for matter with a superpotential. Relations (
6.59
) in
{\mathcal{A}}
translate to

\begin{array}[]{c}\displaystyle[B^{I}(z)_{+},B^{J}(w)_{+}]=-f^{IJ}{}_{K}\frac{B^{K}(z)_{+}-B^{K}(w)_{+}}{z-w}\,,\qquad[B^{I}(z)_{+},c_{J}(w)_{+}]=f^{IK}{}_{J}\frac{c_{K}(z)_{+}-c_{K}(w)_{+}}{z-w}\\[11.38092pt]
Q\,c_{I}(z)_{+}=-\tfrac{1}{2}f^{IJ}{}_{K}c_{I}(z)_{+}c_{J}(z)_{+}\,,\qquad Q\,B^{I}(z)_{+}=k^{IJ}{\partial}c_{J}(z)_{+}-f^{IJ}{}_{K}:c_{J}(z)_{+}B^{K}(z)_{+}:\end{array}

(6.70a)

We resolve the normal ordering using the equal-space commutator
[B^{I}(z)_{+},c_{J}(z)_{+}]=f^{IK}{}_{J}{\partial}c_{K}(z)
, which gives
:\!c_{J}(z)_{+}B^{K}(z)_{+}\!:\,=c_{J}(z)_{+}B^{K}(z)_{+}+\tfrac{1}{2}f^{KL}{}_{J}{\partial}c_{L}(z)=B^{K}(z)_{+}c_{J}(z)_{+}-\tfrac{1}{2}f^{KL}{}_{J}{\partial}c_{L}(z)
, whence

\displaystyle Q\,B^{I}(z)_{+}

\displaystyle=(k^{IJ}+h^{IJ}){\partial}c_{J}(z)_{+}-f^{IJ}{}_{K}c_{J}(z)_{+}B^{K}(z)_{+}

\displaystyle=(k^{IJ}-h^{IJ}){\partial}c_{J}(z)_{+}-f^{IJ}{}_{K}B^{K}(z)_{+}c_{J}(z)_{+}\,.

(6.70b)

Relations (
6.62
) in
{\mathcal{A}}^{!}
translate to

\begin{array}[]{c}\displaystyle[B^{I}(z)_{-},B^{J}(w)_{-}]=f^{IJ}{}_{K}\frac{B^{K}(z)_{-}-B^{K}(w)_{-}}{w-z}\,,\qquad[B^{I}(z)_{-},c_{J}(w)_{-}]=-f^{IK}{}_{J}\frac{c_{K}(z)_{-}-c_{K}(w)_{-}}{w-z}\\[11.38092pt]
Q\,c_{I}(z)_{-}=\tfrac{1}{2}f^{IJ}{}_{K}c_{I}(z)_{-}c_{J}(z)_{-}\,,\qquad Q\,B^{I}(z)_{-}=-k^{IJ}{\partial}c_{J}(z)_{-}+f^{IJ}{}_{K}:c_{J}(z)_{-}B^{K}(z)_{-}:\end{array}

(6.71a)

The equal-space commutator still evaluates to
[B^{I}(z)_{-},c_{J}(z)_{-}]=f^{IK}{}_{J}{\partial}c_{K}(z)
, giving
:\!c_{J}(z)_{+}B^{K}(z)_{+}\!:\,=c_{J}(z)_{-}B^{K}(z)_{-}+\tfrac{1}{2}f^{KL}{}_{J}{\partial}c_{L}(z)=B^{K}(z)_{-}c_{J}(z)_{-}-\tfrac{1}{2}f^{KL}{}_{J}{\partial}c_{L}(z)
,

\displaystyle Q\,B^{I}(z)_{-}

\displaystyle=-(k^{IJ}+h^{IJ}){\partial}c_{J}(z)_{-}+f^{IJ}{}_{K}c_{J}(z)_{-}B^{K}(z)_{-}

\displaystyle=-(k^{IJ}-h^{IJ}){\partial}c_{J}(z)_{-}+f^{IJ}{}_{K}B^{K}(z)_{-}c_{J}(z)_{-}\,.

(6.71b)

6.5.3
Adding a superpotential

To finish, we re-introduce a superpotential
W(X)
in the matter sector. The
A_{\infty}
operations from Section
6.4
simply add to the gauge-theory operations above. The condition that
W
is gauge-invariant is essential to obtain a consistent
A_{\infty}
algebra.

We write down the explicit result for easy reference, using the generating-function formulation. We’ve now got to treat gauge and matter fields separately, so the generators of the algebras are
B^{a}(z)_{\pm},c_{a}(z)_{\pm},X^{i}(z)_{\pm},\psi_{i}(z)_{\pm}
, with
+
for
{\mathcal{A}}
and
-
for
{\mathcal{A}}^{!}
. In
{\mathcal{A}}
the relations are

\begin{array}[]{l}\begin{array}[]{l}Q\,c_{a}(z)_{+}=-\tfrac{1}{2}f^{ab}{}_{c}c_{a}(z)_{+}c_{b}(z)_{+}\\[5.69046pt]
Q\,X^{i}(z)_{+}=(\varphi_{V}^{a})^{i}{}_{j}c_{a}(z)_{+}X^{j}(z)_{+}\\[5.69046pt]
Q\,\psi_{i}(z)_{+}=-(\varphi_{V}^{a})^{j}{}_{i}c_{a}(z)_{+}\psi_{j}(z)_{+}+{\partial}_{X^{i}}W(X(z)_{+})\\[5.69046pt]
\end{array}\begin{array}[]{l}[B^{a}(z)_{+},B^{b}(w)_{+}]=-f^{ab}{}_{c}\frac{B^{a}(z)_{+}-B^{b}(w)_{+}}{z-w}\\[2.84544pt]
[B^{a}(z)_{+},c_{b}(w)_{+}]=f^{ac}{}_{b}\frac{c_{c}(z)_{+}-c_{c}(w)_{+}}{z-w}\\[2.84544pt]
[B^{a}(z)_{+},X^{i}(w)_{+}]=-(\varphi^{a}_{V})^{i}{}_{j}\frac{X^{j}(z)_{+}-X^{j}(w)_{+}}{z-w}\\[2.84544pt]
[B^{a}(z)_{+},\psi_{i}(w)_{+}]=(\varphi^{a}_{V})^{j}{}_{i}\frac{\psi_{j}(z)_{+}-\psi_{j}(w)_{+}}{z-w}\\[2.84544pt]
[X^{i}(z)_{+},\psi^{j}(w)_{+}]=-(\varphi^{a}_{V})^{i}{}_{j}\frac{c_{a}(z)_{+}-c_{a}(w)_{+}}{z-w}\end{array}\\[34.14322pt]
\;\;Q\,B^{a}(z)_{+}=(k^{ab}+h^{ab}){\partial}c_{b}(z)_{+}-f^{ab}{}_{c}c_{b}(z)_{+}B^{c}(z)_{+}-(\varphi_{V}^{a})^{i}{}_{j}\psi_{i}(z)_{+}X^{i}(z)_{+}\\[5.69046pt]
[\psi_{i}(z)_{+},\psi_{j}(w)_{+}]=\frac{{\partial}_{X^{i}}{\partial}_{X^{j}}W(X(z)_{+})-{\partial}_{X^{i}}{\partial}_{X^{j}}W(X(w)_{+})}{z-w}\\[5.69046pt]
m_{n}(\psi_{i_{1}}(z_{1}),...,\psi_{i_{n}}(z_{n}))=\oint_{y>z_{1},...,z_{n}}\frac{{\partial}_{X^{i_{1}}}...{\partial}_{X^{i_{n}}}W(X(y)_{+})}{(y-z_{1})...(y-z_{n})}=\sum_{j=1}^{n}\frac{{\partial}_{X^{i_{1}}}...{\partial}_{X^{i_{n}}}W(X(z_{j})_{+})}{\prod_{k\neq j}(z_{j}-z_{k})}\end{array}

(6.72)

with higher operations for
n\leq\text{deg}(W)-1
.

In
{\mathcal{A}}^{!}
, after a twist by
(-1)^{F}
, the relations have all signs reversed. In addition, the denominators representing delta functions in contour integrals are reversed and expanded in the opposite regime. Thus,

\begin{array}[]{l}\begin{array}[]{l}Q\,c_{a}(z)_{-}=\tfrac{1}{2}f^{ab}{}_{c}c_{a}(z)_{-}c_{b}(z)_{-}\\[5.69046pt]
Q\,X^{i}(z)_{-}=-(\varphi_{V}^{a})^{i}{}_{j}c_{a}(z)_{-}X^{j}(z)_{-}\\[5.69046pt]
Q\,\psi_{i}(z)_{-}=(\varphi_{V}^{a})^{j}{}_{i}c_{a}(z)_{-}\psi_{j}(z)_{-}-{\partial}_{X^{i}}W(X(z)_{-})\\[5.69046pt]
\end{array}\begin{array}[]{l}[B^{a}(z)_{-},B^{b}(w)_{-}]=f^{ab}{}_{c}\frac{B^{a}(z)_{-}-B^{b}(w)_{-}}{w-z}\\[2.84544pt]
[B^{a}(z)_{-},c_{b}(w)_{-}]=-f^{ac}{}_{b}\frac{c_{c}(z)_{-}-c_{c}(w)_{-}}{w-z}\\[2.84544pt]
[B^{a}(z)_{-},X^{i}(w)_{-}]=(\varphi^{a}_{V})^{i}{}_{j}\frac{X^{j}(z)_{-}-X^{j}(w)_{-}}{w-z}\\[2.84544pt]
[B^{a}(z)_{-},\psi_{i}(w)_{-}]=-(\varphi^{a}_{V})^{j}{}_{i}\frac{\psi_{j}(z)_{-}-\psi_{j}(w)_{-}}{w-z}\\[2.84544pt]
[X^{i}(z)_{-},\psi^{j}(w)_{-}]=(\varphi^{a}_{V})^{i}{}_{j}\frac{c_{a}(z)_{-}-c_{a}(w)_{-}}{w-z}\end{array}\\[34.14322pt]
\;\;Q\,B^{a}(z)_{-}=-(k^{ab}+h^{ab}){\partial}c_{b}(z)_{-}+f^{ab}{}_{c}c_{b}(z)_{-}B^{c}(z)_{-}+(\varphi_{V}^{a})^{i}{}_{j}\psi_{i}(z)_{-}X^{i}(z)_{-}\\[5.69046pt]
[\psi_{i}(z)_{-},\psi_{j}(w)_{-}]=-\frac{{\partial}_{X^{i}}{\partial}_{X^{j}}W(X(z)_{-})-{\partial}_{X^{i}}{\partial}_{X^{j}}W(X(w)_{-})}{w-z}\\[5.69046pt]
m_{n}(\psi_{i_{1}}(z_{1}),...,\psi_{i_{n}}(z_{n}))=-\oint_{y<z_{1},...,z_{n}}\frac{{\partial}_{X^{i_{1}}}...{\partial}_{X^{i_{n}}}W(X(y)_{-})}{(z_{1}-y)...(z_{n}-y)}=-\sum_{j=1}^{n}\frac{{\partial}_{X^{i_{1}}}...{\partial}_{X^{i_{n}}}W(X(z_{j})_{-})}{\prod_{k\neq j}(z_{k}-z_{j})}\,.\end{array}

(6.73)

6.6
Trivializing translations in Chern-Simons theory

With explicit formulas on hand, we finish by giving an example of how translations are trivialized in an HT QFT that’s topologically invariant — manifesting the structure discussed in Section
5.6
.

We consider twisted
{\mathcal{N}}=2
gauge theory with any Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
and a
nondegenerate
Chern-Simons level

k\in(\text{Sym}^{2}\mathfrak{g}^{*})^{\mathfrak{g}}\,,\qquad\det k\neq 0\,.

(6.74)

We assume there’s no matter. (Note that if matter is incorporated into an extended Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}\ltimes V
as in (
6.53
), the extended Chern-Simons level (
6.55
) will always be degenerate, violating (
6.74
).) Perturbatively, this theory is topological, and equivalent to pure bosonic Chern-Simons at level
k^{\prime}=k-h^{\vee}

[
ACMV17
]
; it admits Kac-Moody
\hat{\mathfrak{g}}_{k^{\prime}}
as a perturbative boundary vertex algebra
[
GW19
,
CDG20
]
.

Recall from Section
5.4.1
that
{\mathcal{A}}^{!}
has translation isomorphisms
\tau_{z}=\text{exp}(z\,T)
, generated by a derivation
T
that acts as

T(B^{a}(z)_{-})=-{\partial}_{z}B^{a}(z)_{-}\,,\qquad T(c_{a}(z)_{-})=-{\partial}_{z}c_{a}(z)_{-}\,.

(6.75)

The derivation
T
has degrees
R(T)=F(T)=0
and
J(T)=1
.

Consider a second algebra derivation
{\mathcal{L}}
of
{\mathcal{A}}^{!}
, given on generators by

{\mathcal{L}}(B^{a}(z)-)=0\,,\qquad{\mathcal{L}}(c_{a}(z)_{-})=(k^{-1})_{ab}B^{b}(z)_{-}\,.

(6.76)

Observe that
{\mathcal{L}}
has
R({\mathcal{L}})=-1
,
F({\mathcal{L}})=\text{odd}
, and
J({\mathcal{L}})=1
. We claim that
[Q,{\mathcal{L}}]=T
.

The proof is straightforward. When applied to
c_{a}(z)_{-}
, we have

\displaystyle[Q,{\mathcal{L}}]c_{a}(z)_{-}

\displaystyle=(k^{-1})_{ab}QB^{b}(z)_{-}+{\mathcal{L}}Qc_{a}(z)_{-}

(6.77)

\displaystyle=(k^{-1})_{ab}\left(-k^{bc}{\partial}_{z}c_{c}(z)_{-}+f^{bc}{}_{d}:B^{d}(z)_{-}c_{c}(z)_{-}:\right)+{\mathcal{L}}\left(\tfrac{1}{2}f^{bc}{}_{a}c_{b}(z)_{-}c_{c}(z)_{-}\right)

The second term above is equal to

\tfrac{1}{2}f^{bc}{}_{a}(k^{-1})_{bd}B^{d}(z)_{-}c_{c}(z)_{-}-\tfrac{1}{2}f^{bc}{}_{a}c_{b}(z)_{-}(k^{-1})_{cd}B^{d}(z)_{-}\,.

(6.78)

Using the fact that
k^{-1}
is
\mathfrak{g}
-invariant, we can write
f^{bc}{}_{a}(k^{-1})_{bd}=-f^{bc}{}_{d}(k^{-1})_{ab}
and
-f^{bc}{}_{a}(k^{-1})_{cd}=f^{bc}{}_{d}(k^{-1})_{ac}=-f^{cb}{}_{d}(k^{-1})_{ac}
, letting us transform (
6.78
) into

\displaystyle-\tfrac{1}{2}f^{bc}{}_{d}(k^{-1})_{ab}B^{d}(z)_{-}c_{c}(z)_{-}-\tfrac{1}{2}f^{cb}{}_{d}(k^{-1})_{ac}c_{b}(z)_{-}B^{d}(z)_{-}

\displaystyle=-\tfrac{1}{2}(k^{-1})_{ab}f^{bc}{}_{d}\big[B^{d}(z)_{-}c_{c}(z)_{-}+c_{c}(z)_{-}B^{d}(z)_{-}\big]

\displaystyle=-(k^{-1})_{ab}f^{bc}{}_{d}:B^{d}(z)_{-}c_{c}(z)_{-}:

(6.79)

This exactly cancels out the contribution
+f^{bc}{}_{d}:B^{d}(z)_{-}c_{c}(z)_{-}:
in the first term, and we find

[Q,{\mathcal{L}}]c_{a}(z)_{-}=-{\partial}_{z}c_{a}(z)_{-}=Tc_{a}(z)_{-}.

(6.80)

A very similar calculation shows that
[Q,{\mathcal{L}}]B^{a}(z)_{-}=-{\partial}_{z}B^{a}(z)_{-}=TB^{a}(z)_{-}
as well.

7
Dg-shifted Yangians in 3d
{\mathcal{N}}=2
theories

In this final section, we will give algebraic proofs that the perturbative Koszul-dual
A_{\infty}
algebras
{\mathcal{A}}^{!}
in HT-twisted 3d
{\mathcal{N}}=2
QFT’s do have the structure of dg-shifted Yangians, as defined in Section
5.4
and summarized in Section
5.5
.

The first half of the section will focus on pure matter with a polynomial superpotential, and the second half on gauge theory. We’ll consider gauge theory with arbitrary matter and Chern-Simons level but with zero superpotential for simplicity; the results of the two halves can be easily combined to establish the general case.

In each type of theory, we will first show that the algebras
{\mathcal{A}}^{!}
defined in Sections
6.4
–
6.5
really are the Koszul duals of local operators
{\mathcal{A}}
, by proving that the universal Maurer-Cartan element

\mu=\sum_{n\geq 0}\Big(x^{i}_{n}p_{i,-n-1}+p_{i,n}x^{i}_{-n-1}\Big)=\oint_{u}\Big[x^{i}(u)_{-}p_{i}(u)_{+}+p_{i}(u)_{-}x^{i}(u)_{+}\Big]\in{\mathcal{A}}^{!}\otimes{\mathcal{A}}

(7.1)

satisfies the Maurer-Cartan equation. Note that we work in conventions where
{\mathcal{A}}^{!}
has been twisted by the automorphism
(-1)^{F}
; otherwise there would be minus sign in the second term of
\mu
.

We then consider the universal r-matrix, which takes the form

r(z)=-\oint_{s<z}\Big[x^{i}(s)_{-}p_{i}^{\prime}(s+z)-p_{i}(s)_{-}x^{i}{}^{\prime}(s+z)_{-}\Big]\in{\mathcal{A}}^{!}\otimes{\mathcal{A}}^{!}[\![z^{-1}]\!]\,,

(7.2)

where
x^{i},p_{i}
are the bosonic and fermionic fields of the theory, respectively, and we use primes to distinguish the two copies of
{\mathcal{A}}^{!}
. There’s also an overall minus sign due to the
(-1)^{F}
twist, which we were not careful about in Section
5.4
, but which will be important here. We will prove that the r-matrix satisfies a Maurer-Cartan equation in
{\mathcal{A}}^{!}\otimes{\mathcal{A}}^{!}[\![z^{-1}]\!]
.

Finally, we will consider the sum of MC elements that controls OPE’s of line operators,

\mu(z)+\mu^{\prime}(0)+r(z)\in{\mathcal{A}}^{!}\otimes{\mathcal{A}}^{!}\otimes{\mathcal{A}}[\![z^{-1},z]\!]\,,

(7.3)

where

\begin{array}[]{l}\mu(z)=\oint_{u}\big[x^{i}(u)_{-}p_{i}(u+z)_{+}+p_{i}(u)_{-}x^{i}(u+z)_{+}\big]\\[8.5359pt]
\mu^{\prime}(0)=\oint_{u}\big[x^{i}{}^{\prime}(u)_{-}p_{i}(u)_{+}+p_{i}^{\prime}(u)_{-}x^{i}(u)_{+}\big]\,,\end{array}

(7.4)

and
r(z)
is as above. We’ll prove that the sum (
7.3
) satisfies an MC equation. By the fundamental correspondece of Koszul duality (
5.6
), this is algebraically equivalent to the coproduct

\Delta_{z}:{\mathcal{A}}^{!}\to{\mathcal{A}}^{!}\otimes_{r(z)}{\mathcal{A}}^{!}[\![z^{-1},z]

(7.5)

being a map of
A_{\infty}
algebras, where
\Delta_{z}
is the free-field coproduct, acting on generators as in (
5.90
).

We summarize the results of this section in

Theorem 7.1
.

The Koszul-dual algebras
{\mathcal{A}}^{!}
in perturbative HT-twisted 3d
{\mathcal{N}}=2
gauge theory (with CS terms and matter), and in
{\mathcal{N}}=2
matter with polynomial superpotential, have the full structure of dg-shifted Yangians.

Caveat:
We comment that one must interpret carefully the MC equation satisfied by
\mu(z)+\mu^{\prime}(0)+r(z)
in equation (
7.3
), since the RHS of this equation is not really an algebra. However, this kind of issue is standard in the study of vertex algebras and spectral R-matrices. The standard solution to this problem is to consider the MC equation not as an equation in these algebras but on a tensor product of smooth modules. In that case, smoothness truncates either the negative powers of
z
in
r(z)
, or the infinite positive powers of
z
in
\mu(z)
, making the resulting expression into a Laurent series (in
z
or
z^{-1}
, depending on whether we use the topology of
{\mathcal{A}}
or
{\mathcal{A}}^{!}
). In the following proofs, any equality should be understood in this sense.

7.1
Matter with superpotential

Suppose we have
N
chiral multiplets
({\mathbf{X}}^{i},{\mathbf{\Psi}}_{i})_{i=1}^{N}
with a polynomial superpotential
W({\mathbf{X}})
of degree
d+1
. Recall from Section
6.4.1
that our algebras are generated by the modes of the generating functions
X^{i}(z)_{\pm},\psi_{i}(z)_{\pm}
, with
+
for
{\mathcal{A}}
and
-
for
{\mathcal{A}}^{!}
, and the
A_{\infty}
operation take the form

\begin{array}[]{l}{\mathcal{A}}:\quad\displaystyle m_{k}(\psi_{i_{1}}(z_{1})_{+},...,\psi_{i_{k}}(z_{k})_{+})=\frac{1}{k!}\oint_{y>z_{1},...,z_{k}}\frac{{\partial}_{X^{i_{1}}_{+}}...{\partial}_{X^{i_{k}}_{+}}W(X(y)_{+})}{(y-z_{1})...(y-z_{k})}\\
{\mathcal{A}}^{!}:\quad\displaystyle m_{k}(\psi_{i_{1}}(z_{1})_{+},...,\psi_{i_{k}}(z_{k})_{+})=-\frac{1}{k!}\oint_{y<z_{1},...,z_{k}}\frac{{\partial}_{X^{i_{1}}_{+}}...{\partial}_{X^{i_{k}}_{+}}W(X(y)_{+})}{(z_{1}-y)...(z_{k}-y)}\end{array}

(7.6)

for all
1\leq k\leq d
, with
m_{n}=0
for
n>d
. As usual,
m_{1}=Q
is the differential and
m_{2}
represents a deformation of the graded-commutative multiplication already present in the free-field algebra, rather than the entire product.

7.1.1
The MC equation for
\mu

We’d like to verify that the universal MC element
\mu=\oint_{u}\big[\psi_{i}(u)_{-}X^{i}(u)_{+}+X^{i}(u)_{-}\psi_{i}(u)_{+}\big]
does indeed satisfy the
A_{\infty}
MC equation in
{\mathcal{A}}^{!}\otimes{\mathcal{A}}
, thus proving that
{\mathcal{A}}^{!}
is in fact the Koszul dual of
{\mathcal{A}}
.
The first term in the MC equation is
Q(\mu)=m_{1}(\mu)
, which we can write as

\displaystyle Q(\mu)

\displaystyle=-\oint_{y<u}\frac{{\partial}_{X^{i}}W(X(y)_{-})X^{i}(u)_{+}}{u-y}+\oint_{y>u}\frac{X^{i}(u)_{-}{\partial}_{X^{i}}W(X(y)_{+})}{y-u}

\displaystyle=-\oint_{y}{\partial}_{X^{i}}W(X(y)_{-})X^{i}(y)_{+}+\oint_{y}X^{i}(y)_{-}{\partial}_{X^{i}}W(X(y)_{+})\,,

(7.7a)

In the first line, the integration is over both
u
and
y
, with the constraint
|y|<|u|
(resp.
|y|>|u|
) initially imposed on
y
; we swap orders of integration and do the integral over
u
in the domain
|u|>|y|
(resp.
|u|<|y|
) to get to the second line.

Similarly,
\mu^{2}=m_{2}(\mu,\mu)=\frac{1}{2}[\mu,\mu]
is given by

\displaystyle\mu^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{-1}{2}\hskip-10.84006pt\oint_{y<u_{1},u_{2}}\hskip-10.84006pt\frac{{\partial}_{X^{i_{1}}}{\partial}_{X^{i_{2}}}W(X(y)_{-})X^{i_{1}}(u_{1})_{+}X^{i_{2}}(u_{2})_{+}}{(u_{1}-y)(u_{2}-y)}+\frac{1}{2}\hskip-10.84006pt\oint_{y>u_{1},u_{2}}\hskip-10.84006pt\frac{X^{i_{1}}(u_{1})_{-}X^{i_{2}}(u_{2})_{-}{\partial}_{X^{i_{1}}}{\partial}_{X^{i_{2}}}W(X(y)_{+})}{(y-u_{1})(y-u_{2})}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}\oint_{y}{\partial}_{X^{i_{1}}}{\partial}_{X^{i_{2}}}W(X(y)_{-})X^{i_{1}}(y)_{+}X^{i_{2}}(y)_{+}+\frac{1}{2}\oint_{y}X^{i_{1}}(y)_{-}X^{i_{2}}(y)_{-}{\partial}_{X^{i_{1}}}{\partial}_{X^{i_{2}}}W(X(y)_{+})\,.

(7.7b)

Again we swap orders of integration, doing all the integrals over
u
’s in the prescribed domains
|u_{i}|>|y|
(resp.
|u_{i}|<|y|
) to get the second line. The pattern continues in a straightforward way, up to

\displaystyle\hskip-21.68121ptm_{d}(\mu^{\otimes d})=\frac{-1}{d!}\hskip-14.45377pt\oint_{y<u_{1},...,u_{d}}\hskip-14.45377pt\frac{{\partial}_{X^{i_{1}}}...{\partial}_{X^{i_{d}}}W(X(y)_{-})X^{i_{1}}(u_{1})_{+}\cdots X^{i_{d}}(u_{d})_{+}}{(u_{1}-y)\cdots(u_{d}-y)}+\frac{1}{d!}\hskip-14.45377pt\oint_{y>u_{1},...,u_{d}}\hskip-14.45377pt(\ldots)

(7.7c)

\displaystyle\hskip-14.45377pt=\frac{-1}{d!}\oint_{y}{\partial}_{X^{i_{1}}}...{\partial}_{X^{i_{d}}}W(X(y)_{-})X^{i_{1}}(y)_{+}\cdots X^{i_{d}}(y)_{+}+\frac{1}{d!}\oint_{y}X^{i_{1}}(y)_{-}\cdots X^{i_{d}}(y)_{-}{\partial}_{X^{i_{1}}}...{\partial}_{X^{i_{d}}}W(X(y)_{+})

Now we sum up the contributions in (
7.7
). We find that each “column” just comprises a Taylor expansion of the finite polynomial
W\big(X(y)_{-}+X(y)_{+}\big)
, either around
X(y)_{-}
(left column) or around
X(y)_{+}
(right column). Therefore,

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{d}m_{k}(\mu^{\otimes k})=-\oint_{y}W\big(X(y)_{-}+X(y)_{+}\big)+\oint_{y}W\big(X(y)_{+}+X(y)_{-}\big)=0\,,

(7.8)

which vanishes, proving that the
A_{\infty}
MC equation is satisfied.

7.1.2
The MC equation for
r

Next, consider the universal r-matrix. It’s useful to shift by
z
and flip the integration contour in
one
of the two terms in
r(z)
, so that
z
only appears in
X
’s rather than in
\psi
’s — because only the
\psi
’s will participate in
A_{\infty}
operations. Thus:

\displaystyle r(z)

\displaystyle=\oint_{s<z}\Big[\psi_{i}(s)_{-}X^{\prime}{}^{i}(s+z)_{-}-X{}^{i}(s)_{-}\psi_{i}^{\prime}(s+z)_{-}\Big]

\displaystyle=\oint_{s<z}\Big[\psi_{i}(s)_{-}X^{\prime}{}^{i}(s+z)_{-}+\psi_{i}^{\prime}(s)_{-}X{}^{i}(s-z)_{-}\Big]\,.

(7.9)

Using the second expression, we find that the
A_{\infty}
operations are

\displaystyle m_{k}(r(z)^{\otimes k})=-\frac{1}{d!}\hskip-14.45377pt\oint_{y<s_{1},...,s_{k}<z}\hskip-14.45377pt\frac{{\partial}_{X^{i_{1}}}...{\partial}_{X^{i_{k}}}W(X(y)_{-})X^{\prime}{}^{i_{1}}(s_{1}+z)_{-}\cdots X^{\prime}{}^{i_{k}}(s_{k}+z)_{-}}{(s_{1}-y)\cdots(s_{k}-y)}

\displaystyle\hskip 72.26999pt-\frac{1}{d!}\hskip-14.45377pt\oint_{y<s_{1},...,s_{k}<z}\hskip-14.45377pt\frac{{\partial}_{X^{\prime}{}^{i_{1}}}...{\partial}_{X^{\prime}{}^{i_{k}}}W(X^{\prime}(y)_{-})X{}^{i_{1}}(s_{1}-z)_{-}\cdots X{}^{i_{k}}(s_{k}-z)_{-}}{(s_{1}-y)\cdots(s_{k}-y)}

(7.10)

\displaystyle\quad=-\frac{1}{d!}\hskip-3.61371pt\oint_{y<z}\!\!\Big[{\partial}_{X^{i_{1}}}...{\partial}_{X^{i_{k}}}W(X(y)_{-})X^{\prime}{}^{i_{1}}(y+z)_{-}\cdots X^{\prime}{}^{i_{k}}(y+z)_{-}\hskip-130.08621pt\raisebox{-21.68121pt}{$+\,{\partial}_{X^{\prime}{}^{i_{1}}}...{\partial}_{X^{\prime}{}^{i_{k}}}W(X^{\prime}(y)_{-})X{}^{i_{1}}(y-z)_{-}\cdots X{}^{i_{k}}(y-z)_{-}\Big]\,.$}

In the first line, the integrals are over
y
and
s_{1},...,s_{k}
within the indicated domains. We swap orders of integration, doing all the
s_{i}
integrals, to obtain the second line.

Summing up all the products, we again find two Taylor expansions of
W
:

\displaystyle\hskip-14.45377pt\sum_{k=1}^{d}m_{k}(r(z)^{\otimes k})

\displaystyle=-\oint_{y<z}\Big[W\big(X(y)_{-}+X^{\prime}(y+z)_{-}\big)+W\big(X^{\prime}(y)_{-}+X(y-z)_{-}\big)\Big]

(7.11)

\displaystyle=-\oint_{y<z}\Big[W\big(X(y)_{-}+X^{\prime}(y+z)_{-}\big)-W\big(X^{\prime}(y+z)_{-}+X(y)_{-}\big)\Big]=0

After flipping the contour in the second term to shift
y\mapsto y+z
, we see that the two terms cancel, so the MC equation for
r(z)
in
{\mathcal{A}}^{!}\otimes{\mathcal{A}}^{!}[\![z^{-1}]\!]
is indeed satisfied.

7.1.3
The twisted coproduct as algebra morphism

Finally, we’d like to show that the sum of terms
\mu(z)+\mu^{\prime}(0)+r(z)
satisfies the MC equation in
{\mathcal{A}}^{!}\otimes{\mathcal{A}}^{!}\otimes{\mathcal{A}}[\![z^{-1},z]\!]
, in order to establish that the twisted coproduct
\Delta_{z}:{\mathcal{A}}^{!}\to{\mathcal{A}}^{!}\otimes_{r(z)}{\mathcal{A}}^{!}[\![z^{-1},z]
is an
A_{\infty}
-algebra morphism.

We use the same form of
r(z)
as in (
7.9
). We also shift
z
into the
X
fields in
\mu(z)
,

\mu(z)=\oint_{u>z}\Big[\psi_{i}(u)_{-}X^{i}(u+z)_{+}+X^{i}(u)_{-}\psi_{i}(u+z)_{+}\Big]=\oint_{u>z}\Big[\psi_{i}(u)_{-}X^{i}(u+z)_{+}+\psi_{i}(u)_{+}X^{i}(u-z)_{-}\Big]\,,

(7.12)

where, in contrast to
r(z)
, no flip of contour is required during the shift. And we have

\mu^{\prime}(0)=\oint_{v>z}\Big[\psi_{i}^{\prime}(v)_{-}X^{i}(v)_{+}+\psi_{i}(v)_{+}X^{\prime}{}^{i}(v)_{-}\Big]\,.

(7.13)

Each
A_{\infty}
product
m_{k}(\mu(z)+\mu^{\prime}(0)+r(z))^{\otimes k}
contains just three types of terms: either
m_{k}
is applied to
k

\psi_{-}
’s, or
k

\psi^{\prime}_{-}
’s, or
k

\psi_{+}
’s (there are no other cross-terms). Evaluating each term using (
7.6
), then swapping orders of integration to do all the respective
s,u,v
integrals, and summing up over all
k
, we find:
