\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{d}m_{k}(\mu(z)+\mu^{\prime}(0)+r(z))^{\otimes k}=-\oint_{y<z}W\Big(X(y)_{-}+X^{\prime}(y+z)_{-}+X(y+z)_{+}\Big)\quad\text{(from $\psi_{-}^{\otimes k}$)}

\displaystyle\hskip 72.26999pt-\oint_{y<z}W\Big(X(y-z)_{-}+X^{\prime}(y)_{-}+X(y)_{+}\Big)\quad\text{(from $\psi^{\prime}_{-}{}^{\otimes k}$)}

(7.14)

\displaystyle\hskip 72.26999pt+\oint_{y>z}W\Big(X(y-z)_{-}+X^{\prime}(y)_{-}+X(y)_{+}\Big)\quad\text{(from $\psi_{+}{}^{\otimes k}$)}

A graphical depiction of the three terms appearing in (
7.14
) looks like this:

(7.15)

The different domains for the integration contours are (as usual) an algebraic prescription for how to expand the integrands and which residues to compute. For example, in the first term (from
\psi_{-}^{\otimes k}
) the integrand has singularities at
y=0
(from
X(y)_{-}
),
y=-z
(from
X^{\prime}(y+z)_{-}
) and
y=\infty
(from
X(y+z)_{+}
), and we are meant to take the residue
only
at
y=0
. Thus the first term is

-\text{Res}_{y=0}W\Big(X(y)_{-}+X^{\prime}(y+z)_{-}+X(y+z)_{+}\Big)\,.

(7.16)

The second and third terms in (
7.14
) have singularities at
y=0
,
y=z
, and
y=\infty
. They nearly cancel against each other; the third term includes the residue at
y=z
while the second term does not, so their sum is

\text{Res}_{y=z}W\Big(X(y-z)_{-}+X^{\prime}(y)_{-}+X(y)_{+}\Big)\,.

(7.17)

After shifting
y\mapsto y+z
, (
7.17
) perfectly cancels (
7.16
). Therefore,
\sum_{k=1}^{d}m_{k}(\mu(z)+\mu^{\prime}(0)+r(z))^{\otimes k}=0
and the MC equation is satisfied.

7.2
Gauge theory

Now consider perturbative gauge theory with Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
, matter representation
V
, and Chern-Simons level
k
. We assume for simplicity that the matter superpotential vanishes; the following arguments combine with those of Section
7.1
in a straightforward way to handle the case of
W\neq 0
as well.

When the superpotential vanishes, we can treat gauge theory with matter as a pure gauge theory for superalgebra
\mathfrak{h}=\mathfrak{g}\ltimes\Pi V
, as discussed in Section
6.5
. The generators of
{\mathcal{A}}
and
{\mathcal{A}}^{!}
are the modes of
B^{I}(z)_{\pm},c_{I}(z)_{\pm}
, where ‘
I
’ indexes a combined basis of
\mathfrak{g}
and
V
. Since the interaction is cubic, all
A_{\infty}
operations
m_{n}
with
n\geq 3
vanish, and we just get dg algebras with relations summarized in Section
6.5.2
. The differentials are

\begin{array}[]{ll}Q\,c_{I}(z)_{+}=-\tfrac{1}{2}f^{IJ}{}_{K}c_{I}(z)_{+}c_{J}(z)_{+}\,,&Q\,B^{I}(z)_{+}=(k^{IJ}+h^{IJ}){\partial}c_{J}(z)_{+}-f^{IJ}{}_{K}c_{J}(z)_{+}B^{K}(z)_{+}\,,\\[5.69046pt]
Q\,c_{I}(z)_{-}=\tfrac{1}{2}f^{IJ}{}_{K}c_{I}(z)_{-}c_{J}(z)_{-}\,,&Q\,B^{I}(z)_{-}=-(k^{IJ}+h^{IJ}){\partial}c_{J}(z)_{-}+f^{IJ}{}_{K}c_{J}(z)_{-}B^{K}(z)_{-}\,.\end{array}

(7.18)

It’s convenient to express the commutators as contour integrals (to enable a swap of orders of integration); they are

\begin{array}[]{ll}\displaystyle[B^{I}(z)_{+},B^{J}(w)_{+}]=\hskip-3.61371pt\oint_{y>z,w}\hskip-3.61371pt\frac{-f^{IJ}{}_{K}B^{K}(y)_{+}}{(y-z)(y-w)}\,,&\displaystyle[B^{I}(z)_{+},c_{J}(w)_{+}]=\hskip-3.61371pt\oint_{y>z,w}\hskip-3.61371pt\frac{f^{IK}{}_{J}c_{K}(y)_{+}}{(y-z)(y-w)}\,,\\
{}\displaystyle[B^{I}(z)_{-},B^{J}(w)_{-}]=\hskip-3.61371pt\oint_{y<z,w}\hskip-3.61371pt\frac{f^{IJ}{}_{K}B^{K}(y)_{-}}{(z-y)(w-y)}\,,&\displaystyle[B^{I}(z)_{-},c_{J}(w)_{-}]=\hskip-3.61371pt\oint_{y<z,w}\hskip-3.61371pt\frac{-f^{IK}{}_{J}c_{K}(y)_{-}}{(z-y)(w-y)}\,.\end{array}

(7.19)

The MC equations for
\mu
,
r(z)
, and the sum
\mu(z)+\mu^{\prime}(0)+r(z)
all follow from manipulations very similar to matter with a superpotential in Section
7.1
. In particular, terms in the MC equations re-sum into Taylor expansions of the interaction terms. However, since the interaction terms in gauge theory contain both bosons and fermions (in particular, both parts of the conjugate
{\mathbf{B}}
and
{\mathbf{A}}
fields), signs are quite tricky to keep track of in these expansions. As the interaction is just cubic, and the MC equations have just two terms to sum, we’ll analyze them directly rather than trying to organize them into Taylor expansions.

7.2.1
The MC equation for
\mu

For the universal MC element
\mu=\oint_{u}\big[c_{I}(u)_{-}B^{I}(u)_{+}+B^{I}(u)_{-}c_{I}(u)_{+}\big]
, we want to show that the MC equation
Q(\mu)+\tfrac{1}{2}[\mu,\mu]=0
is satisfied.

From (
7.18
), relabeling the integration variables
u\mapsto y
, we’ve got

\displaystyle\hskip-21.68121ptQ(\mu)

\displaystyle=\oint_{y}\Big[\tfrac{1}{2}f^{JK}{}_{I}c_{J}(y)_{-}c_{K}(y)_{-}B^{I}(y)_{+}-c_{I}(y)_{-}(k+h)^{IJ}{\partial}c_{J}(y)_{+}+c_{I}(y)_{-}f^{IJ}{}_{K}c_{J}(y)_{+}B^{K}(y)_{+}

\displaystyle\qquad-(k+h)^{IJ}{\partial}c_{J}(y)_{-}c_{I}(y)_{+}+f^{IJ}{}_{K}c_{J}(y)_{-}B^{K}(y)_{-}c_{I}(y)_{+}-\tfrac{1}{2}B^{I}(y)_{-}f^{JK}{}_{I}c_{J}(y)_{+}c_{K}(y)_{+}\Big]

(7.20)

The two terms proportional to
(k+h)
cancel against each other after an algebraic integration by parts:
\oint_{y}{\partial}c_{J}(y)_{-}c_{I}(y)_{+}=-\oint_{y}c_{J}(y)_{-}{\partial}c_{I}(y)_{+}
. For the remaining cubic terms, we look at
\frac{1}{2}[\mu,\mu]
. We compute this by using (
7.19
) and swapping orders of integration, just like in (
7.7
):

\displaystyle\tfrac{1}{2}[\mu,\mu]=

\displaystyle\hskip-21.68121pt\oint_{u,v}\tfrac{1}{2}\Big[c_{I}(u)_{-}c_{J}(v)_{-}[B^{I}(u)_{+},B^{J}(v)_{+}]+\underbrace{[c_{I}(u)_{-}B^{I}(u)_{+},B^{J}(v)_{-}c_{J}(v)_{+}]}_{c_{I-}B^{J}_{-}[B^{I}_{+},c_{J+}]+[c_{I-},B^{J}_{-}]c_{J+}B^{I}_{+}}+\tfrac{1}{2}[B^{I}(u)_{-},B^{J}(v)_{-}]c_{I}(u)_{+}c_{J}(v)_{+}\Big]

\displaystyle=\oint_{y}\Big[-\tfrac{1}{2}c_{I}(y)_{-}c_{J}(y)_{-}f^{IJ}{}_{K}B^{K}(y)_{+}+c_{I}(y)_{-}B^{J}(y)_{-}f^{IK}{}_{J}c_{K}(y)_{+}

(7.21)

\displaystyle\hskip 108.405pt+f^{JK}{}_{I}c_{K}(y)_{-}c_{J}(y)_{+}B^{I}(y)_{+}+\tfrac{1}{2}f^{IJ}{}_{K}B^{K}(y)_{-}c_{I}(y)_{+}c_{J}(y)_{+}\Big]\,.

This exactly cancels the cubic part of
Q(\mu)
after using antisymmetry
f^{IJ}{}_{K}=-f^{JI}{}_{K}
.

7.2.2
The MC equation for
r

Next, we’d like to show that the universal r-matrix

r(z)=\oint_{s<z}\Big[c_{I}(s)_{-}B^{\prime}{}^{I}(s+z)_{-}-B^{I}(s)_{-}c^{\prime}_{I}(s+z)_{-}\Big]

(7.22)

satisfies the MC equation
Q(r(z))+\tfrac{1}{2}[r(z),r(z)]=0
in
{\mathcal{A}}^{!}\otimes{\mathcal{A}}^{!}[\![z,z]
.

We compute
Q(r(z))
directly using (
7.18
),

\displaystyle Q\,r(z)

\displaystyle=\oint_{s<z}\Big[\tfrac{1}{2}f^{JK}{}_{I}c_{J}(s)_{-}c_{K}(s)_{-}B^{\prime}{}^{I}(s+z)_{-}+c_{I}(s)_{-}(k+h)^{IJ}{\partial}c^{\prime}_{J}(s+z)_{-}

(7.23)

\displaystyle\hskip 36.135pt-c_{I}(s)_{-}f^{IJ}{}_{K}c^{\prime}_{J}(s+z)_{-}B^{\prime}{}^{K}(s+z)_{-}+(k+h)^{IJ}{\partial}c_{J}(s)_{-}c^{\prime}_{I}(s+z)_{-}

\displaystyle\hskip 36.135pt-f^{IJ}{}_{K}c_{J}(s)_{-}B^{K}(s)_{-}c^{\prime}_{I}(s+z)_{-}-B^{I}(s)_{-}\tfrac{1}{2}f^{JK}{}_{I}c^{\prime}_{J}(s+z)_{-}c^{\prime}_{K}(s+z)_{-}\Big]

The quadratic terms, proportional to
(k+h)
, vanish after an algebraic integration by parts
\oint{\partial}c_{J}(s)c^{\prime}_{I}(s+z)=-\oint c_{J}(s){\partial}c^{\prime}_{I}(s+z)
.

For the cubic terms, we compare add the
[r(z),r(z)]
commutator. To compute
[r(z),r(z)]
, we shift integration variables (and do contour flips) in
r(z)
to ensure that the individual fields involved in each term of the commutator are independent of
z
. This assures that after applying (
7.19
) we can swap orders of integration and perform all the
s,t
integrals below cleanly to simplify the result. We get:

\displaystyle\tfrac{1}{2}[r(z),r(z)]=

\displaystyle\oint_{s,t<z}\Big[\tfrac{1}{2}c_{I}(s-z)_{-}c_{J}(t-z)_{-}[B^{\prime}{}^{I}(s)_{-},B^{\prime}{}^{J}(t)_{-}]-c_{I}(s-z)_{-}B^{J}(t-z)_{-}[B^{\prime}{}^{I}(s)_{-},c^{\prime}_{J}(t)_{-}]

\displaystyle\hskip 36.135pt-[c_{I}(s)_{-},B^{J}(t)_{-}]c^{\prime}_{J}(t+z)_{-}B^{\prime}{}^{I}(s+z)_{-}+\tfrac{1}{2}[B^{I}(s)_{-},B^{J}(t)_{-}]c^{\prime}_{I}(s+z)_{-}c^{\prime}_{J}(t+z)_{-}\Big]

\displaystyle=\oint_{y<z}\Big[\tfrac{1}{2}c_{I}(y-z)_{-}c_{J}(y-z)_{-}f^{IJ}{}_{K}B^{\prime}{}^{K}(y)_{-}+c_{I}(y-z)_{-}B^{J}(y-z)_{-}f^{IK}{}_{J}c^{\prime}_{K}(y)_{-}

\displaystyle\hskip 36.135pt-f^{JK}{}_{I}c_{K}(y)_{-}c^{\prime}_{J}(y+z)_{-}B^{\prime}{}^{I}(y+z)_{-}+\tfrac{1}{2}f^{IJ}{}_{K}B^{K}(y)_{-}c^{\prime}_{I}(y+z)_{-}c^{\prime}_{J}(y+z)_{-}\Big]

\displaystyle=\oint_{y<z}\Big[-f^{IJ}{}_{K}\tfrac{1}{2}c_{I}(y)_{-}c_{J}(y)_{-}B^{\prime}{}^{K}(y+z)_{-}-f^{IK}{}_{J}c_{I}(y)_{-}B^{J}(y)_{-}c^{\prime}_{K}(y+z)_{-}

\displaystyle\hskip 36.135pt-f^{JK}{}_{I}c_{K}(y)_{-}c^{\prime}_{J}(y+z)_{-}B^{\prime}{}^{I}(y+z)_{-}+\tfrac{1}{2}f^{IJ}{}_{K}B^{K}(y)_{-}c^{\prime}_{I}(y+z)_{-}c^{\prime}_{J}(y+z)_{-}\Big]

(7.24)

In the final line, we’ve shifted the first two terms back to
(y,y+z)
form, flipping contours and adding minus signs. The four terms in (
7.24
) exactly cancel the remaining cubic terms in (
7.23
).

7.2.3
The twisted coproduct as algebra morphism

Finally, we consider the sum
\mu(z)+\mu^{\prime}(0)+r(z)
, with

\displaystyle\hskip-14.45377ptr(z)=\oint_{s<z}\Big[c_{I}(s)_{-}B^{\prime}{}^{I}(s+z)_{-}-B^{I}(s)_{-}c^{\prime}_{I}(s+z)_{-}\Big]=\oint_{s<z}\Big[-c_{I}(s-z)_{-}B^{\prime}{}^{I}(s)_{-}+B^{I}(s-z)_{-}c^{\prime}_{I}(s)_{-}\Big]\,,

\displaystyle\hskip-14.45377pt\mu(z)=\oint_{u}\Big[c_{I}(u)_{-}B^{I}(u+z)_{+}+B^{I}(u)_{-}c_{I}(u+z)_{+}\Big]=\oint_{u>z}\Big[c_{I}(u-z)_{-}B^{I}(u)_{+}+B^{I}(u-z)_{-}c_{I}(u)_{+}\Big]\,,

\displaystyle\hskip-14.45377pt\mu^{\prime}(0)=\oint_{v}\Big[c_{I}^{\prime}(u)_{-}B^{I}(u)_{+}+B^{\prime}{}^{I}(u)_{-}c_{I}(u)_{+}\Big]\,.

(7.25)

We want to show that
Q(\mu(z)+\mu^{\prime}(0)+r(z))+(\mu(z)+\mu^{\prime}(0)+r(z))^{2}=0
. We already know that
\mu,\mu^{\prime},
and
r
separately satisfy the MC equation, so it remains to show that

[\mu(z),\mu^{\prime}(0)]+[r(z),\mu^{\prime}(0)]+[r(z),\mu(z)]=0\,.

(7.26)

[\mu(z),\mu^{\prime}(0)]
gets contributions from commutators of
B_{+},c_{+}
fields. We shift
u\mapsto u-z
in
\mu(z)
, using the second expression in (
7.25
), so that the fields in the commutators are independent of
z
. Applying (
7.19
) and doing the
u,v
integrals then gives

\displaystyle[\mu(z),\mu^{\prime}(0)]=\!\!\oint_{y>z}\!\!\Big[-f^{IJ}{}_{K}c_{I}(y-z)_{-}c^{\prime}_{J}(y)_{-}B_{K}(y)_{+}+f^{IK}{}_{J}c_{I}(y-z)_{-}B^{\prime}{}^{J}(y)_{-}c_{K}(y)_{+}\hskip-216.81pt\raisebox{-25.29494pt}{$+f^{JK}{}_{I}B^{I}(y-z)_{-}c^{\prime}_{J}(y)_{-}c_{K}(y)_{+}\Big]$}

(7.27)

Similarly,
[r(z),\mu^{\prime}(0)]
gets contributions from commutators of
B_{-}^{\prime},c_{-}^{\prime}
fields. We first shift
s\to s-z
in
r(z)
so that these fields are independent of
z
. Then

\displaystyle[r(z),\mu^{\prime}(0)]=\!\!\oint_{y<z}\!\!\Big[f^{IK}{}_{J}c_{I}(y-z)c^{\prime}_{K}(y)_{-}B^{J}(y)_{+}-f^{IJ}{}_{K}c_{I}(y-z)B^{\prime}{}^{K}(y)_{-}c_{J}(y)_{+}\hskip-216.81pt\raisebox{-25.29494pt}{$+f^{JK}{}_{I}B^{I}(y-z)_{-}c^{\prime}_{K}(y)_{-}c_{I}(y)_{+}\Big]$}

(7.28)

Observe that the integrands in
[\mu(z),\mu^{\prime}(0)]
and
[r(z),\mu^{\prime}(0)]
are identical up to a sign, but integration domains differ. Taking the difference leaves behind the algebraic residue at
y=z
, schematically
\oint_{y>z}f(y)-\oint_{y<z}f(y)=\text{Res}_{y=z}f(y)
. After shifting
y\mapsto y+z
to make this the residue at zero
\text{Res}_{y=0}f(y+z)
, we get

\displaystyle[\mu(z),\mu^{\prime}(0)]+[r(z),\mu^{\prime}(0)]=\oint_{y<z}\!\!\Big[-f^{IJ}{}_{K}c_{I}(y)_{-}c^{\prime}_{J}(y+z)_{-}B_{K}(y+z)_{+}

\displaystyle+f^{IK}{}_{J}c_{I}(y)_{-}B^{\prime}{}^{J}(y+z)_{-}c_{K}(y+z)_{+}+f^{JK}{}_{I}B^{I}(y)_{-}c^{\prime}_{J}(y+z)_{-}c_{K}(y+z)_{+}\Big]

(7.29)

This precisely cancels
[r(z),\mu(z)]
, which gets its contributions from
c_{-},B_{-}
commutators. We use the form of
r(z)
on the LHS so that
c_{-},B_{-}
fields are independent of
z
,

\displaystyle[r(z),\mu(z)]=\oint_{y<z}\Big[f^{JK}{}_{I}c_{K}(y)_{-}B^{\prime}{}^{I}(y+z)_{-}c_{J}(y+z)_{+}

(7.30)

\displaystyle\hskip 21.68121pt-f^{IK}{}_{J}c_{K}(y)_{-}c^{\prime}_{I}(y+z)_{-}B^{J}(y+z)_{+}-f^{IJ}{}_{K}B^{K}(y)c^{\prime}_{I}(y+z)_{-}c_{J}(y+z)_{+}\Big]

obtaining
[\mu(z),\mu^{\prime}(0)]+[r(z),\mu^{\prime}(0)]+[r(z),\mu(z)]=0
.

Geometrically, the cancellations among these three terms can be depicted as

(7.31)

Appendix A
The r-matrix and the
\lambda
-bracket

We’d like to explain a particular relation between the universal r-matrix
r(z)\in{\mathcal{A}}^{!}\otimes{\mathcal{A}}^{!}[\![z^{-1}]\!]
and the
\lambda
-bracket
\{-{}_{\lambda}-\}:{\mathcal{A}}\otimes{\mathcal{A}}\to{\mathcal{A}}[\![\lambda]\!]
in a perturbative 3d HT QFT — roughly that the two are inverses of each other. We’ll do this via explicit formulas.
We note, however, that the result parallels other known (or expected) transfers of structure across Koszul duality; in particular:

•

In a 3d perturbative, topological QFT, local operators
{\mathcal{A}}
form an
E_{3}
algebra, with a non-degenerate Poisson bracket of cohomological degree
-2
,
cf.

[
Lur09
,
AF15
,
BBZB+18
]
and references therein for a modern and physical perspective. Their Koszul dual
{\mathcal{A}}^{!}
then naturally forms a quasi-triangular bialgebra, with R-matrix given by the inverse of the Poisson bracket
[
Tam03
,
Tam07
,
Lur09
,
AF14
,
CFG17
]
. See
[
DN24
, Appendix C]
for a geometric derivation of the correspondence, using integrated descendants.

•

In 4d perturbative HT QFT, the structure is similar, except one of the topological directions becomes chiral. Local operators
{\mathcal{A}}
form an (
E_{2}
, chiral) algebra with a
\lambda
-bracket (the chiral extension of a Poisson bracket) of degree
-2

[
OY19b
]
. The Koszul dual
{\mathcal{A}}^{!}
forms a generalized Yangian (a chiralization of a quasi-triangular bialgebra), and one expects that its R-matrix
R(z)
is the inverse of the
\lambda
-bracket.

•

In our 3d perturbative HT QFT’s, local operators
{\mathcal{A}}
form an (
E_{1}
, chiral) algebra, with a
\lambda
-bracket of degree
-1

[
OY19b
,
GW23
,
AKY24
]
. Following the pattern, one would
expect
that the Koszul dual
{\mathcal{A}}^{!}
has an R-matrix given by the inverse of the
\lambda
-bracket — except that due to the degree shift, the R-matrix lands in cohomological degree
+1
. Being an odd element, it’s necessarily infinitesimal (an “r-matrix”), and we claim that it’s precisely our
r(z)
.

Notice that we always have
\text{deg($R/r$-matrix)}-\text{deg}(\text{bracket})=2
;
the
R/r
-matrix and the brackets are chiral precisely when there’s a
\mathbb{C}
direction; and there’s a quantum
R
(vs. infinitesimal
r
) matrix when there are three (vs. two) cohomologically trivial spacetime directions (either
\mathbb{R}
or
\mathbb{C}
).

This suggests another basic case one might consider: in a perturbative 2d topological QFT, local operators
{\mathcal{A}}
form an
E_{2}
algebras, with nondegenerate Poisson bracket of degree -1, better known as the Gerstenhaber bracket
[
WZ92
,
LZ92
,
PS92
,
Get92
]
. Extrapolating from the current paper, one might expect the Koszul dual
{\mathcal{A}}^{!}
to be a “dg-shifted quantum group,”
i.e.
to have an MC element
r\in{\mathcal{A}}^{!}\otimes{\mathcal{A}}^{!}
of degree
1
, that behaves like an infinitesimal r-matrix and inverts the bracket on
{\mathcal{A}}
.
We hope to explore this in future work.

Now let’s come back to perturbative 3d HT QFT’s. Choose a basis
\{x^{i}_{-n-1},p_{i,-n-1}\}_{n\geq 0}=\big\{\tfrac{1}{n!}{\partial}^{n}x^{i},\tfrac{1}{n!}{\partial}^{n}p_{i}\big\}
for the linear subspace
{\mathcal{A}}_{1}\subset{\mathcal{A}}
that generates
{\mathcal{A}}
, which is element-wise dual to the basis
\{p_{i,n},x^{i}_{n}\}_{n\geq 0}
for the linear subspace
{\mathcal{A}}_{1}^{!}
that generates
{\mathcal{A}}^{!}
. Specifically, these bases are dual under the shifted-symplectic pairing in the BV action (
5.29
), which identifies

{\mathcal{A}}^{!}_{1}\simeq(\Pi{\mathcal{A}}_{1}[1])^{*}\,,

(A.1)

where
\Pi
denotes parity shift,
[1]
denotes cohomological shift, and
(\,)^{*}
is linear dual.

Both the
\lambda
-bracket and the r-matrix are ultimately controlled by the free-field propagator.
We can compute the
\lambda
-bracket of basis elements using (
2.16
) to get

\displaystyle\{x^{i}_{-n-1}{}_{\,\lambda\,}p_{j,-m-1}\}(0)

\displaystyle:=\oint_{S^{2}}e^{\lambda z}\frac{1}{n!m!}\big\langle{\partial}^{n}x^{i}{}^{(1)}(z,t){\partial}^{m}p_{j}(0)\big\rangle

\displaystyle=\oint_{S^{2}}e^{\lambda z}\frac{(n+m)!}{n!m!}(-1)^{n}\delta^{i}{}_{j}\frac{1}{z^{n+m+1}}\delta^{(1)}(t)dz

\displaystyle=\frac{(n+m)!}{n!m!}(-1)^{n}\delta^{i}{}_{j}\oint_{S^{1}}e^{\lambda z}z^{-n-m-1}dz=\frac{(-1)^{n}}{n!m!}\delta^{i}{}_{j}\,\lambda^{n+m}

(A.2)

Similarly,
\{p_{i,-n-1}{}_{\,\lambda\,}x^{j}_{-m-1}\}=-\frac{(-1)^{n}}{n!m!}\delta^{j}{}_{i}\,\lambda^{n+m}
. Therefore, we may re-write
r(z)
(using the form (
5.4.2
), after a
(-1)^{F}
twist in
{\mathcal{A}}^{!}
) as

\displaystyle r(z)

\displaystyle=\sum_{m,n\geq 0}\bigg(\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}n+m\\
n\end{array}\bigg)\frac{x^{i}_{n}\otimes p_{i,m}-p_{i,n}\otimes x^{i}_{m}}{(-1)^{n}z^{n+m+1}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{n,m=0}^{\infty}x^{i}_{n}\otimes p_{j,m}\int_{0}^{\infty}d\lambda\,e^{-\lambda z}\,\{p_{i,-n-1}{}_{\,\lambda\,}x^{j}_{-m-1}\}+\sum_{n,m=0}^{\infty}p_{i,n}\otimes x^{j}_{m}\int_{0}^{\infty}d\lambda\,e^{-\lambda z}\,\{x^{i}_{-n-1}{}_{\,\lambda\,}p_{j,-m-1}\}

(A.5)

In greater generality, let
\{\Phi_{\alpha}\}
denote a basis for the linear generators of
{\mathcal{A}}
and
\{\Phi^{\alpha}\}
the dual linear generators for
{\mathcal{A}}^{!}
, canonically paired under the identification (
A.1
). Then

r(z)=\sum_{\alpha,\beta}\Phi^{\alpha}\otimes\Phi^{\beta}\int_{0}^{\infty}d\lambda\,e^{-\lambda z}\,\{\Phi_{\alpha}{}_{\,\lambda\,}\Phi_{\beta}\}\,.

(A.6)
